7.1
). By (
28.2
) and (
29.4
) there are massive, respectively massless Poincaré covariant WL with causal time evolution, which do not localize frame-independently. Consequently, by (
7.2
) they are not causal. Finally, as mentioned there are Poincaré covariant frame-independent WL with causal time evolution, which are not causal, see (
29.4
). Hence the converse of (
7.2
) does not hold.

7.3.
Measurability of the region of influence

For the causality condition Eq. (
7.3
) it is necessary that the region of influence
\Gamma_{\sigma}
(
7.2
) is measurable.
Borel measurability has been abandoned in sec.
6.2

just because
\Gamma_{\sigma}
might not be a Borel set although
\Gamma
itself is Borel. Note that
\Gamma_{\sigma}
can be obtained from
\Gamma
by harmless mappings as
k:\tau\times\sigma\to\tau\times\mathbb{R}
,
k(\mathfrak{y},\mathfrak{x}):=\big(\mathfrak{y},(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\big)
and the projection
\pi_{\sigma}
. One has
\Gamma_{\sigma}=\pi_{\sigma}\big(k^{-1}(\Gamma\times[0,\infty[\,)\big)
. The problem is that the projection of a Borel set need not be Borel, but indeed, by the Measurable Projection Theorem, it is Lebesgue measurable. — The measurability of
\Gamma_{\sigma}
follows also from the analysis of
\Gamma_{\sigma}
given in (
7.3
).
Compare
Leiseifer 2014
[
46
, chapter 2]
, which treats a special case.

(16) Lemma.
Let
\sigma,\tau
be two different spacelike hyperplanes. Let
\Gamma
be any subset of
\tau
and
\Gamma_{\sigma}=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma}\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\geq 0\}
the region of influence of
\Gamma
in
\sigma
. Define
M(\Gamma,\sigma):=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma}\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}>0\}
,
N(\Gamma,\sigma):=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:\sup_{\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=0\}
. Then

(a)

M(\Gamma,\sigma)\subset\Gamma_{\sigma}

and
M(\Gamma,\sigma)
is open in
\sigma

(b)

N(\Gamma,\sigma)
is closed in
\sigma
and a null set of
\sigma

(c)

\Gamma_{\sigma}\subset M(\Gamma,\sigma)\cup N(\Gamma,\sigma)

(d)

\Gamma_{\sigma}
is measurable

Proof.
(a)
M(\Gamma,\sigma)\subset\Gamma_{\sigma}
is obvious.
M(\Gamma,\sigma)
is open in
\sigma
, since
M(\Gamma,\sigma)=\sigma\cap\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma}(\mathfrak{y}+C)
with
C:=\{\mathfrak{z}:\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}>0\}
the open double cone.

(c) Let
\mathfrak{x}\in\Gamma_{\sigma}\setminus M(\Gamma,\sigma)
. Then there is
\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma
with
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\geq 0
. Therefore
\sup_{\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\Gamma}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}\geq 0
. But
\sup_{\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\Gamma}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}>0
is excluded as
\mathfrak{x}\not\in M(\Gamma,\sigma)
. Hence
\mathfrak{x}\in N(\Gamma,\sigma)
.

(d) is an obvious consequence of (a) – (c).

(b) We show that
N(\Gamma,\sigma)
is closed. Let
\mathfrak{x}_{n}\in N(\Gamma,\sigma)
with
\mathfrak{x}_{n}\to\mathfrak{x}
for some
\mathfrak{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Then, obviously,
\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma
and

\sup_{\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\Gamma}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
.
For every
n
there is
\mathfrak{y}_{n}\in\Gamma
such that
-\frac{1}{n}\leq(\mathfrak{x}_{n}-\mathfrak{y}_{n})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
. We use now the representation
\tau=\{\mathfrak{y}:\mathfrak{y}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\tau}=\rho_{\tau}\}
with
\mathfrak{e}_{\tau}=(1,e_{\tau})
,
|e_{\tau}|<1
and
\rho_{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}
, see after (
11.5
). Hence
\mathfrak{y}\in\tau\Leftrightarrow y_{0}=\rho_{\tau}+ye_{\tau}
.
Then
-\frac{1}{n}\leq(x_{n0}-\rho_{\tau}-y_{n}e_{\tau})^{2}-(x_{n}-y_{n})^{2}\leq 0
. As
|y_{n}e_{\tau}|\leq|y_{n}||e_{\tau}|
with
|e_{\tau}|<1
this implies that
(y_{n})_{n}
is bounded. So there is a subsequence of
(\mathfrak{y}_{n})_{n}
, which converges to some
\mathfrak{y}\in\overline{\Gamma}
. It satisfies
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=0
. Hence
\sup_{\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\Gamma}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}=0
, whence
\mathfrak{x}\in N(\Gamma,\sigma)
.

It remains to show that
N(\Gamma,\sigma)
is a null set of
\sigma
. Obviously
N(\Gamma,\sigma)=N(\overline{\Gamma},\sigma)
. Hence assume without restriction that
\Gamma
is closed. Then the foregoing considerations show that for every
\mathfrak{x}\in N(\Gamma,\sigma)
there is
\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma
with
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=0
. Moreover, due to the covariance
g\cdot N(\Gamma,\sigma)=N(g\cdot\Gamma,g\cdot\sigma)
, without restriction
\sigma=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
and either (i)
\tau=\{\mathfrak{y}:y_{0}=sy_{3}\}
for some
s\in]0,1[
or (ii)
\tau=\{\mathfrak{y}:y_{0}=s\}
for some
s\neq 0
.

We turn to the case (i). Let
Q:=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}^{3}:q_{3}\neq 0\}
. For
q\in Q
let
r_{q}:=\frac{s}{4+s}|q_{3}|
and
K_{q}:=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|y-q|\leq r_{q}\}
. Check that
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{y:y_{3}=0\}=\bigcup_{q\in Q}K_{q}
. Put

\Gamma_{q}:=\Gamma\cap\{\mathfrak{y}\in\tau:y\in K_{q}\}
, and
\Gamma_{0}:=\{\mathfrak{y}\in\tau:y_{3}=0\}
. We claim
N(\Gamma,\sigma)\subset\bigcup_{q\in Q\cup\{0\}}N(\Gamma_{q},\sigma)
. Indeed, let
\mathfrak{x}\in N(\Gamma,\sigma)
. Choose
\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma
with
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=0
. There is

q\in Q\cup\{0\}
with
\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma_{q}
. Plainly
\sup_{\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{q}}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
. Hence
\sup_{\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{q}}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}=0
, whence
\mathfrak{x}\in N(\Gamma_{q},\sigma)
.

Note that
N(\Gamma_{0},\sigma)\subset\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:x_{3}=0\}
is a null set of
\sigma
. Hence for the following fix
q\in Q
. Obviously it suffices to consider
\Gamma\subset\{\mathfrak{y}\in\tau:|y-q|\leq r_{q}\}
and
to show that
N(\Gamma,\sigma)
is a null set of
\sigma
.

For
\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma
one has
|q_{3}|-|y_{3}|\leq|q_{3}-y_{3}|\leq|y-q|\leq r_{q}
, whence
|y_{3}|\geq|q_{3}|-r_{q}=\frac{4}{4+s}|q_{3}|
and hence
|y_{0}|=s|y_{3}|\geq 4r_{q}
. Further note that
N(\Gamma,\sigma)
is bounded and hence compact. Indeed, every
\mathfrak{x}\in N(\Gamma,\sigma)
satisfies
|sy_{3}|=|x-y|
for some
\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma
, whence
|x|<2(r_{q}+|q|)
.

For a further reductional step consider
U:=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(u_{1}-q_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}-q_{2})^{2}<r_{q}^{2}\}
. Obviously
\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:x\in R_{q}\,U\}
, where
R_{q}\equiv(q-Rq,R)\in ISO(3)
is any rotation around
q
, provides an open cover of
\sigma
. So finitely many of these sets cover
N(\Gamma,\sigma)
. Then there are also finitely many
R_{q}
such that the sets

\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:x\in R_{q}\,H\}
with
H:=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(u_{1}-q_{1})^{2}+(u_{2}-q_{2})^{2}\leq r_{q}^{2},\,u_{3}-q_{3}\geq 0\}
cover
N(\Gamma,\sigma)
. So it suffices to show that for every
R_{q}
the set
S_{0}:=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:x\in R_{q}\,H\}\cap N(\Gamma,\sigma)
is a null set of
\sigma
. Recall
\Gamma\subset\{\mathfrak{y}\in\tau:|y-q|\leq r_{q}\}
.

So far the reductional steps. Now put
S_{\alpha}:=\mathfrak{a}+S_{0}
with
\mathfrak{a}:=\alpha(0,R_{q}e)
,
e:=(0,0,1)
,
\alpha\geq 0
. We show now that the sets
S_{\alpha}
are mutually disjoint. Let
0\leq\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
and let
\mathfrak{z}\in S_{\alpha}\cap S_{\alpha^{\prime}}
. There are
\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}\in S_{0}
such that
\mathfrak{z}=\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{x}=\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}+\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}
.
As
\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}\in N(\Gamma,\sigma)
, there are
\mathfrak{y},\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\Gamma
with
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=0
,
(\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}=0
. As
\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}\in N(\Gamma,\sigma)
and
\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma
one has
(\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
. Use
\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{x}+\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
. Then
0\geq(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}+\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{a}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}=(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}+2(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})\cdot(\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{a}^{\prime})+(\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{a}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}=-2(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime})(x-y)R_{q}e-(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime})^{2}=(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha)\big(2(x-y)R_{q}e+(x^{\prime}-x)R_{q}e\big)=(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha)(x+x^{\prime}-2y)R_{q}e
. Introduce
u:=R_{q}^{-1}x\in H
, similarly
u^{\prime}
, and
v:=R_{q}^{-1}y
. Note
|v-q|\leq r_{q}
. Then we find
0\geq(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha)(u+u^{\prime}-2v)e=(\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha)(u_{3}+u^{\prime}_{3}-2v_{3})
. —
We exploit now
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=0
. Recall
4r_{q}\leq|y_{0}|
. So
16r_{q}^{2}\leq y_{0}^{2}=|x-y|^{2}=|(u-q)-(v-q)|^{2}\leq 2(|u-q|^{2}+|v-q|^{2})\leq 2r_{q}^{2}+2(u_{3}-q_{3})^{2}+2r_{q}^{2}
, whence
u_{3}-q_{3}>2r_{q}
. Analogously
u^{\prime}_{3}-q_{3}>2r_{q}
holds. Therefore,

u_{3}+u^{\prime}_{3}-2v_{3}=u_{3}-q_{3}+u^{\prime}_{3}-q_{3}-2(v_{3}-q_{3})>2r_{q}+2r_{q}-2r_{q}=2r_{q}>0
. This implies
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha
.

The closed sets
S_{\alpha}
,
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
lie in a compact set of
\sigma
, which has finite measure. By the translational invariance of the Lebesgue measure each
S_{\alpha}
has the same measure. Hence this measure is zero, thus accomplishing the proof for the case (i).

The proof for the case (ii) is simpler as
|y_{0}|=|s|>0
holds independently of
y
. Therefore it suffices to consider the cover of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by
K_{q}:=\{y:|y-q|\leq|s|/4\}
with
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{3}
.∎

8.
Point-Localized Sequences of States

Provided the spectral condition (
4
) holds, (
4
) shows that for a POL with causal time evolution there cannot exist a state localized in a non-essentially dense region
\Delta
, i.e., which is an eigenvector of
T(\Delta)
with eigenvalue
1
. This means that in any state the probability of localization in a closed
\Delta\neq\mathbb{R}^{3}
is less than
1
. So in
[
47
]
a property is introduced, which is mathematically weaker than that of a localized state in
\Delta
but physically equivalent to it, i.e.,
||T(\Delta)||=1
. Indeed, as

||T(\Delta)||=\sup\left\{\langle\varphi,T(\Delta)\varphi\rangle:\,||\varphi||=1\right\}

norm
1
means that the system can be localized within that region
\Delta
by a suitable preparation, not strictly but as accurately as desired.

For obvious physical reasons one is interested in POM with
||T(B)||=1
for every however small open ball
B\neq\emptyset
. In
[
5
, sec. G]
they are called
separated
POM. If the POM
T
is separated, then obviously for every point
b\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

there is a sequence
(\varphi_{n})
of states satisfying

\big\langle\varphi_{n},T(B)\,\varphi_{n}\big\rangle\to 1,\quad n\to\infty

(8.1)

for every open ball
B
around
b
. This means that by a suitable preparation the system can be localized around
b
as good as desired, thus distinguishing
b
from any other point. According to
[
5
, sec. G]
, any
(\varphi_{n})
satisfying Eq. (
8.1
) is called a
sequence of states localized at

b
.

8.1.
Properties of point-localized sequences

Euclidean covariance Eq. (
6.1
) and causal time evolution Eq. (
7.1
) of a POL
T
imply the following transformation properties of point-localized sequences of states.

(17) Theorem.
Let
(\varphi_{n})
be localized at
b
. Then
(U(g)\varphi_{n})
is localized at
g\cdot b
for
g\in ISU(2)
. If
V
is a causal time evolution for
T
then
(V(t)\varphi_{n})
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies
\langle V(t)\varphi_{n},T(B)\,V(t)\varphi_{n}\rangle\to 1
for every ball
B
around
b
with radius greater than
|t|
.

Proof.
If
B
is a ball around
g\cdot b
, then
g^{-1}\cdot B
is a ball around
b
and,
\langle U(g)\varphi_{n},T(B)U(g)\varphi_{n}\rangle=\langle\varphi_{n},T(g^{-1}\cdot B)\varphi_{n}\rangle\to 1
. — Let
V
be causal time evolution and
B
a ball around
b
with radius
r>|t|
. Then
(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)_{t}=\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B^{\prime}
with
B^{\prime}
the ball around
b
with radius
r-|t|
. Therefore
0\leq 1-\langle V(t)\varphi_{n},T(B)\,V(t)\varphi_{n}\rangle=\langle V(t)\varphi_{n},T(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)\,V(t)\varphi_{n}\rangle\leq\langle\varphi_{n},T((\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)_{t})\varphi_{n}\rangle=\langle\varphi_{n},T(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B^{\prime})\varphi_{n}\rangle=1-\langle\varphi_{n},T(B^{\prime})\varphi_{n}\rangle\to 0
.
Hence
\langle V(t)\varphi_{n},T(B)\,V(t)\varphi_{n}\rangle\to 1
.

\Box

In (
8.1
) one cannot expect
\langle V(t)\varphi_{n},T(B)\,V(t)\varphi_{n}\rangle\to 1

for a ball
B
around
b
with radius smaller than
|t|
, as
\{b\}_{t}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|x-b|\leq|t|\}
is the region of influence of the event
(t,b)
. If
T
is even causal then due to causality (
7.2
)
(c)
one has more generally

(18) Theorem.
Let
T
be a causal POL for
W
. Let
(\varphi_{n})
be localized at
b
. Let
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
and put
(t,b^{\prime}):=g\cdot(0,b)
. Then

\langle W(g)\varphi_{n},T(B)\,W(g)\varphi_{n}\rangle\to 1

for every ball
B
around
b^{\prime}
with radius greater than
|t|
. In particular, if
(\varphi_{n})
is localized at
0
, then
(W(A)\varphi_{n})
is so for
A\in SL(2,\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.
Note that
g=(\mathfrak{a},A)=(t,0,I_{2})(0,b^{\prime},I_{2})(0,0,B^{\prime})(0,0,A_{\rho})(0,0,B)(0,-b,I_{2})
as
(t,b^{\prime})=\mathfrak{a}+A\cdot(0,b)
and
A=B^{\prime}A_{\rho}B
some
B,B^{\prime}\in SU(2)
and
A_{\rho}:=\operatorname{e}^{\frac{\rho}{2}\sigma_{3}}
,
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\varphi^{\prime}_{n}:=W(B)W(-b)\varphi_{n}
. By (
8.1
),
(\varphi^{\prime}_{n})
is localized at
0
. Therefore it suffices to show that
(W(A_{\rho})\varphi_{n})
is localized at
0
if
(\varphi_{n})
is so. The result follows applying (
8.1
).

Let
B
be a ball around
0
with radius
r>0
. First we verify that
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)_{A_{\rho}^{-1}}
contains the ball
B^{\prime}
around
0
with radius
\frac{r}{c+|s|}
,
c:=\cosh(\rho)
,
s:=\sinh(\rho)
. Indeed,

\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)_{A^{-1}_{\rho}}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|y-z|>|z_{0}|\text{ for all }(z_{0},z)\in A_{\rho}^{-1}\cdot(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)\}
. Here
A_{\rho}^{-1}\cdot(0,x)=(-x_{3}s,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}c)
. Consider
y\in B^{\prime}
. Then for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B
one has
|y|<\frac{|x|}{c+|s|}\leq\frac{|x|^{2}}{|z|+|z_{0}|}=|z|-|z_{0}|
, i.e.,
|z_{0}|<|z|-|y|
, and hence
|z_{0}|<|z-y|
. — Now, by (
7.2
)
(c)
,
0\leq 1-\langle W(A_{\rho})\varphi_{n},T(B)\,W(A_{\rho})\varphi_{n}\rangle=\langle W(A_{\rho})\varphi_{n},T(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)\,W(A_{\rho})\varphi_{n}\rangle\leq\langle\varphi_{n},T\big((\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)_{A_{\rho}^{-1}}\big)\varphi_{n}\rangle\leq\langle\varphi_{n},T(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B^{\prime})\varphi_{n}\rangle=1-\langle\varphi_{n},T(B^{\prime})\varphi_{n}\rangle\to 0
.
\Box

Finally we add

(19) Lemma.
A normalized linear combination of two sequences of states localized at
b
is localized at
b
. More generally, let
(\alpha_{j})\in\ell^{1}
and, for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(\varphi_{jn})
be a sequence of states
localized at
b
. Suppose that
\varphi_{n}:=c_{n}\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}\varphi_{jn}
with
\sup_{n}|c_{n}|<\infty
is normalized for every
n
. Then
(\varphi_{n})
is localized at
b
.

Proof.
Let
B
be a ball around
b
. One has to show that
||\sqrt{T(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)}\varphi_{n}||^{2}=\langle\varphi_{n},T(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)\varphi_{n}\rangle\to 0
.
Now,
||\sqrt{T(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)}\varphi_{n}||\leq\sum_{j}|\alpha_{j}|\,|c_{n}|\,||\sqrt{T(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)}\varphi_{jn}||\leq\sum_{j}|\alpha_{j}|\,c<\infty
with
c:=\sup_{n}|c_{n}|
. By assumption
c>|c_{n}|\,||\sqrt{T(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B)}\varphi_{jn}||\to 0
,
n\to\infty
for every
j
. The result follows by dominated convergence.
\Box

8.2.
Dilational covariance

Dilational covariance is a useful tool in order to show that a POM is separated (cf.
[
47
]
,
[
5
]
).

(20) Definition.
A POM
T
is said to admit dilational covariance if there is a representation

D
of the group
\mathbb{R}_{+}
of dilations satisfying
D_{\lambda}T(\Delta)D_{\lambda}^{-1}=T(\lambda\Delta)
for all
\lambda>0
.

Plainly, since PM are special POM, (
8.2
) concerns also PM. — By
[
47
, Lemma 3]
a POM is separated if it admits dilational covariance. Such POM seem to be good candidates for the norm-
1
-property, i.e.
T(\Delta)\neq 0\;\Rightarrow\;||T(\Delta)||=1
(cf.
[
42
]
for this property). The following criterion (
8.2
) for separated POM generalizes
[
47
, Lemma 3]
and applies to cases like the Dirac electron considered in sec.
13
, and the Weyl particles (
21
).

(21) Definition.

A POM
T
on
\mathcal{H}
is said to be the
trace
of the PM
E
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}
, if
\mathcal{H}
is a subspace of
\mathcal{K}
and
T(\Delta)\varphi=PE(\Delta)\varphi

\forall

\varphi\in\mathcal{H}
, where
P
is the projection in
\mathcal{K}
on
\mathcal{H}
.

One easily checks that the trace of a PM is a POM. It is well known that actually every POM is the trace of some PM (see
[
48
, Appendix]
).

(22) Theorem

[
5
, Theorem 7]
.
Let the POM
T
be the trace of a PM
E
by the projection
P
. Suppose that
E
admits dilation covariance.
Suppose further that
Q:=\text{s-}\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}D_{\lambda}PD_{\lambda}^{-1}
exists with
Q\neq 0
.
Let
\phi\in\mathcal{K}
with
Q\phi\neq 0
.
Then
||PD_{\lambda}^{-1}\phi||\to||Q\phi||\neq 0

and
\varphi_{\lambda}:=\frac{1}{||PD_{\lambda}^{-1}\phi||}PD_{\lambda}^{-1}\phi
satisfies

||T(B)\varphi_{\lambda}||\to 1,\quad\lambda\to\infty

for every ball
B
around
0
. In particular,
||T(B)||=1
and
(\varphi_{n})
, up to finitely many
n
, is a sequence of states localized at
0
.

Proof.
First,
||PD_{\lambda}^{-1}\phi||=||D_{\lambda}PD_{\lambda}^{-1}\phi||\to||Q\phi||\neq 0
, whence
PD_{\lambda}^{-1}\phi\neq 0
for all large
\lambda>0
. — Now, put
P_{\lambda}:=D_{\lambda}PD_{\lambda}^{-1}
. Keep in mind that
Q^{2}=Q
. Then
||T(B)PD_{\lambda}^{-1}\phi||=||P_{\lambda}E(\lambda B)P_{\lambda}\phi||
, and
||P_{\lambda}E(\lambda B)P_{\lambda}\phi-Q\phi||\leq||P_{\lambda}E(\lambda B)\big(P_{\lambda}\phi-Q\phi\big)||+||P_{\lambda}\big(E(\lambda B)-I\big)Q\phi||+||\big(P_{\lambda}-Q\big)Q\phi||\leq||P_{\lambda}\phi-Q\phi||+||\big(E(\lambda B)-I\big)Q\phi||+||\big(P_{\lambda}-Q\big)Q\phi||\to 0

for
\lambda\to\infty
, since
E(\lambda B)\to I
strongly. This proves
||T(B)PD_{\lambda}^{-1}\phi||\to||Q\phi||
.
\Box

For the proof of (
8.2
) the existence of the limit
Q
is crucial. — In (
8.2
), in case of a POL,
T
is separated because of translational covariance. Moreover, by translating
(\varphi_{n})
in (
8.2
) one gets a sequence of states localized at any given point. If
E
in (
8.2
) is Euclidean covariant, i.e., if
E
is a WL, then
E
automatically admits dilation covariance
[
47
, sec. 2]
. This will be exploited for the Dirac system and the Weyl systems, see sec.
18
and (
21
).

8.3.
Decay of spatial probability

According to Hegerfeldt 1985
[
49
]
the spatial probability in any state
\varphi
satisfies

\langle\varphi,T(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\,|x|>r\})\varphi\rangle\notin\mathcal{O}(\operatorname{e}^{-Kr}),\quad r\to\infty

(8.2)

for
K>2\frac{mc}{\hbar}
, if the causal time evolution for the POL
T
is relativistic with mass
m\geq 0
and semi-bounded energy. So causal time evolution not only forbids localized states in non-essentially dense regions but requires also a limited exponential decay of the spatial probability. The limit is determined by the Compton wavelength
\lambda_{C}=\frac{\hbar}{mc}
. This is an interesting behavior of free relativistic systems. But certainly it is not a paradox as sometimes
it is referred to in the literature, neither does it mean that “arbitrarily good localization”
[
19
, sec. 5]
is impossible. We consider in sec.
18
the POL
T^{e}
for the Dirac electron and in (
21
) the POL
T^{\chi\eta}
for the Weyl particles, which are causal
and
separated.

There are myriads of inequivalent POL for massive as well massless systems. The POL studied for the two-component photon in
[
32
]
,
[
33
]
are not causal and have
states localized in bounded regions. POL for massless irreducible systems with non-zero helicity never have states localized in bounded regions. This is shown in
[
32
, (2.5)]
adapting the proof in Galindo 1968
[
50
, sec. 3]
. For the latter systems,
a complete description of POL admitting dilation covariance can be given, see Castrigiano 1981
[
47
, Theorem]
.

Up to here there are two related problems concerning POL for irreducible relativistic systems, viz., their great variety and their unknown nature. However, as we will see, imposing causality will lead to uniqueness and furnish
an interpretation of the POL.

9.
Causal Systems

We turn to (
C2
) from sec.
5
. Within this frame there is no a-priori-objection to returning to PM describing localizability. In the following definition of a causal system we resume the considerations in sec.
3.1
,
6.2
and (
7.2
) on Poincaré covariance and causality of localization.

(23) Definition.
Let
W
be a representation of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
describing a relativistic quantum system. Let
E
be a causal WL for
W
according to (
7.2
). Then
(W,E)
is called a
causal system
.
8
8

8

Let us note that Poincaré covariant WL with causal time evolution (sec.
7.1
) are called causal systems in
[
5
]
, whereas causal systems according to (
9
) are not considered in
[
5
]
. The present notations leave the name causal system to the more fundamental concept.

Recall that
E
in (
9
) is a map on the set
\mathfrak{S}
of all measurable subsets of spacelike hyperplanes, which is a PM on every spacelike hyperplane and which is Poincaré covariant, viz.
W(g)E(\Gamma)W(g)^{-1}=E(g\cdot\Gamma)
for all
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{S}
,
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
, and which is causal, viz.

E(\Gamma)\leq E(\Gamma_{\sigma})

for all
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{S}
and spacelike hyperplanes
\sigma
. Recall that
\Gamma_{\sigma}=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{z})^{\cdot 2}\geq 0\textrm{ for some }\mathfrak{z}\in\Gamma\}
is the region of influence in
\sigma
of
\Gamma
, see Eq. (
7.2
). Recall also that by (
7.2
)(f)
E
is causal if and only if it is locally orthogonal, i.e.,

E(\Gamma)\,E(\Gamma^{\prime})=0

for all spacelike separated
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}
.

The trace (
8.2
) of a causal WL on a group-invariant subspace clearly yields a causal POL. This is worth to be stated explicitly.

(24) Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a Hilbert space and
\mathcal{H}
a Hilbert subspace of
\mathcal{K}
. Let
(W,E)
be a causal system in
\mathcal{K}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{H}
is invariant under
W
. Then the trace
T
of
E
on
\mathcal{H}
is a causal POL for the subrepresentation
W^{\prime}
of
W
on
\mathcal{H}
.

As mentioned after (
8.2
), every POM is the trace of a PM. Similarly, every POL is the trace of a WL (see e.g.
[
36
]
,
[
32
]
,
[
37
]
,
[
38
]
). However, it is most likely that the causal POL of the massive scalar boson
[
51
]
are not the trace of a causal WL.

10.
A Property of Causal Systems

Let
(W,E)
be a causal system (
9
).
By causality
E(\Delta_{\tau})-E(\Delta)\geq 0
holds for all
\Delta\in\mathfrak{S}
and spacelike hyperplanes
\tau
. Recall that
\Delta_{\tau}:=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\tau:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{z})\cdot(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{z})\geq 0\textrm{ for some }\mathfrak{z}\in\Delta\}
is the region of influence in
\tau
of
\Delta
. We study the particular case where
\Delta
is an open spacelike half-hyperplane
\sigma^{>}
. We write
\sigma^{>}_{\tau}:=(\sigma^{>})_{\tau}
.

The section heading refers to the results (
10
) – (
10
) concerning
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})
.
The proposition (
10
) implies the no-go result in
(
12
)(c) about the representability of the lattice of causally complete regions, and (
10
)(f) is needed for the proof of the Lorentz contraction of the Dirac wave-functions in (
17.1
) and of the Weyl wave-functions (
21.6
).

Consider a spacelike hyperplane
\sigma
and let
\pi\subset\sigma
be a plane. Let
\sigma^{>}
denote one of the two open half-hyperplanes constituting
\sigma\setminus\pi
.
9
9

9

To be explicit write
\sigma=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma}=\rho_{\sigma}\}
for unique
\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma}=(1,e_{\sigma})
with
|e_{\sigma}|<1
and
\rho_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}
(cf. after (
11.5
)). Moreover, there are unique
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|e|=1
and
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:xe=\rho\}
and
\sigma^{>}=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:xe>\rho\}
.
Let
\tau
be a further spacelike hyperplane.

(25) Lemma.
If
\tau
contains
\pi
and if
\tau^{>}
is the half-hyperplane in
\tau\setminus\pi
, which is at the same side of
\pi
as
\sigma^{>}
,
10
10

10

M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
is said to be at the
same side of
\pi
as
\sigma^{>}
if
M\subset\Pi^{\sigma,>}:=\{\mathfrak{x}:\frac{e_{\sigma}e}{1-e^{2}_{\sigma}}(\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma}-\rho_{\sigma})-xe>\rho\}
, which means that the spatial component
of every
\mathfrak{y}\in M
lies in
\sigma^{>}
. Indeed, note that
\hat{\mathfrak{e}}_{\sigma}:=(1-e^{2}_{\sigma})^{-1/2}\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma}
is the direction of time referring to
\sigma
, as
\hat{\mathfrak{e}}_{\sigma}^{\cdot 2}=1
and
\hat{\mathfrak{e}}_{\sigma}\cdot(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{x}^{\prime})=0
for all
\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}\in\sigma
, and that
\mathfrak{y}-s\hat{\mathfrak{e}}_{\sigma}
lies in
\sigma
exactly for
s=(1-e^{2}_{\sigma})^{-1/2}(\mathfrak{y}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma}-\rho_{\sigma})
. — Note that
\{\mathfrak{y}\}
is at the same side of
\pi
as
\sigma^{>}
if the timelike line
l=\mathfrak{y}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma}
intersects
\sigma^{>}
.
then

\tau^{>}_{\sigma}=\sigma^{>},\quad\sigma^{>}_{\tau}=\tau^{>}
and
E(\tau^{>})=E(\sigma^{>})
. In particular,
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})=0
.

Proof.
Because of Poincaré symmetry it satisfies to check
\tau^{>}_{\sigma}=\sigma^{>}
,
\sigma^{>}_{\tau}=\tau^{>}
for the half-hyperplanes of
\sigma:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
and
\tau:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}-\alpha x_{3}=0\}
,
\alpha\in]0,1[
determined by
x_{3}>0
.
11
11

11

Explicitly one has
\sigma=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
for
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
if and only if
g=\big((\rho_{\sigma},0),Q(\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma})\big)(b,B)
with arbitrary
(b,B)\in ISU(2)
and
Q
the canonical cross section.
Put
\mathfrak{d}=(d_{0},d):=Q(\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma})^{-1}\cdot(0,e)
. Note
\mathfrak{d}^{\cdot 2}=-1
, whence
|d|^{2}=1+d_{0}^{2}>0
. Then, in addition to
\sigma=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
, one has also
\pi=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}=0\}
if and only if
bd=\rho
,
B^{-1}\cdot d=\pm|d|(0,0,1)
. If
(b,B)
is specified in this way then one checks
\Pi^{\sigma,>}=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{3}>0\}
using
d_{0}=(1-e^{2}_{\sigma})^{-1/2}e_{\sigma}e
, and one finds
\tau=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}-\alpha x_{3}=0\}
for some
|\alpha|<1
. If necessary then replace
B
by
B\operatorname{i}\sigma_{2}
to ensure
\alpha\geq 0
, as
\operatorname{i}\sigma_{2}
acts on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
by
\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,1,-1)
.

So
E(\tau^{>})=E(\sigma^{>})
holds by causality (
7.2
)(a) applied to
\sigma^{>}
and
\tau^{>}
.∎

Analogous equalities hold true for the closed half-hyperplanes
\sigma^{\geq}
,
\tau^{\geq}
. Since the closed and respective open half-hyperplanes differ by a Lebesgue null set only, one has also
E(\sigma^{>})=E(\sigma^{\geq})
,
E(\tau^{>})=E(\tau^{\geq})
. — Note that
\tau=g\cdot\sigma
holds for some
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
satisfying
g\cdot\pi=\pi
. Then
W(g)
commutes with
E(\sigma^{>})
by Eq. (
3.1
) as
E(\sigma^{>})=E(g\cdot\sigma^{>})
.

Now we turn to the more interesting case that
\sigma
and
\tau
do not intersect.
12
12

12

Explicitly,
\sigma=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\rho_{\sigma}\}
,
\tau=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\rho_{\tau}\}
with
\mathfrak{e}=(1,e)
,
|e|<1
. Then
\sigma=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
and
\tau=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=t\}
with
t:=(1-e^{2}_{\sigma})^{-1/2}(\rho_{\tau}-\rho_{\sigma})
for
g=\big((\rho_{\sigma},0),Q(\mathfrak{e})\big)(b,B)
with arbitrary
(b,B)\in ISU(2)
.

(26) Lemma.
If
\tau
does not intersect
\sigma
, and if
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
is any rotation satisfying
g\cdot\pi=\pi
and
g\cdot\sigma^{>}=\sigma\setminus\sigma^{\geq}
,
then

\big(E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})\big)+\big(E(g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(g\cdot\sigma^{>})\big)=E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau}\cap(g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau}))

and the eigenspaces of the non-trivial projection
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau}\cap(g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau}))
are infinite dimensional.

Proof.
Note
E(g\cdot\sigma^{>})=E(\sigma\setminus\sigma^{\geq})=I-E(\sigma^{\geq})=I-E(\sigma^{>})
,
I-E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})=E(\tau\setminus\sigma^{>}_{\tau})
, and
(g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau})\supset(\tau\setminus\sigma^{>}_{\tau})
. As to the latter, it suffices to check that
\sigma^{>}_{\tau}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\alpha,x_{3}>-|\alpha|\}

and

g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\alpha,x_{3}<|\alpha|\}

for
\sigma^{>}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,x_{3}>0\}
,
\tau=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\alpha\}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, and
g=\operatorname{i}\sigma_{2}
, which acts on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
by
\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,1,-1)
.
This shows in addition that neither
(g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau})\setminus(\tau\setminus\sigma^{>}_{\tau})=(g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau})\cap\sigma^{>}_{\tau}
nor its complement is a
Lebesgue null set. Hence the eigenspaces of
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau}\cap(g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau}))
are infinite dimensional.
∎

Using obvious notations the formula in (
10
) reads more easily as

\big(E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})\big)+\big(E(\sigma^{<}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{<})\big)=E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau}\cap\sigma^{<}_{\tau})

(10.1)

due to the relations
g\cdot\sigma^{>}=\sigma^{<}
and
g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau}=\sigma^{<}_{\tau}
. Moreover, remember

E(\sigma^{<}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{<})=W(g)\big(E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})\big)W(g)^{-1}

(10.2)

with
g
from (
10
).

(27) Proposition.
Let
\sigma
,
\tau
be two non-intersecting spacelike hyperplanes. Then the eigenspaces of the
non-trivial projection
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})
are infinite dimensional. The same holds true for the projection
E(\sigma^{<}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{<})
. Moreover,
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})
and
E(\sigma^{<}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{<})
are orthogonal.

Proof.
Since
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})\geq E(\sigma^{>})
by causality,
P^{<}:=E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})\geq 0
is a projection. Analogously define
P^{>}
. Let
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
be as in (
10
). Due to Eq. (
3.1
),
E(g\cdot\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(g\cdot\sigma^{>})=W(g)P^{<}W(g)^{-1}
is a projection, too. By (
10
) their sum is a projection with infinite dimensional eigenspaces. Therefore
P^{<}
and
W(g)P^{<}W(g)^{-1}
are orthogonal and none of the four eigenspaces is finite dimensional. It remains to note that
P^{>}=W(g)P^{<}W(g)^{-1}
. ∎

-5,0)(3.5,4.2)

Recall
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})\geq E(\sigma^{>})
. Hence

E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})=E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})E(\sigma^{<})

(10.3)

as well the analogous formula, where
<
and
>
are interchanged.

We are going to characterize the ranges of
these projections. Let
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
determine the time coordinate for the spacelike hyperplane
\sigma
. It satisfies
\mathfrak{e}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=1
,
\mathfrak{e}\cdot(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{x}^{\prime})=0
for all
\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}\in\sigma
, and
e_{0}>0
.
\mathfrak{e}
is uniquely determined. Then
\tau=\alpha\mathfrak{e}+\sigma
for some unique
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\tau^{>}
be determined with respect to the plane
\alpha\mathfrak{e}+\pi
in
\tau
such that
\tau^{>}=\alpha\mathfrak{e}+\sigma^{>}
. Then
\sigma^{>}_{\tau}=\alpha\mathfrak{e}+\tau^{>}_{\sigma}
. By Poincaré symmetry it suffices to check all the foregoing relations for

\sigma=\mathfrak{a}+\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
,
\sigma^{>}=\mathfrak{a}+\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,x_{3}>0\}
,
\mathfrak{e}=(1,0,0,0)
, and
\tau=\mathfrak{a}+\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\alpha\}
,
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. As a consequence one has

E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})=W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}

(10.4)

as well the formula for
>
replaced with
<
.

(28) Proposition.
Let
\sigma
,
\tau
be two non-intersecting spacelike hyperplanes. Then a state
\varphi
is in the range of
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})
if and only if
\varphi
is localized in
\sigma^{<}
and
W(-\alpha\mathfrak{e})\varphi
is localized in
\tau^{>}_{\sigma}
. The analogous statement with
>
replaced with
<
holds true, too.

Proof.
By Eqs. (
10.3
), (
10.4
)
\varphi
is in the range of
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})
if and only if
\varphi
is in the range of
E(\sigma^{<})
and in the range of
W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}
. The proof is easily accomplished.∎

Recall
\tau=\alpha\mathfrak{e}+\sigma
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. By causality
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})\leq E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau^{\prime}})

if
0\leq\alpha\leq\alpha^{\prime}
or
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha\leq 0
.
Hence the strong limit
P^{<}_{\sigma}:=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\big(E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})\big)=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})E(\sigma^{<})
exists. Analogously define
P^{>}_{\sigma}
. For the strong limits for
\alpha\to-\infty
see (
10
)(g).

(29) Proposition.
Let
\sigma
be a spacelike hyperplane. Let
\tau:=\alpha\mathfrak{e}+\sigma
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathfrak{e}
the four-vector of time with respect to
\sigma
. Recall
P^{<}_{\sigma}=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})E(\sigma^{<})
and
P^{>}_{\sigma}=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}E(\sigma^{<}_{\tau})E(\sigma^{>})
. Then

(a)

the eigenspaces of
P^{<}_{\sigma}
and
P^{>}_{\sigma}
are infinite dimensional

(b)

P^{<}_{\sigma}\leq E(\sigma^{<})
,
P^{>}_{\sigma}\leq E(\sigma^{>})

(c)

P^{<}_{\sigma}P^{>}_{\sigma}=0

(d)

P^{>}_{\sigma}=W(g)P^{<}_{\sigma}W(g)^{-1}
with
g
from
(
10
)
.

(e)

P^{<}_{\sigma}+P^{>}_{\sigma}=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau}\cap\sigma^{<}_{\tau})=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma}\cap\tau^{<}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}

(f)

P^{<}_{\sigma}=E(\sigma^{<})

\Leftrightarrow

P^{>}_{\sigma}=E(\sigma^{>})

\Leftrightarrow

P^{<}_{\sigma}+P^{>}_{\sigma}=I

\Leftrightarrow

\not\exists
state
\varphi
satisfying
E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma}\cap\tau^{<}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi=0

\forall

\alpha>0

\Leftrightarrow

\not\exists
state
\varphi
satisfying
E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi=0

\forall

\alpha>0

\Leftrightarrow

\not\exists
state
\varphi
satisfying
E(\tau^{<}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi=0

\forall

\alpha>0

(g)

Analogously, the strong limits for
\alpha\to-\infty
exist and they satisfy
(a) – (f)
for
\alpha<0
and
\alpha\to-\infty
.

Proof.
Keep the definition of
P^{\lessgtr}_{\sigma}
in mind. (g) is obvious. Now (a) follows from (
10
), Eq. (
10.3
). (b) is obvious. (c) holds by (b). One has (d) due to Eq. (
10.2
). (e) follows from Eqs. (
10.1
), (
10.3
), (
10.4
).

We turn to (f). The first two
\Leftrightarrow
hold by (b), (d). As
W(g)E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}W(g)^{-1}\varphi=W(g)E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})W(g)^{-1}W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi=E(\tau^{<}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi
, also the last
\Leftrightarrow
holds true. — As to the forth
\Leftrightarrow
, the direction
\Rightarrow
is obvious. For the proof of the reverse direction assume the contrary and define
\varphi^{<}:=E(\sigma^{<})\varphi
,
\varphi^{>}:=E(\sigma^{>})\varphi
. Then
\varphi=\varphi^{<}+\varphi^{>}
, whence
\varphi^{<}\neq 0
or
\varphi^{>}\neq 0
. Let
Q:=W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}E(\sigma^{<})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})
. By causality
I-E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})\leq Q\leq E(\tau^{<}_{\sigma})
. Therefore these three projections commute and
E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi^{<}=E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})QW(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi=QE(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi=E(\tau^{<}_{\sigma})QE(\tau^{>}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi=QE(\tau^{<}_{\sigma}\cap\tau^{>}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi=0.
Similarly
E(\tau^{<}_{\sigma})W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi^{>}=0
holds. Hence the assertion follows, using the last
\Leftrightarrow
in case that
\varphi^{<}=0
.

It remains to prove the third
\Leftrightarrow
of (f). The implication
\Rightarrow
is obvious by (e). As to
\Leftarrow
, suppose
P^{<}_{\sigma}+P^{>}_{\sigma}<I
. Let
V:=\{\varphi:\big(I-P(\alpha)\big)\varphi_{-\alpha}\to 0,\alpha\to\infty\}
, where
P(\alpha):=E(\tau^{>}_{\sigma}\cap\tau^{<}_{\sigma})
,
\varphi_{-\alpha}:=W(\alpha\mathfrak{e})^{-1}\varphi
. Check
V=\operatorname{ran}(P^{<}_{\sigma}+P^{>}_{\sigma})
. Then
V^{\perp}\neq\{0\}
by (e). Let
\varphi^{*}\in V^{\perp}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
\beta>0
and
\chi\in\operatorname{ran}P(\beta)
. We claim
\chi_{\beta}\in V
. Indeed, by causality
\chi_{\beta-\alpha}\in\operatorname{ran}P(\beta+|\alpha-\beta|)
for
\alpha\geq 0
, whence
\chi_{\beta-\alpha}\in\operatorname{ran}P(\alpha)
for
\alpha\geq\beta
and hence
\chi_{\beta}\in V
. So one infers
0=\langle\varphi^{*},\chi_{\beta}\rangle=\langle\varphi^{*}_{-\beta},\chi\rangle
for all
\chi\in\operatorname{ran}P(\beta)
,
\beta>0
. This means
\varphi^{*}_{-\beta}\in\operatorname{ran}\big(I-P(\beta)\big)
and hence
P(\beta)\varphi^{*}_{-\beta}=0
for all
\beta>0
.
∎

To comment on (
10
) consider
\sigma=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
,
\sigma^{>}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,x_{3}>0\}
,
\mathfrak{e}=(1,0,0,0)
,
\tau=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\alpha\}
,
and
\tau^{<}_{\sigma}=\{x_{0}=0,x_{3}<|\alpha|\}
.
The question raised by the last item of (f) is whether there exists a state
\varphi
such that the states
W(-t)\varphi
evolved in time are localized in
\{x_{0}=0,x_{3}>t\}
for
t>0
. In case of the Dirac system, (
16
) shows
that such a state does not exist, thus proving
I=P^{<}_{\sigma}+P^{>}_{\sigma}
by (
10
)(f). In case of the Weyl systems,
I=P^{<}_{\sigma}+P^{>}_{\sigma}
holds as well. However its proof in (
22
) (see the comments to (
22
) and (
22
)) requires a different method. There is an interesting alternative proof of
I=P^{<}_{\sigma}+P^{>}_{\sigma}
for Dirac and Weyl systems. It arises from group representation theoretical considerations leading to farther-reaching results, see (
26
).

11.
The Lattice of Causally Complete Regions

The lattice
\mathcal{M}
of causally complete regions of Minkowski space, generated and orthocomplemented by the spacelike relation

\mathfrak{x}\perp\mathfrak{y}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}<0

is well-known from local quantum physics. In view of Poincaré covariance and local orthogonality (equivalent to causality by (
7.2
)) one may ask whether a causal system
(W,E)
gives rise to a
representation

(rep)

(W,F)
of
\mathcal{M}
by orthogonal projections on the space of states. The properties to impose on
F
are Poincaré covariance with respect to
W
, normalization
F(\emptyset)=0
,
F(\mathbb{R}^{4})=I
, monotony, local orthogonality, and
\sigma
-orthoadditivity.
It is important to point out that
F
is not required to be a lattice homomorphism of
\mathcal{M}
into the lattice of the projections on the Hilbert space of states.

One is tempted to think such a rep
(W,F)
to be possible just because of the fact that the energy need not be semi-bounded. But actually is does not exist, see (
12
) and (
12
)(c).

In local quantum theory to every finite open contractible region
M
a von Neumann ring
\mathcal{R}(M)
is assigned. The causality principle relates the orthocomplement
M^{\perp}
to the commutant
\mathcal{R}(M)^{\prime}
. So in Haag 1992
[
52
, III.4.2.1]
it is postulated tentatively that for the vacuum sector the assignment
\mathcal{R}
actually extends to a homomorphism from
\mathcal{M}
into the lattice of von Neumann rings on a Hilbert space. Obviously, the tentative to construct a rep
F
describing localization within the frame of local quantum theory may be inspired by these considerations. But a localization operator
E(\Delta)
in general cannot be regarded to be an element of
\mathcal{R}(\hat{\Delta})
by
[
5
, Lemma 4]
just because of the semi-boundedness of energy.

Before showing the mentioned no-go results we like to study
\mathcal{M}
in some detail. Many examples at the level of exercises serve to become more familiar with

\mathcal{M}
and can be skipped. Only (
11.5
) is needed in the sequel. In particular it shows that there are maximal spacelike sets, which are not bases. In (
11.3
) it will be proven that causally complete sets are automatically measurable, i.e., Lebesgue measurable subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Regarding the definition of
\mathcal{M}
we follow
[
52
, III.4.1 The Lattice of Causally Complete Regions]
.

11.1.
Causal completion

Let
M
be any subset of Minkowski space and let
M^{\perp}
denote the causal complement of
M
consisting of all points, which lie spacelike to all points of
M
, i.e.,

M^{\perp}:=\{\mathfrak{x}:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}<0\;\forall\,\mathfrak{y}\in M\}

(11.1)

Clearly,
M\cap M^{\perp}=\emptyset
, and
M^{\perp}\subset L^{\perp}
if
L\subset M
. The set
\hat{M}:=(M^{\perp})^{\perp}
is called the causal completion of
M
. If
M=\hat{M}
then
M
is said to be causally complete.

Clearly,
\emptyset
and
\mathbb{R}^{4}
are causally complete. Any causal complement
M^{\perp}
is causally complete and, equivalently,
M^{\perp}=\hat{M}^{\perp}
holds. (Indeed,
M\subset\hat{M}
implies
M^{\perp}\supset\hat{M}^{\perp}=M^{\perp\perp\perp}=(M^{\perp})^{\land}\supset M^{\perp}
.) Therefore
(\hat{M})^{\land}=\hat{M}
. For any family
(M_{\iota})_{\iota}
of sets one has

\big(\bigcup_{\iota}M_{\iota}\big)^{\perp}=\bigcap_{\iota}M_{\iota}^{\perp}
, as both sides describe the set of points lying spacelike to all
M_{\iota}
. This implies
\big(\bigcup M_{\iota}^{\perp}\big)^{\perp}=\bigcap_{\iota}\hat{M}_{\iota}
, which proves that the intersection of any family of causally complete sets is causally complete. Moreover,
\big(\bigcup_{\iota}M_{\iota}\big)^{\land}=\big(\bigcap_{\iota}M_{\iota}^{\perp}\big)^{\perp}
and
\big(\bigcup_{\iota}M_{\iota}^{\perp}\big)^{\land}=\big(\bigcap_{\iota}\hat{M}_{\iota}\big)^{\perp}
follow. Finally one has
\big(\bigcup_{\iota}M_{\iota}\big)^{\land}=\big(\bigcup_{\iota}\hat{M}_{\iota}\big)^{\land}
. (Indeed, the inclusion
\subset
holds by monotony of causal completion. Conversely, still by this monotony,
\big(\bigcup_{\iota}M_{\iota}\big)^{\land}\supset\hat{M}_{\kappa}
for every
\kappa
. Hence
\big(\bigcup_{\iota}M_{\iota}\big)^{\land}\supset\bigcup_{\kappa}\hat{M}_{\kappa}
, where
\supset
still holds for the causal completion of the right hand side.)

Note, however, that in general
(M_{1}\cap M_{2})^{\perp}\neq(M_{1}^{\perp}\cup M_{2}^{\perp})^{\land}
. (Indeed, let
M_{1}:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}-x_{3}=0\},M_{2}:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}+x_{3}=0\}
. Then
M_{1}^{\perp}=M_{2}^{\perp}=\emptyset
and
M_{1}\cap M_{2}=\{x_{0}=x_{3}=0\}
, whence
(M_{1}\cap M_{2})^{\perp}=\{|x_{0}|<|x_{3}|\}
and
(M_{1}^{\perp}\cup M_{2}^{\perp})^{\land}=\emptyset
.)

Moreover, in general,
M_{1}\cup M_{2}
is not causally complete, even if
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
are causally complete spacelike separated. (Indeed, let
M_{1}:=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}|\leq-x_{3}\}
,
M_{2}:=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}|<x_{3}\}
. Then, by (
11.1
)(b),
M_{1}=h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 0)
,
M_{2}=h(<0)\cap k(>0)
, whence
M_{1}=M_{2}^{\perp}
are causally complete spacelike separated. As
M_{1}\cup M_{2}
contains the spacelike hyperplane
\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
,
(M_{1}\cup M_{2})^{\land}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
follows.)

(30) Lemma.
The one-point sets
\{\mathfrak{x}\}
are causally complete.

Proof.
It suffices to consider
\mathfrak{x}=0
. Since
\{0\}^{\perp}=\{\mathfrak{y}:|y_{0}|<|y|\}
, one has
\mathfrak{x}\in\{0\}^{\land}
if
|x_{0}-y_{0}|<|x-y|
for all
|y_{0}|<|y|
. Then
x=0
as otherwise
\mathfrak{y}:=(0,x)
yields the contradiction
|x_{0}|<0
. Now
x_{0}=0
follows similarly.∎

(31) Example
for the causal completion of a two-point set. Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\{0,(s,0,0,0)\}^{\land}=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}-\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{s}{2}$}}|+|x|\leq|\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{s}{2}$}}|\}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{s}{2}$}},|x|\leq|\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{s}{2}$}}|\}^{\land}

Proof.

\{0,(s,0,0,0)\}^{\land}=(\{0\}^{\perp}\cap\{(s,0,0,0)\}^{\perp})^{\perp}=(\{|y_{0}|<|y|\}\cap\{|y_{0}-s|<|y|\})^{\perp}=\{|y_{0}-\frac{s}{2}|+|\frac{s}{2}|<|y|\}^{\perp}=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}-y_{0}|<|x-y|\textrm{ for all }\mathfrak{y}\textrm{ with }|y_{0}-\frac{s}{2}|+|\frac{s}{2}|<|y|\}=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}-\frac{s}{2}|+|y_{0}-\frac{s}{2}|<\big||y|-|x|\big|\textrm{ for all }\mathfrak{y}\textrm{ with }|y_{0}-\frac{s}{2}|+|\frac{s}{2}|<|y|\}
, whence
|x|\leq|\frac{s}{2}|
and hence the first equality
=
. As to the second
=
, the inclusion
\supset
holds, since
\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\frac{s}{2},|x|\leq|\frac{s}{2}|\}
is contained in the left hand side. For the inclusion
\subset
it suffices to show that
\{0,(s,0,0,0)\}
is contained in the right hand side. Anticipating (
11.2
), consider
\mathfrak{z}=(1,z)
with
|z|\leq 1
and note that
\mathfrak{x}:=\frac{s}{2}\mathfrak{z}
satisfies
x_{0}=\frac{s}{2},|x|\leq|\frac{s}{2}|
. Hence the assertion holds true for
0
. Similarly the assertion is verified for
(s,0,0,0)
.∎

(32) Example
for some causally complete sets. Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider the half-spaces

h(\leq\gamma):=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}-x_{3}\leq\gamma\}
,
k(\leq\gamma):=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}+x_{3}\leq\gamma\}
and, generally,
h(\sim\gamma)
,
k(\sim\gamma)
for
\sim\,\in\{<,\,\leq,\,>,\,\geq\}
.

Obviously, their causal complements
h^{\perp}
,
k^{\perp}
are empty and, hence, their causal completions
\hat{h}
,
\hat{k}
equal
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. For the causal complement of the intersection of two half-spaces of the type
h
and
k
one finds

(a)

\big(h(\sim\gamma)\cap k(\backsim\delta)\big)^{\perp}=\emptyset

if
\sim
and
\backsim
are both in
\{<,\,\leq\}
or both in
\{>,\,\geq\}
, and

(b)

\big(h(\sim\gamma)\cap k(\backsim\delta)\big)^{\perp}=h(\not\sim\gamma)\cap k(\not\backsim\delta)

otherwise. It follows that the intersections
h\cap k
in
(b)
are causally complete. Note
h(\not\sim\gamma)=\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus h(\sim\gamma)
,
k(\not\backsim\,\delta)=\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus k(\backsim\delta)
.

Proof.
As to (a), for every
\mathfrak{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(y_{0}+s)-y_{3}<\gamma
and
(y_{0}+s)+y_{3}<\delta
. Hence
\mathfrak{x}:=(y_{0}+s,y)\in h(<\gamma)\cap k(<\delta)
and
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=(s,0)^{\cdot 2}=s^{2}\geq 0
. Therefore
\mathfrak{y}\not\in\big(h(<\gamma)\cap k(<\delta)\big)^{\perp}
. This proves the cases

\sim,\backsim\,\in\{<,\leq\}
. The cases
\sim,\backsim\,\in\{>,\geq\}
follow analogously. — We turn to (b). Consider the case
(\sim,\backsim)=(\leq,>)
. Let

\mathfrak{y}\in\big(h(\leq\gamma)\cap k(>\delta)\big)^{\perp}
. This means that for every
\mathfrak{x}
satisfying
x_{0}-x_{3}\leq\gamma
,

x_{0}+x_{3}>\delta
one has
|y_{0}-x_{0}|<|y_{3}-x_{3}|
, i.e., either
[

y_{0}-y_{3}<x_{0}-x_{3}
and
y_{0}+y_{3}>x_{0}+x_{3}

]
or
[

y_{0}-y_{3}>x_{0}-x_{3}
and
y_{0}+y_{3}<x_{0}+x_{3}

]
. Obviously this holds true if and only if
y_{0}-y_{3}>\gamma
and
y_{0}+y_{3}\leq\delta
. — The other cases follow analogously.∎

11.2.
Set of determinacy

For any subset
M
of Minkowski space its
set of determinacy

\tilde{M}:=\{\mathfrak{x}:\forall\,\mathfrak{z}\neq 0,\,\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}\geq 0\;\exists\,s\in\mathbb{R}\textrm{ with }\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z}\in M\}

(11.2)

consists of all points
\mathfrak{x}
such that every timelike or lightlike line
13
13

13

throughout line means straight line

\mathfrak{x}
meets
M
.

Obviously,
M\subset\tilde{M}
. Further,
\tilde{\tilde{M}}=\tilde{M}
holds. (Indeed, let
\mathfrak{x}\in\tilde{\tilde{M}}
. For
\mathfrak{z}\neq 0,\,\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}\geq 0
there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z}\in\tilde{M}
. Hence, there is
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
with
(\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z})+s^{\prime}\mathfrak{z}\in M
, whence
\mathfrak{x}\in\tilde{M}
.) Clearly
\tilde{M}_{1}\subset\tilde{M}_{2}
if
M_{1}\subset M_{2}
.

(33) Lemma.

\tilde{M}\subset\hat{M}
.

Proof.
Fix
\mathfrak{x}\in\tilde{M}
. Note first that
\mathfrak{x}\not\in M^{\perp}
, as
\mathfrak{x}+\mathfrak{w}\in M
for some not spacelike
\mathfrak{w}
. Now let
\mathfrak{y}\in M^{\perp}
. Then
\mathfrak{z}:=\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}\neq 0
. Obviously, it suffices to show

\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}<0
. Assume the contrary. Then there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z}\in M
. This implies the contradiction
0>(\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=(1-s)^{2}\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}\geq 0
.∎

Thus, by (
11.2
), one has
M\subset\tilde{M}=\tilde{\tilde{M}}\subset\hat{M}
, whence
M=\tilde{M}
if
M=\hat{M}
. But
M=\tilde{M}
does not imply that
M
is causally complete. A simple example is
M:=\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
for
\mathfrak{e}=(1,0,0,0)
with
M=\tilde{M}
and
\hat{M}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
. (Indeed,
M=\tilde{M}
is obvious. Next note
M^{\perp}\subset\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
, since generally
M^{\perp}\cap M=\emptyset
, and
s\mathfrak{e}\not\perp(2+s,0,0,1)
. Hence
M^{\perp}=\emptyset
, whence
\hat{M}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
.) Another example is
M
in (
11.2
).
For the computations we anticipate (
11.6
). Furthermore,
M:=\tilde{P}
for
P
from (
11.5
) is a particularly interesting example as
P
is maximal spacelike but not a base.

(34) Example
for a non causally complete set of determinacy.
Let
M:=K\cup L
for

K:=\{\mathfrak{x}:-1\leq x_{0}-x_{3}\leq 1,-3\leq x_{0}+x_{3}\leq-1\}
,
L:=\{\mathfrak{x}:-1\leq x_{0}-x_{3}\leq 1,1\leq x_{0}+x_{3}\leq 3\}
.
K,L
are causally complete by
(
11.1
)(b)
.
One has
M=\tilde{M}
and
\hat{M}=\{\mathfrak{x}:-1\leq x_{0}-x_{3}\leq 1,-3\leq x_{0}+x_{3}\leq 3\}
,
whence

\{\mathfrak{x}:-1<x_{0}-x_{3}<1,-1<x_{0}+x_{3}<1\}\subset\hat{M}\setminus\tilde{M}
.

Proof.
First compute
\hat{M}=M^{\perp\perp}
. As
M=K\cup L=\big(h(\leq 1)\cap k(\geq-3)\cap h(\geq-1)\cap k(\leq-1)\big)\cup\big(h(\leq 1)\cap k(\geq 1)\cap h(\geq-1)\cap k(\leq 3)\big)
, by (
11.6
)(ii) one has
M^{\perp}=\big(\big(h(>1)\cap k(<-3)\big)\cup\big(h(<-1)\cap k(>-1)\big)\big)\cap\big(\big(h(>1)\cap k(<1)\big)\cup\big(h(<-1)\cap k(>3)\big)\big)=\big(h(>1)\cap k(<-3)\cap h(>1)\cap k(<1)\big)\cup\big(h(>1)\cap k(<-3)\cap h(<-1)\cap k(>3)\big)\cup\big(h(<-1)\cap k(>-1)\cap h(>1)\cap k(<1)\big)\cup\big(h(<-1)\cap k(>-1)\cap h(<-1)\cap k(>3)\big)=\big(h(>1)\cap k(<-3)\big)\cup\big(h(<-1)\cap k(>3)\big)
, and further,
M^{\perp\perp}=h(\leq 1)\cap k(\geq-3)\cap h(\geq-1)\cap k(\leq 3)
as asserted.

We turn to
\tilde{M}
. Recall
M\subset\tilde{M}\subset\hat{M}
. One easily checks
M\cap\big(k(>-1)\cap k(<1)\big)=\emptyset
and
\hat{M}\setminus M=h(\leq 1)\cap h(\geq-1)\cap k(>-1)\cap k(<1)
. Now,
\mathfrak{x}\in k(>-1)\cap k(<1)
means
-1<x_{0}+x_{3}<1
. So the lightlike line
\mathfrak{x}+\mathbb{R}(1,0,0,-1)
stays in
k(>-1)\cap k(<1)
and does not meet
M
. This proves
\tilde{M}=M
. Obviously
\{\mathfrak{x}:-1<x_{0}-x_{3}<1,-1<x_{0}+x_{3}<1\}\subset\hat{M}\setminus\tilde{M}
. ∎

11.3.
The lattice
\mathcal{M}

In oder to be clear in the sequel we recall the definition (
11.3
), see e.g.
[
53
, sec. 11]
.

(35) Definition.
Let
(L,\leq)
be a partially ordered set. Then
L
is a
lattice
, resp. a
\sigma
-complete lattice, resp. a complete lattice if every finite subset, resp. every countable subset, resp. every subset
S\subset L
has a least upper bound
\bigvee S
and a greatest lower bound
\bigwedge S
. A lattice
L
is
modular
, if for
a
,
b
,
c
in
L
with
a\leq b
one has
(a\lor c)\land b=a\lor(c\land b)
. A lattice
L
is bounded from below, if there is a minimal element
0
in
L
, and it is
bounded
, if there is also a maximal element
I
in
L
.

Let
L
have a minimal element
0
. Then
P\in L
is called an
atom
, if
P\neq 0
and
a\leq P
implies
a=P
for all
a\in L\setminus\{0\}
.
L
is
atomic
, if for every
a\in L\setminus\{0\}
there is an atom
P
with
P\leq a
.
L
satisfies the
covering property
, if
for every
a,b\in L
and atom
P
with
a\leq b\leq a\lor P
one has
b\in\{a,a\lor P\}
.

A lattice
L
is
orthocomplemented
if it is bounded and if there is an order-reversing involution
\perp:L\to L
such that
a\land a^{\perp}=0
,
a\lor a^{\perp}=I
for
a\in L
. An orthocomplemented lattice
L
is
orthomodular
if for
a
,
b
in
L
with
a\leq b
one
has
a=(a\lor b^{\perp})\land b
(or equivalently
b=a\lor(a^{\perp}\land b))
.∎

Note that for an orthocomplemented lattice modularity implies orthomodularity. Indeed, choose simply
c:=b^{\perp}
in the modularity relation. The reverse implication in general does not hold. For instance, the lattice of the orthogonal projections on an infinite dimensional Hilbert space, occasionally called
quantum proposition system
or
quantum logic
, is not modular (see
[
54
, p. 444]
) but obviously orthomodular.

Orthomodularity, also called
weak modularity
, is equivalent to the
axiom (P)
introduced and studied in Piron 1964
[
54
, sec. 5]
. According to
[
54
]
orthomodularity and atomicity with the covering property constitute the fundamental properties of a quantum proposition system. For a thorough exposition of this topic see also Jauch 1968
[
55
]
.

In
[
55
, sec. 5.8]
a further property, which we call q-modularity, is asserted to be equivalent to orthomodularity. An orthocomplemented lattice
L
is
q-modular
if, for
a
,
b
,
c
in
L
with
a\leq b
and
c\leq b^{\perp}
, the equality
c\lor a=c\lor b
implies
a=b
. We verify the equivalence.
First assume orthomodularity.
As
a\leq b\leq c^{\perp}
it yields
(c\lor a)\land c^{\perp}=a
and
(c\lor b)\land c^{\perp}=b
. — Now assume q-modularity. Set
d:=(a\lor b^{\perp})\land b
. Then obviously
a\leq d\leq b
. Hence
c:=b^{\perp}
satisfies
c\leq d^{\perp}
and
c\lor a\leq c\lor d\leq c\lor(a\lor b^{\perp})=c\lor a
, whence
c\lor a=c\lor d
, and, by q-modularity, the assertion
a=d
.

For later use in sec.
26
we note

(36) Lemma.
Let
L
be a
\sigma
-complete
orthomodular lattice. Let
a_{n}\in L
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq a_{3}\geq\dots
. Then one has the orthogonal decomposition

a_{1}\land(\bigwedge_{n}a_{n})^{\perp}=\bigvee_{n}(a_{n}\land a_{n+1}^{\perp})

of the left hand side.

Proof.
First use the general identity
(\bigwedge_{n}a_{n})^{\perp}=(\bigvee_{n}a_{n}^{\perp})
. Then, by
[
54
, Théorèm VIII]
due to orthomodularity,
a_{1}\land(\bigvee_{n}a_{n}^{\perp})=\bigvee_{n}(a_{1}\land a_{n}^{\perp})
. Obviously the proof is accomplished showing
a_{1}\land a_{n}^{\perp}=\bigvee_{i=1}^{n-1}(a_{i}\land a_{i+1}^{\perp})
for every
n\geq 2
. Hence, using induction, it suffices to verify
a_{1}\land a_{n+1}^{\perp}=(a_{1}\land a_{n}^{\perp})\lor(a_{n}\land a_{n+1}^{\perp})
. To this end note
(a_{1}\land a_{n}^{\perp})\leq(a_{1}\land a_{n+1}^{\perp})
and
a_{n}\leq a_{1}
. Therefore, applying twice orthomodularity, one has
(a_{1}\land a_{n+1}^{\perp})=(a_{1}\land a_{n}^{\perp})\lor\big(a_{1}\land a_{n+1}^{\perp}\land(a_{1}^{\perp}\lor a_{n})\big)=(a_{1}\land a_{n}^{\perp})\lor(a_{n+1}^{\perp}\land a_{n})
as claimed.∎

According to the above definition, the considerations so far show that the set
\mathcal{M}
of all causally complete sets endowed with set inclusion
\subset
and causal complementation
\perp
is a complete orthocomplemented lattice. The greatest lower bound
\bigwedge\mathcal{K}
for any subset
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{M}
is given by the intersection
\bigcap\mathcal{K}
of all its elements. Its smallest upper bound
\bigvee\mathcal{K}
is
\big(\bigcup\mathcal{K}\big)^{\land}=\big(\bigcap\{M^{\perp}:M\in\mathcal{K}\}\big)^{\perp}
.

\mathcal{M}
is
atomic
. Indeed,
\emptyset
is the minimal element, and due to (
11.1
), the atoms of
\mathcal{M}
are the one-point sets. Obviously, every
M\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\{\emptyset\}
contains an atom.

\mathcal{M}
is atomic
without the covering property
. For an example let
L
be the lightlike line
\mathbb{R}(1,0,0,1)
and
L^{\prime}
the segment
\{s(1,0,0,1):s\in[0,1]\}
. Then
L\cap\{0\}^{\perp}=\emptyset
, since
\{0\}^{\perp}=\{\mathfrak{y}:|y_{0}|<|y|\}
. By (
11.4
),
L
and
L^{\prime}
are causally complete. Therefore

L\cap\{0\}^{\perp}\subsetneq L^{\prime}\subsetneq L
implies
M\subsetneq M^{\prime}\subsetneq M\lor\{0\}
for
M:=L^{\perp}
, and
M^{\prime}:=L^{\prime\perp}
, contradicting atomicity. — Another example is given by
\{0\}\subsetneq\{0\}\lor\{(1,0,0,0)\}\subsetneq\{0\}\lor\{(2,0,0,0)\}
. This holds true by (
11.1
).

\mathcal{M}
is
not orthomodular
. For an example let
L:=h(<0)\cap k(>2)
and
M:=h(<0)\cap k(>0)
. Then clearly
L\subset M
. By (
11.1
)(b),
L,M\in\mathcal{M}
with
L^{\perp}=h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 2)
,
M^{\perp}=h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 0)
, and
L^{\perp}\cap M=h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 2)\cap h(<0)\cap k(>0)=\emptyset
. Hence
(L\cup M^{\perp})^{\land}=(L^{\perp}\cap M)^{\perp}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Therefore
(L\lor M^{\perp})\land M=(L\cup M^{\perp})^{\land}\cap M=M\neq L
,
and orthomodularity does not hold.
14
14

14

The presumed proof of orthomodularity of
\mathcal{M}
in
[
52
, Proposition 4.1.3]
is not correct, since there one infers
\mathfrak{x}\in(L\cup M^{\perp})^{\sim}\cap M
from
\mathfrak{x}\in(L\cup M^{\perp})^{\land}\cap M
. Indeed, this does not hold true in general. In the above example one has
(L\cup M^{\perp})^{\land}\cap M=M
and
(L\cup M^{\perp})^{\sim}\cap M=L
with
L\subsetneq M
by (
11.5
) as
L=\hat{\Gamma},M=\Delta^{\perp}
.

The non-orthomodularity of
\mathcal{M}
is already stated in
[
56
, sec. 3]
. See also the general analysis in
[
56
]
following Theorem 1.

Note that a causally complete set need not be Borel. (For an example take
\Gamma:=\{0\}\times(A\setminus\mathbb{Q}^{3})
with
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
not Borel. So
\Gamma
is not Borel, too. Let
\mathfrak{x}\in\tilde{\Gamma}
. Then one easily checks
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|x-y|\leq|x_{0}|\}\subset(A\setminus\mathbb{Q}^{3})
. This implies
x_{0}=0
, whence
x=y
and hence
\mathfrak{x}\in\Gamma
. This proves
\tilde{\Gamma}=\Gamma
. As (
11.5
)(v) shows,
\tilde{\Gamma}=\hat{\Gamma}
holds since
\Gamma
is a subset of a spacelike hyperplane. Therefore
\Gamma
is causally complete.) However by (
11.3
), causally complete sets are measurable. For
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
let

M^{\lor}:=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in M}\{\mathfrak{x}:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\geq 0\}

be the
maximal set of influence
of
M
. Obviously
M^{\lor}=\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus M^{\perp}
.

(37) Lemma.
Let
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
Define
U(M):=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in M}\{\mathfrak{x}:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}>0\}
. Then

(a)

U(M)\subset M^{\lor}
and
U(M)
is open

(b)

M^{\lor}\setminus U(M)
is contained in an
F_{\sigma}
-null-set

(c)

M^{\lor}
,
M^{\perp}
,
\hat{M}
are measurable

Proof.
(a) is obvious. (c)
M^{\lor}
is measurable as an immediate consequence of (a), (b). The remainder is obvious.

So we turn to (b). Put
N(M):=\{\mathfrak{x}:\sup_{\mathfrak{y}\in M}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=0\}
. Obviously (i)
N(M)=N(\overline{M})
. — Note (ii)
M^{\lor}\setminus U(M)\subset N(M)
. Indeed,
let
\mathfrak{x}\in M^{\lor}\setminus U(M)
. Then there is
\mathfrak{y}\in M
with
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\geq 0
and
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
for all
\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in M
. Hence
\sup_{\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in M}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}^{\prime})^{\cdot 2}=0
, whence
\mathfrak{x}\in N(M)
.

Suppose now that
M
is compact. Then, (iii) for every
\mathfrak{x}\in N(M)
there is
\mathfrak{y}\in M
with
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=0
. Indeed, there are
\mathfrak{y}_{n}\in M
satisfying
-\frac{1}{n}\leq(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}_{n})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
. Without restriction
\mathfrak{y}_{n}\to\mathfrak{y}\in M
, whence the claim. — Next claim that (iv)
N(M)
is closed.
Indeed, let
\mathfrak{x}_{n}\in N(M)
with
\mathfrak{x}_{n}\to\mathfrak{x}_{0}
. There are
\mathfrak{y}_{n}\in M
satisfying
-\frac{1}{n}\leq(\mathfrak{x}_{n}-\mathfrak{y}_{n})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
. Without restriction
\mathfrak{y}_{n}\to\mathfrak{y}_{0}\in M
. Hence
(\mathfrak{x}_{0}-\mathfrak{y}_{0})^{\cdot 2}=0
. Furthermore, for every
\mathfrak{y}\in M
,
(\mathfrak{x}_{n}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
, whence
(\mathfrak{x}_{0}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
. Therefore

\mathfrak{x}_{0}\in N(M)
as claimed. — Now claim that (v) for every
a\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
the section
N(M)_{a}=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:(\xi,a)\in N(M)\}
contains at most two points. Indeed, assume that there are
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
with
\alpha<\beta<\gamma
such that
(\alpha,a),(\beta,a),(\gamma,a)\in N(M)
. By (iii) there is
\mathfrak{y}\in M
with
\big((\beta,a)-\mathfrak{y}\big)^{\cdot 2}=0
. Furthermore,
\big((\alpha,a)-\mathfrak{y}\big)^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
and
\big((\gamma,a)-\mathfrak{y}\big)^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
hold. Hence, one finds
(\beta-y_{0})^{2}=|a-y|^{2}
,

(\alpha-y_{0})^{2}\leq|a-y|^{2}
, and
(\gamma-y_{0})^{2}\leq|a-y|^{2}
, whence
(\alpha-y_{0})^{2}\leq(\beta-y_{0})^{2}
,
(\gamma-y_{0})^{2}\leq(\beta-y_{0})^{2}
, and further the contradiction
\gamma+\beta\leq 2y_{0}\leq\beta+\alpha
, proving the claim. — Finally claim (vi)
\lambda^{4}\big(N(M)\big)=0
.
15
15

15

\lambda^{d}
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
Indeed, this is a ready consequence of (iv), (v) due to Tonelli’s theorem by which
\lambda^{4}\big(N(M)\big)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\lambda^{1}\big(N(M)_{x}\big)\operatorname{d}\lambda^{3}(x)=\int 0\operatorname{d}\lambda^{3}=0
.

We are ready to proof (b) for general
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Let
M_{n}\subset M
be bounded with
M=\cup_{n}M_{n}
. Then
M^{\lor}\setminus U(M)=\big(\cup_{n}M^{\lor}_{n}\big)\setminus\big(\cup_{n}U(M_{n})\big)\subset\cup_{n}\big(M^{\lor}_{n}\setminus U(M_{n})\big)\subset\cup_{n}N(M_{n})
by (ii), and further
=\cup_{n}N(\overline{M}_{n})
by (i), whence the assertion by (iv), (vi).∎

Summarizing the considerations so far one has

(38) Theorem.

The causally complete subsets of Minkowski space are measurable and form a complete orthocomplemented atomic lattice
\mathcal{M}
without the covering property and which is not orthomodular.

\mathcal{M}
is called the
lattice of causally complete regions
. The lattice
\mathcal{M}
and versions of it occasionally are addressed as
relativistic causal logic
. We restrict ourselves to refer to Cegla, Jancewicz 2013
[
57
]
and the literature cited therein for a partial overview on the subject. More relevance than
\mathcal{M}
regarding causal systems will have the lattice
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
generated by the non-timelike relation, see sec.
24
,
25
,
26
.

11.4.
Spacelike sets

A subset
A
of Minkowski space is said to be
spacelike
or causally independent or a
\perp
-set if any two different points
\mathfrak{x}
,
\mathfrak{y}
of
A
are spacelike separated, i.e.,
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}<0
. Clearly, if
A
is spacelike and
B\subset A
, then
A\setminus B\subset B^{\perp}
.

A spacelike set
A
is
maximal
if
A^{\perp}=\emptyset
. E.g., spacelike hyperplanes are maximal. Every spacelike set is contained in a maximal one. More generally, if
A
is spacelike contained in some set
M
, then there is a spacelike set
A^{\prime}
satisfying
A\subset A^{\prime}\subset M
, which is maximal in
M
, i.e., there is no spacelike set
B\subset M
with
A^{\prime}\subsetneq B
. This holds true by an obvious application of Zorn’s lemma.

(39) Definition.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}
denote the set of all those subsets of Minkowski space which are the causal completion of a spacelike set.

Of course,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is interesting with regard to a possible extension of the localization
E
of a causal system to causally complete regions. One observes first that
\mathcal{M}_{1}\subsetneq\mathcal{M}
.

(40) Example.
There are causally complete sets
M
, which are not the causal completion of a spacelike set and, a fortiori, are not determined by a spacelike set, i.e.,
M\neq\hat{\Delta}
and, hence,
M\neq\tilde{\Delta}
for every spacelike
\Delta
. Such sets are for instance
(i)
the lightlike lines and
(ii)
segments of them, and
(iii)

M:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\in[0,1]\}
.

Proof.
As to (i), let without restriction
M:=\mathbb{R}(1,0,0,1)
. First we show
M=\hat{M}
. Note,
\mathfrak{y}\in M^{\perp}
if and only if
y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}>(y_{0}-y_{3})(y_{0}+y_{3}-2s)
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence
M^{\perp}=\{\mathfrak{y}:y_{0}=y_{3},(y_{1},y_{2})\neq(0,0)\}
. Therefore
\mathfrak{x}\in M^{\perp\perp}
if and only if
(x_{1}-r)^{2}+(x_{2}-t)^{2}>(x_{0}-x_{3})(x_{0}+x_{3}-2s)
for all
r,t,s\in\mathbb{R}
with
(r,t)\neq(0,0)
. This implies
x_{1}=x_{2}=0
and further
x_{0}=x_{3}
. It follows
M=\hat{M}
. — As
\big((s,0,0,s)-(r,0,0,r)\big)^{\cdot 2}=0
, every spacelike
\Delta\subset M
contains at most one point, whence
\hat{\Delta}=\Delta\neq M
.

As to (ii), let
M:=\{(s,0,0,s):s\in[0,1]\}
. We show
M=\hat{M}
. Note
M^{\perp}=\{\mathfrak{y}:(y_{0}-s)^{2}<y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}+(y_{3}-s)^{2},\;s\in[0,1]\}
. Now consider
\mathfrak{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus M
. This means either
x_{0}<x_{3}
, or
x_{0}>x_{3}
, or
x_{0}=x_{3}=r<0
, or
x_{0}=x_{3}=r>1
. If
x_{0}<x_{3}
, then
\mathfrak{x}\not\in M^{\perp\perp}
, since
\mathfrak{y}:=(1,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}-x_{0}+1)\in M^{\perp}
, but
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=(x_{0}-1,0,0,x_{0}-1)^{\cdot 2}=0
.
If
x_{0}>x_{3}
, then
\mathfrak{x}\not\in M^{\perp\perp}
, since
\mathfrak{y}:=(0,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}-x_{0})\in M^{\perp}
, but
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=(x_{0},0,0,x_{0})^{\cdot 2}=0
.
If
x_{0}=x_{3}=r<0
, then
\mathfrak{x}\not\in M^{\perp\perp}
, since
\mathfrak{y}:=(0,x_{1},x_{2},r)\in M^{\perp}
, but
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=(r,0,0,0)^{\cdot 2}>0
. Finally, if
x_{0}=x_{3}=r>1
, then
\mathfrak{x}\not\in M^{\perp\perp}
, since
\mathfrak{y}:=(1,x_{1},x_{2},r)\in M^{\perp}
, but
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=(r-1,0,0,0)^{\cdot 2}>0
. Thus
M=\hat{M}
. — Finish the proof as in (i).

As to (iii), let
M_{n}:=\big(h(\geq-\frac{1}{n})\cap k(\leq 2)\big)\cap\big(h(\leq 0)\cap k(\geq 0)\big)
. Then
M_{n}=\hat{M}_{n}
by (
11.6
)(ii), whence
M=\hat{M}
, since
M=\bigcap_{n}M_{n}
. Now let
A\subset M
be spacelike. We show
\hat{A}\subsetneq M
. If
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a^{\prime}}\in A
,
\mathfrak{a}\neq\mathfrak{a^{\prime}}
, then
(a_{1},a_{2})\neq(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2})
. Hence there is
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,1]
such that
A\subset\{(f(r,s),r,s,f(r,s)):r,s\in\mathbb{R}\}
. (Equality holds if
A
is maximal.) — If
f=0
, then
A^{\perp}\supset\{(0,r,s,0):r,s\in\mathbb{R}\}^{\perp}=\{\mathfrak{y}:|y_{0}|<|y_{3}|\}=\big(h(<0)\cap k(>0)\big)\cup\big(h(>0)\cap k(<0)\big)
, whence
\hat{A}\subset h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 0)\cap h(\leq 0)\cap k(\geq 0)=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}=0\}\subsetneq M
by (
11.6
)(ii). — If
f\neq 0
, there is
f(r_{0},s_{0})\neq 0
. Then
\mathfrak{y}:=(t,r_{0},s_{0},-t)
for
t>0
lies in
A^{\perp}
. Indeed,
(f(r,s)-t)^{2}<(r-r_{0})^{2}+(s-s_{0})^{2}+(f(r,s)+t)^{2}
holds, since
|f(r,s)-t|\leq f(r,s)+t
and
|f(r,s)-t|=f(r,s)+t
only if
f(r,s)=0
, in which case
(r-r_{0})^{2}+(s-s_{0})^{2}>0
. But
\mathfrak{y}\not\in M^{\perp}
, as
\mathfrak{x}:=(0,r_{0},s_{0},0)\in M
and
(\mathfrak{y}-\mathfrak{x})^{\cdot 2}=t^{2}-t^{2}=0
. Hence
M^{\perp}\subsetneq A^{\perp}
.
∎

What is more, it turns out that

(41) Lemma.

\mathcal{M}_{1}
is not a sublattice of
\mathcal{M}
and it is not closed under causal complementation.

Proof.
See (
11.4
) and (
11.4
).∎

(42) Example.
There are
M,L\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
with
M\cap L\not\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
, for instance
M:=h(\leq 0)\cap k(\geq 0)
and
L:=h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 2)
.

Proof.

M,L\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
by (
11.6
)(i). But
M\cap L=\{x_{0}-x_{3}=0,0\leq x_{0}+x_{3}\leq 2\}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\in[0,1]\}\not\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
by (
11.4
)(iii).∎

(43) Example.
There is
M\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
with
M^{\perp}\not\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
, for instance
M:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3},(x_{1},x_{2})\neq(0,0)\}
.

Proof.
As shown in (
11.4
)(i) one has
M=\{(s,0,0,s):s\in\mathbb{R}\}^{\perp}
and
M^{\perp}=\{(s,0,0,s):s\in\mathbb{R}\}\not\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
. It remains to show
M\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
.

Clearly,
A:=\{(f(r,s),r,s,f(r,s)):(r,s)\neq(0,0)\}
with
f(r,s):=\textrm{sgn}(r)(r^{2}+s^{2})^{-1}
is a spacelike subset of
M
. It suffices to show
A^{\perp}\subset M^{\perp}
. Let
\mathfrak{y}\in A^{\perp}
. This means
(y_{0}-y_{3})\big(y_{0}+y_{3}-2f(r,s)\big)<(y_{1}-r)^{2}+(y_{2}-s)^{2}
for all
(r,s)\neq(0,0)
. Note
(y_{1}-r)^{2}+(y_{2}-s)^{2}\leq C:=(|y_{1}|+1)^{2}+(|y_{2}|+1)^{2}
for all
|r|\leq 1
,
|s|\leq 1
. — Assume first
y_{0}>y_{3}
. Then for
(r,s):=(-\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
one gets the contradiction
(y_{0}-y_{3})(y_{0}+y_{3}+n^{2})\leq C
for all
n
. — Now assume
y_{0}<y_{3}
. Then for
(r,s):=(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
one gets the contradiction
(y_{0}-y_{3})(y_{0}+y_{3}-n^{2})\leq C
for all
n
. — So
y_{0}=y_{3}
remains. Therefore
0<(y_{1}-r)^{2}+(y_{2}-s)^{2}
for all
(r,s)\neq(0,0)
. This implies
y_{1}=y_{2}=0
. Hence
\mathfrak{y}\in M^{\perp}
.∎

11.5.
Bases

A familiar idea of a causal evolution is that the
Cauchy data
given on a spacelike set
\Delta
determine by means of a hyperbolic PDE the events in
\tilde{\Delta}
. Inspired by this we call a spacelike set
\Sigma
a (causal)
base
if
\tilde{\Sigma}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Every base
\Sigma
is maximal spacelike, since
\mathbb{R}^{4}=\tilde{\Sigma}\subset\hat{\Sigma}=\Sigma^{\perp\perp}
, whence
\Sigma^{\perp}=(\mathbb{R}^{4})^{\perp}=\emptyset
. Plainly, every spacelike hyperplane is a base.

(44) Lemma.
Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
(i)
If
\Sigma
is a base, then there is
\xi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
|\xi(x)-\xi(y)|<|x-y|
for all
x\neq y
such that
\Sigma=\{(\xi(x),x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\}
.
(ii)

Conversely,
\Sigma
is a base if there is
\xi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
|\xi(x)-\xi(y)|<|x-y|
for all
x\neq y
and
\limsup_{|x|\to\infty}|\xi(x)|/|x|<1
such that
\Sigma=\{(\xi(x),x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\}
.

Proof.
As to (i), for every
a\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
there is
\xi(a)\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
(0,a)+\xi(a)(1,0)=(\xi(a),a)\in\Sigma
. Let
(\xi(x),x)\neq\mathfrak{y}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\mathfrak{y}\in\Sigma
. Since
\Sigma
is spacelike,
|\xi(x)-y_{0}|<|x-y|
follows. This proves also
\Sigma=\{(\xi(x),x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\}
.

As to (ii), clearly
\Sigma
is spacelike. Assume
\mathfrak{a}\not\in\tilde{\Sigma}
. Let
a_{0}>\xi(a)
. (The case
a_{0}<\xi(a)
is analogous.) Then there is
\mathfrak{z}=(1,e)
with
0<|e|\leq 1
such that
(\mathfrak{a}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{z})\cap\Sigma=\emptyset
. It follows
a_{0}+s>\xi(a+se)
for all
s
, since otherwise there were
s
with
a_{0}+s\leq\xi(a+se)
and by Bolzano’s theorem there were
s^{*}
satisfying
a_{0}+s^{*}=\xi(a+s^{*}e)
, i.e.,
(\mathfrak{a}+s^{*}\mathfrak{z})\in\Sigma
. Hence for
s<-a_{0}
one has
|\xi(a+se)|/|a+se|>|a_{0}+s|/|a+se|\to 1/|e|\geq 1
for
s\to-\infty
. This contradicts the assumption
\limsup_{|x|\to\infty}|\xi(x)|/|x|<1
.∎

Clearly, in (
11.5
)(i),
\xi
is Lipschitz continuous and
\Sigma
is a closed set. By (
11.5
)(i)
,\sigma
is a spacelike hyperplane if and only if it is the graph of
\xi(x)=\rho+xe
for some
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
and
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|e|<1
. Equivalently,
\sigma=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\rho\}
for uniquely determined
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
and timelike

\mathfrak{e}=(1,e)
.

There are maximal spacelike sets which are not a base. Two examples follow.

(45) Example.
The mass shell
M:=\{(\xi(x),x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\}
for
\xi(x):=\sqrt{|x|^{2}+m^{2}}
with mass
m>0
is a maximal spacelike set but not a base. One has
\tilde{M}=\{\mathfrak{y}:y_{0}>|y|\}
. Note that
|\xi(x)-\xi(y)|<|x-y|
for all
x\neq y
, cf.
(
11.5
)
.

Proof.
If
x\neq y
then
|\xi(x)-\xi(y)|=\frac{|\,|x|^{2}-|y|^{2}|}{\xi(x)+\xi(y)}<\frac{|\,|x|^{2}-|y|^{2}|}{|x|+|y|}=\big||x|-|y|\big|\leq|x-y|
. Hence, in particular,
M
is spacelike. —
M
is maximal, since for every
\mathfrak{y}\not\in M
,
|y_{0}-\xi(x)|<|y-x|
is not satisfied by
x:=y
.

We turn to
\tilde{M}
. Let
\mathfrak{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. If
y_{0}\leq|y|
, then
y_{0}+s\leq|y|+s<\xi(y+se)=\sqrt{(|y|+s)^{2}+m^{2}}
for all
s
choosing
e=\frac{1}{|y|}y
if
y\neq 0
and any unit vector otherwise. This proves
\mathfrak{y}\not\in\tilde{M}
. Next consider the case
y_{0}\geq\xi(y)
. Let
\mathfrak{z}=(1,e)
with
|e|\leq 1
. Then the function
f(s):=y_{0}+s-\xi(y+se)
has a zero for some
s\in\mathbb{R}
by the intermediate value theorem, since
f(y_{0})=-\xi(y+y_{0}e)<0
and
f(0)\geq 0
by the assumption. Hence

\mathfrak{y}\in\tilde{M}
. Finally let
|y|<y_{0}<\xi(y)
. In this case
f
has a zero, since
f(0)<0
by the assumption and
f(\bar{s})\geq 0
for
\bar{s}:=\frac{1}{2}\big(\xi(y)^{2}-y_{0}^{2}\big)/(y_{0}-|y|)
. As to the latter, note first that
\bar{s}>0
by the assumption and, hence,
f(\bar{s})=y_{0}+\bar{s}-\xi(y+\bar{s}e)\geq y_{0}+\bar{s}-\sqrt{(|y|+\bar{s})^{2}+m^{2}}=0
. Hence
\mathfrak{y}\in\tilde{M}
.∎

Particularly important is

(46) Example.

Let
P:=\Delta\cup\Gamma
with
\Delta:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,x_{3}\leq 0\}
,
\Gamma:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=1,x_{3}>1\}
.

Then
P
is maximal spacelike, whence
\hat{\Delta}\perp\hat{\Gamma}
, i.e.,
\hat{\Gamma}\subset\Delta^{\perp}
, and

\Delta^{\perp}\cap\Gamma^{\perp}=\emptyset
. But
P
is not a base. More precisely,

\tilde{P}=(\hat{\Delta}\cup\hat{\Gamma})^{\sim}=\hat{\Delta}\cup\hat{\Gamma}\cup\{\mathfrak{x}:2-x_{3}<x_{0}=x_{3}\}=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}|\leq-x_{3}\}\cup\{\mathfrak{x}:2-x_{3}<x_{0}\leq x_{3}\}\neq\hat{P}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
In particular,
\tilde{P}\cap\Delta^{\perp}=\hat{\Gamma}
.

-8,-1)(2,2.5)

Proof.

P
is spacelike, since
\Delta
and
\Gamma
are spacelike and since
(\mathfrak{y}-\mathfrak{x})^{\cdot 2}=1-|y-x|^{2}\leq 1-|y_{3}|^{2}<0
for
\mathfrak{x}\in\Delta
,
\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma
. Then check
\Delta^{\perp}=h(<0)\cap k(>0)
,
\Gamma^{\perp}=h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 2)
, whence
P^{\perp}=\Delta^{\perp}\cap\Gamma^{\perp}=\emptyset
is maximal spacelike.
— We turn to
\tilde{P}
. First we show
A\subset\tilde{P}
for
A:=\hat{\Delta}\cup\hat{\Gamma}\cup\{\mathfrak{x}:2-x_{3}<x_{0}=x_{3}\}
. Indeed, as
\Delta\subset P
it follows
\hat{\Delta}=\tilde{\Delta}\subset\tilde{P}
by (
11.5
). Similarly,
\hat{\Gamma}\subset\tilde{P}
. Finally let
2-x_{3}<x_{0}=x_{3}
. For
\mathfrak{z}=(1,0,0,1)
one has
\mathfrak{x}-x_{0}\mathfrak{z}=(0,x_{1},x_{2},0)\in P
. For any other
\mathfrak{z}=(1,e)
check

\mathfrak{x}-(x_{0}-1)\mathfrak{z}=(1,x-(x_{0}-1)e)
with
(x-(x_{0}-1)e)_{3}=x_{0}-(x_{0}-1)e_{3}=(x_{0}-1)(1-e_{3})+1>1
as
x_{0}>1
and
e_{3}<1
. Therefore
\mathfrak{x}-(x_{0}-1)\mathfrak{z}\in P
. Hence
\mathfrak{x}\in\tilde{P}
. — Next we show
B\cap\tilde{P}=\emptyset
for
B:=\{\mathfrak{x}:0<x_{0}+x_{3}\leq 2\}\cup\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{3}-1|<|x_{0}-1|\}
. Obviously
B\cap P=\emptyset
. If
0<y_{0}+y_{3}\leq 2
then the whole lightlike line
\mathfrak{y}+\mathbb{R}(1,0,0,-1)
through
\mathfrak{y}
lies in
\{0<x_{0}+x_{3}\leq 2\}
. If
|y_{3}-1|<|y_{0}-1|
, then the whole timelike line
\mathfrak{y}+\mathbb{R}(y_{0}-1,0,0,y_{3}-1)
through
\mathfrak{y}
lies in
\{|x_{3}-1|<|x_{0}-1|\}\cup\{x_{0}=x_{3}=1\}
.
Note
\{x_{0}=x_{3}=1\}\cap P=\emptyset
. Thus
B\cap\tilde{P}=\emptyset
. — It remains to show
A\cup B=\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
Since
\hat{\Delta}=h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 0)
,
\hat{\Gamma}=h(<0)\cap k(>2)
by (
11.1
)(b),
one has
A=\big(h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 0)\big)\cup\big(h(\leq 0)\cap k(>2)\big)
and
B=\big(k(>0)\cap k(\leq 2)\big)\cup\big(h(>0)\cap k(>2)\big)\cup\big(h(<0)\cap k(<2)\big)
. So
A\cup B=k(>2)\cup\big(h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 0)\big)\cup\big(h(<0)\cap k(<2)\big)\cup\big(k(>0)\cap k(\leq 2)\big)=k(>0)\cup\big(h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 0)\big)\cup\big(h(<0)\cap k(<2)\big)\supset k(>0)\cup\big(h(\geq 0)\cap k(\leq 0)\big)\cup\big(h(<0)\cap k(\leq 0)\big)=k(<0)\cup k(\leq 0)=\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
∎

(47) Lemma.
Let
A
be a subset of a base
\Sigma
. Then
(i)

\Sigma\setminus A=A^{\perp}\cap\Sigma
,
(ii)

(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\perp}=\hat{A}
,
(iii)

(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\land}=A^{\perp}
,
(iv)

A=\hat{A}\cap\Sigma
,
(v)

\hat{A}=\tilde{A}
, and finally
(vi)

A=\hat{A}_{\Sigma}=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in\hat{A}}\{\mathfrak{x}\in\Sigma:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\geq 0\}
the set of influence in
\Sigma
of
\hat{A}
.

Proof.
Recall first
A\subset\Sigma
,
A\cap A^{\perp}=\emptyset
, and
\Sigma\setminus A\subset A^{\perp}
as
\Sigma
is spacelike. Hence (i). — Then obviously
\hat{A}\subset(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\perp}
. We show now
(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\perp}\subset\tilde{A}
. Then (ii) follows by (
11.2
). So let
\mathfrak{x}\in(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\perp}
. This means
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}<0
for all
\mathfrak{y}\in\Sigma\setminus A
. Let
\mathfrak{z}\neq 0
,
\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}\geq 0
. As
\tilde{\Sigma}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z}\in\Sigma
. Assume
\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z}\in\Sigma\setminus A
. Then the contradiction
0>(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z})^{\cdot 2}=s^{2}\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}\geq 0
follows. Therefore
\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z}\in A
showing
\mathfrak{x}\in\tilde{A}
. — By orthocomplementation (ii) implies (iii). — Further, (iv) equals (i) for
\Sigma\setminus A
in place of
A
because of (ii). — (v) is already shown in the proof of (ii). — Finally, as to the less obvious inclusion
\supset
in (vi), consider
\mathfrak{x}\in\Sigma\setminus A
. This implies
\mathfrak{x}\in A^{\perp}
and hence, by definition,
\mathfrak{x}
lies spacelike to
\hat{A}
.
∎

The examples (
11.5
), (
11.5
) also show that
\Sigma
in (
11.5
)(v) in general cannot be replaced with a maximal spacelike set.

Contrary to
\mathcal{M}_{1}
, the set
\mathcal{M}_{2}
of all causally complete sets of the kind
\hat{A}
for
A
a subset of a base is closed under causal complementation. This follows from (
11.5
)(iii). Moreover,
\emptyset,\,\mathbb{R}^{4}\in\mathcal{M}_{2}
. So
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is an
orthoposet
, which is not orthomodular (see the definition
[
53
, sec. 11, Def. 129]
). Also
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is not a sublattice of
\mathcal{M}
still by (
11.4
).

11.6.
Diamonds

Recall that spacelike hyperplanes are special bases. A set
M
of the kind
M=\tilde{\Delta}
for
\Delta
in some spacelike hyperplane
\sigma
is called a
diamond
.
\Delta
is called a
flat base
of
M
.
If
M
is a diamond then, by (
11.5
)(v) and (iii),
M=\hat{M}=\tilde{M}
, whence
M
is causally complete, and
M^{\perp}=(\sigma\setminus\Delta)^{\land}=(\sigma\setminus\Delta)^{\sim}
is a diamond, too.

(48) Example
for some diamonds and their flat bases.

(i)
Let
\alpha,\delta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then for
(\sim,\backsim)\in\{(\geq,\leq),(>,<),(\leq,\geq),(<,>)\}

(a)

h(\sim\alpha)\cap k(\backsim\delta)

is a diamond with causal complement

(b)

h(\not\sim\alpha)\cap k(\not\backsim\delta)

and

(c)

\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\frac{\delta+\alpha}{2},\,x_{3}\backsim\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}\}

a flat base of it.

(ii)
Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\alpha<\beta
and
\gamma<\delta
. Then for
(\sim,\backsim)\in\{(\geq,\leq),(>,<)\}
and
(\sim^{\prime},\backsim^{\prime})\in\{(\leq,\geq),(<,>)\}

(d)

h(\sim\alpha)\cap k(\backsim\delta)\cap h(\sim^{\prime}\beta)\cap k(\backsim^{\prime}\gamma)

is a diamond with causal complement

(e)

\big(h(\not\sim\alpha)\cap k(\not\backsim\delta)\big)\cup\big(h(\not\sim^{\prime}\beta)\cap k(\not\backsim^{\prime}\gamma)\big)

and with unique flat base

(f)

\{\mathfrak{x}:(x_{0},x_{3})=s(\frac{\delta+\alpha}{2},\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2})+(1-s)(\frac{\gamma+\beta}{2},\frac{\gamma-\beta}{2}),\,0\sim^{\prime}s\backsim 1\}

Proof.
As to the proof of (i), we treat the case
(\sim,\backsim)=(\geq,\leq)
. The other cases follow analogously. Let
\mathfrak{y}\in\Delta^{\perp}
for
\Delta:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\frac{\delta+\alpha}{2},\,x_{3}\leq\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}\}
. This means for
x_{0}=\frac{\delta+\alpha}{2}
and all
x_{3}\leq\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2}
that
|y_{0}-x_{0}|<|y_{3}-x_{3}|
or, equivalently,

[

y_{0}-y_{3}<x_{0}-x_{3}
and
y_{0}+y_{3}>x_{0}+x_{3}

]
or
[

y_{0}-y_{3}>x_{0}-x_{3}
and
y_{0}+y_{3}<x_{0}+x_{3}

]
. Obviously this holds if and only if
y_{0}-y_{3}<\alpha
and
y_{0}+y_{3}>\delta
. So
\Delta^{\perp}=h(<\alpha)\cap k(>\delta)
, cf. (b). Then, by (
11.1
)(b),
\hat{\Delta}=\Delta^{\perp\perp}=h(\geq\alpha)\cap k(\leq\delta)
. One infers (i). — For the proof of (ii) we proceed analogously treating the case
(\sim,\backsim)=(\geq,\leq)
,
(\sim^{\prime},\backsim^{\prime})=(<,>)
explicitly. Let
\mathfrak{y}\in\Delta^{\perp}
for
