\Delta:=\{\mathfrak{x}:(x_{0},x_{3})=s(\frac{\delta+\alpha}{2},\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2})+(1-s)(\frac{\gamma+\beta}{2},\frac{\gamma-\beta}{2}),\,0<s\leq 1\}
. This means that for all
(x_{0},x_{3})=s(\frac{\delta+\alpha}{2},\frac{\delta-\alpha}{2})+(1-s)(\frac{\gamma+\beta}{2},\frac{\gamma-\beta}{2}),\,0<s\leq 1
one has
|y_{0}-x_{0}|<|y_{3}-x_{3}|
or, equivalently,

[

y_{0}-y_{3}<x_{0}-x_{3}
and
y_{0}+y_{3}>x_{0}+x_{3}

]
or
[

y_{0}-y_{3}>x_{0}-x_{3}
and
y_{0}+y_{3}<x_{0}+x_{3}

]
. Since
x_{0}-x_{3}=s\alpha+(1-s)\beta
,
x_{0}+x_{3}=s\delta+(1-s)\gamma
, this holds if and only if
[

y_{0}-y_{3}<\alpha
and
y_{0}+y_{3}>\delta

]

or
[

y_{0}-y_{3}\geq\beta
and
y_{0}+y_{3}\leq\gamma

]
. So
\Delta^{\perp}=\big(h(<\alpha)\cap k(>\delta)\big)\cup\big(h(\geq\beta)\cap k(\leq\gamma)\big)
, cf. (e). Therefore, by (
11.1
)(b),
\hat{\Delta}=\Delta^{\perp\perp}=h(\geq\alpha)\cap k(\leq\delta)\cap h(<\beta)\cap k(>\gamma)
. Hence (ii) follows. Note that uniqueness of the flat base follows from (
11.6
). ∎

(49) Lemma.
Let
M
be a diamond. Then there is at most one flat basis
\Delta
of
M
in a given spacelike hyperplane
\sigma
.
If
M
has two different flat bases, then either
M=\mathbb{R}^{4}
(and the flat bases of
M
are the spacelike hyperplanes
\sigma
) or there are a spacelike
2
-dimensional plane
\pi
and a subset
\Lambda\subset\pi
such that the flat bases of
M
are the intersections
M\cap\sigma
with the spacelike hyperplanes
\sigma
containing
\pi
. The intersections
M\cap\sigma
are equal to

\Lambda\cup\sigma^{c}
or
\Lambda\cup(\sigma\setminus\pi)
with
\sigma^{c}
one of the two components of
\sigma\setminus\pi
.

Proof.
The first assertion holds since
M\cap\sigma=\hat{\Delta}\cap\sigma=\Delta
. — Now, let
\Delta\subset\sigma
,
\Gamma\subset\tau
be different flat bases of
M
in spacelike hyperplanes
\sigma
,
\tau
. First note that
\Delta\cap\tau=\Gamma\cap\sigma
, since otherwise, without restriction, one had
\mathfrak{x}\in\Delta\cap\tau\in\tau\setminus\Gamma
, whence
\mathfrak{x}\in\Gamma^{\perp}
, which contradicts
\mathfrak{x}\in\hat{\Gamma}
. Next,
\Delta\setminus\tau\neq\emptyset
, since otherwise
\Delta,\Gamma\subset\tau
, whence the contradiction
\Delta=\Gamma
. In particular,
\tau\neq\sigma
. Now, without restriction let
\sigma=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Write
\tau=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\eta=\rho\}
for some
\eta=(1,e)
with
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|<1
,
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
, and
(\rho,e)\neq 0
. For
x\in\sigma\setminus\tau
obviously
\xi:=xe+\rho\neq 0
. — We claim that for every
x\in\Delta\setminus\tau
the whole closed ball

B(x,|\xi|)
around
x
with radius
|\xi|
is contained in
\Delta
. Indeed, as
(0,x)\in\Delta\subset M=\hat{\Gamma}=\tilde{\Gamma}
, there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(0,x)+s(\xi,0)\in\Gamma\subset\tau
. It follows
s=1
and
(\xi,x)\in\Gamma\subset\hat{\Delta}=\tilde{\Delta}
. Hence for every
|z|\leq 1
there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(\xi,x)+s(1,z)
is in
\Delta
. This implies the claim. — If
e=0
, then
\rho\neq 0
,
\sigma\cap\tau=\emptyset
, so that
B(x,|\rho|)\subset\Delta
for all
x\in\Delta
, whence
\Delta=\sigma
and
M=\mathbb{R}^{4}
. It remains the case
e\neq 0
. Here
\sigma\setminus\tau=\sigma^{>}\cup\sigma^{<}
, where
\sigma^{\gtrless}:=\{x\in\sigma:\xi\gtrless 0\}
are the components of
\sigma\setminus\tau
, which are open (in
\sigma
).
Accordingly,
\Delta\setminus\tau=\Delta^{>}\cup\Delta^{<}
with
\Delta^{\gtrless}:=\Delta\cap\sigma^{\gtrless}
. — We claim
\Delta^{>}=\sigma^{>}
if
\Delta^{>}\neq\emptyset
. Indeed, let
x\in\Delta^{>}
. Then
B(x,\xi)\subset\Delta
. For every
x^{\prime}\in B(x,\xi)
one has
\xi^{\prime}\neq 0
. Therefore, by continuity,
\xi^{\prime}>0
, whence
B(x,\xi)\subset\Delta^{>}
. So
\Delta^{>}
is open. It remains to show that
\Delta^{>}
is also closed in
\sigma^{>}
. Let
\bar{x}
be in the closure of
\Delta^{>}
. As
\bar{x}\in\sigma^{>}
one has
\bar{\xi}>0
. There is
x\in\Delta^{>}
with
|x-\bar{x}|<\frac{1}{2}\bar{\xi}
. Then
\xi=\bar{\xi}+(x-\bar{x})e>\frac{1}{2}\bar{\xi}
. So
\bar{x}\in B(x,\frac{1}{2}\bar{\xi})\subset\Delta^{>}
. — Analogously
\Delta^{<}=\sigma^{<}
if
\Delta^{<}\neq\emptyset
. Therefore,
\Delta\setminus\tau
equals
\sigma^{>}
or
\sigma^{<}
or
\sigma^{>}\cup\sigma^{<}=\sigma\setminus\tau
. — Finally the result follows for
\Lambda:=\Delta\cap\tau
and
\pi:=\sigma\cap\tau
.∎

In particular, the above result shows that a diamond has a
unique flat base
if it does not contain a spacelike half-hyperplane or, a fortiori, if it is bounded.

In general, the intersection of two diamonds is not a diamond. (For instance, let
\Delta:=\{x_{0}=0,-4<x_{3}<4\}\subset\sigma:=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\Gamma:=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}\subset\tau:=\{2\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
\Gamma_{1}:=\{x_{0}=2,-2<x_{3}<0\}
,
\Gamma_{2}:=\{x_{0}=2,0<x_{3}<4\}
. Then the intersection
\hat{\Delta}\cap\hat{\Gamma}=\hat{\Lambda}\cup\hat{\Gamma}_{1}
with
\Lambda=\{(2-s,0,0,3s):0\leq s\leq 1\}
is not a diamond, as
\Lambda
and
\Gamma_{1}
do not lie in a common spacelike hyperplane.)

12.
Localization Operators for Causally Complete Regions

We turn to the question about a rep
(W,F)
of the lattice
\mathcal{M}
of causally complete regions posed at the beginning of sec.
11
.
There is a technical reason concerning measurability (see (
12
)) and also a structural reason concerning maximal spacelike sets (see (
12
)(c)) why such a rep does not exist.

(50) Theorem.

There is no rep
(W,F)
of
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.
Assume the existence of a rep
(W,F)
.
For every subset
X
of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
put
Q(X):=F\big((\{0\}\times X)^{\land}\big)
. (Recall that causally complete sets are measurable (
11.3
).) Clearly
Q(\mathbb{R}^{3})=I
,
Q(\emptyset)=0
. Let
X_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be disjoint. Then
(\{0\}\times X_{n})^{\land}
are spacelike separated and
\big(\cup_{n}(\{0\}\times X_{n})\big)^{\land}=\lor_{n}(\{0\}\times X_{n})^{\land}
. Hence
Q(\cup_{n}X_{n})=F\big(\big(\{0\}\times(\cup_{n}X_{n})\big)^{\land}\big)=F\big(\big(\cup_{n}(\{0\}\times X_{n})\big)^{\land}\big)=F\big(\lor_{n}(\{0\}\times X_{n})^{\land}\big)=\sum_{n}F\big((\{0\}\times X_{n})^{\land}\big)=\sum_{n}Q(X_{n})
. Furthermore, for every translation
b\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
one has
W(b)Q(X)W(b)^{-1}=W(b)F\big((\{0\}\times X)^{\land}\big)W(b)^{-1}=F\big((0,b)+(\{0\}\times X)^{\land}\big)=F\big((\{0\}\times(b+X))^{\land}\big)=Q(b+X)
.

Hence
Q
is a translation covariant PM on the power set of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We are going to argue that such a measure does not exist. Choose any section
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
\mathbb{R}^{3}/\mathbb{Q}^{3}
, i.e.,
\{b+K:b\in\mathbb{Q}^{3}\}
is a countable disjoint cover of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Therefore
I=Q(\mathbb{R}^{3})=\sum_{b\in\mathbb{Q}^{3}}Q(b+K)
. Since
Q(b+K)=W(b)Q(K)W(b)^{-1}
it follows
Q(K)\neq 0
. Now for every eigenvector
\varphi=Q(K)\varphi
one has
W(b)\varphi=Q(b+K)W(b)\varphi
and hence
\langle\varphi,W(b)\varphi\rangle=0
for
b\in\mathbb{Q}^{3}
since
Q(K)Q(b+K)=0
. Then by continuity
0=\langle\varphi,W(b)\varphi\rangle\to\parallel\!\!\varphi\!\!\parallel^{2}
for
b\in\mathbb{Q}^{3}
,
b\to 0
, whence the contradiction
\varphi=0
.∎

Note that by the proof of (
12
) there are even no reps of
\mathcal{M}
which are merely translation covariant rather than Poincaré covariant.

The proof of (
12
) makes clear that the lattice
\mathcal{M}
is too large for a covariant rep in that all causally complete sets are measurable. However there is a more relevant obstruction for such a rep which already becomes apparent if we restrict our focus to the special set of diamonds with measurable flat bases.
Consider first a causal system
(W,E)
. One observes

(51) Lemma.
Let
\sigma
,
\tau
be spacelike hyperplanes.
Let
M=\hat{\Delta}
be a diamond, where
\Delta
is a measurable subset of
\sigma
. Let
\Gamma\subset M\cap\tau
be a measurable subset of
\tau
. Then
E(\Gamma)\leq E(\Delta)
.
If
\hat{\Gamma}=M
then
E(\Delta)=E(\Gamma)
.

Proof.
As
\Gamma\subset\hat{\Delta}
, obviously
\Gamma_{\sigma}\subset\hat{\Delta}_{\sigma}
. Since the latter equals
\Delta
by (
11.5
)(vi), one has
\Gamma_{\sigma}\subset\Delta
. Hence the result follows by
causality (
7.2
)(a).∎

Hence
E
assigns to all measurable flat bases of a diamond the same operator. Therefore
E
induces by

F_{\textsc{e}}(M):=E(\Delta)\textrm{ for }\Delta\in\mathfrak{S}\textrm{ with }\hat{\Delta}=M

a map
F_{\textsc{e}}
on the set of diamonds with measurable flat base. Obviously it has the properties assumed for
F
in (
12
).

(52) Theorem.
Let
W
be a representation of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
and let
F
be an orthogonal projection-valued map on the set of diamonds with measurable flat bases. Let
F
be Poincaré covariant by
W
and suppose that
F
is normalized, local orthogonal, and
\sigma
-orthoadditive
\sum_{n}F(M_{n})=F(\lor_{n}M_{n})
if the diamonds
M_{n}
are mutually spacelike separated with bases in a common spacelike hyperplane. Then
(a), (b), (c)
hold.

(a)

F
is monotone and
\sigma
-supraorthoadditive, i.e.,
\sum_{n}F(M_{n})\leq F(M)
for mutually spacelike separated
M_{n}\subset M
.

(b)

(W,E)
with
E(\Delta):=F(\hat{\Delta})
for
\Delta\in\mathfrak{S}
is a causal system.

(c)

F
fails to be orthoadditive since there are spacelike separated
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
satisfying
M_{1}\lor M_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
F(M_{1})+F(M_{2})<I
.

Proof.
(b) Let
\sigma
be a spacelike hyperplane. Let
\Delta_{n}\subset\sigma
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
be measurable and disjoint. Put
M_{n}:=\hat{\Delta}_{n}
. Then
M_{n}^{\perp}=\Delta_{n}^{\perp}=(\sigma\setminus\Delta_{n})^{\land}\supset\hat{\Delta}_{m}=M_{m}
for
n\neq m
by (
11.5
)(iii). Hence the diamonds
M_{n}
are mutually spacelike separated, whence
\sum_{n}E(\Delta_{n})=\sum_{n}F(M_{n})=F(M)
for
M:=\lor_{n}M_{n}
by
\sigma
-orthoadditvity of
F
. For
\Delta:=\bigcup_{n}\Delta_{n}
obviously one has
\hat{\Delta}=(\bigcup_{n}M_{n})^{\land}=M
, whence
E(\Delta)=F(\hat{\Delta})=F(M)
. This proves
\sigma
-additivity for
E
. — Moreover,
E(\sigma)=F(\mathbb{R}^{4})=I
and
E(\emptyset)=F(\emptyset)=0
. —
Poincaré covariance carries over from
F
to
E
since
(g\cdot\Delta)^{\land}=g\cdot\hat{\Delta}
. — Let
\Delta,\Gamma\in\mathfrak{S}
be spacelike separated. So are the completions
\hat{\Delta}
and
\hat{\Gamma}
, whence
E(\Delta)\,E(\Gamma)=F(\hat{\Delta})\,F(\hat{\Gamma})=0
by local orthogonality of
F
. Hence
E
is causal by (
7.2
)(f). — Thus
(W,E)
is a causal system.

(a) Let
M_{1}\subset M_{2}
. Let
\Delta_{i}\in\mathfrak{S}
be a base of
M_{i}
. Then, by (
12
),
F(M_{1})=E(\Delta_{1})\leq E(\Delta_{2})=F(M_{2})
. —

\sigma
-supraorthoadditivity of
F
is obvious as
F(M_{n})
are mutually orthogonal projections
\leq F(M)
.

(c) Consider

\Delta:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,x_{3}\leq 0\}
and
\Gamma:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=1,x_{3}>1\}
. By (
11.5
),

M_{1}:=\hat{\Delta}
and
M_{2}:=\hat{\Gamma}
are spacelike separated and satisfy
M_{1}\lor M_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
. For
\tau:=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\sigma:=\{1\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
\sigma^{>}:=\Gamma
, one has
F(M_{1})+F(M_{2})=E(\Delta)+E(\Gamma)=I-\big(E(\tau\setminus\Delta)-E(\Gamma)\big)=I-\big(E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})\big)<I
,
since
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})>0
by (
10
).∎

F
in (
12
) is by no means a restricted lattice homomorphism. For instance,
\{0\}
,
\{2,0,0,0)\}
, and
\{0\}\lor\{2,0,0,0)\}
are diamonds by (
11.1
), (
11.1
). Clearly
F(\{0\})=F(\{2,0,0,0)\})=0
and hence
F(\{0\})\lor F(\{2,0,0,0)\})=0
, but by (
11.1
)
F(\{0\}\lor\{2,0,0,0)\})=E(\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=1,|x|\leq 1\})>0
.

One feels that one should not content oneself with the no-go-result on orthoadditivity (
12
)(c) for
F_{\textsc{e}}
. What is really behind it is revealed by a thorough analysis of the eigenstates of the projections
E(\sigma^{>}_{\tau})-E(\sigma^{>})
considered in (
10
). In (
17
), (
17
) and Eq. (
21.5
) for the Dirac and the Weyl systems an intrinsic relation between time evolution and boosts is established. This relation in turn is inextricably linked with the lattice
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
generated by the non-timelike relation (in place of the more restrictive spacelike relation) and possible reps of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
. Sec.
24
,
25
,
26
are concerned with this topic.

13.
The Dirac System

For every mass
m>0
the Dirac system is shown to be a causal system as defined in (
9
). It will turn
out to be the only irreducible massive causal system with finite spinor dimension.
16
16

16

Actually we did not succeed as yet to rule out certain irreducible massive SCT, see (
28.2
).
As being of fundamental importance we are going to study it in detail.

In momentum space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
, which is the image of position space under Fourier transformation
\mathcal{F}
, the representation
W^{\textsc{d}}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
describing the Dirac system with mass
m>0

is given by

\big(W^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(t,b,A)\varphi\big)(p)=\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}\sum_{\eta=+,-}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\eta\epsilon(p)}\pi^{\eta}(p)s(A)^{*-1}\varphi(q^{\eta})

(13.1)

(see e.g.
[
5
, Theorem 5, case
n=1
]
)
with the Dirac Hamiltonian
H

\big(H^{mom}\varphi\big)(p)=h(p)\varphi(p),\quad\;h(p):=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\alpha_{k}p_{k}+\beta m

(13.2)

being the matrix multiplication operator by
h
. For the energy and the shell representation of the Dirac system see sec.
30.4
.

As to the notations,
\beta,\alpha_{k}
denote the Dirac matrices, which in the Weyl representation read

\beta=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&I_{2}\\
I_{2}&0\end{array}\right),\quad\alpha_{k}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma_{k}&0\\
0&-\sigma_{k}\end{array}\right)

with
\sigma_{k}
the Pauli matrices. So
h(p)^{*}=h(p)
,
h(p)^{2}=\epsilon(p)^{2}I_{4}

with
\epsilon(p)=\sqrt{|p|^{2}+m^{2}}
. As to
q^{\eta}
, recall
\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}=(\eta\epsilon(p),p)
and
\mathfrak{q}^{\eta}=(q_{0}^{\eta},q^{\eta})=A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}
from Eq. (
3.6
).
Furthermore,
\pi^{\eta}(p):=\frac{1}{2}\big(I_{2}+\frac{\eta}{\epsilon(p)}h(p)\big)
for
\eta\in\{+,-\}
. The multiplication operator
by
\pi^{\eta}
is the orthogonal projection onto the subspace of positive energy
(
\eta=+
) and negative energy (
\eta=-
), respectively.
Finally, in the Weyl representation one has
s(A):=\operatorname{diag}(A,A^{*-1})
.
17
17

17

Let
S\in U(4)
and let
S
be the unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
with
(\text{\small{{\it S}}}\psi)(x):=S\,\psi(x)
. Then

\text{\small{{\it S}}}\;W^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(g)\,\text{\small{{\it S}}}^{-1}
is given by the right hand side of Eq. (
13.1
) with respect to

\beta^{\prime}:=S\,\beta\,S^{-1}
,
\alpha^{\prime}_{k}:=S\,\alpha_{k}\,S^{-1}
, and
s^{\prime}(A):=S\,s(A)\,S^{-1}
. — Moreover, there is a formula valid for any permissible choice (cf.
[
5
, Lemma 2]
) of the
\beta
,
\alpha
’s, cf.
[
58
, (1-44)]

s(A)=\big(a_{0}1_{4}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}a_{k}\gamma_{5}\alpha_{k}\big)\frac{1}{2}(1_{4}+\gamma_{5})+\big(\bar{a}_{0}1_{4}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\bar{a}_{k}\gamma_{5}\alpha_{k}\big)\frac{1}{2}(1_{4}-\gamma_{5})

where
\gamma_{5}:=-i\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}
and
A=a_{0}1_{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}a_{k}\sigma_{k}
with
a_{0},a_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
,
a^{2}_{0}-a_{1}^{2}-a_{2}^{2}-a_{3}^{2}=1
.

In (
14
) we will need the formula

\pi^{\eta}(p)s(A^{*-1})=\big(\epsilon(q^{\eta})/\epsilon(p)\big)s(A)\pi^{\eta}(q^{\eta})

(13.3)

which is mainly a consequence of the fundamental relation
A(\sum_{\mu=0}^{3}\mathfrak{p}_{\mu}\sigma_{\mu})A^{*}=\sum_{\mu=0}^{3}(A\cdot\mathfrak{p})_{\mu}\sigma_{\mu}
with
\sigma_{0}:=I_{2}
for
A\in\mathbb{C}^{2,2}
,
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

The Dirac representation
W^{\textsc{d}}
is reducible. It is the sum of the representations of the electron on the subspace of positive energy, and its antisystem, the positron, see appendix
30.4
. Hence

W^{\textsc{d}}=[m,\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{2}$}},+]\oplus[m,\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{2}$}},-]

with
m>0
. Any unit vector in the carrier space of
W^{\textsc{d}}
is called a
Dirac state
.

The Euclidean covariant Dirac position operator
X^{\textsc{d}}
is related to
W^{\textsc{d}}
by

N=\textnormal{\small{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\big(X^{\textsc{d}}H+HX^{\textsc{d}}\big)

(13.4)

In position space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
the components of
X^{\textsc{d}}

are the multiplication operators by the coordinate functions. So one has a WL, the
Dirac localization

E^{\textsc{d}}
, with the localization operators in position space given by

E^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(\Delta)\psi=1_{\Delta}\psi,\quad\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})

(13.5)

As known, the Dirac time evolution is causal, i.e.

W^{\textsc{d}}(t)E^{\textsc{d}}(\Delta)W^{\textsc{d}}(t)^{-1}\leq E^{\textsc{d}}(\Delta_{t})

(13.6)

for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
measurable. See e.g.
[
28
, Remark on Theorem 1.2]
. Here we infer this by
[
5
, Theorem 10 (b)]
from the fact that the entire matrix-valued function
z\mapsto\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th(z)}
on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
is exponentially bounded
18
18

18

An entire matrix-valued function
f
on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
is called
exponentially bounded
or
of exponential type
with exponent
\delta\geq 0
if there is a finite constant
C_{\delta}
such that
\parallel\!\!f(z)\!\!\parallel\leq C_{\delta}\operatorname{e}^{\delta|z|}
with
|z|^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|z_{j}|^{2}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
. The
type

\tau
of
f
is the infimum of all its exponents. If, in addition,

f|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\in L^{2}
and
\tau^{\prime}>0,\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau
then
\parallel\!\!f(z)\!\!\parallel\leq C_{\tau^{\prime}}\operatorname{e}^{\tau^{\prime}|\operatorname{Im}z|}
follows (see
[
46
, Cor. 10.10]
).

by
C_{t}\operatorname{e}^{|t|\,|z|}

\forall

z
with
|z|^{2}=|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}+|z_{3}|^{2}
and some finite constant
C_{t}
.

E^{\textsc{d}}
and
X^{\textsc{d}}
, in contrast to
E^{\textsc{nw}}
and
X^{\textsc{nw}}
, do not commute with the sign of energy
\operatorname{sgn}(H)
, since otherwise the trace of

E^{\textsc{d}}
on the positive energy subspace would yield a WL with causal time evolution for the electron, which does not exist by (
4
). Consequently the system
(W^{\textsc{d}},E^{\textsc{d}})
is irreducible. By the same reason, Eq. (
13.4
) cannot be solved for
X^{\textsc{d}}
, as occurs in case of the Bakamjian-Thomas-Foldy formula Eq. (
2.3
).

One observes that
E^{\textsc{d}}
admits dilational covariance as
D_{\lambda}E^{\textsc{d}}(\Delta)D_{\lambda}^{-1}=E^{\textsc{d}}(\lambda\Delta)
,
\lambda>0
holds for

(D_{\lambda}^{pos}\psi)(x):=\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}}\psi(\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{\lambda}$}}x)

(13.7)

Finally, by (
6.2
), we extend
E^{\textsc{d}}
on
\mathfrak{S}
by Poincaré covariance.

14.
Dirac localization is causal

In position space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
Dirac’s Hamiltonian
H
is represented by
H^{pos}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}H^{mom}\mathcal{F}=\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}\,\frac{1}{\operatorname{i}}\partial_{k}+\beta m
.

(53) Lemma.

In position space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
the Dirac representation
W^{\textsc{d}}
reads

\big(W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(g)\psi\big)(x)=s(A)\big(\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}y_{0}H^{pos}}\psi\big)(y)

where
g=(t,b,A)\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
,
and
(y_{0},y):=g^{-1}\cdot(0,x)=A^{-1}\cdot(-t,x-b)
.

Proof.
By definition
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}\equiv\mathcal{F}^{-1}W^{\textsc{d}\,mom}\mathcal{F}
with
W^{\textsc{d}\,mom}
from Eq. (
13.1
).
Apply first
Eq. (
13.3
). Then
\big(W^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(g)\varphi\big)(p)=s(A)\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}(t,b)\cdot(\eta\epsilon(p),p)}(\epsilon(q^{\eta})/\epsilon(p))\pi^{\eta}(q^{\eta})\varphi(q^{\eta})
. So
\big(\mathcal{F}^{-1}W^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(g)\mathcal{F}\psi\big)(x)=s(A)(2\pi)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sum_{\eta}\int\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}xp}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}}\pi^{\eta}(q^{\eta})\varphi(q^{\eta})\,(\epsilon(q^{\eta})/\epsilon(p))\operatorname{d}^{3}p
with
\mathfrak{a}=(t,b)
,
\varphi:=\mathcal{F}\psi
. It equals
s(A)(2\pi)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sum_{\eta}\int\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}xp}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}}\pi^{\eta}(k)\varphi(k)\,\operatorname{d}^{3}k
by Lorentz-invariant integration and renaming the integration variable
q^{\eta}
by
k
. Furthermore, using
\mathfrak{y}=(y_{0},y)=g^{-1}\cdot(0,x)
one has
xp+\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}=-((0,x)-\mathfrak{a})\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}=-\mathfrak{y}\cdot\mathfrak{q}^{\eta}=-y_{0}\eta\epsilon(q^{\eta})+yq^{\eta}=-y_{0}\eta\epsilon(k)+yk
. Hence
\sum_{\eta}\ \operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}xp}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}}\pi^{\eta}(k)=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}yk}\sum_{\eta}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}y_{0}\eta\epsilon(k)}\pi^{\eta}(k)=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}yk}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}y_{0}h(k)}
. Therefore, one concludes
\big(\mathcal{F}^{-1}W^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(g)\mathcal{F}\psi\big)(x)=s(A)(2\pi)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\int\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}yk}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}y_{0}h(k)}\varphi(k)\,\operatorname{d}^{3}k=s(A)\big(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\operatorname{exp}(-\operatorname{i}y_{0}H^{mom})\varphi\big)(y)=s(A)\big(\operatorname{exp}(-\operatorname{i}y_{0}H^{pos})\psi\big)(y)
as asserted.∎

More familiar than the position space wave-function
\psi
in (
14
) is the time-dependent Dirac wave-function
\Psi
. The latter is the solution of the initial-value problem for the
Dirac equation

i\partial_{t}\Psi=H^{pos}\Psi,\quad\Psi(0,\cdot)=\psi\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{3}\text{ with }\psi\in\operatorname{dom}(H^{pos})

(14.1)

Hence
\psi
and
\Psi
are related by
\Psi(t,x)=\big(\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}tH^{pos}}\psi\big)(x)=\big(W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(-t)\psi\big)(x)
. From this relation and (
14
) one gets
the transformation law under Poincaré transformations for
\Psi
. Indeed, let
\Psi_{g}
be the wave-function with initial value
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(g)\psi
. Then

\Psi_{g}(\mathfrak{x})=s(A)\,\Psi(g^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x})

(14.2)

for
\mathfrak{x}=(t,x)
, since

W^{\textsc{d}}(-t)W^{\textsc{d}}(g)=W^{\textsc{d}}(g^{\prime})
for
g^{\prime}:=(-t,0,I_{2})g
satisfying
g^{\prime-1}\cdot(0,x)=g^{-1}\cdot(t,x)
.

(54) Theorem.

(W^{\textsc{d}},E^{\textsc{d}})
is a causal system
(
9
)
.

Proof.
We are going to show
W^{\textsc{d}}(h)\,E^{\textsc{d}}(\Gamma)\,W^{\textsc{d}}(h)^{-1}\,\leq\,E^{\textsc{d}}(\Gamma_{h})
for
h\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
and
\Gamma
a measurable subset of a spacelike hyperplane
\sigma
(see (
7.2
)(c)).

There are a subset
\Delta
of the hyperplane
\varepsilon=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
k\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\sigma=k\cdot\varepsilon
,
\Gamma=k\cdot\Delta
.
By Poincaré covariance Eq. (
3.1
), the left hand side of the inequality reads
W^{\textsc{d}}(hk)\,E^{\textsc{d}}(\Delta)\,W^{\textsc{d}}(hk)^{-1}
. At the right hand side one has
(k\cdot\Delta)_{h}=k\cdot\Delta_{k^{-1}hk}
and hence
E^{\textsc{d}}(\Gamma_{h})=W^{\textsc{d}}(k)E^{\textsc{d}}(\Delta_{k^{-1}hk})W^{\textsc{d}}(k)^{-1}
. Put
g:=k^{-1}hk
. Thus it suffices to show
W^{\textsc{d}}(g)\,E^{\textsc{d}}(\Delta)\,W^{\textsc{d}}(g)^{-1}\,\leq\,E^{\textsc{d}}(\Delta_{g})
,
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
.

For this consider any state
\psi
localized in
\Delta
, i.e.
E^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(\Delta)\psi=\psi
. By the causal time evolution Eq. (
13.6
),
\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}tH^{pos}}\psi
is localized in
\Delta_{t}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore and by (
13.5
), according to (
14
),
\big(W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(g)\psi\big)(x)=0
if
y\not\in\Delta_{y_{0}}
, where
\mathfrak{y}=g^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x}
and
\mathfrak{x}=(0,x)
. Now,
y\not\in\Delta_{y_{0}}
means
|y-a|>|y_{0}|

\forall

a\in\Delta
or equivalently
(\mathfrak{y}-\mathfrak{a})^{\cdot 2}<0

\forall

\mathfrak{a}:=(0,a)
,
a\in\Delta
. Put
(z_{0},z):=g\cdot(0,a)
. Then
(\mathfrak{y}-\mathfrak{a})^{\cdot 2}=(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{z})^{\cdot 2}
. Therefore
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(g)\psi
is localized in

\{\mathfrak{x}\in\varepsilon:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{z})^{\cdot 2}\geq 0\textrm{ for some }\mathfrak{z}\in g\cdot\Delta\}=\Delta_{g}
. This implies the assertion.∎

In establishing (
14
) we avoided the use of the cumbersome propagator (see e.g.
[
28
, (1.87)]
) but applied the position representation (
14
) and exploited causal time evolution Eq. (
13.6
).

We like to recall the equivalent descriptions of causality given in (
7.2
). In particular, causality is equivalent to local orthogonality, viz.
E^{\textsc{d}}(\Gamma)\;E^{\textsc{d}}(\Gamma^{\prime})=0
if
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
are spacelike separated. Moreover, by (
7.2
), Dirac localization is frame-independent.

15.
Dirac localization and time evolution

One is interested in the temporal behavior of the carrier of a wavefunction. The following observation is instructive.
Let
R>0
and let
\psi
be a Dirac state localized in the ball
B_{R}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|x|\leq R\}
. Let
\psi_{t}
denote the state
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(t)\psi
evolved in time
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Fix
t_{0}\neq 0
. Then, due to causality,
\psi_{t_{0}}
is localized in
B_{R+|t_{0}|}
. This, however, does not exclude that
\psi_{t_{0}}
actually is localized in a smaller ball
B_{r}
. Here every
r>\big||t_{0}|-R\big|
occurs.

Indeed, let
0<\rho<R
and let the state
\chi
be localized in
B_{\rho}
. Let
\varsigma
denote the sign of
t_{0}
. Then
\psi:=W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}\big(\varsigma(\rho-R)\big)\chi
is localized in
B_{R}
, and
\psi_{t}=W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}\big(t+\varsigma(\rho-R)\big)\chi
is localized in
B_{r(t)}
for
r(t):=\rho+|t+\varsigma(\rho-R)|
, whence the assertion for
r=r(t_{0})=\rho+\big||t_{0}|-R+\rho\big|
.

Actually a rather detailed picture of how in general the border of a wavefunction moves is available. It is drawn in the discussion (
15.2
), which is based on the results (
15.2
), (
15.2
) and the subsequent corollaries and lemma up to (
15.2
).

15.1.
Mathematical preliminaries

The main mathematical tool is a theorem by Pólya and Plancherel (see e.g.
[
59
]
,
[
60
]
), which is reported here in (
15.1
). First recall the P-indicator (i.e., the Pólya-Plancherel indicator)
h_{f}
of an entire function
f
on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
being

h_{f}(\lambda)=\sup\{h_{f}(\lambda,x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\},\,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\textrm{\, with\, }h_{f}(\lambda,x)=\varlimsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{1}{r}\ln|f(x+\operatorname{i}\lambda\,r)|

(15.1)

and the support function
H_{C}
for a convex set
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
being

H_{C}(\lambda)=\sup\{x\lambda:x\in C\},\,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(15.2)

(55) Theorem.

A function
f:\mathbb{C}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
is entire and exponentially bounded (see footnote in
sec.
13
) with
f|_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\in L^{2}
if and only if there is
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
vanishing outside a bounded set with

f(z)=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\operatorname{e}^{-iqz}g(q)\operatorname{d}q

i.e.,
f
is the Fourier-Laplace transform of
g
.

Then
h_{f}=H_{C(g)}
, where
C(g)
is the smallest convex set outside of which
g
vanishes almost everywhere.

Moreover,
f|_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is bounded by
(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g(q)|\operatorname{d}q
and, by the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma,
it vanishes at infinity. Also,
for each
\lambda
one has
h_{f}(\lambda,x)=h_{f}(\lambda)
for almost all
x
, and
h_{kf}=h_{k}+h_{f}
holds for any exponentially bounded entire function
k
.

The Fourier-Laplace transform of
\psi_{t}
localized in
B_{R+|t|}
is an entire function
\varphi_{t}
, which
is exponentially bounded with exponent
R+|t|
, i.e.,
|\varphi_{t}(z)|\leq C\operatorname{e}^{(R+|t|)|z|}
,
z\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Recall (
13.2
). Due to
h(z)^{2}=(z^{2}_{1}+z^{2}_{2}+z^{2}_{3}+m^{2})I_{4}
, the time evolution yields

\varphi_{t}(z)=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th(z)}\varphi(z)=\cos\big(t\epsilon(z)\big)\,\varphi(z)+\operatorname{i}t\operatorname{sinc}\big(t\epsilon(z)\big)\,h(z)\varphi(z)

(15.3)

for every
z\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Here
\epsilon
satisfies
\epsilon(z)^{2}=z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}+z_{3}^{2}+m^{2}
, and

\operatorname{sinc}(w)=\sin(w)/w
for
w\neq 0
,
\operatorname{sinc}(0)=1
. From Eq. (
15.3
) one obtains

\varphi_{t}+\varphi_{-t}=2\cos(t\epsilon)\,\varphi,\quad\varphi_{t}-\varphi_{-t}=2\operatorname{i}t\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)\,h\varphi

(15.4)

and

(\varphi_{t})_{k}\varphi_{l}=\cos(t\epsilon)\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}+\operatorname{i}t\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)(h\varphi)_{k}\varphi_{l}
and hence

\phi_{kl}=\operatorname{i}t\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)\chi_{kl}

for
\phi_{kl}:=(\varphi_{t})_{k}\varphi_{l}-(\varphi_{t})_{l}\varphi_{k}
and
\chi_{kl}:=(h\varphi)_{k}\varphi_{l}-(h\varphi)_{l}\varphi_{k}
, where
k,l=1,\dots,4
enumerate the spinor components.

There are
k,l
such that
\chi_{kl}\neq 0
. Indeed, assume
\chi_{kl}=0
for all
k,l
. Then
\varphi_{k}\,h\varphi=(h\varphi)_{k}\,\varphi
and hence
\epsilon^{2}\,\varphi_{k}\,\varphi=(h\varphi)_{k}\,h\varphi
. Then
(h\varphi)_{k}^{2}-\epsilon^{2}\,\varphi_{k}^{2}=0
. Fix
k,z_{2},z_{3}
such that
f(\zeta):=\varphi_{k}(\zeta,z_{2},z_{3})
is not the null function. Set
g(\zeta):=(h\varphi)_{k}(\zeta,z_{2},z_{3})
. Choose the square root
\mu(\zeta)
of
\zeta^{2}+z_{2}^{2}+z_{3}^{2}
such that
g=\mu f
. This, however, is impossible as
g/f
is meromorphic whereas
\mu
is not.
Thus
\chi:=\chi_{kl}
,
\phi:=\phi_{kl}
are non-zero entire exponentially bounded functions with exponents
2R
and
2R+|t|
, respectively, satisfying

\phi=\operatorname{i}t\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)\,\chi

(15.5)

We are going to exploit the relations Eqs. (
15.4
) and (
15.5
).

(56) Lemma.
Let
\mu,t,u,v
be real,
\mu\geq 0
. Then there are finite constants
A_{t}>0,B_{t},C_{t}
independent of
u,v
such that

(a)

A_{t}\operatorname{e}^{|tv|}\leq\big|\cos\big(t\sqrt{\mu^{2}+(u+\operatorname{i}v)^{2}}\,\big)\big|\leq B_{t}\operatorname{e}^{|tv|}

(b)

A_{t}|u+\operatorname{i}v|^{-1}\operatorname{e}^{|tv|}\leq\big|\operatorname{sinc}\big(t\sqrt{\mu^{2}+(u+\operatorname{i}v)^{2}}\,\big)\big|\leq B_{t}|u+\operatorname{i}v|^{-1}\operatorname{e}^{|tv|}

for all
u
and
|v|>C_{t}
.

Proof.

First we show

z\in\mathbb{C},|z|\leq\frac{1}{2}\;\Rightarrow\;\sqrt{1+z}=1+\zeta\textrm{\; with\; }|\zeta|\leq\frac{3}{4}|z|

(
\star
)

Indeed, let
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}
,
f(r):=(1+rz)^{1/2}
. As
f^{\prime}(r)=\frac{1}{2}(1+rz)^{-1/2}z
one has
f(1)=1+\zeta

with
\zeta:=\int_{0}^{1}f^{\prime}(r)\operatorname{d}r
and
|\zeta|\leq 1\cdot\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{2})^{-1/2}|z|
, whence (
\star
).

Now assume at once
t\neq 0
and let in the following
|v|>\sqrt{2}\mu
. Put
s(u,v):=\sqrt{\mu^{2}+(u+\operatorname{i}v)^{2}}
. More precisely,

s(u,v):=(u+\operatorname{i}v)\sqrt{1+z}
for
z:=\mu^{2}(u+\operatorname{i}v)^{-2}
with
|z|=\mu^{2}(u^{2}+v^{2})^{-1}\leq\mu^{2}v^{-2}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

By (
\star
),

s(u,v)=(u+\operatorname{i}v)(1+\zeta)
with
|u+\operatorname{i}v|\,|\zeta|\leq\frac{3}{4}|u+\operatorname{i}v|\,|z|\leq\mu^{2}|v|^{-1}\leq\mu
. This implies
|\operatorname{e}^{\pm\operatorname{i}ts(u,v)}|=|\operatorname{e}^{\pm\operatorname{i}t(u+\operatorname{i}v)(1+\zeta)}|=\operatorname{e}^{\mp tv}|\operatorname{e}^{\pm\operatorname{i}t(u+\operatorname{i}v)\zeta}|
. One concludes

\operatorname{e}^{-|t|\mu}\operatorname{e}^{-tv}\leq|\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}ts(u,v)}|\leq\operatorname{e}^{|t|\mu}\operatorname{e}^{-tv},\quad\operatorname{e}^{-|t|\mu}\operatorname{e}^{tv}\leq|\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}ts(u,v)}|\leq\operatorname{e}^{|t|\mu}\operatorname{e}^{tv}

(
\star\star
)

for all
v\in\mathbb{R}
with
|v|>\sqrt{2}\mu
.

(a)
|\cos(w)|=\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}w}+\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}w}|\leq\frac{1}{2}(|\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}w}|+|\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}w}|)
. For
w=ts(u,v)
this yields by (
\star\star
)
|\cos(ts(u,v))|\leq\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{e}^{|t|\mu}\operatorname{e}^{-tv}+\operatorname{e}^{|t|\mu}\operatorname{e}^{tv})\leq\operatorname{e}^{|t|\mu}\operatorname{e}^{|tv|}
. Hence the right part of the inequality of (a) holds for
B_{t}:=\operatorname{e}^{|t|\mu}
and
C^{\prime}_{t}=\sqrt{2}\mu
.

For the left part of the inequality use
|\cos(w)|=\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}w}+\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}w}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\big||\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}w}|-|\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}w}|\big|
. Then for
w=ts(u,v)
one gets by (
\star\star
)
|\cos(ts(u,v))|\geq\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{e}^{-|t|\mu}\operatorname{e}^{|tv|}-\operatorname{e}^{|t|\mu}\operatorname{e}^{-|tv|})=\sinh\big(|t|(|v|-\mu)\big)
. Check
\sinh(x)\geq\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{e}^{x}
for
x\geq\frac{\ln(2)}{2}
. Thus we conclude that the left part of the inequality holds for
A_{t}:=\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{e}^{-|t|\mu}
and
C^{\prime\prime}_{t}:=\sqrt{2}\mu+\frac{\ln(2)}{2}\frac{1}{|t|}
.

(b) Check first
|t\,s(u,v)|\geq\frac{|t|}{\sqrt{2}}|u+\operatorname{i}v|
, using
|\sqrt{1+z}|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
for
|z|\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Furthermore,
|\sin(w)|=\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}w}-\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}w}|\leq\frac{1}{2}(|\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}w}|+|\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}w}|)
. Hence, as for (a), the right part of the inequality holds for
B_{t}:=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{|t|}\operatorname{e}^{|t|\mu}
and
C^{\prime}_{t}=\sqrt{2}\mu
.

Regarding the left part of the inequality of (b), we estimate
|ts(u,v)|^{-1}\geq(\frac{2}{3})^{1/2}|t(u+\operatorname{i}v)|^{-1}
, as
|s(u,v)|=|u+\operatorname{i}v|\,|\sqrt{1+z}\,|\leq(\frac{3}{2})^{1/2}|u+\operatorname{i}v|
. Furthermore, one has
|\sin(w)|=\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}w}-\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}w}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\big||\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}w}|-|\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}w}|\big|
. Hence, proceeding as in (a), it follows that the left part of the inequality holds for
A_{t}:=(\frac{1}{24})^{1/2}\frac{1}{|t|}\operatorname{e}^{-|t|\mu}
and
C^{\prime\prime}_{t}:=\sqrt{2}\mu+\frac{\ln(2)}{2}\frac{1}{|t|}
.
∎

We use now (
15.1
) to compute the P-indicator for
\cos(t\epsilon)
and
\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)
.

(57) Lemma.
For
t\in\mathbb{R}
the functions
z\mapsto\cos\big(t\epsilon(z)\big)
and
z\mapsto\operatorname{sinc}\big(t\epsilon(z)\big)
are bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and entire on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
with exponent
|t|
, which is smallest. Moreover,
h_{\cos(t\epsilon)}(\lambda)=h_{\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)}(\lambda)=|t|\,|\lambda|
holds for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. More precisely,
|t|\,|\lambda|=\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{1}{r}\ln|f(p+\operatorname{i}\lambda r)|
,
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
f\in\{\cos(t\epsilon),\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)\}
. These statements are equally valid if
\epsilon(z)^{2}
is replaced with
z^{2}
, i.e., if the mass
m=0
.

Proof.

In the following the case
\epsilon(z)^{2}=z^{2}
, i.e.
m=0
, is included.
We show the assertion for
\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)
. Regarding
\cos(t\epsilon)
the proof is analogous. Assume at once
t\neq 0
.

Obviously,
\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)
is bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and entire on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Also, there is an entire function
s
satisfying
s(z^{2})=\operatorname{sinc}\big(t\epsilon(z)\big)
with
z^{2}=z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}+z_{3}^{2}
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
. —
Now
|\epsilon(z)|^{2}=|z^{2}+m^{2}|\leq|z^{2}|+m^{2}=|z|^{2}+m^{2}\leq(|z|+m)^{2}
, whence
|\epsilon(z)|\leq|z|+m
. Therefore,
|\sin\big(t\epsilon(z)\big)|=\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\epsilon(z)}-\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}t\epsilon(z)}|\leq\operatorname{e}^{|t|\,|\epsilon(z)|}\leq\operatorname{e}^{|t|m}\operatorname{e}^{|t|\,|z|}
for all
z
.

If
|z^{2}|\leq 2m^{2}+1
then
|\operatorname{sinc}\big(t\epsilon(z)\big)|=|s(z^{2})|\leq C
for some finite constant
C
. For
|z^{2}|>2m^{2}+1
one has
|\epsilon(z)|^{2}=|z^{2}+m^{2}|\geq|z^{2}|-m^{2}>m^{2}+1
, whence
|\epsilon(z)|^{-1}<1
.
Hence
|\operatorname{sinc}\big(t\epsilon(z)\big)|\leq C^{\prime}\operatorname{e}^{|t|\,|z|}
for all
z
, where
C^{\prime}:=C+\frac{\operatorname{e}^{|t|m}}{|t|}
. So
|t|
is an exponent for
\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)
.

In order to show that
|t|
is smallest assume that
0\leq\delta<|t|
is an exponent for

\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)
. Let
\delta<\delta^{\prime}<|t|
. Then obviously
|\sin\big(t\epsilon(z)\big)|\leq C\operatorname{e}^{\delta^{\prime}|z|}
,
z\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
for some finite constant
C
. Let
w\in\mathbb{C}
. Choose
\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
with
\zeta^{2}=w^{2}-m^{2}
. Then
w\in\{\pm\epsilon(0,0,\zeta)\}
and
|\zeta|\leq|w|+m
. Hence
|\sin(tw)|\leq C\operatorname{e}^{\delta^{\prime}|\zeta|}\leq C^{\prime}\operatorname{e}^{\delta^{\prime}|w|}
with
C^{\prime}:=C\operatorname{e}^{\delta^{\prime}m}
.
Therefore also
|\cos(tw)|=|\sin(tw+\frac{\pi}{2})|\leq C^{\prime}\operatorname{e}^{\frac{\pi\delta^{\prime}}{2|t|}}\operatorname{e}^{\delta^{\prime}|w|}
, whence finally

|\operatorname{e}^{tw}|\leq C^{\prime\prime}\operatorname{e}^{\delta^{\prime}|w|}
,
w\in\mathbb{C}
for some finite constant
C^{\prime\prime}
. This implies the contradiction
\operatorname{e}^{(|t|-\delta^{\prime})r}\leq C^{\prime\prime}
for all
r>0
.

We turn to the P-indicator of
\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)
. Assume at once
\lambda\neq 0
. Then
\epsilon(p+\operatorname{i}\lambda r)=\big(\mu^{2}+(\frac{p\lambda}{|\lambda|}+\operatorname{i}|\lambda|r)^{2}\big)^{1/2}
with
\mu^{2}:=m^{2}+p^{2}-\big(\frac{p\lambda}{|\lambda|}\big)^{2}\geq 0
independent of
r
. Hence by
(
15.1
) there are finite constants
A_{t}>0
,
B_{t}
independent of
r
that such
A_{t}\big|\frac{p\lambda}{|\lambda|}+\operatorname{i}|\lambda|\,r\big|^{-1}\operatorname{e}^{|t||\lambda|r}\leq\operatorname{sinc}\big(t\epsilon(p+\operatorname{i}\lambda r)\big)\leq B_{t}\big|\frac{p\lambda}{|\lambda|}+\operatorname{i}|\lambda|\,r\big|^{-1}\operatorname{e}^{|t||\lambda|r}
, whence the assertion.∎

15.2.
Motion of the border of a bounded localized wave function

The location of the carrier of a wavefunction is described by the positions of the supporting planes in every direction of the space. We are going to study the movements of these supporting planes.

(58) Definition.
The unit vector
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\alpha\in[-\infty,\infty]
determine the half-space

\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x\,e\leq\alpha\}
(which equals
\emptyset
or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
if
\alpha\in\{-\infty,\infty\}
). For every
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})\setminus\{0\}
let
e(\psi)\in[-\infty,\infty]
denote the largest
\alpha
satisfying
E^{\textsc{d}}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x\,e\leq\alpha\})\psi=0
. Similarly for
\eta\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C})

let
e(\eta)
be the largest
\alpha
with
1_{\{xe\leq\alpha\}}\eta=0
up to a null set.

The meaning of
e(\psi)
is best elucidated by
the following result. Recall
E^{\textsc{d}}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x\operatorname{e}=\alpha\})=0
. Let

\overline{e}:=-e

(59) Lemma.

\psi
is localized in

\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:e(\psi)\leq xe\leq-\overline{e}(\psi)\}

with
]e(\psi),-\overline{e}(\psi)[
the smallest interval with this property.

Proof.
By definition
E^{\textsc{d}}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x\,e\leq\alpha\})\psi=0
,
E^{\textsc{d}}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x\,\overline{e}\leq\beta\})\psi=0
exactly for all
\alpha\leq e(\psi)
and
\beta\leq\overline{e}(\psi)
. From this it follows
E^{\textsc{d}}\big(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:e(\psi)\leq xe\leq-\overline{e}(\psi)\}\big)\psi=\psi
, whence the assertion.∎

3.5,2.5)

carrier

Clearly,
e(\psi)=\min_{l}e(\psi_{l})
. — Let
C(\psi)
denote the smallest convex set outside of which
\psi
vanishes almost everywhere. Clearly,
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x\,e\leq e(\psi)\}\cap C(\psi)=\emptyset
and
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x\,e\leq\alpha\}\cap C(\psi)
is not a null set if
\alpha>e(\psi)
. Hence
e(\psi)=\inf\{xe:x\in C(\psi)\}
. These considerations are applicable as well to every component
\psi_{l}
of
\psi
. Therefore (see Eq. (
15.2
))

e(\psi)=-H_{C(\psi)}(-e),\quad e(\psi_{l})=-H_{C(\psi_{l})}(-e)

(15.6)

(60) Lemma.
Let
\psi
be a Dirac state.
Then
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
t\mapsto e(\psi_{t})
is continuous.

Proof.
Let
t,t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. By causality
e(\psi_{t})\geq e(\psi_{t_{0}})-|t-t_{0}|
. This implies
\varliminf_{t\to t_{0}}e(\psi_{t})\geq e(\psi_{t_{0}})
. Furthermore, for
P_{t}:=E^{\textsc{d}}(\{x:x\,e\leq e(\psi_{t})\})
one has
0=P_{t}\psi_{t}=P_{t}\psi_{t_{0}}+P_{t}(\psi_{t}-\psi_{t_{0}})
, whence
\lim_{t\to t_{0}}P_{t}\psi_{t_{0}}=0
as
\psi_{t}\to\psi_{t_{0}}
. This implies
\varlimsup_{t\to t_{0}}e(\psi_{t})\leq e(\psi_{t_{0}})
. Thus continuity of
t\to e(\psi_{t})
at
t_{0}
holds.∎

(61) Lemma.
Let
\psi
be a Dirac state localized in a bounded region. Then

\min\{e(\psi_{t}),e(\psi_{-t})\}=e(\psi)-|t|

holds for every direction
e
and all times
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

By causality
e(\psi_{t})\geq e(\psi)-|t|
for all
t
, whence
\min\{e(\psi_{t}),e(\psi_{-t})\}\geq e(\psi)-|t|
.

We prove now the reverse inequality. Recall
\phi=2\cos(t\epsilon)\,\varphi
for
\phi:=\varphi_{t}+\varphi_{-t}
from Eq. (
15.4
). Let
\theta:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\phi|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
. Theorem (
15.1
) applies to the components of
\varphi
and, due to (
15.1
), also to those of
\cos(t\epsilon)\,\varphi
.
Hence, using Eq. (
15.6
) and by (
15.1
),
e(\theta_{l})=-H_{C(\theta_{l})}(-e)=-h_{\cos(t\epsilon)\,\varphi_{l}}(-e)=-h_{\cos(t\epsilon)}(-e)-h_{\varphi_{l}}(-e)=-|t|-H_{C(\psi_{l})}(-e)=-|t|+e(\psi_{l})
. Therefore
e(\theta)=\min_{l}e(\theta_{l})=-|t|+\min_{l}e(\psi_{l})=-|t|+e(\psi)
.

It remains to show
\alpha:=\min\{e(\psi_{t}),e(\psi_{-t})\}\leq e(\psi_{t}+\psi_{-t})
. Put
E_{\alpha}:=E^{\textsc{d}}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x\operatorname{e}\leq\alpha\})
. Then
E_{\alpha}\psi_{t}=0
and
E_{\alpha}\psi_{-t}=0
. Hence
E_{\alpha}(\psi_{t}+\psi_{-t})=0
, whence the claim. ∎

(62) Theorem.
Let
\psi
be a Dirac state localized in a bounded region. Then

e(\psi_{t})\leq-2\,\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)-|t|

holds for all directions
e
and all times
t
. Recall
(
13.2
)
. If
\psi\in\operatorname{dom}(H^{pos})
or if more generally

h\,\mathcal{F}\psi
is bounded
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
then the inequality holds even with
<
in place of
\leq
.

Proof.
We start from Eq. (
15.5
)
\phi=\operatorname{i}t\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)\,\chi
. Put here
\phi_{kl}:=(\varphi_{t})_{k}\varphi_{l}
,
\chi_{kl}:=(h\varphi)_{k}\varphi_{l}
, whence
\phi=\phi_{kl}-\phi_{lk}
and
\chi=\chi_{kl}-\chi_{lk}
.

As
\varphi_{l}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\in L^{2}
and
(\varphi_{t})_{k}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
is bounded,
\phi_{kl}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\in L^{2}
so that
(
15.1
) applies to
\phi_{kl}
. Let
\theta:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\phi|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
,
\theta_{kl}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\phi_{kl}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
. Obviously,
e(\theta)\geq\min\{e(\theta_{kl}),e(\theta_{lk})\}
.
Using Eq. (
15.6
) one gets
e(\theta_{kl})=-H_{C(\theta_{kl})}(-e)=-h_{\phi_{kl}}(-e)=-h_{(\varphi_{t})_{k}}(-e)-h_{\varphi_{l}}(-e)=-H_{C((\psi_{t})_{k})}(-e)-H_{C(\psi_{l})}(-e)=e((\psi_{t})_{k})+e(\psi_{l})\geq e(\psi_{t})+e(\psi)
. It follows
e(\theta)\geq e(\psi_{t})+e(\psi)
.

We turn to the right hand side
\operatorname{i}t\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)\,\chi
of Eq. (
15.5
). Recall
\operatorname{i}t\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)\,\chi_{kl}=\phi_{kl}-\cos(t\epsilon)\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}
by Eq. (
15.3
). Note that
\cos(t\epsilon)\varphi_{k}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
is bounded. Hence
\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)\,\chi_{kl}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\in L^{2}
. However,
\chi_{kl}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
need not be square-integrable. Therefore we consider instead
\chi^{\prime}_{kl}:=s_{\delta}\chi_{kl}
with
s_{\delta}:=\operatorname{sinc}(\delta\epsilon)
for
\delta>0
. Then
\phi^{\prime}=\operatorname{i}t\operatorname{sinc}(t\epsilon)\,\chi^{\prime}
for
\phi^{\prime}:=s_{\delta}\phi
holds. As
h_{s_{\delta}}(e)=\delta
by (
15.1
), the analogous computation for

\theta^{\prime}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\phi^{\prime}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
in place of
\theta
yields
e(\theta^{\prime})\geq-\delta+e(\psi_{t})+e(\psi)
.
Moreover, (
15.1
) applies to
\chi^{\prime}
. Let
\xi^{\prime}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\chi^{\prime}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
. Then again, in the same way
e(\theta^{\prime})=-|t|+e(\xi^{\prime})
follows.

Next we examine
-\overline{e}(\xi^{\prime})
. Obviously
-\overline{e}(\xi^{\prime})\leq\max\{-\overline{e}(\xi^{\prime}_{kl}),-\overline{e}(\xi^{\prime}_{lk})\}
. By Eq. (
15.6
) and (
15.1
) one has
-\overline{e}(\xi^{\prime}_{kl})=H_{C(\xi^{\prime}_{kl})}(e)=h_{\chi^{\prime}_{kl}}(e)=h_{s_{\delta}(h\varphi)_{k}}(e)+h_{\varphi_{l}}(e)
as
s_{\delta}(h\varphi)_{k}
is exponentially bounded. Note
|(h\varphi)_{k}(z)|\leq q(z)\max_{m}|\varphi_{m}(z)|
with

q(z)^{2}:=4\sum_{m=1}^{4}|h(z)_{km}|^{2}
, where
h(z)_{km}
is linear. Therefore
h_{s_{\delta}(h\varphi)_{k}}(e,x)=\varlimsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{1}{r}\big\{\ln|s_{\delta}(x+\operatorname{i}e\,r))|+\ln|(h\varphi)_{k}(x+\operatorname{i}e\,r)|\big\}=\delta+\varlimsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{1}{r}\ln|(h\varphi)_{k}(x+\operatorname{i}e\,r)|\}
(by (
15.1
))
\leq\delta+\varlimsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{1}{r}\big\{\ln|q(x+\operatorname{i}e\,r)|+\ln(\max_{m}|\varphi_{m}(x+\operatorname{i}e\,r)|)\big\}=\delta+0+\max_{m}\varlimsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{1}{r}\ln|\varphi_{m}(x+\operatorname{i}e\,r)|=\delta+\max_{m}h_{\varphi_{m}}(e,x)
. Furthermore,
\max_{m}h_{\varphi_{m}}(e)=\max_{m}H_{C(\psi_{m})}(e)=\max_{m}\{-\overline{e}(\psi_{m})\}=-\overline{e}(\psi)
. Also
h_{\varphi_{l}}(e)\leq-\overline{e}(\psi)
. It follows
-\overline{e}(\xi^{\prime})\leq\delta-2\,\overline{e}(\psi)
.

Now, using
e(\xi^{\prime})<-\overline{e}(\xi^{\prime})
, one has the chain of inequalities
-\delta+e(\psi_{t})+e(\psi)\leq e(\theta^{\prime})=-|t|+e(\xi^{\prime})<-|t|-\overline{e}(\xi^{\prime})\leq-|t|+\delta-2\,\overline{e}(\psi)
for
\delta>0
. The limit
\delta\to 0
yields the final result
e(\psi_{t})+e(\psi)\leq-|t|+-2\,\overline{e}(\psi)
. It remains to note that if
h\varphi
is bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
one has
\chi_{kl}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\in L^{2}
so that
e(\xi)<-\overline{e}(\xi)
, and the chain holds even for
\delta=0
.
∎

In other words, suppose that, after or before time
|t|
,
\psi_{t}
is localized in the half-space
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:x\operatorname{e}\geq\alpha\}
. Then, by (
15.2
),
|t|\leq-\alpha-2\,\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)
. — If
\psi
is localized in
B_{R}
, then obviously
e(\psi)\geq-R
for every direction
e
. So, if
\psi_{t}
is localized in
B_{r}
, then by (
15.2
),
|t|\leq r+3R
.

(63) Theorem.
Let
\psi
be a Dirac state localized in a bounded region. Then there exists a unique time
t_{e}=t_{e}(\psi)\in\mathbb{R}
such that

e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi)+|t_{e}|-|t-t_{e}|

for all times
t\in\mathbb{R}
and directions
e
.

Proof.

Since
t\to e(\psi_{t})
is continuous by (
15.2
) and bounded above by (
15.2
) there is
t_{e}\in\mathbb{R}
with
e(\psi_{t_{e}})=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}e(\psi_{t})
. Fix
t>0
.

Now we apply (
15.2
)
to
\psi^{\prime}:=\psi_{t_{e}-t/2}
. Then
\min\{e(\psi^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}),e(\psi^{\prime}_{-t^{\prime}})\}=e(\psi^{\prime})-|t^{\prime}|
for all
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. As
e(\psi^{\prime}_{t/2})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})\geq e(\psi_{t_{e}-t})=e(\psi^{\prime}_{-t/2})
it follows
e(\psi_{t_{e}-t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}-t/2})-t/2
. For
t/2
in place of
t
this reads
e(\psi_{t_{e}-t/2})=e(\psi_{t_{e}-t/4})-t/4
. Hence
e(\psi_{t_{e}-t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}-t/4})-t/2-t/4
. From this one obtains in the same way
e(\psi_{t_{e}-t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}-t/8})-t/2-t/4-t/8
and finally
e(\psi_{t_{e}-t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}-t/2^{n}})-\sum_{k=1}^{n}t/2^{k}
after
n
steps. Then by continuity (
15.2
) the limit
n\to\infty
yields
e(\psi_{t_{e}-t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})-t
. —
Analogously, applying (
15.2
) to
\psi^{\prime}:=\psi_{t_{e}+t/2}
, one obtains
e(\psi_{t_{e}+t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})-t
.

Thus
e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})-|t-t_{e}|
holds for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular
e(\psi)=e(\psi_{t_{e}})-|t_{e}|
, whence the formula.
Uniqueness of
t_{e}
is obvious as
t\to e(\psi_{t})
has just one maximum at
t=t_{e}
.∎

(64) Corollary.
Let
\psi
be a bounded localized Dirac state. Then for every direction
e

(a)

t\to e(\psi_{t})
assumes the maximum, which is the only extremum, at
t=t_{e}

(b)

the width
-\overline{e}(\psi_{t})-e(\psi_{t})
of the carrier in direction
e
assumes its minimum
-\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})-e(\psi_{t_{e}})+|t_{\overline{e}}-t_{e}|
at the times
t
between
t_{e}
and
t_{\overline{e}}

(c)

t_{e}(\psi_{t})=t_{e}(\psi)-t
and
e\big((\psi_{t})_{t_{e}(\psi_{t})}\big)=e(\psi_{t_{e}(\psi)})
for
t\in\mathbb{R}

(d)

e(\psi_{t_{e}})\leq-\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})

Proof.
(a) and (b) follow easily from (
15.2
).

(c) Let
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,
\psi^{\prime}:=\psi_{\tau}
, and
t^{\prime}_{e}:=t_{e}(\psi^{\prime})
. Apply (
15.2
). Then
e(\psi^{\prime}_{t})=e(\psi^{\prime})+|t^{\prime}_{e}|-|t-t^{\prime}_{e}|
and
e(\psi^{\prime})=e(\psi)+|t_{e}|-|\tau-t_{e}|
. As
\psi^{\prime}_{t}=\psi_{t+\tau}
also
e(\psi^{\prime}_{t})=e(\psi)+|t_{e}|-|t+\tau-t_{e}|
holds. Therefore
|t+\tau-t_{e}|-|t-t^{\prime}_{e}|=|\tau-t_{e}|-|t^{\prime}_{e}|
for all
t
, whence
t^{\prime}_{e}=t_{e}-\tau
. Using this result,
e(\psi^{\prime}_{t})=e(\psi^{\prime})+|t^{\prime}_{e}|-|t-t^{\prime}_{e}|=e(\psi)+|t_{e}|-|t-t^{\prime}_{e}|=e(\psi_{t_{e}})-|t-t^{\prime}_{e}|
, whence the claim putting
t=t^{\prime}_{e}
.

(d) Apply (
15.2
) to
\eta:=\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}}
. For
t=t_{e}(\eta)
one obtains
e(\eta_{t_{e}(\eta)})\leq-2\overline{e}(\eta)-e(\eta)-|t_{e(\eta)}|
.
Since

e(\eta_{t_{e}(\eta)})=e(\eta)+|t_{e(\eta)}|
by (
15.2
), one has
e(\eta_{t_{e}(\eta)})\leq-\overline{e}(\eta)
. By
(c),
e(\eta_{t_{e}(\eta)})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})
. The result follows.∎

The result (
15.2
)(d) will be improved by (
15.2
)(b).

(65) Corollary.
Let
\psi
be a bounded localized Dirac state. Then for every direction
e

(a)

e(\psi_{t})\leq-\overline{e}(\psi)-|t-t_{e}|

(b)

|t_{e}|+|t_{\overline{e}}|\leq-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)

(c)

2|t_{e}|<-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)
if
t_{e}=t_{\overline{e}}

(d)

If
h\,\mathcal{F}\psi
is bounded
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
\psi
in position representation, then the inequalities in
(a), (b)
hold even with
<
in place of
\leq
.

Proof.
(a) By (
15.2
) and (
15.2
) one has
e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi)+|t_{e}|-|t-t_{e}|\leq-2\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)-|t|
. For
t=t_{e}
this yields
|t_{e}|\leq-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)
and consequently
e(\psi_{t})\leq-\overline{e}(\psi)-|t-t_{e}|
.

(b) Let
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
and consider
e(\psi_{t+s})
. One the one hand, by (
15.2
),
e(\psi_{t+s})=e(\psi)+|t_{e}|-|t+s-t_{e}|
. On the other hand, first using (
15.2
) and then applying (
15.2
), one has
e(\psi_{t+s})\leq-2\overline{e}(\psi_{t})-e(\psi_{t})-|s|=-2\big(\overline{e}(\psi)+|t_{\overline{e}}|-|t-t_{\overline{e}}|\big)-e(\psi)-|t_{e}|+|t-t_{e}|-|s|
. Hence

-2\big(\overline{e}(\psi)+e(\psi)\big)\geq 2|t_{e}|+2|t_{\overline{e}}|-2|t-t_{\overline{e}}|-|t-t_{e}|+|s|-|t+s-t_{e}|
. For
s=t_{e}-t
this yields
-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)\geq|t_{e}|+|t_{\overline{e}}|-|t-t_{\overline{e}}|
. Then
|t_{e}|+|t_{\overline{e}}|\leq-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)
follows for
t=t_{e}
.

(c) By (
15.2
),
0<-\overline{e}(\psi_{t})-e(\psi_{t})
for all
t
. Hence (
15.2
) yields
|t_{\overline{e}}|+|t_{e}|-|t-t_{\overline{e}}|-|t-t_{e}|<-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)
.
This implies (c).

(d) follows from the last part of (
15.2
).
∎

The result (
15.2
)(b) will be improved by (
15.2
)(a).

Rather obvious are the transformations of
e(\psi)
and
t_{e}(\psi)
when
\psi
is time-shifted (
15.2
)(c) ore space-shifted (
15.7
). They are less obvious if
\psi
is boosted. Recall
A_{\rho e}
in (
7.2
)(c) representing the boost in direction
e
with rapidity
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
.

(66) Lemma.
Let
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
and
e
be any direction. Put
\psi^{\prime}:=W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})\psi
. One has

(a)

e(\psi^{\prime})=-\sinh(\rho)\,t_{e}+\cosh(\rho)\,e(\psi_{t_{e}})-\big|\cosh(\rho)\,t_{e}-\sinh(\rho)\,e(\psi_{t_{e}})\big|

(b)

t_{e}(\psi^{\prime})=\cosh(\rho)\,t_{e}-\sinh(\rho)\,e(\psi_{t_{e}})

(c)

e(\psi^{\prime}_{t_{e}(\psi^{\prime})})=-\sinh(\rho)\,t_{e}+\cosh(\rho)\,e(\psi_{t_{e}})

Proof.

Assume without restriction
e=(0,0,1)
, whence
A_{\rho e}=A_{\rho}
, and use the position representation. By (
14
) one has
\psi^{\prime}_{t}(x)=s(A_{\rho})\big(\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}y_{0}H^{pos}}\psi\big)(y)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
with
y_{0}:=-\cosh(\rho)\,t-\sinh(\rho)\,x_{3}
,
y_{1}:=x_{1}
,
y_{2}:=x_{2}
,
y_{3}:=\sinh(\rho)\,t+\cosh(\rho)\,x_{3}
. Now,
e(\psi_{-y_{0}})
is the largest
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
such that
1_{\{x^{\prime}_{3}\leq\alpha\}}\psi_{-y_{0}}(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{3})=0
a.e., whence
\psi^{\prime}_{t}(x)=0
for
y_{3}\leq\alpha
. Use
e(\psi_{-y_{0}})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})-|-y_{0}-t_{e}(\psi)|
. Then
y_{3}\leq\alpha
means

x_{3}\leq e(\psi_{t_{e}})/\cosh(\rho)-\tanh(\rho)\,t-|\tanh(\rho)x_{3}-t_{e}/\cosh(\rho)+t|

Elementary computation shows that the set
J:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x\leq a-|\lambda x-b|\}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda\in]-1,1[
equals
J=]-\infty,\beta]
for
\beta:=\frac{1}{1-\lambda^{2}}(a-\lambda b-|b-\lambda a|)
. Thus, in the specific case
\beta
equals
e(\psi^{\prime}_{t})
and

e(\psi^{\prime}_{t})=-\sinh(\rho)\,t_{e}+\cosh(\rho)\,e(\psi_{t_{e}})-|t-\cosh(\rho)\,t_{e}+\sinh(\rho)\,e(\psi_{t_{e}})|

for all
t
. This implies (b) and (c), and hence (a).∎

As an application of (
15.2
) we improve the results in (
15.2
)(b) and (
15.2
)(d).

(67) Corollary.
Let
\psi
be a bounded localized Dirac state. Then

(a)

|t_{\overline{e}}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\big(-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)\big)
,
|t_{e}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\big(-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)\big)

(b)

|t_{\overline{e}}-t_{e}|\leq-\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})-e(\psi_{t_{e}})

for every direction
e
.

Proof.
(a) Obviously it suffices to prove the first relation. Perform the translation (
15.7
) by
b:=-e(\psi)\,e
, whence without restriction
e(\psi)=0
and hence
-\overline{e}(\psi)>0
. Now assume the contrary
|t_{\overline{e}}|>-\frac{1}{2}\overline{e}(\psi)
. Consider the boosted state
\psi^{\prime}:=W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})\psi
. (
15.2
)(a) shows
e(\psi^{\prime})=0
since
e(\psi_{t_{e}})=|t_{e}|
, and
by (
15.2
)(b) one has
t_{\overline{e}}(\psi^{\prime})=\cosh(\rho)\,t_{\overline{e}}+\sinh(\rho)\,\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})=\cosh(\rho)\,t_{\overline{e}}+\sinh(\rho)\,\overline{e}(\psi)+\sinh(\rho)\,|t_{\overline{e}}|
.

Treat first the case
t_{\overline{e}}\geq 0
. Then
t_{\overline{e}}(\psi^{\prime})=\operatorname{e}^{\rho}\big(t_{\overline{e}}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{e}(\psi)\big)-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\overline{e}(\psi)>0
. Therefore, by (
15.2
)(a),
\overline{e}(\psi^{\prime})=\sinh(\rho)\,t_{\overline{e}}+\cosh(\rho)\,\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})-t_{\overline{e}}(\psi^{\prime})=-\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\,t_{\overline{e}}+\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\,\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})=\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\,\overline{e}(\psi)
. Since
e(\psi^{\prime})=0
and
t_{\overline{e}}(\psi^{\prime})\to\infty
,
\overline{e}(\psi^{\prime})\to 0
for
\rho\to\infty
, this yields a contradiction to (
15.2
)(b). — Now let
t_{\overline{e}}\leq 0
. Similarly, one gets
t_{\overline{e}}(\psi^{\prime})=\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\big(t_{\overline{e}}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{e}(\psi)\big)-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{e}^{\rho}\overline{e}(\psi)
and
\overline{e}(\psi^{\prime})=\operatorname{e}^{\rho}\,\overline{e}(\psi)
. Then
e(\psi^{\prime})=0
,
t_{\overline{e}}(\psi^{\prime})\to-\infty
,
\overline{e}(\psi^{\prime})\to 0
for
\rho\to-\infty
contradicts again (
15.2
)(b).

(b) Consider the first relation in (a) for
\psi_{t_{e}}
in place of
\psi
. By (
15.2
)(c),(b) it reads
2|t_{\overline{e}}-t_{e}|\leq-\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})-e(\psi_{t_{e}})+|t_{\overline{e}}-t_{e}|
, whence the claim.
∎

By (
15.2
)(a), (
15.2
)(b)

\emptyset\neq\;]e(\psi_{t_{e}}),-\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})[\;\subset[e(\psi_{t}),-\overline{e}(\psi_{t^{\prime}})]\quad\forall\,t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}

This means that in direction
e
the carrier of
\psi_{t}
shrinks and expands around the nonempty time fixed strip
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:e(\psi_{t_{e}})<xe<-\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})\}
.

(68) Corollary.
Let
\psi
be a Dirac state localized in
B_{R}
for some
R>0
. Then one has

e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi_{R})+R-t\quad\forall\;t\geq R,\quad e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi_{-R})+R+t\quad\forall\;t\leq-R

independently of the direction
e
.

Proof.
From (
15.2
) and (
15.2
)(a) it follows for
t\geq R
that
e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})+t_{e}-t
and in particular
e(\psi_{R})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})+t_{e}-R
, whence
e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi_{R})+R-t
. Similarly, for
t\leq-R
one has
e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})-t_{e}+t
and in particular
e(\psi_{-R})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})-t_{e}-R
, whence
e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi_{-R})+R+t
.∎

(69) Discussion.
The results achieved so far provide the following picture. As long as
t<t_{e}
,

e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})-t_{e}+t
holds by (
15.2
), which means that the carrier of
\psi_{t}
retreats in direction
e
at the speed of light. Only after time
t_{e}
the carrier advances in direction
-e
at the speed of light as
e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi_{t_{e}})+t_{e}-t
. So only then the wave function expands in the direction
-e
as expected. The abrupt change at the time
t_{e}
of the direction of the motion with light velocity to the opposite direction is like a bounce. It reminds of the phenomenon of the zitterbewegung. But this behavior is easy to understand. Let
\psi^{\prime}:=\psi_{t_{e}}
. Then due to homogeneity of time, i.e., the translational symmetry of time evolution,
\psi^{\prime}
satisfies
e(\psi^{\prime}_{t})=e(\psi^{\prime})-|t|
by (
15.2
) and (
15.2
)(c). So, as maximal permissible by causality,
\psi^{\prime}
expands in the future as well in the past in direction
-e
at the speed of light. In particular the result in (
15.2
) does not single out some direction of time. On the contrary, the reversal of motion is required by time reversal symmetry. Nevertheless for a short period the picture is complicated as the time of change
t_{e}
depends in general on the direction
e
(see the example (
15.3
)). Therefore the carrier of the wave function performs the change from shrinking to expanding not isotropicly. According to (
15.2
)(a), in every direction
e
the retreat equals at most the width of the carrier.
Furthermore, the times
t_{e}
and
t_{\overline{e}}
, which pass until the motion changes, are limited
by (
15.2
)(a).
After, respectively before, the time corresponding to the diameter of the carrier a simultaneous isotropic expansion of the wave function with light velocity takes place in the future respectively in the past (
15.2
). So the phase of rebound is limited in time and space in the order of the diameter of the carrier at its minimum extension.

15.3.
Late-change states

For the following construction (
15.3
) we use the easily verifiable formulae

e\big(W^{\textsc{d}}(b)\psi\big)=e(\psi)+eb,\quad t_{e}\big(W^{\textsc{d}}(b)\psi\big)=t_{e}(\psi)

(15.7)

for all directions
e
and translations
b\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. As to the first use translational covariance of
E^{\textsc{d}}
and for the latter recall that time and space translations commute.

(70) Construction
of a Dirac state
\psi
with given values of
t_{e}
and
t_{\overline{e}}
. Let
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|=1
and
\overline{e}=-e
, and let
\tau\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
\delta>0
.
Let
\psi^{(1)}
be any Dirac state localized in a bounded region.
Set
\psi^{(2)}:=W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(\delta e)\psi^{(1)}_{\tau}
. Finally, put

\psi:=\psi^{(1)}+\psi^{(2)}

It is not necessary to normalize
\psi
here. In the following we express the characteristic dates
e(\psi),t_{e}
,
\overline{e}(\psi),t_{\overline{e}}
referring to
\psi
by the input dates

e(\psi^{(1)}),t^{(1)}_{e}
,
\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)}),t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}
and the parameters
\tau,\delta
.

By (
15.2
), (
15.2
)(c) and Eq. (
15.7
) one has

t^{(2)}_{e}=t^{(1)}_{e}-\tau
and
t^{(2)}_{\overline{e}}=t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}-\tau
,
and
e(\psi^{(1)}_{t})=e(\psi^{(1)})+|t^{(1)}_{e}|-|t-t^{(1)}_{e}|
,
e(\psi^{(2)}_{t})=e(\psi^{(1)}_{t+\tau})+\delta=e(\psi^{(1)})+|t^{(1)}_{e}|-|t+\tau-t^{(1)}_{e}|+\delta
and similarly
\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)}_{t})=\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)})+|t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|-|t-t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|
,
\overline{e}(\psi^{(2)}_{t})=\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)})+|t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|-|t+\tau-t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|-\delta
.

Now, obviously
e(\psi_{t})=\min\{e(\psi^{(1)}_{t}),e(\psi^{(2)}_{t})\}
and
\overline{e}(\psi_{t})=\min\{\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)}_{t}),\overline{e}(\psi^{(2)}_{t})\}
. Hence
t_{e}
and
t_{\overline{e}}
are determined by (
15.2
). Write
e(\psi^{(2)}_{t})-e(\psi^{(1)}_{t})=d(t-t_{e}^{(1)})
with
d(x):=|x|-|x+\tau|+\delta
and
\overline{e}(\psi^{(2)}_{t})-\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)}_{t})=\overline{d}(t-t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}})
with
\overline{d}(x):=|x|-|x+\tau|-\delta
.

We will distinguish the cases (i)
|\tau|\leq\delta
, (ii)
\tau>\delta
, and (iii)
-\tau>\delta
. Note first

|\tau|\leq\delta\,\Leftrightarrow\,d(t-t_{e}^{(1)})\geq 0\;\forall t\,\Leftrightarrow\,\overline{d}(t-t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}})\leq 0\;\forall t

(
\star
)

Indeed,
d(t-t_{e}^{(1)})\geq 0
is equivalent to
|\tau|\leq\delta
as
d
takes
its minimum
-|\tau|+\delta
at

x=0
. Similarly,
\overline{d}
takes its maximum
|\tau|-\delta
at
x=-\tau
.

(i) Consider the case
|\tau|\leq\delta
. By (
\star
) one has
e(\psi_{t})=e(\psi^{(1)}_{t})
,
\overline{e}(\psi_{t})=\overline{e}(\psi^{(2)}_{t})
, whence
t_{e}=t^{(1)}_{e}
and
t_{\overline{e}}=t^{(2)}_{\overline{e}}=t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}-\tau
.
So one obtains the given value of

t_{\overline{e}}-t_{e}
by choosing
\tau=(t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}-t^{(1)}_{e})-(t_{\overline{e}}-t_{e})
. By a subsequent time translation according to (
15.2
)(c) one gets the prescribed values of
t_{e}
and
t_{\overline{e}}
.

We examine the remaining cases (ii) and (iii) in order to verify that, as mentioned in (
15.3
), the simple construction (
15.3
) does not yield a Dirac state like (
15.3
).

(ii) Now let
\tau>\delta
. Then
d(x)=\tau+\delta>0
for
x\leq-\tau
,
d(x)=-2x-\tau+\delta
for
-\tau\leq x\leq 0
, and
d(x)=-\tau+\delta
for
x\geq 0
. Hence
d\big(\frac{1}{2}(-\tau+\delta)\big)=0
. One infers
t_{e}=t_{e}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{2}(-\tau+\delta)
. It follows
e(\psi)=e(\psi^{(1)})
if
t_{e}\geq 0
and
e(\psi)=e(\psi^{(2)})
if
t_{e}\leq 0
. In other words,
e(\psi)=e(\psi^{(1)})
if

t^{(1)}_{e}\geq\frac{1}{2}(\tau-\delta)
and
e(\psi)=e(\psi^{(1)})+|t^{(1)}_{e}|-|\tau-t^{(1)}_{e}|+\delta
if
t^{(1)}_{e}\leq\frac{1}{2}(\tau-\delta)
.

Similarly,
\overline{d}(x)=\tau-\delta>0
for
x\leq-\tau
,
\overline{d}(x)=-2x-\tau-\delta
for
-\tau\leq x\leq 0
, and
\overline{d}(x)=-\tau-\delta
for
x\geq 0
. Hence
\overline{d}\big(\frac{1}{2}(-\tau-\delta)\big)=0
. One infers
t_{\overline{e}}=t_{\overline{e}}^{(1)}-\frac{1}{2}(\tau+\delta)
. It follows
\overline{e}(\psi)=\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)})
if
t_{\overline{e}}\geq 0
and

\overline{e}(\psi)=\overline{e}(\psi^{(2)})
if
t_{\overline{e}}\leq 0
. In other words,
\overline{e}(\psi)=\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)})
if
t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}\geq\frac{1}{2}(\tau+\delta)
and
\overline{e}(\psi)=\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)})+|t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|-|\tau-t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|-\delta
if
t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}\leq\frac{1}{2}(\tau+\delta)
.

(iii) Finally assume
-\tau>\delta
. Then
d(x)=\tau+\delta<0
for
x\leq 0
,
d(x)=2x+\tau+\delta
for
0\leq x\leq-\tau
, and
d(x)=-\tau+\delta
for
x\geq-\tau
. Hence
d\big(\frac{1}{2}(-\tau-\delta)\big)=0
. One infers
t_{e}=t_{e}^{(1)}-\frac{1}{2}(\tau+\delta)
. It follows
e(\psi)=e(\psi^{(2)})
if
t_{e}\geq 0
and
e(\psi)=e(\psi^{(1)})
if
t_{e}\leq 0
. In other words,
e(\psi)=e(\psi^{(1)})+|t^{(1)}_{e}|-|\tau-t^{(1)}_{e}|+\delta
if

t^{(1)}_{e}\geq\frac{1}{2}(\tau+\delta)
and
e(\psi)=e(\psi^{(1)})
if
t^{(1)}_{e}\leq\frac{1}{2}(\tau+\delta)
.

Similarly,
\overline{d}(x)=\tau-\delta<0
for
x\leq 0
,
\overline{d}(x)=2x+\tau-\delta
for
0\leq x\leq-\tau
, and
\overline{d}(x)=-\tau-\delta
for
x\geq-\tau
. Hence
\overline{d}\big(\frac{1}{2}(-\tau+\delta)\big)=0
. One infers
t_{\overline{e}}=t_{\overline{e}}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{2}(-\tau+\delta)
. It follows
\overline{e}(\psi)=\overline{e}(\psi^{(2)})
if
t_{\overline{e}}\geq 0
and

\overline{e}(\psi)=\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)})
if
t_{\overline{e}}\leq 0
. In other words,
\overline{e}(\psi)=\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)})+|t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|-|\tau-t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|-\delta
if
t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}\geq\frac{1}{2}(\tau-\delta)
and
\overline{e}(\psi)=\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)})
if
t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}\leq\frac{1}{2}(\tau-\delta)
.∎

As an example, the construction in (
15.3
) for
\tau:=t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}-t^{(1)}_{e}
,
\delta:=|\tau|
with subsequent time translation by
t_{e}=t_{\overline{e}}
yields a Dirac state
\psi
satisfying

t_{e}=t_{\overline{e}}=0\textrm{\, and }-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)=-\overline{e}(\psi^{(1)})-e(\psi^{(1)})+|t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}-t^{(1)}_{e}|-|t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|-|t^{(1)}_{e}|

(15.8)

The width of the carrier in direction
e
in not increased since
|t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}-t^{(1)}_{e}|-|t^{(1)}_{\overline{e}}|-|t^{(1)}_{e}|\leq 0
.

(71) Lemma.

For every
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
and
|\tau|<\frac{1}{2}(b-a)

there is a Dirac state
\psi
localized in a bounded region such that
a\leq e(\psi)<-\overline{e}(\psi)\leq b
and
t_{e}=t_{\overline{e}}=\tau
.

Proof.
Due to Eq. (
15.7
) it is no restriction to assume
a=-b
. Let
0<\rho<b
. By Eq. (
15.8
) there is a Dirac state
\eta
localized in a bounded region contained in
\{-\rho\leq xe\leq\rho\}
with
t_{e}(\eta)=t_{\overline{e}}(\eta)=0
. Let
\varsigma
denote the sign of
\tau
. Then, by causality,
\psi:=\eta_{\varsigma(-b+\rho)}
is localized in
\{-b\leq xe\leq b\}
. Moreover,
t_{e}=t_{\overline{e}}=\varsigma(b-\rho)
holds by (
15.2
)(c). The assertion follows for
\rho:=b-|\tau|
.∎

Before proceeding we remind of the symmetry of

(72) Time Reversal.

For the Dirac system time reversal is represented by the antiunitary operator
\mathcal{T}
, which in position representation Eq. (
14
) using the Weyl matrices reads

\mathcal{T}^{pos}\psi=\omega\,\overline{\psi}

with
\omega:=-\operatorname{i}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}=-\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{2})
. Note
\mathcal{T}^{2}=-I
. One finds
(\mathcal{T}^{mom}\varphi)(p)=\omega\overline{\varphi(-p)}
. Moreover
\big(\mathcal{T}^{pos}\,W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(g)\,\mathcal{T}^{pos\,-1}\psi\big)(x)=s(\sigma_{2}\overline{A}\sigma_{2})\,\big(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}y_{0}H^{pos}}\psi\big)(y)
(cf. Eq. (
14
)). Therefore
W^{\textsc{d}}(b,B)=\mathcal{T}\,W^{\textsc{d}}(b,B)\,\mathcal{T}^{-1}
and

W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\,-\rho e})=\mathcal{T}\,W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})\,\mathcal{T}^{-1},\quad W^{\textsc{d}}(-t)=\mathcal{T}\,W^{\textsc{d}}(t)\,\mathcal{T}^{-1}

(15.9)

i.e., Euclidean transformations are time-reversal invariant, boost and time direction are reversed. Recall that
A_{\rho e}=\operatorname{exp}(\frac{\rho}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{3}e_{k}\sigma_{k})
represents the boost in direction
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|=1
with rapidity
\rho
.

The crucial relations for this are
\sigma_{2}^{2}=I_{2}
and
-\sigma_{k}=\sigma_{2}\,\overline{\sigma}_{k}\,\sigma_{2}
for
k=1,2,3
, and hence
\overline{h(-\overline{z})}=\omega\,h(z)\,\omega
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

Obviously
\mathcal{T}E^{\textsc{d}}\mathcal{T}^{-1}=E^{\textsc{d}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}
commutes with the Dirac localization, and
\mathcal{T}\psi
is localized in the same region as
\psi
for every
\psi
. It follows for every
e

e(\psi)=e(\mathcal{T}\psi)\textrm{\; and\; }t_{e}(\mathcal{T}\psi)=-t_{e}(\psi)

(15.10)

Indeed, the first formula is obvious. As

(\mathcal{T}\psi)_{t}=\mathcal{T}\psi_{-t}
, on the one hand one has
e\big((\mathcal{T}\psi)_{t}\big)=e(\psi)+|t_{e}(\mathcal{T}\psi)|+|t-t_{e}(\mathcal{T}\psi)|
and on the other hand
e\big((\mathcal{T}\psi)_{t}\big)=e(\psi_{-t})=e(\psi)+|t_{e}(\psi)|+|t+t_{e}(\psi)|
for all
t
, whence the second formula.∎

(73) Large-
t_{\overline{e}}
-states.
In (
15.3
) we construct a bounded localized Dirac state
\psi
satisfying
\varsigma t_{\overline{e}}=\frac{1}{2}\big(-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)\big)
for a given direction
e
and
\varsigma\in\{+,-\}
. (One verifies explicitly that it is not possible to obtain such a state by (
15.3
), except in the trivial case that the initial state
\psi^{(1)}
already satisfies this condition.) By the spatial translation
W^{\textsc{d}}(b)
for
b:=-e(\psi)e
according to Eq. (
15.7
) one achieves

e(\psi)=0,\quad-\overline{e}(\psi)=2|t_{\overline{e}}|

(15.11)

or equivalently by (
15.2
)

e(\psi_{t_{e}})=|t_{e}|,\quad-\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})=|t_{\overline{e}}|

Bounded localized states
\psi
satisfying these relations
are particularly interesting. In view of (
15.2
)(a) we call them
large -
t_{\overline{e}}
- states
or, less specifically,
late-change states
.

These states are characterized in (
17
) by equivalent properties. Those of them, which are localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\}
,
\alpha>0
, form an infinite dimensional subspace.
This is proved (for
\varsigma=+
) in sec.
10
(see (
10
), (
10
)) within the general frame of causal systems. What is more, every Dirac state localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\geq 0\}
can be approximated by late-change states with
\varsigma=+
(as well with
\varsigma=-
),
see (
16
).
This property is essential for the proof of the Lorentz contraction in (
17.1
). Moreover, for the existence of these states there are no reps of the lattice of causally complete regions in sec.
12
, and there were no reps of the lattice generated by the non-timelike relation in sec.
24
without these states.

As a corollary of (
15.2
) one has the particularly simple formulae for boosted late-change states.

(74) Corollary.
Let
e
be a direction. Let
\psi
be a large -
t_{\overline{e}}
- state
(
15.11
)
. Put
\varsigma:=\operatorname{sign}t_{\overline{e}}
. Consider the boosted state
\psi^{\prime}:=W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})\psi
in direction
e
with rapidity
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

•

|t_{e}|<|t_{\overline{e}}|
,
\operatorname{sign}t_{e}\in\{0,\varsigma\}
,
t_{e}(\psi^{\prime})=\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}t_{e}

•

|t_{\overline{e}}-t_{e}|=-\overline{e}(\psi_{t_{\overline{e}}})-e(\psi_{t_{e}})

•

e(\psi^{\prime})=0

•

t_{\overline{e}}(\psi^{\prime})=\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}t_{\overline{e}}

•

\overline{e}(\psi^{\prime})=\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\overline{e}(\psi)

•

\psi^{\prime}
is a large -
t_{\overline{e}}
- state

Proof.
The second claim follows from the first one by (
15.11
). —
By (
15.2
)(b),(a) one has
t_{e}(\psi^{\prime})=e^{-\operatorname{sign}(t_{e})\rho}\,t_{e}
and the items three, four, and five. — Then (
15.2
)(a) yields
|t_{e}(\psi^{\prime})|\leq-\frac{1}{2}\overline{e}(\psi^{\prime})
for all
\rho
, whence either
t_{e}=0
or
\operatorname{sign}t_{e}=\varsigma
. Hence
2|t_{\overline{e}}(\psi^{\prime})|=2\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}|t_{\overline{e}}|=-\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\overline{e}(\psi)=-\overline{e}(\psi^{\prime})
and the last item follows. — Finally,
|t_{e}|<|t_{\overline{e}}|
holds because of (
15.2
)(c).∎

It follows the

(75) Construction of a late-change state.
Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
. Let
\varsigma\in\{+,-\}
. Then there is a bounded localized Dirac state
\psi
such that
a\leq e(\psi)<-\overline{e}(\psi)\leq b
and
\varsigma t_{\overline{e}}=\frac{1}{2}\big(-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)\big)
. The spatial translation by
-e(\psi)e
yields
(
15.11
)
.

Proof.
By Eq. (
15.10
) and due to (
15.2
)(a) it suffices to exhibit
\psi
with
t_{\overline{e}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\big(-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)\big)
.
As constructed in (
15.3
), there is a bounded localized Dirac state
\eta
with
a\leq e(\eta)<-\overline{e}(\eta)\leq b
and
\upsilon:=t_{\overline{e}}(\eta)>0
. By (
15.2
)(b),
\upsilon\leq-\overline{e}(\eta)-e(\eta)
. Let
0<\delta<\upsilon
. Set
\psi:=E^{\textsc{d}}\big(\{-\overline{e}(\eta)-\delta\leq xe\leq-\overline{e}(\eta)\}\big)\,\eta
(it is not necessary to normalize
\psi
here),
\tau:=t_{\overline{e}}
and define
\eta^{\prime}:=\eta-\psi
. Obviously
a\leq e(\psi)<-\overline{e}(\psi)\leq b
and

-\overline{e}(\eta)=-\overline{e}(\psi),\quad-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime})\leq-\overline{e}(\eta)-\delta\leq e(\psi)<-\overline{e}(\psi)

(
\star
)

By (
15.2
) and by (
\star
),
-\overline{e}(\psi_{\tau})=-\overline{e}(\psi)-|\tau|
and
-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime}_{\tau})=-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime})-|\upsilon^{\prime}|+|\tau-\upsilon^{\prime}|\leq-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime})+|\tau|\leq e(\psi)+|\tau|
.

Assume

|\tau|<\frac{1}{2}\big(-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)\big)
, which means
e(\psi)+|\tau|<-\overline{e}(\psi)-|\tau|
.
Hence
-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime}_{\tau})<-\overline{e}(\psi_{\tau})
. By (
15.2
),

-\overline{e}(\psi_{\tau})\leq-\overline{e}(\psi_{t})
for all
t
. Still by (
15.2
), this implies

-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime}_{t})<-\overline{e}(\psi_{t})
for all
t
. Now note
\eta_{t}=\eta^{\prime}_{t}+\psi_{t}
. One infers
-\overline{e}(\eta_{t})=-\overline{e}(\psi_{t})
for all
t
. In particular,
\upsilon=\tau
, whence by (
\star
) the contradiction
\tau>\delta\geq-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)
.

Thus
|\tau|\geq\frac{1}{2}\big(-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)\big)
holds. It remains to show
\tau\geq 0
. Assume the contrary. We start from
-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime})<-\overline{e}(\psi)
by (
\star
). Then by (
15.2
), for all
t\geq 0
one has
-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime}_{t})=-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime})-|\upsilon^{\prime}|+|t-\upsilon^{\prime}|\leq-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime})+t<-\overline{e}(\psi)+t=-\overline{e}(\psi)-|\tau|+|t-\tau|=-\overline{e}(\psi_{t})
. So

-\overline{e}(\eta^{\prime}_{t})<-\overline{e}(\psi_{t})
for all
t\geq 0
. This implies
-\overline{e}(\eta_{t})=-\overline{e}(\psi_{t})
for all
t\geq 0
. For
t=\upsilon>0
this means
-\overline{e}(\eta)-\upsilon=-\overline{e}(\psi)+\upsilon
. Since
-\overline{e}(\eta)=-\overline{e}(\psi)
by (
\star
), it contradicts
\upsilon\neq 0
.
∎

16.
Long-term behavior of the probability of localization of Dirac states

Of course, the probability of localization within the carrier of the wave function evolving in time stays at
1
. Insomuch the foregoing results on the movement of the border of the wave function yield also an information about the time dependence of the probability of localization. However the probability stays not equally distributed across the carrier. We will show that for every Dirac state (not necessarily bounded localized) in the long term the probability of localization concentrates up to
1
in the spherical shell
B_{|t|}\setminus B_{r}
for every radius
r>0
.

The main mathematical tool is an application of the non-stationary phase method as shown in
[
28
, Theorem 1.8.]
estimating Eq. (
16.1
) for large
|x|+|t|
. The part of the result, according to which the spatial probability in
B_{r}
tends to zero (see (
16
)),
is a corollary to
[
28
, Corollary 1.9.]
by the fact that
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\})
, the set of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-functions with compact support in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
, is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
Rather analogously we prove in (
16
) the fact that asymptotically the spatial probability vanishes outside
B_{|t|}
.

For the following proofs the obvious reduction to scalar-valued wave functions in momentum representation is used. Let
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
be a Dirac state in position representation and let
\varphi=\mathcal{F}\psi
be its momentum representation. Regarding the time evolution one has
\varphi_{t}=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th}\varphi
, i.e.,
\varphi_{t}(p)=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th(p)}\varphi(p)\,\forall\;p
. Let
\eta\in\{+,-\}
and recall that
\pi^{\eta}(p)=\frac{1}{2}(I+\frac{\eta}{\epsilon(p)}h(p))
with
\epsilon(p)=\sqrt{|p|^{2}+m^{2}}
is the projection in
\mathbb{C}^{4}
onto the
2
-dimensional eigenspace of
h(p)
with eigenvalue
\eta\,\epsilon(p)
. Then
\varphi^{\eta}:=\pi^{\eta}\varphi

is the projection of
\varphi
onto the positive, respectively negative, energy eigenspace. Analogously

(\varphi_{t})^{\eta}:=\pi^{\eta}\varphi_{t}
. Note that
(\varphi_{t})^{\eta}=(\varphi^{\eta})_{t}=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\eta\epsilon}\varphi^{\eta}
, as
\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th}
and
\pi^{\eta}
commute. One concludes
(\psi_{t})_{l}=\sum_{\eta}(\psi^{\eta}_{t})_{l}
,
(\psi^{\eta}_{t})_{l}:=\big(\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{\eta}_{t}\big)_{l}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\eta\epsilon}(\varphi^{\eta})_{l}\big)
for the
l
-th component of
\psi_{t}
,
l=1,\dots,4
. If
\varphi
is also integrable, then
\varphi^{\eta}
is so and for each
l
one has

(\psi^{\eta}_{t}(x))_{l}=(2\pi)^{-3/2}\int\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}(px+t\eta\epsilon(p))}(\varphi^{\eta}(p))_{l}\,\operatorname{d}^{3}p

(16.1)

(76) Theorem.
Let
\psi
be a Dirac state. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then there are
v\in\,]0,1[
and
\tau>0
such that
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}(B_{v|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon
for all
|t|\geq\tau
. In particular

E^{\textsc{d}}(B_{r})\psi_{t}\to 0,\quad|t|\to\infty

holds for every radius
r>0
.

Proof.
Recall
\varphi=\mathcal{F}\psi
and choose
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{C}^{4})
with
\parallel\!\!\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
. Hence
\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
for
\psi^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{\prime}
. Choose
0<v<\inf\{\frac{|p|}{\epsilon(p)}:p\in\operatorname{supp}(\varphi^{\prime})\}
. Let
P_{t}:=E^{\textsc{d}}(B_{v|t|})
. Now, according to
[
28
, Corollary 1.9.]
, there is a constant
C_{1}
such that
\parallel\!\!P_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq C_{1}(1+|t|)^{-1}
for all
t
. Let
\tau:=2C_{1}/\varepsilon
. Then
\parallel\!\!P_{t}\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!P_{t}(\psi_{t}-\psi^{\prime}_{t})\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!P_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq

\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+\,C_{1}(1+|t|)^{-1}\leq\varepsilon
for
|t|\geq\tau
. — Now fix
r>0
. Then for
|t|\geq\max\{\tau,\frac{r}{v}\}
one has
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}(B_{r})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}(B_{v|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon
.
∎

(77) Theorem.
Let
\psi
be a Dirac state. Then

E^{\textsc{d}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\to 0,\;\;|t|\to\infty

If
\mathcal{F}\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
holds then for every
N>0
there is a finite constant
C_{N}
such that
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq C_{N}(1+|t|)^{-N}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.
Suppose first
\varphi:=\mathcal{F}\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
. Let
K:=\operatorname{supp}(\varphi)
. Set
\gamma:=\max\{\frac{|p|}{\epsilon(p)}:p\in K\}
. Clearly
0<\gamma<1
. For the estimation of the integral in Eq. (
16.1
) consider
\phi^{\eta}(p):=(|x|+|t|)^{-1}\big(px-t\eta\epsilon(p)\big)
. Then
\nabla\phi^{\eta}(p)=(|x|+|t|)^{-1}\big(x-\frac{t\eta}{\epsilon(p)}p\big)
and
|\nabla\phi^{\eta}(p)|\geq(|x|+|t|)^{-1}\big(|x|-|t|\frac{|p|}{\epsilon(p)}\big)\geq\frac{|x|-\gamma|t|}{|x|+|t|}
for
p\in K
. Now suppose
|x|\geq|t|
. Then
|\nabla\phi^{\eta}(p)|\geq\frac{|x|-\gamma|x|}{|x|+|x|}=\frac{1-\gamma}{2}>0
. This implies (cf.
[
28
, (1.209)]
) for
\eta\in\{+,-\}
,
l=1,\dots,4
, and for every
N>0
that there is a finite constant
A_{N}
with

\big|\big(\psi^{\eta}_{t}(x)\big)_{l}\big|\leq A_{N}(1+|x|+|t|)^{-N}\textrm{ \;if\; }|x|\geq|t|

Hence
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\sum_{\eta}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\psi^{\eta}_{t}\!\!\parallel
and
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\psi^{\eta}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|}}\parallel\!\!\psi^{\eta}_{t}(x)\!\!\parallel^{2}\operatorname{d}x^{3}=\sum_{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|}}\big|\big(\psi^{\eta}_{t}(x)\big)_{l}\big|^{2}\operatorname{d}x^{3}\leq 16\pi A_{N}^{2}\int_{|t|}^{\infty}(1+r+|t|)^{-2N}r^{2}\operatorname{d}r\leq 16\pi A_{N}^{2}\int_{|t|}^{\infty}(1+r)^{-2N+2}\operatorname{d}r=\frac{16\pi}{2N-3}A_{N}^{2}(1+|t|)^{-2N+3}
if
N>\frac{3}{2}
. Hence
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq C_{N}(1+|t|)^{-N}
for
N>0
and
C_{N}:=(32\pi/N)^{\frac{1}{2}}A_{N+\frac{3}{2}}
.

Now consider a general Dirac state
\psi
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Set
\varphi:=\mathcal{F}\psi
and choose
