\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
with
\parallel\!\!\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
. Hence
\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
for
\psi^{\prime}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{\prime}
. Let
P_{t}:=E^{\textsc{d}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})
. By the foregoing result there is a constant
C_{1}
such that
\parallel\!\!P_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq C_{1}(1+|t|)^{-1}
for all
t
. Let
\tau:=2C_{1}/\varepsilon
. Then
\parallel\!\!P_{t}\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!P_{t}W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(t)(\psi-\psi^{\prime})\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!P_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq

\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+C_{1}(1+|t|)^{-1}\leq\varepsilon
for
|t|\geq\tau
.∎

(78) Asymptotic causality.
The results (
16
), (
16
) hold also with respect to the Newton-Wigner localization of Dirac states
\psi
. In particular one has the asymptotic causality

E^{\textsc{nw}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\to 0,\;\;|t|\to\infty

(16.2)

More generally these results are valid for all massive systems
[m,j,\eta]
(
m>0,j\in\mathbb{Z}/2,\eta=+,-
) endowed with the Newton-Wigner localization. Indeed, by (
3.6
) the evolution of the state
\psi
in position representation is

\psi_{t}(x)=(2\pi)^{-3/2}\int\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}(px+t\eta\epsilon(p))}\big(\mathcal{F}\psi\big)(p)\,\operatorname{d}^{3}p
.
It equals Eq. (
16.1
). Hence it suffices to apply the proofs of (
16
) and (
16
).

Asymptotic causality is shown in
[
17
, Proposition]
.
Note that (
16.2
) holds for every state, even if the initial state
\psi_{0}
is localized far away from the origin. In
[
17
]
it is also pointed out that (
16.2
) is false for the massless system
[0,0,\eta]
.
But the failure of (
16.2
) must not mean at all an acausal behavior. We think of the fact that for some states of the Weyl systems, which are causal, one has
\lim_{|t|\to\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\geq 1/2
by (
22
)(b).
∎

Let
\psi
be a Dirac state (not necessarily bounded localized) and let
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|=1
be a direction. Suppose that
e(\psi)
is finite. This implies that
\psi
is localized in the half-space
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe>e(\psi)\}
. By causality
\psi_{t}
is localized in the half-space
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe>e(\psi)-|t|\}
. Therefore
e(\psi_{t})\geq e(\psi)-|t|
. (Clearly, we imagine that even (
15.2
) holds for
e(\psi_{t})
.) The following result on
e(\psi_{t})
is a trivial consequence of (
16
). Nevertheless, because of its importance in view of (
10
)(f), we display it by

(79) Corollary.
Let
e
be a direction. There is no Dirac state
\psi
such that
e(\psi_{t_{n}})\geq|t_{n}|
for some sequence
|t_{n}|\to\infty
.

Therefore,
as already announced in the comment following (
10
), one has

P^{>}_{\sigma}=E(\sigma^{>})
. More precisely one has (
16
). Recall that
W^{\textsc{d}}(\alpha)
represents the time shift by
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and that
\psi_{\alpha}=W^{\textsc{d}}(\alpha)\psi
.

(80) Theorem.

Let
\psi
be a Dirac state localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\geq 0\}
. Then for

\alpha>0
and
\varsigma\in\{1,-1\}

\psi=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\psi^{\alpha}\textnormal{\; for \;
}\psi^{\alpha}:=W^{\textsc{d}}(\varsigma\alpha)E^{\textsc{d}}(\{xe\leq\alpha\})W^{\textsc{d}}(\varsigma\alpha)^{-1}\psi

and
\psi^{\alpha}
is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\geq 0\}
. In the definition of
\psi^{\alpha}
,
xe
can be replaced with
|xe|
. Furthermore,
(\psi^{\alpha})_{-\varsigma\alpha}
is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\leq\alpha\}
and
\psi^{\alpha}
is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\leq 2\alpha\}
. If
\psi
is localized in the (bounded) region
\Delta
then
\psi^{\alpha}
is localized in the (bounded) region
\Delta_{2\alpha}
.

Proof.
Recall the definition of
P^{>}_{\sigma}
. Then due to (
16
) the first part of the assertion follows from (
10
)(f),(g) for
\sigma=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{3}
. As
\psi_{-\varsigma\alpha}
is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\geq-\alpha\}
by causality,
xe
can be replaced with
|xe|
.
Obviously,
(\psi^{\alpha})_{-\varsigma\alpha}
is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\leq\alpha\}
. So
\psi^{\alpha}
is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\leq 2\alpha\}
by causality. Also the last part of the assertion holds by causality. ∎

(81) Corollary.

I=\lim_{|\alpha|\to\infty}W^{\textsc{d}}(\alpha)E^{\textsc{d}}(\{|xe|\leq|\alpha|\})W^{\textsc{d}}(\alpha)^{-1}
.

The result (
16
) is essential in proving the Lorentz contraction for Dirac wave functions in (
17.1
).
An alternative proof of (
16
) and (
22
) (see (
26
)) and
farther-reaching results are obtained
in sec.
26
by group-theoretical methods.

17.
Dirac localization and boosts

Recall that
A_{\rho e}=\operatorname{exp}(\frac{\rho}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{3}e_{k}\sigma_{k})
represents the boost in direction
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|=1
with rapidity
\rho
.
The formula from (
7.2
)(c)

\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq b\}_{A_{\rho e}}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq b\operatorname{e}^{|\rho|}\}

determines the region of influence of the boosted region
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq b\}
,
b>0
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. By causality this implies for a Dirac state
\psi
localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq b\}
that the boosted state
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho e})\psi
is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq b\operatorname{e}^{|\rho|}\}
for all
\rho
. Moreover, in case that
e(\psi)<0<-\overline{e}(\psi)
one has
\psi=\psi^{l}+\psi^{r}
, where
\psi^{l}:=1_{\{x\overline{e}\geq 0\}}\psi
,
\psi^{r}:=1_{\{xe\geq 0\}}\psi
are not zero. As the foregoing consideration shows, the boosted wave functions
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho e})\psi^{l}
and
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho e})\psi^{r}
are still localized in
\{x\overline{e}\geq 0\}
and
\{xe\geq 0\}
, respectively. So they do not interfere. Therefore for the study of the carrier of a boosted Dirac state
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho e})\psi
it is no restriction to assume that
\psi
is localized in the half-space
\{xe\geq 0\}
.

(82) Theorem.
Let
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|e|=1
,
\alpha>0
, and
\varsigma\in\{+,-\}
. Let
\psi
be a Dirac state localized in a bounded region contained in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\}
. Then the statements
(a) – (d)
are equivalent.

(a)

\psi
is a late-change state, i.e.
2\,\varsigma\,t_{\overline{e}}(\psi)=-\,\overline{e}(\psi)

(b)

W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})\psi
is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\,\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\}
for all
\varsigma\,\rho\geq 0
.

(c)

There is
c>0
such that
W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})\psi
is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq c\}
for all
\varsigma\,\rho\geq 0
.

(d)

\psi_{t}
for
t=\varsigma\alpha

is localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\leq\alpha\}
.

Proof.
We prove the assertion for the case
\varsigma=+
. Then the case
\varsigma=-
follows by Eq. (
15.9
), Eq. (
15.10
) applying the former to
\mathcal{T}\psi
. Moreover, due to rotational symmetry it suffices to consider the special case
e=(0,0,1)
, cf. the proof of (
7.2
)(c).

We start from (b). Then (c) holds trivially. We turn to (c)
\Rightarrow
(a). By (
14
) and the footnote in sec.
3.3
,

s(\operatorname{e}^{\rho\,\sigma_{3}/2})^{-1}\big(W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho})\psi\big)(x)=\big(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\sinh(\rho)x_{3}H^{pos}}\psi\big)\big(x_{1},x_{2},\cosh(\rho)x_{3}\big)
. Introduce
t:=\sinh(\rho)x_{3}
for
\rho>0
. Since
x_{3}\geq c\Leftrightarrow t\geq\sinh(\rho)\,c
, so
\psi_{t}\big(x_{1},x_{2},\coth(\rho)\,t\big)=0
for
t\geq\sinh(\rho)\,c
. Fix
t>0
. Then
J:=\{\coth(\rho)\,t:\sinh(\rho)\leq t/c,\rho>0\}=\{\sqrt{1+x^{-2}}\,t:0<x\leq t/c\}
. As
\{\sqrt{1+x^{-2}}:0<x\leq t/c\}=[\sqrt{1+c^{2}/t^{2}},\infty[
, one has
J=[\sqrt{c^{2}+t^{2}},\infty[
. Hence one finds
\psi_{t}(x)=0
if
x_{3}\geq\sqrt{c^{2}+t^{2}}
,
t\geq 0
. In other words,
-\overline{e}(\psi_{t})\leq\sqrt{c^{2}+t^{2}}

\forall

t\geq 0
. —
Apply (
15.2
) to
\overline{e}(\psi_{t})
for
t\geq\max\{0,t_{\overline{e}},\gamma\}
with
\gamma:=-\overline{e}(\psi)-|t_{\overline{e}}|-t_{\overline{e}}
. This yields
t+\gamma\leq\sqrt{c^{2}+t^{2}}
and hence
t^{2}+2t\gamma+\gamma^{2}\leq t^{2}+c^{2}
. This implies
\gamma\leq 0
. As
-\overline{e}(\psi)>0
it follows

t_{\overline{e}}>0
and then
2\,t_{\overline{e}}\geq-\overline{e}(\psi)
. Thus (a) holds by (
15.2
)(a).

We show (a)
\Rightarrow
(d). By (
15.2
) one has
-\overline{e}(\psi_{\alpha})=-\overline{e}(\psi)-|t_{\overline{e}}|+|\alpha-t_{\overline{e}}|
. Since
t_{\overline{e}}=-\overline{e}(\psi)/2\leq\alpha
, it follows
-\overline{e}(\psi_{\alpha})=\alpha
, whence (d).

It remains to prove (d)
\Rightarrow
(b). Since
\psi_{\alpha}
is localized in
\{x_{3}\leq\alpha\}
and
\psi
is localized in
\{0\leq x_{3}\}
, by causality
\psi_{t}=\psi_{\alpha+t-\alpha}
is localized in
\{-|t|\leq x_{3}\leq\alpha+|t-\alpha|\}
for every
t
. Therefore
\psi_{t}(x)=0
for
t\leq 0
,
x_{3}\leq t
or
t\geq 0
,
x_{3}\geq\alpha+|t-\alpha|
. This implies that
\psi_{t}\big(x_{1},x_{2},\coth(\rho)t\big)=0
if
t<0
,
\rho>0
or
t\geq(1-\operatorname{e}^{-2\rho})\,\alpha
,
\rho>0
(since the latter is equivalent to
\coth(\rho)t\geq 2\alpha-t
,
\rho>0
). Now introducing
x_{3}:=t/\sinh(\rho)
one has
\psi_{\sinh(\rho)x_{3}}\big(x_{1},x_{2},\cosh(\rho)x_{3}\big)=0
for
x_{3}<0
,
\rho>0
or
x_{3}\geq 2\alpha\,\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}
,
\rho>0
. Hence (b) follows.∎

Recall (
15.3
) for the introduction and the existence of late-change states. Furthermore
see the comment following (
17
) and the corollary (
17
).

Most remarkable in (
17
) is the equivalence (b)
\Leftrightarrow
(d) establishing a close relationship between the localization of the boosted state and the state evolved in time. It gives rise to the formula in (
17
). Recall also (
15.3
) by which (
17
)(b) can be replaced by

e(W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho})\psi)=0
,
\overline{e}(W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho})\psi)=\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\overline{e}(\psi)
for all
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
.

(83) Corollary.
Let
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|e|=1
,
\alpha>0
, and
\varsigma\in\{+,-\}
. Then, for
W=W^{\textsc{d}},E=E^{\textsc{d}}
, one has

W(\varsigma\alpha)E(\{xe\leq\alpha\})W(\varsigma\alpha)^{-1}E(\{xe\geq 0\})=\lim_{\varsigma\rho\to\infty}W(A_{\rho e})E(\{0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\,\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\})W(A_{\rho e})^{-1}

where the limit exists since the projections decrease as
\varsigma\rho
increases.

Proof.
Like for the proof of (
17
) we may restrict ourselves to the case
\varsigma=+
and
e=(0,0,1)
. Then the projections on the right hand side read
E(\Gamma_{\rho})
for
\Gamma_{\rho}:=A_{\rho}\cdot\{0\leq x_{3}\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\}
. Check
\hat{\Gamma}_{\rho}=\{-2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-2\rho}\leq x_{0}-x_{3}\leq 0,0\leq x_{0}+x_{3}\leq 2\alpha\}
. Hence
\hat{\Gamma}_{\rho^{\prime}}\subset\hat{\Gamma}_{\rho}
for
\rho<\rho^{\prime}
, whence
E(\Gamma_{\rho^{\prime}})\leq E(\Gamma_{\rho})
by (
12
). Therefore the limit
\lim_{\rho\to\infty}E(\Gamma_{\rho})
exists yielding a projection
P_{r}
. Furthermore, according to Eq. (
10.3
), Eq. (
10.4
), the left hand side, denoted by
P_{l}
, is a projection. In particular,
E(\{x_{3}\geq 0\})
commutes with
W(\alpha)E(\{x_{3}\leq\alpha\})W(\alpha)^{-1}
.

Suppose
P_{l}\varphi=\varphi
. The claim is
P_{r}\varphi=\varphi
. It is no restriction to assume that
\varphi
is bounded localized. Indeed, there is a bounded localized
\varphi^{\prime}
with
\parallel\!\!\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel<\varepsilon
. By assumption
P_{r}P_{l}\varphi^{\prime}=P_{l}\varphi^{\prime}
since
P_{l}\varphi^{\prime}
is bounded localized by (
16
). Then
\parallel\!\!P_{r}\varphi-\varphi\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!P_{r}\varphi-P_{r}P_{l}\varphi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!P_{r}P_{l}\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!\varphi-P_{l}\varphi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!P_{l}\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi\!\!\parallel=2\parallel\!\!P_{l}\varphi^{\prime}-P_{l}\varphi\!\!\parallel\leq 2\varepsilon
. — Thus suppose
P_{l}\varphi=\varphi
and
\varphi
bounded localized.
Then
\varphi
is localized in
\{x_{3}\geq 0\}
and
\varphi_{-\alpha}
is localized in
\{x_{3}\leq\alpha\}
. The latter implies by causality that
\varphi
is localized also in
\{x_{3}\leq 2\alpha\}
. Hence we may apply (
17
)(d)
\Rightarrow
(b) for
\varsigma=-1
. This yields
E(\{0\leq x_{3}\leq 2\alpha\,\operatorname{e}^{\rho}\})W(A_{\rho})\varphi=W(A_{\rho})\varphi
for all
-\rho\geq 0
. Therefore
E(\Gamma_{\rho})\varphi=\varphi
for all
\rho>0
.

Conversely, suppose now
P_{r}\varphi=\varphi
. Recall
E(\Gamma_{\rho^{\prime}})\leq E(\Gamma_{\rho})
for
\rho<\rho^{\prime}
. Then,
\varphi
is localized in
\{0\leq x_{3}\leq 2\alpha\}
since
\varphi=E(\Gamma_{0})\varphi
, and

W(A_{\rho})^{-1}\varphi=W(A_{-\rho})\varphi
is localized in
\{0\leq x_{3}\leq 2\alpha\,\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\}
since
\varphi=E(\Gamma_{\rho})\varphi
. In particular
P_{r}\varphi
is bounded localized if
\varphi
is so. Thus assume as above without restriction that
\varphi
is bounded localized.
Hence we may apply (
17
)(b)
\Rightarrow
(d) for
\varsigma=-1
. Then
W(\alpha)E(\{x_{3}\leq\alpha\})W(\alpha)^{-1}\varphi=\varphi
. Now recall that
\varphi
is localized in
\{x_{3}\geq 0\}
.
P_{l}\varphi=\varphi
follows.∎

Applying Eq. (
10.4
), Eq. (
10.3
) to the projection
P_{l}
at the left hand side of (
17
) it follows from (
10
) that
P_{l}
is non-trivial and has infinite dimensional eigenspaces.
So, only now we know that (the scalar multiples of) the large -
t_{\overline{e}}
- states for
t_{\overline{e}}>0
(as well for
t_{\overline{e}}<0
) localized in
\{0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\}
,
\alpha>0
actually form
an infinite dimensional subspace, namely the range of
P_{l}
.

(84) Corollary.
For every direction
e
the large -
t_{\overline{e}}
- states for
t_{\overline{e}}>0
(as well for
t_{\overline{e}}<0
) are dense in the set of states localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\geq 0\}
.

Proof.
Let the Dirac state
\psi
be localized in
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe\geq 0\}
and let
\varepsilon>0
. Choose a state
\psi^{\prime}
localized in a bounded region contained in
\{xe\geq 0\}
with
\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
. For
\varsigma\in\{1,-1\}
choose
\alpha>0
according to (
16
) such that
\parallel\!\!\psi^{\prime}-\psi^{\prime\alpha}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
. Note that by (
16
) and by (
17
),(d)
\Rightarrow
(a) the state
\psi^{\prime\alpha}
is a
large -
t_{\overline{e}}
- state.∎

17.1.
Lorentz contraction

By (
17
)(a) it is clear that the cases
\varsigma=+
and
\varsigma=-
exclude each other. Consequently by (
17
)(c), there is no Dirac state
\psi
localized in a bounded region such that the boosted states
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho e})\psi
,
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
stay confined in any strip
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:a\leq xe\leq b\}
. If
|t_{\overline{e}}|<\frac{1}{2}(-\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi))
then
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho e})\psi
is confined neither for
\rho\to\infty
nor for
\rho\to-\infty
.

Nevertheless, a series of numerical computations performed for the Dirac equation in one-dimensional discrete space by Mutze 2016
[
61
]
supports the idea that, for every Dirac state
\psi
, the probability of localization of the boosted states
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho e})\psi
in a strip
\{-\delta\leq xe\leq\delta\}
around the origin tends to
1
if the rapidity
\rho
tends to
\infty
or
-\infty
. We like to call this behavior the Lorentz contraction of the Dirac wave functions, which indeed is proven to hold true by the following

(85) Theorem.
Let
\psi
be any Dirac state, in particular an electron or positron state. Let
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|=1
be a direction. Then for every
\delta>0

\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}\big(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:-\delta\leq xe\leq\delta\}\big)\,W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})\psi\!\!\parallel\,\to 1,\;\;|\rho|\to\infty

Proof.
As argued at the beginning of sec.
17
it is no restriction to assume that
\psi
is localized in

\{xe\geq 0\}
. Then
W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})\psi
stays localized there for all
\rho
. Let
\varepsilon>0
.
According to (
17
) choose a large -
t_{\overline{e}}
- state
\psi^{\prime}
localized in
\{0\leq xe\leq c\}
for some
c>0
with
\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon
. Let
Q_{\rho}:=E^{\textsc{d}}\big(\{xe>\delta\}\big)\,W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})
.
Then, for all
\rho\leq\rho_{-}:=\min\{0,\ln\frac{\delta}{c}\}
, one has
Q_{\rho}\psi^{\prime}=0
and hence
\parallel\!\!Q_{\rho}\psi\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!Q_{\rho}\psi-Q_{\rho}\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!Q_{\rho}\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon
.

Now apply this result to
\mathcal{T}\psi
with
\mathcal{T}
the time reversal operator (see (
15.3
)). Accordingly

\parallel\!\!Q_{\rho}\mathcal{T}\psi\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon
for all
\rho\leq-\rho_{+}
for some
\rho_{+}\geq 0
.
Since
Q_{\rho}\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}^{-1}Q_{-\rho}
by Eqs. (
15.9
), (
15.10
),
it follows
\parallel\!\!Q_{\rho}\psi\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon
for
\rho\geq\rho_{+}
, thus accomplishing the proof.∎

Let
\psi
be any Dirac state localized in the half-space
\{xe\geq 0\}
. As shown in the proof of (
17.1
) there is an appropriately prepared large -
t_{\overline{e}}
- state
\chi^{\alpha}
localized in a bounded region contained in the strip
\{0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\}
,
\alpha>0
, which approximates
\psi
arbitrarily well such that the probability of localization of
\psi
in the carrier of
\chi^{\alpha}
is almost one. Due to the unitarity of the transformation the latter holds true also for the respective boosted states. The particular feature of
\chi^{\alpha}
is that for every
\rho\geq 0
the boosted state
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho e})\chi^{\alpha}
is localized in the strip
\{0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\}
. This shows a Lorentz factor
\operatorname{e}^{\rho}
which for large
\rho
is almost twice the classical one
\gamma=\cosh(\rho)
.

What happens to the carrier of
\chi^{\alpha}
in directions perpendicular to that of the boost?
Let
c>0
such that
\chi^{\alpha}
is localized in the cylinder
\Gamma:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha,|x-(xe)e|\leq c\}
with axis
\mathbb{R}e
, length
2\alpha
and radius
c
. Then, due to causality,
W^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(A_{\rho e})\chi^{\alpha}
is localized in
\{0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\}\cap\Gamma_{A_{\rho e}}
, which by (
7.2
)(d) is a truncated cone with axis
\mathbb{R}e
, length
2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}
, and radii
c
and
c+\alpha(1-\operatorname{e}^{-2\rho})
at
0
and
2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}e
, respectively. Hence for
\rho\to\infty
the carrier shrinks to the disc
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:xe=0,|x|\leq c+\alpha\}
.

17.2.
Frame-dependence of Lorentz contraction

In classical mechanics it is widely discussed whether the length contraction really exists or not. According to Einstein 1911
[
62
]
it is a misleading question as length contraction does not “really” exist, in so far as it does not exist for a comoving observer; though it “really” exists, i.e. in such a way that it could be demonstrated in principle by physical means by a non-comoving observer.

We turn to the question about the reality of the Lorentz contraction of a Dirac wave function. In order to be clear let us first recall briefly some well-known general facts (see e.g.
[
10
, sec. VIII]
). Let
\mathfrak{R}
denote the origin and the standard basis
\big(\mathfrak{o},(\mathfrak{e}^{0},\mathfrak{e}^{1},\mathfrak{e}^{2},\mathfrak{e}^{3})\big)
of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
as well the reference frame related to it.
The description of the physical system will refer to
\mathfrak{R}

in determining the representation of its states
S
and observables
O
by unit vectors
\psi
and self-adjoint operators
A
, respectively, in the Hilbert space of states. The outcomes of measurements are the expectation values

\langle\psi,A\psi\rangle
. The state space is unique up to Hilbert space isomorphisms
\iota
, as
\iota\psi
and
\iota A\iota^{-1}
yield the same expectation values as
\psi
and
A
, i.e.,
\langle\iota\psi,(\iota A\iota^{-1})\,\iota\psi\rangle=\langle\psi,A\psi\rangle
. So every
\iota
determines another representation of the states
S
and observables
O
of the system with

\iota\psi
and
\iota A\iota^{-1}
denoting the same state and observable, respectively, as
\psi
and
A
do. This freedom plays a decisive role in the description of symmetries. In particular
relativistic symmetry
gives rise to a representation
W
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
in the state space.

Let
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\equiv g^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{R}

with
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
be any other reference frame.
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is the frame for which
g\cdot\mathfrak{x}
denotes the same event as
\mathfrak{x}
for

\mathfrak{R}
. (Recall that

g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
acts as the Poincaré transformation Eq. (
3.2
). Its coordinates refer to
\mathfrak{R}
and are the same with respect to
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
.) Consequently a Poincaré transformation described by
h\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
with respect to

\mathfrak{R}
reads
h^{\prime}:=ghg^{-1}
with respect to

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
. If a state
S
and an observable
O
are described by
\psi
and
A
with respect to

\mathfrak{R}
, then they are described by
\psi^{\prime}:=W(g)\psi
and
A^{\prime}:=W(g)\,A\,W(g)^{-1}
with respect to

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
. In this way invariance of the expectation values under change of the reference frame is ensured. Similarly, since the Poincaré transformation
h
is represented by
W(h)
with respect to

\mathfrak{R}
, its description is
W(g)W(h)W(g)^{-1}=W(ghg^{-1})
with respect to

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
. Finally, what looks like
W^{\prime}
denoting the representation
W
when described with respect to

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
? Recall that
h^{\prime}=ghg^{-1}
denotes the Poincaré transformation
h
when described in
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
. Hence, by the foregoing result,
W^{\prime}(h^{\prime})
has to equal
W(ghg^{-1})
. So one ends up with

W^{\prime}(k)=W(k)\quad\forall\;k\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}

(17.1)

where
k
is expressed on both sides by the coordinates of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
.

Back to the initial question, note first that the Lorentz contraction (
17.1
) can be observed. Indeed, let
S
be any Dirac state represented by the state vector
\psi
. For given
\varepsilon>0
consider the observable
O
described by
A:=E^{\textsc{d}}(\{|xe|\leq\delta\})
with
\delta>0
so small that
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{d}}(\{|xe|\leq\delta\})\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}\,\leq\varepsilon
. According to (
17.1
) there is
\tilde{\rho}>0
such that
||E^{\textsc{d}}\big(\{|xe|\leq\delta\}\big)\,W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\rho e})\psi||^{2}\geq 1-\varepsilon
for all
\rho\geq\tilde{\rho}
. Let
\tilde{S}
be the boosted state described by
\tilde{\psi}:=W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{\tilde{\rho}e})\psi
. Then

\langle\psi,A\psi\rangle\leq\varepsilon\;\textrm{ and }\;\langle\tilde{\psi},A\tilde{\psi}\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon

(17.2)

Hence the observable
O
distinguishes the state
S
from the boosted state
\tilde{S}
. So the observer related to
\mathfrak{R}
can ascertain the Lorentz contraction of the Dirac system.

What are the ascertainments of an observer related to
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
provided with the localization
(E^{\textsc{d}})^{\prime}
?
By (
3.3
), (
17.1
) one has
A^{\prime}=E^{\textsc{d}}(g\cdot\{x_{0}=0,|xe|\leq\delta\})
,
\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}=W^{\textsc{d}}(h^{\prime})\psi^{\prime}
for
h^{\prime}=ghg^{-1}
,
h:=A_{\tilde{\rho}e}
. Then
\langle\psi^{\prime},A^{\prime}\psi^{\prime}\rangle\leq\varepsilon
,
\langle\tilde{\psi}^{\prime},A^{\prime}\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon
. Hence, observed from
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
, the Dirac system in the state
S
is highly localized in the spacelike region
g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,|xe|>\delta\}
, whereas in the boosted state
\tilde{S}
it is highly localized in
g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,|xe|\leq\delta\}
. The expected conclusion is that, due to relativistic symmetry, the Lorentz contraction of the Dirac system can be ascertained in the same way and with the same result by any Lorentz observer.

On the other hand there is the
frame-dependence
of the Lorentz contraction, which is discussed now. By virtue of the Poincaré covariance of the localization no reference to a moving observer is needed. Let a four vector
\mathfrak{e}
be called a spacelike direction if

\mathfrak{e}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=-1
.

(86) Corollary.
Let
\psi
be a Dirac state. Let
\sigma
be a spacelike hyperplane and
\mathfrak{e}
a spacelike direction parallel to
\sigma
. Boost them along
\mathfrak{e}
with rapidity
\rho
obtaining

\sigma_{\rho}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{\rho}
. Then

||E^{\textsc{d}}\big(\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma_{\rho}:|(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{o})\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\rho}|\leq\delta\}\big)\psi||\to 1\textrm{ for }|\rho|\to\infty

where
\mathfrak{o}\in\sigma
is the fixed point of the boost.

Proof.
Due to relativistic symmetry we may identify
\sigma
with
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
\mathfrak{o}
with the origin
0
, and
\mathfrak{e}
with
(0,e)
. Then the claim follows immediately from (
17.1
) due to the covariance of
E^{\textsc{d}}
, by which
||E^{\textsc{d}}\big(A_{\rho e}\cdot\{|xe|\leq\delta\}\big)\,\psi||^{2}=||E^{\textsc{d}}\big(\{|xe|\leq\delta\}\big)\,W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{-\rho e})\psi||^{2}
.
∎

Thus, if the frame is moving fast enough depending on the state, then the Dirac system is highly localized in a narrow strip perpendicular to the direction of motion.
One likes to remember the frame-dependence of the NWL (
3.3
): As soon as the frame of the bounded localized Newton-Wigner system moves, the latter is no longer localized in any bounded region. Nevertheless, presumably the Newton-Wigner system undergoes the same Lorentz contraction (
17.2
) as the Dirac system.

The frame-dependence of the Lorentz contraction in classical mechanics is striking by the fact that for the comoving observer it does not even exist. The same holds true for the Lorentz contraction of the Dirac wavefunctions. Moreover, due to the Poincaré covariance of the localization no reference to a moving observer is needed, but the fact refers to the expectation value of the corresponding localization observable. Indeed, recalling (
17.2
), let
\mathcal{A}
be an apparatus realizing the localization observable
O
described by
A
. Boost
\mathcal{A}
according to
A_{\tilde{\rho}e}
thus obtaining the comoving apparatus
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
. It realizes the localization observable
\tilde{O}
for the space-like region
A_{\rho e}\cdot\{|xe|\leq\delta\}
(to which a comoving observer refers) described by
\tilde{A}:=E^{\textsc{d}}\big(A_{\rho e}\cdot\{|xe|\leq\delta\}\big)
. Then due to the covariance of localization

\langle\tilde{\psi},A\tilde{\psi}\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon\;\textrm{ and }\;\langle\tilde{\psi},\tilde{A}\tilde{\psi}\rangle\leq\varepsilon

(17.3)

holds. This means that the non-comoving apparatus
\mathcal{A}
ascertains the Lorentz contraction of the Dirac system whereas the comoving apparatus
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
ascertains non-contraction.

In view of these clear results it is worth to consider also the particular reference frame

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}:=A_{\tilde{\rho}e}\cdot\mathfrak{R}
. Imagine an object resting at
0
with respect to

\mathfrak{R}
, i.e., with world line
t\mapsto t(1,0,0,0)
. When boosted according to
A_{\tilde{\rho}e}
it moves with velocity
\tilde{v}=\tanh(\tilde{\rho})
. Its world line is
t\mapsto t\cosh(\tilde{\rho})(1,\tilde{v}e)
. Hence

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is the rest frame of this boosted object.

Now, with respect to

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
the boosted state

\tilde{S}
is represented by
\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}=W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{-\tilde{\rho}})\tilde{\psi}=\psi
. Accordingly one is tempted to consider

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
to be the rest frame of
S
. But this interpretation seems to make sense only in the context of the history of the system. Anyway, the discerning observable
O
is no longer represented by
A
but by
A^{\prime}=W^{\textsc{d}}(A_{-\tilde{\rho}e})AW^{\textsc{d}}(A_{-\tilde{\rho}e})^{-1}
so that
\langle\psi,A^{\prime}\psi\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon
. By the Poincaré covariance of the localization one has
A^{\prime}=E^{\textsc{d}}(A_{-\tilde{\rho}e}\cdot\{x_{0}=0,|xe|\leq\delta\})
.
Thus
\langle\psi,A^{\prime}\psi\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon
means also that the Dirac system in the state
\tilde{S}
is almost localized in the spacelike region
\{\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:-\delta\leq-x^{\prime}_{0}/\sinh\tilde{\rho}=x^{\prime}e/\cosh\tilde{\rho}\leq\delta\}
in Minkowski space.

18.
Causal PO-Localization for the Electron

By (
4
) there is no causal WL for the electron. But the trace
T^{e}
of
E^{\textsc{d}}
on the positive energy subspace of
W^{\textsc{d}}
yields a causal POL for the Dirac electron by (
9
). Let
P^{+}
denote the projection on the subspace of positive energy. Then

T^{e}(\Gamma)\varphi=P^{+}E^{\textsc{d}}(\Gamma)\varphi

(18.1)

for all measurable subsets
\Gamma
of spacelike hyperplanes and electron states
\varphi
.

18.1.
Properties of
T^{e}

For the following compare
[
28
, Corollary 1.7]
. Call
\operatorname{supp}(\psi)=\bigcap_{\psi^{\prime}\in[\psi]}\overline{\{\psi^{\prime}\neq 0\}}
the support of
\psi
. There is
\psi^{\prime}\in[\psi]
satisfying
\operatorname{supp}(\psi)=\overline{\{\psi^{\prime}\neq 0\}}
.
19
19

19

Generally, let
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be measurable and set
C:=\bigcap\{\overline{\Delta\setminus N}:N\textrm{ null set}\}
,

C^{\prime}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lambda(\Delta\cap U)>0\,\forall\,U\textrm{ open, }x\in U\}
, and
C^{\prime\prime}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup\{U\textrm{ open}:\lambda(U\cap\Delta)=0\}
with
\lambda
the Lebesgue measure. Then
C=C^{\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover,
N:=\Delta\setminus C
is a null set and
C=\overline{\Delta\setminus N}
. — Indeed, one easily checks
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime\prime}
. If
x\in C^{\prime}
then
\lambda(\Delta\cap U)>0
for all open
U
with
x\in U
. This implies that
(\Delta\setminus N)\cap U\neq\emptyset
for every null set
N
. Hence
x\in\overline{\Delta\setminus N}
for all
N
. Therefore
x\in C
. Thus
C^{\prime}\subset C
. For the proof of the reverse inclusion consider any countable base
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and put
V:=\bigcup\{U\in\mathcal{B}:\lambda(U\cap\Delta)=0\}
. Obviously
\Delta\cap V
is a null set. Let
N\supset\Delta\cap V
be any null set and let
x\in\overline{\Delta\setminus N}
. Then for every open
U
with
x\in U
there is
x^{\prime}\in(\Delta\setminus V)\cap U
. One infers
\lambda(\Delta\cap U)>0
. Hence
x\in C^{\prime}
. Thus
C\subset C^{\prime}
. Finally, note
V=\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus C
. So
N:=\Delta\setminus C
is a null set. Therefore
C\subset\overline{\Delta\setminus N}=\overline{C\cap N}\subset C
, whence the last part of the assertion. — The question remains open whether
C\setminus\Delta
is a null set.

(87) Corollary.
Let
\psi
be an electron state in position space representation and let
\Delta\neq\mathbb{R}^{3}
be closed. Then
T^{e\,pos}(\Delta)\psi\neq\psi
and
the support of
\psi
is
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Proof.

T^{e\,pos}(\Delta)\psi\neq\psi
holds
by (
4
).
Hence
E^{\textsc{d}pos}(\Delta)\psi\neq\psi
since
P^{+pos}\psi=\psi
. On the other hand
1_{\overline{\{\psi^{\prime}\neq 0\}}}\psi^{\prime}=\psi^{\prime}
for every
\psi^{\prime}\in[\psi]
, whence
E^{\textsc{d}pos}(\overline{\{\psi^{\prime}\neq 0\}})\psi=\psi
. Therefore
\overline{\{\psi^{\prime}\neq 0\}}=\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which ends the proof.∎

We show now by (
8.2
) that
T^{e}
is separated. The result (
18.1
) is a particular case of
[
5
, Theorem 8]
. In momentum space
one has

\big(P^{+mom}\varphi\big)(p)=\pi^{+}(p)\varphi(p),\quad\>\pi^{+}(p):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{{\it\small{I}}}_{4}+\frac{1}{\epsilon(p)}h(p)\right)

with
\epsilon(p):=\sqrt{|p|^{2}+m^{2}}
. Since

(D_{\lambda}^{mom}\varphi)(p)=\lambda^{\frac{3}{2}}\varphi(\lambda p)
, for
P^{+}_{\lambda}:=D_{\lambda}P^{+}D_{\lambda}^{-1}
one finds
\big(P_{\lambda}^{+mom}\varphi\big)(p)=\pi^{+}(\lambda p)\varphi(p)
.
For every
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the matrices
\pi^{+}(\lambda p)
and

\pi_{0}(p):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{{\it\small{I}}}_{4}+\frac{1}{|p|}\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}p_{k}\right)

are orthogonal projection
with
\pi^{+}(\lambda p)\to\pi_{0}(p)
for
\lambda\to\infty
. Then
\big(Q^{mom}\varphi\big)(p):=\pi_{0}(p)\varphi(p)
defines a non-zero orthogonal projection operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
.
By dominated convergence
P^{+}_{\lambda}\to Q
strongly as
\lambda\to\infty
.
Thus the premises of (
8.2
) hold true and one has

(88) Theorem.
The trace
T^{e}
of
E^{\textsc{d}}
on the positive energy subspace is a causal separated POL of the Dirac electron. If
\phi
is any Dirac state with
Q\,\phi\neq 0
, then
P^{+}D_{n}^{-1}\phi\neq 0
up to finitely many
n
and

\varphi_{n}:=\frac{1}{||P^{+}D_{n}^{-1}\phi||}\,P^{+}D_{n}^{-1}\phi

yields a sequence of electron states localized at
0
.

One may imagine a point-localized sequence
(\varphi_{n})
of electron states to be a progressive preparation of the system in order to get an increasing localization around a point. Regarding the sequences in (
18.1
), the result in (
18.1
), which is a special case of

[
5
, Theorem 9]
, shows that this process needs unlimited energy. — Let
|P|
denote, in momentum representation, the multiplication operator by
|p|
.

(89) Lemma.

Let
\phi
be a Dirac state with
Q\,\phi\neq 0
. Consider
\varphi_{n}
from
(
18.1
)
. Then, for every
n
,

\varphi_{n}\in\operatorname{dom}(H)
if and only if
Q\,\phi\in\operatorname{dom}(|P|)
. Let
Q\,\phi\in\operatorname{dom}(|P|)
. Then

\frac{1}{n}\langle\varphi_{n},H\varphi_{n}\rangle\to\frac{1}{||Q\,\phi||^{2}}\langle Q\,\phi,|P|\,Q\,\phi\rangle>0\quad\text{ for }\;n\to\infty

Proof.
We work in momentum representation omitting the superscript
mom
. — Let
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. One easily verifies that the positive matrices
\text{{\it\small{M}}}_{0}(p):=|p|\pi_{0}(p)
and
\text{{\it\small{M}}}_{n}(p):=\frac{\epsilon(np)}{n}\pi^{+}(np)
satisfy

\text{{\it\small{M}}}_{0}(p)\leq\text{{\it\small{M}}}_{n}(p)+mI_{4},\quad\text{{\it\small{M}}}_{n}(p)\leq\text{{\it\small{M}}}_{0}(p)+mI_{4}

Now note that
|P|Q
is the matrix multiplication operator by
\text{{\it\small{M}}}_{0}
. Since
HP^{+}
is the matrix multiplication operator by
\epsilon(\cdot)\pi^{+}
, it follows that

\frac{1}{n}D_{n}HP^{+}D_{n}^{-1}
is the matrix multiplication operator by
\text{{\it\small{M}}}_{n}
. The first part of the assertion follows. — As to the second part,
\frac{1}{n}\langle\varphi_{n},H\varphi_{n}\rangle=\frac{1}{n}\langle D_{n}\varphi_{n},D_{n}H\varphi_{n}\rangle=||P^{+}_{n}\phi||^{-2}\int\frac{\epsilon(np)}{n}\big|\pi^{+}(np)\phi(p)\big|^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}p
, which by dominated convergence tends to
||Q\phi||^{-2}\int|p|\big|\pi_{0}(p)\phi(p)\big|^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}p=||Q\phi||^{-2}\langle Q\phi,|P|Q\phi\rangle
. Finally,
\langle Q\,\phi,|P|Q\,\phi\rangle>0
as
|P|Q\,\phi\neq 0
.∎

As to the relevance of (
18.1
) one notes that the assumption
Q\,\phi\in\operatorname{dom}(|P|)\setminus\{0\}
is satisfied by all
\phi=(I+|P|)^{-1}\phi^{\prime}
with
Q\phi^{\prime}\neq 0
, since
Q
commutes with
|P|
.
Presumably, the result
\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle\varphi_{n},H\varphi_{n}\rangle=\infty
obtained in (
18.1
) holds for every point-localized sequence
(\varphi_{n})
of electron states.

18.2.
Point-localized sequences of electron states

As to the behavior of point-localized sequences of electron states with respect to
T^{e}
under Poincaré transformations we recall the general result (
8.1
).
Certain point-localized sequences of electron states (see Eq. (
18.6
) below) have already been constructed and studied in great detail in
[
63
]
1999 and
[
7
]
2005. The concept is further formalized in
[
64
]
2001. Essentially this section is taken from
[
5
, sec. I]
.

For the construction of point-localized sequences of electron states we provide

h(p)=\epsilon(p)\;y(p)^{-1}\,\gamma_{5}\,y(p),\quad y(p):=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon(p)}{\epsilon(p)+m}}(I_{4}+\gamma_{5}\beta)\left(\beta+\frac{1}{\epsilon(p)}h(p)\right)

(18.2)

with
\gamma_{5}=-\operatorname{i}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}
. Note that
\gamma_{5}
equals
\operatorname{diag}(I_{2},-I_{2})
in the Weyl representation. Moreover,
y(p)
is self-adjoint unitary, whence
y(p)^{-1}=y(p)
. Hence, if
c(p)\in\mathbb{C}^{4}
with
||c(p)||=1
and
c(p)=\gamma_{5}c(p)
, then
u(p):=y(p)^{-1}c(p)
satisfies

h(p)u(p)=\epsilon(p)u(p)
and
\pi^{+}(p)u(p)=u(p)
.

Now, in addition, let
c(p)
be constant or, more generally, choose
c(p),\;p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
c_{0}(p):=\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}c(\lambda p)
exists and satisfies
c_{0}(\lambda p)=c_{0}(p)
for
\lambda>0
. Then

u(\lambda p)\to u_{0}(p):=\frac{1}{2}(I_{4}+\gamma_{5}\beta)\left(\beta+\frac{1}{|p|}\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}p_{k}\right)\,c_{0}(p)

(18.3)

and

\pi_{0}(p)u_{0}(p)=u_{0}(p),\quad u_{0}(\lambda p)=u_{0}(p),\,\lambda>0

(18.4)

(90) Example.

The formula for
(\varphi_{n})
in (
18.1
) yields rather general point-localized sequences of electron states. Multiplying
\varphi_{n}
in momentum representation by
\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}
, one obtains a sequence localized at
b
.
For some concrete examples in momentum representation
we specialize
\phi:=f\,u_{0}
with
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
||f||_{2}=1
and
u_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}^{4}
measurable, bounded, and

\pi_{0}(p)\,u_{0}(p)=0
not a.e. The latter guarantees
Q^{mom}\phi\neq 0
.

The simplest choice is
u_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{4}\setminus\{0\}
constant. Another useful choice is a dilation invariant unit eigenvector
u_{0}(p)
of

\pi_{0}(p)
(cf. Eq. (
18.4
)).
Then (
18.1
) yields, up to the normalizing constant, the localized sequence at
b

\varphi^{\prime}_{n}(p)=n^{-\frac{3}{2}}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}f(\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{n}$}}p)\,\pi^{+}(p)\,u_{0}(p)

(18.5)

Closely related to Eq. (
18.5
) is the amplitude for a wave packet Eq. (
18.6
). Following
[
63
, Eq. (14)]
,
[
7
, Eq. (2.1)]
,
[
64
, Eq. (29)]
we consider

\varphi_{n}(p):=n^{-\frac{3}{2}}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}f(\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{n}$}}p)\,u(p)

(18.6)

with
u(p)
a unit eigenvector of
\pi^{+}(p)
.
\varphi_{n}
in Eq. (
18.6
) is already normalized. In the corollary below it is supposed that the dilational limit
u_{0}(p):=\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}u(\lambda p)
exists. Obviously this is the case if
u(p):=y(p)^{-1}c
with
y(p)
in Eq. (
18.2
) and
c\in\mathbb{C}^{4}
a unit vector with
c=\gamma_{5}c
, see Eq. (
18.4
).

(91) Corollary.
The amplitude for a wave packet
(\varphi_{n})
in
Eq. (
18.6
)
is a sequence of electron states localized at
b
if the dilational limit
u_{0}(p):=\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}u(\lambda p)
exists.

Proof.

Clearly
u_{0}(p)
is a dilation invariant unit eigenvector of
\pi_{0}(p)
. So

(\varphi_{n})
equals asymptotically
(\varphi^{\prime}_{n})
in Eq. (
18.5
), since obviously
||\varphi_{n}-\varphi^{\prime}_{n}||=||D_{n}\varphi_{n}-D_{n}\varphi^{\prime}_{n}||\to 0
. The result follows. ∎

Section 4 of
[
63
]
deals with a proof of a weaker result than this for Schwartz functions
f
in Eq. (
18.6
). The choice
f(p):=\pi^{-3/4}\operatorname{e}^{-|p|^{2}/2}
is discussed in detail in
[

,

,
[

]
.

(92) Example.
In view of Hegerfeldt’s result reported in sec.
8.3

it is worth verifying that there are sequences
(\varphi_{n})
of electron states localized at
0
, for which
the spatial probability decays exponentially

\langle\varphi_{n},T^{e}(\{|x|>r\})\,\varphi_{n}\rangle\in\mathcal{O}(\operatorname{e}^{-Kr}),\quad r\to\infty

(18.7)

for
K<2m
(
c=\hbar=1
).
For the following example, inspired by
[
65
, sec. 2]
, choose in (
18.2
)
u_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{4}\setminus\{0\}
constant and
f:=\mathcal{F}g
with
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\setminus\{0\}
satisfying
\operatorname{e}^{b|\cdot|}g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
b\in[0,a[
for some
a>0
. E.g.
g(x):=\sqrt{\frac{a^{3}}{\pi}}\operatorname{e}^{-a|x|}
and
f(p)=\frac{\sqrt{8a^{5}}}{\pi}\big(a^{2}+|p|^{2}\big)^{-2}
. By (
18.1
) the sequence

\varphi_{n}(p)=C_{n}f(\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{n}$}}p)\,\pi^{+}(p)\,u_{0}

(18.8)

with
C_{n}
a normalizing constant,
is localized at
0
. The claim is that, if
n\geq\frac{m}{a}
, then
\varphi_{n}
satisfies Eq. (
18.7
) for all
K<2m
.

Proof.
For the proof a result on Fourier transformation (see e.g.
[
66
, Theorem IX.13]
) is used. By this result,
f
has an analytic continuation to the strip
\{w\in\mathbb{C}^{3}:|\operatorname{Im}w|<a\}

such that
f_{q}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
f_{q}(p):=f(p+\operatorname{i}q)
, for
q\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|q|<a
with
\sup_{|q|\leq b}||f_{q}||<\infty
for every
b\in[0,a[
. Then, for
n\geq\frac{m}{a}
, the strip of analyticity of
p\to f(\frac{1}{n}p)
has the half-width
na\geq m
. Thus, because of the factor
\frac{1}{\epsilon(p)}
present in

\pi^{+}(p)
,
the strip of analyticity for every component of
\varphi_{n}
is limited to

\{w\in\mathbb{C}^{3}:|\operatorname{Im}w|<m\}
. This is the crucial point. The remaining premises in
[
66
, Theorem IX.13]
) on
\varphi_{n}
concerning square-integrability are easily checked. Put
\psi_{n}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi_{n}
.
It follows
\operatorname{e}^{c|\cdot|}\psi_{n}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for all
c\in[0,m[
. As a consequence
\langle\varphi_{n},T^{e\,mom}(\{|x|>r\})\,\varphi_{n}\rangle=\langle\psi_{n},E^{\textsc{d}\,pos}(\{|x|>r\})\,\psi_{n}\rangle=\int_{\{|x|>r\}}|\psi_{n}(x)|^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}x=\int_{\{|x|>r\}}\operatorname{e}^{-2c|x|}|\operatorname{e}^{c|x|}\psi_{n}(x)|^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}x\leq\operatorname{e}^{-2cr}\int_{\{|x|>r\}}|\operatorname{e}^{c|x|}\psi_{n}(x)|^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}x\leq||\operatorname{e}^{c|\cdot|}\psi_{n}||^{2}\operatorname{e}^{-2cr}
.
\Box

There are as well point-localized sequences of electron states with only power-like decay of the spatial probability, as e.g.,
\langle\varphi_{n},T^{e\,mom}(\{|x|>r\})\,\varphi_{n}\rangle\in\Theta(r^{-5})
for
\varphi_{n}(p)=C_{n}\operatorname{e}^{-|p|/n}\pi^{+}(p)\,u_{0}
. What is more, this example suggests, contrary to some literature, that the exponential decay of the spatial probability is
not
typical. The electron wave functions with non-exponentially decaying tails in the position representation of the Dirac system are even predominant, at least from a mathematical point of view. The reason is that exponential decay requires analyticity properties of the electron wave function in momentum representation, like in the example on Eq. (
18.7
). This is clearly explained in
[
65
, sec. 2]
.

Summarizing, the formalism of POL yields an arbitrarily good localization obeying causality. More precisely,
T^{e}
is separated so that there are sequences of electron states localized at any point, and
T^{e}
is causal in that the probability of localization in the region of influence does not decrease. Moreover,
good localization is not related to the spatial decay of the spatial probability. But generally, due to causality, massive relativistic particles have tails of the spatial probability which do not
decay faster than
\operatorname{e}^{-4\pi r/\lambda_{C}}
. The preparation of a point-localized sequence of electron states requires unlimited energy as the expectation values of the energy in electron states of a point-localized sequence tend to infinity.

19.
Negative Energy States

Let
\Delta
be a closed region
\neq\mathbb{R}^{3}
. As we know the localization of an electron in
\Delta
is not possible. For every electron state
\psi
the probability of localization
\langle\psi,T^{e}(\Delta)\psi\rangle
is less than
1
. The reason one imagines is that attempting the localization causes such a large uncertainty in energy that
pair-production occurs. Therefore, as it stands, a one-particle theory cannot furnish a complete description. We share this view. At the same time we think that PO-localization takes account of this situation in an effective manner. We will argue that applying the localization operator
E(\Delta)
of a causal system to a particle state does not create a pure state but the mixed state (
19.1
) of particle and antiparticle states. As such their energies are positive for stability. Thus there is no negative energy problem. On the other hand one recalls that these states continue to be not localized in
\Delta
, despite the attempt in this regard. But we will see (
19
) that there a particle state is created with an increased probability of localization in
\Delta
with respect to the original one.

This scenario appears quite reasonable. Of course its validity has to be confirmed at concrete situations. For instance the situation underlying the Klein paradox could be examined as a device for the localization in a half-space. For this purpose one can employ the thorough investigations on the former available in the literature (see
[
67
]
and its references). —
In the following we simply write
E,T
for
E^{\textsc{d}},T^{e}
and
P
for
P^{+}
. We follow
[
5
, sec. J]
.

Let the electron be in the state
\varphi_{1}
, consider a region
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and suppose
T(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\neq 0
.
Since
\varphi_{1}
is a state of the Dirac system,
\langle\varphi_{1},E(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\rangle
is the probability to get an affirmative answer at the position measurement to the question whether the Dirac system is in the region
\Delta
. Recall that

\langle\varphi_{1},T(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\rangle=\langle\varphi_{1},E(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\rangle
as
P\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{1}
. Hence

\langle\varphi_{1},T(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\rangle
is the probability
\omega_{1}>0
of localization in
\Delta
of the electron in the state
\varphi_{1}
. It is also the probability that after the measurement the Dirac system is in the state

\phi_{1}:=\parallel\!\!E(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{-1}E(\Delta)\varphi_{1}

This state is regarded to be a non-observable virtual superposition
\phi_{1}=P\phi_{1}+\bar{P}\phi_{1}
,
\bar{P}:=I-P
, of an electron and a positron state. Positrons are described by “negative energy” states since, being antiparticles, they travel “backwards” in time. More precisely one has

(93) Lemma.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{m,\eta}
for
m>0
,
\eta=\pm
denote the mass shell
\{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:p_{0}=\eta\epsilon(p)\}
. Let
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}/2
. Then the antiunitary operator
\mathcal{C}:L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{m,+},\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{m,-},\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})
,
(\mathcal{C}F)(\mathfrak{p}):=D^{(j)}(\operatorname{i}\sigma_{2})\overline{F(-\mathfrak{p}})
satisfies

U^{m,j,-}=\mathcal{C}\,U^{m,j,+}\,\mathcal{C}^{-1}

where
\big(U^{m,j,\eta}(\mathfrak{a},A)F\big)(\mathfrak{p})=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}D^{(j)}\big(Q(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}A\,Q(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})\big)\,F(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})
is the irreducible massive representation
[m,j,\eta]
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
.
\mathcal{C}
is uniquely determined up to a constant phase.

Proof.
Note that
\operatorname{i}\sigma_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)
satisfies
\overline{B}=(\operatorname{i}\sigma_{2})\,B\,(\operatorname{i}\sigma_{2})^{-1}
for
B\in SU(2)
. Further recall that
U^{m,j,\eta}
is irreducible. Then the assertion is easily verified.∎

So, by (
19
), the representation
[m,j,-]
is antiunitarily equivalent to
[m,j,+]
. Hence, like
[m,j,+]
, it also represents a particle with mass
m
and spin
j
. For
stability
its energy has to be positive, too.

(94) Interpretation.

[m,j,+]
represents a particle with
positive energy
, mass
m>0
, and spin
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}/2
, and

[m,j,-]
represents its antiparticle, which has
positive energy
, too, the same mass
m
and spin
j
.

At this stage one need not worry about the conservation of charge as it concerns the compound system consisting of the Dirac system and the measuring apparatus. Accordingly, after the measurement the electron state

\varphi_{2}:=\parallel\!\!P\phi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{-1}P\phi_{1}=\parallel\!\!T(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{-1}T(\Delta)\varphi_{1}

occurs with probability
\sigma_{2}
given by the product
\langle\varphi_{1},T(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\rangle\parallel\!\!P\phi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{2}=\parallel\!\!T(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{2}
.

As
I-E(\Delta)=E(\Delta^{\prime})
with
\Delta^{\prime}:=\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Delta
, one has at the same time the information that there is the electron state
\varphi_{2}^{\prime}:=\parallel\!\!T(\Delta^{\prime})\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{-1}T(\Delta^{\prime})\varphi_{1}
with probability
\sigma_{2}^{\prime}:=\parallel\!\!T(\Delta^{\prime})\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{2}
. Thus the probability of the occurrence of an electron after the position measurement is

\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{2}^{\prime}=1-2\langle\varphi_{1},T(\Delta)T(\Delta^{\prime})\varphi_{1}\rangle

The state
\phi_{1}
also gives rise to the positron state
\bar{\varphi}_{2}=\parallel\!\!\bar{P}E(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{-1}\bar{P}E(\Delta)\varphi_{1}
with probability
\bar{\sigma}_{2}=\parallel\!\!\bar{P}E(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{2}
. Also, there is the positron state
\bar{\varphi}^{\prime}_{2}=\parallel\!\!\bar{P}E(\Delta^{\prime})\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{-1}\bar{P}E(\Delta^{\prime})\varphi_{1}
with probability
\bar{\sigma}^{\prime}_{2}=\parallel\!\!\bar{P}E(\Delta^{\prime})\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{2}
. One easily checks that
\bar{\sigma}_{2}+\bar{\sigma}^{\prime}_{2}=2\langle\varphi_{1},T(\Delta)T(\Delta^{\prime})\varphi_{1}\rangle=1-\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
.

These considerations suggest that the influence of the apparatus represented by
E(\Delta)
does not create eigenstates of
E(\Delta)
as being unobservable for a super-selection rule, but a mixed state given by the density operator

W:=PEP_{\varphi_{1}}EP+PE^{\prime}P_{\varphi_{1}}E^{\prime}P+\bar{P}EP_{\varphi_{1}}E\bar{P}+\bar{P}E^{\prime}P_{\varphi_{1}}E^{\prime}\bar{P}

(19.1)

Here, for short,
E=E(\Delta)
,
E^{\prime}=E(\Delta^{\prime})
, and
P_{\varphi_{1}}
the projection on the subspace
\mathbb{C}\varphi_{1}
. Clearly
W\geq 0
and, using the formula
QP_{\phi}Q=\parallel\!\!Q\phi\!\!\parallel^{2}\parallel\!\!\phi\!\!\parallel^{-2}P_{Q\phi}
for any projection
Q
, one easily verifies
\operatorname{tr}(W)=1
. Note that the sum in Eq. (
19.1
) is not orthogonal.

(95) Theorem.
One has
\frac{1}{2}\leq\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\leq 1
. If
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
contain each an open ball up to a Lebesgue null set, then
\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{2}^{\prime}<1
.

Proof.
By the Spectral Theorem there is a probability measure
\mu
on
[0,1]
such that
\langle\varphi_{1},T(\Delta)^{k}\varphi_{1}\rangle=\int t^{k}\operatorname{d}\mu(t)
for
k\geq 0
.
Hence
\langle\varphi_{1},T(\Delta)T(\Delta^{\prime})\varphi_{1}\rangle=\int(t-t^{2})\operatorname{d}\mu(t)\leq\int\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{d}\mu=\frac{1}{4}
. Clearly
\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\leq 1
. —
As to the second part of the assertion, one notes first that, due to the translational covariance,
T
vanishes at the Lebesgue null sets. Hence without restriction
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
have a non-empty interior. Then it follows from (
4
)
that the kernels of
T(\Delta)
and
T(\Delta^{\prime})
are trivial, whence the assertion.∎

In conclusion, one infers that the missing probability
1-\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
for the occurrence of an electron state is due to a possible positron state. As to the interpretation of this result, we imagine that at the measurement of the position of the electron in
\Delta
there is a probability not greater than
\frac{1}{2}
(and arbitrarily small for a good localized electron as (
19
) will show) that a positron state is created by the energy released
at the separation of the measuring apparatus from the Dirac system.

We ask now if the probability of localization
\omega_{1}
increases by performing repeatedly the position measurement. One finds for
n\in\mathbb{N}

\omega_{n}=\langle\varphi_{n},T(\Delta)\varphi_{n}\rangle=\frac{\langle\varphi_{1},T(\Delta)^{2n-1}\varphi_{1}\rangle}{\langle\varphi_{1},T(\Delta)^{2n-2}\varphi_{1}\rangle}

where the electron state
\varphi_{n}\,=\,\parallel\!\!T(\Delta)^{n-1}\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{-1}T(\Delta)^{n-1}\varphi_{1}
occurs with probability
\sigma_{n}=

\parallel\!\!T(\Delta)^{n-1}\varphi_{1}\!\!\parallel^{2}
.

(96) Theorem.

\omega_{n}\nearrow\omega:=\sup_{m}\omega_{m}
and
\sigma_{n}\searrow\sigma:=\inf_{m}\sigma_{m}
. Moreover,
\sigma>0
if and only if

\varphi_{1}
is not orthogonal to the kernel of
I-T(\Delta)
. If
\sigma>0
than
\omega=1
.

Proof.
Let
\mu
be the measure introduced in the proof of (
19
). Set
\gamma_{k}:=\int t^{k}\operatorname{d}\mu(t)
. Then
\sigma_{n}=\gamma_{2n-2}
,
\omega_{n}=\gamma_{2n-1}/\gamma_{2n-2}
. Since
t^{k}\searrow 0
as
k\to\infty
for
t\in[0,1[
, by dominated convergence
\gamma_{k}\searrow\mu(\{1\})=\langle\varphi_{1},P_{1}\varphi_{1}\rangle
, where
P_{1}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the kernel of
I-T(\Delta)
. The assertions on
(\sigma_{n})
follow.

Now we prove
\gamma_{k-1}\gamma_{k+1}-\gamma_{k}\gamma_{k}\geq 0
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, which obviously implies monotony
\omega_{n}\leq\omega_{n+1}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
m:=\mu\otimes\mu
be the product measure on
[0,1]^{2}
. Then
0\leq\int(t-s)^{2}(st)^{k-1}\operatorname{d}m(s,t)=\int(s^{k-1}t^{k+1}+s^{k+1}t^{k-1}-2s^{k}t^{k})\operatorname{d}m(s,t)=\gamma_{k-1}\gamma_{k+1}+\gamma_{k+1}\gamma_{k-1}-2\gamma_{k}\gamma_{k}
. ∎

We add

(97) Lemma.

\omega>0
,
\mu(]\omega,1])=0
and
\mu(]t,1])>0
for
t<\omega
.

Proof.
Set
\mu_{k}:=\rho_{k}\mu
with the density
\rho_{k}(t):=\gamma_{k}^{-1}t^{k}
. Then
\mu_{k}
is a probability measure on
[0,1]
and
\omega_{n}=\int t\operatorname{d}\mu_{2n-2}(t)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Let
H:=\bigcup\big\{\,]a,1]:a\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q},\mu(]a,1])=0\big\}
. Obviously there is
b\in[0,1]
such that
H=]b,1]
, and
\mu(H)=0
. One has
b>0
since otherwise
\mu=\delta_{0}
contradicting
T(\Delta)\varphi_{1}\neq 0
. The claim is
\omega=b
.

Let
0\leq c<c^{\prime}<b
with
c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Then
\mu_{k}([0,c])=\gamma_{k}^{-1}\int_{[0,c]}t^{k}\operatorname{d}\mu(t)\leq\gamma_{k}^{-1}c^{k}\mu([0.c])
, and
\gamma_{k}c^{-k}=\int(\frac{t}{c})^{k}\operatorname{d}\mu(t)\geq\int_{]c^{\prime},1]}(\frac{t}{c})^{k}\operatorname{d}\mu(t)\geq(\frac{c^{\prime}}{c})^{k}\mu(]c^{\prime},1])\to\infty
for
k\to\infty
since
\mu(]c^{\prime},1])>0
by definition of
H
.
Hence
\mu_{k}([0,c])\to 0
for
k\to\infty
.
Then
c-c\,\mu_{k}([0,c])=c\,\mu_{k}(]c,1])\leq\int t\operatorname{d}\mu_{k}(t)=\int_{[0,c]}\cdots+\int_{]c,1]}\cdots\leq c\,\mu_{k}([0,c])+b\,\mu_{k}(]c,1])=b+(c-b)\mu_{k}([0,c])
implies
c\leq\liminf_{k}\int t\operatorname{d}\mu_{k}(t)\leq\limsup_{k}\int t\operatorname{d}\mu_{k}(t)\leq b
for all
c<b
. Hence
\lim_{n}\omega_{n}=b
. ∎

So, indeed, according to (
19
) one gets the localization improved by the position measurements. But, at the same time, the observation of the localized states becomes seldom as the probabilities
\sigma_{n}
in general tend to zero. Moreover (
19
) shows that, depending on the initial state
\varphi_{1}
,
\lim_{n}\omega_{n}
may be less than
1
even if
\parallel\!\!T(\Delta)\!\!\parallel=1
. Hence the repeated measurement of the position in general is not suited to prepare a point-localized sequence of states. On the other hand, as shown in the following theorem, the measurement of the position for electron states of a point-localized sequence generates positron states only with vanishing probability.

(98) Theorem.
Let
(\varphi_{n})
be a sequence of electron states localized at
b\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then there is a sequence of Dirac states
\phi_{m}
satisfying
E(B)\phi_{m}=\phi_{m}
up to finitely many
m
for every ball
B
around
b
and such that
(I-P)\phi_{m}\to 0,\;m\to\infty
.

Proof.
Fix the ball
B^{m}
with radius
1/m
. As
P\varphi_{n}=\varphi_{n}
,
\parallel\!\!E(B^{m})\varphi_{n}\!\!\parallel\geq\parallel\!\!PE(B^{m})P\varphi_{n}\!\!\parallel=||T(B^{m})\varphi_{n}||\to 1
, whence
C^{m}_{n}:=

||E(B^{m})\varphi_{n}||>0
up to finitely many
n
. Put
\phi^{m}_{n}:=(1/C^{m}_{n})E(B^{m})\varphi_{n}
. Then
C^{m}_{n}||P\phi^{m}_{n}||=||PE(B^{m})P\varphi_{n}||=||T(B^{m})\varphi_{n}||\to 1
, whence
\parallel\!\!P\phi^{m}_{n}\!\!\parallel\to 1
since
C^{m}_{n}\leq 1
. Hence
\parallel\!\!(I-P)\phi^{m}_{n}\!\!\parallel^{2}=1-\parallel\!\!P\phi^{m}_{n}\!\!\parallel^{2}\to 0
for
n\to\infty
. This holds for every
m
. Now choose a subsequence
n_{m}
such that
1-\parallel\!\!P\phi_{m}\!\!\parallel^{2}<\frac{1}{m}
for
\phi_{m}:=\phi^{m}_{n_{m}}
.∎

Hence according to (
19
) an arbitrarily small negative energy component may suffice to localize the Dirac system in a bounded region.
We examine more closely the decomposition into positive energy and negative energy part of a Dirac state
\phi
. Let us say that
the spatial probability
decays fast
if
\langle\phi,E(\{|x|>r\})\phi\rangle\in\mathcal{O}(\operatorname{e}^{-Kr})
as
r\to\infty
for some
K>2m
.

(99) Lemma.
Let
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
be Dirac states with fast decaying spatial probabilities. If
P\phi=P\phi^{\prime}
then
\phi=\phi^{\prime}
holds.

Proof.
Check the estimate
\langle\phi-\phi^{\prime},E(\{|x|>r\})\,(\phi-\phi^{\prime})\rangle\leq 2\langle\phi,E(\{|x|>r\})\,\phi\rangle+2\langle\phi^{\prime},E(\{|x|>r\})\,\phi^{\prime}\rangle
. Assume now
\phi\neq\phi^{\prime}
. Let
\chi
denote the normalized
\phi-\phi^{\prime}
. Then
\chi
has a fast decaying spatial probability.
By assumption
\chi
is a negative energy state. This contradicts the result by Hegerfeldt reported in sec.
8.3
. ∎

In particular, the bounded localized Dirac states
\phi
are uniquely determined by their positive energy components
P\phi
.
Since, due to causality (
7.2
)(c), the dense subspace of these states
\phi
is invariant under
W^{\textsc{d}}
and since the subspaces of positive and negative energy are also invariant,
W^{\textsc{d}}
is already determined by its action on the subspace of positive energy.

20.
Dependence on positive mass and the case of zero mass

The results on the Dirac system are equally
valid for every mass
m>0
. They depend on the mass due to the time evolution
t\mapsto W^{\textsc{d}\,m}(t)=\operatorname{exp}(itH)
with the Dirac Hamiltonian
H=\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}P_{k}+\beta m
. The superscript
m
is introduced here in order to remind of this dependence. It is worthwhile to reflect a moment about the dependence on
m>0
.

By means of the dilations
D_{\lambda}
,
\lambda>0
(see Eq. (
13.7
)) one establishes the following relationship between the time evolutions for different masses

W^{\textsc{d}\,\lambda m}(t)=D_{\lambda}^{-1}W^{\textsc{d}\,m}(\lambda t)D_{\lambda}

The question is about the dependence of
e(\psi_{t})=e(W^{\textsc{d}m}(t)\psi)
on
m>0
. Using the relation
E^{\textsc{d}}(\lambda\Delta)=D_{\lambda}E^{\textsc{d}}(\Delta)D_{\lambda}^{-1}

one finds
e(D^{pos}_{\lambda}\psi)=\lambda e(\psi)
and

t^{\lambda m}_{e}(\psi)=\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{\lambda}$}}t^{m}_{e}(D^{pos}_{\lambda}\psi)

Hence the maximum of
e(\psi_{t})
and its position
t^{m}_{e}(\psi)
may depend on
m>0
.
Independent of
m>0
are
\min\{e(\psi_{t}),e(\psi_{-t})\}
, since equal to
e(\psi)-|t|
by (
15.2
), and the upper bound
e(\psi_{t})\leq-2\,\overline{e}(\psi)-e(\psi)-|t|
(
15.2
). This subject would merit more analysis.

In view of the study of the causal Weyl systems in sec.
21
the question is which results in sec.
15
,
17
persist for mass zero. Indeed, for
m=0
, the Dirac Hamiltonian
H
becomes the orthogonal sum
\bigoplus_{\chi=\pm}H^{\chi}
of the Weyl Hamiltonians so that by (
21.1
) and Eq. (
14
) the Dirac system

(W^{\textsc{d}\,m},E^{\textsc{d}})
is reduced to the orthogonal sum of the Weyl systems, i.e.,

(W^{\textsc{d}\,0},E^{\textsc{d}})=\bigoplus_{\chi=\pm}(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})

By (
21.1
) the latter is still a causal system. Note that also the time reversal operator
\mathcal{T}
(
15.3
) is the orthogonal sum of the respective operators for the Weyl systems (see before sec.
21.1
).

Therefore and since (
15.1
) holds also for
\epsilon(z)^{2}=z^{2}
, as a consequence, all results in sec.
15.2
hold, by the same proofs, also for the Weyl systems.

The results in sec.
16
about the long term behavior of the probability of localization of Dirac states are not equally valid for Weyl states. In sec.
22
they are replaced with results, which are stronger or necessarily weaker than the analogous ones for Dirac states.

However, it is important that (
22
) holds which is the analogous result (
16
) for Dirac states. As a consequence, all results for Dirac states in sec.
17
equally hold true for Weyl states.

21.
The Weyl Systems

We study now the prominent
massless
causal systems (
29.3
), the Weyl systems
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
. They are derived in (
29.3
). In momentum representation on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2})
they read

\big(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,mom}(t,b,A)\varphi\big)(p)=\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\eta|p|}\pi^{\chi\eta}(p)s^{\chi}(A)^{*-1}\varphi(q^{\eta}),\quad E^{\textsc{w}mom}=\mathcal{F}E^{can}\mathcal{F}^{-1}

(21.1)

where
s^{+}(A)=A
,
s^{-}(A)=A^{*-1}
,

h^{\chi}(p)=\chi(\sigma_{1}p_{1}+\sigma_{2}p_{2}+\sigma_{3}p_{3})
and

\pi^{\chi\eta}(p)=\frac{1}{2}\big(I_{2}+\frac{\eta}{|p|}h^{\chi}(p)\big)
. For the energy and the cone representation of the Weyl systems see (
30.5
).

They may turn out to be relevant for the description of the recently discovered
Weyl fermions

[
34
]
,
[
35
]
. By Eq. (
29.12
) the Weyl representation
W^{\textsc{w}\chi}
is the orthogonal sum

[0,\chi\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{2}$}},\chi]\oplus[0,\chi\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{2}$}},-\chi]

The case
\chi=+
means a massless fermion with helicity
+\frac{1}{2}
, which is called the right-handed Weyl fermion as its spin points in the direction of its motion. It is
accompanied by its left-handed antiparticle. There is also a left-handed Weyl fermion accompanied by its right-handed antiparticle in the case
\chi=-
. We like to refer to the interpretation (
29.2
). Note that, in accordance with experimental evidence, other combinations of a Weyl fermion with its antiparticle are excluded by the requirement of a causal localization, see (
29.1
). This is a further fact in favor of causal localization.

(100) POL for Weyl particles.

E^{\textsc{w}}
admits dilational covariance quite as
E^{\textsc{d}}
due to
D^{pos}_{\lambda}
in Eq. (
13.7
).
Since
\pi^{\chi\eta}(\lambda p)=\pi^{\chi\eta}(p)
for
\lambda>0
the projection
P^{\chi\eta}
onto the state space of the Weyl particle
[0,\chi/2,\eta]
commutes with
D_{\lambda}
. Let
T^{\chi\eta}
denote the trace of
E^{\textsc{w}}
by
P^{\chi\eta}
, cf. (
18.1
). Obviously the results in sec.
18.1
on the electron carry over on the Weyl particle. In particular,
T^{\chi\eta}
is separated and the formula analogous to that in (
18.1
) holds, which here results to be particularly simple, i.e.,
\varphi_{n}:=D_{n}^{-1}\varphi
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) for every state
\varphi
in the range of
P^{\chi\eta}
. Finally
recall that
E^{\textsc{w}}
extends on
\mathfrak{S}
by Poincaré covariance (
6.2
). So
T^{\chi\eta}
is defined on
\mathfrak{S}
and, by (
9
) and (
21.1
) below,
T^{\chi\eta}
is causal.

Like (
13.4
) for the Dirac system one has
N=\textnormal{\small{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\{X^{\textsc{w}},H\}
. Note that,
as the proof of (
29.3
) shows, for a massless SCT
(W_{E},E)
the booster
N
satisfies

N=\textrm{\scriptsize{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\{X,H\}

if and only if
(W_{E},E)
is unitarily equivalent to the orthogonal sum for
\chi\in\{+,-\}
of multiples of
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
.

Since time reversal operator
\mathcal{T}

is used in the proof of some results, let us note that for the Weyl systems it is given by
\mathcal{T}^{pos}\psi=-\sigma_{2}\overline{\psi}
(cf. (
15.3
)).

21.1.
Weyl systems are causal

Compare this section with sec.
14
. As to the notations recall (
14
) and put
H^{\chi pos}:=\chi\sum_{k}\sigma_{k}\,\frac{1}{\operatorname{i}}\partial_{k}
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
.

(101) Lemma.

In position space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2})
the Weyl representation
W^{\textsc{w}\chi}
reads

\big(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,pos}(g)\psi\big)(x)=s^{\chi}(A)\big(\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}y_{0}H^{\chi pos}}\psi\big)(y)

Proof.
By definition
W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,pos}\equiv\mathcal{F}^{-1}W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,mom}\mathcal{F}
with
W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,mom}
from Eq. (
21.1
).
Apply first
Eq. (
29.10
). Then
\big(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,mom}(g)\varphi\big)(p)=s^{\chi}(A)\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}(t,b)\cdot(\eta|p|,p)}(|q^{\eta}|/|p|)\pi^{\chi\eta}(q^{\eta})\varphi(q^{\eta})
. Now proceed as in the proof of (
14
).∎

As in the case of the Dirac representation (
14
) one introduces the time-dependent wave-function
\Psi
related to the position space wave-function
\psi
by
\Psi(t,x)=\big(\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}tH^{\chi pos}}\psi\big)(x)
.
Then the former is the solution of the initial-value problem for the
Weyl equation

\operatorname{i}\partial_{t}\Psi=H^{\chi pos}\Psi,\quad\Psi(0,\cdot)=\psi\text{ with }\psi\in\operatorname{dom}(H^{\chi pos})

(21.2)

See also

[
68
, (5.234), (5.235)]
. Under Poincaré transformations
g
it transforms like

\Psi_{g}(\mathfrak{x})=s^{\chi}(A)\,\Psi(g^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x})

for
\mathfrak{x}=(t,x)
.

(102) Corollary.

(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
are causal systems
(
9
)
.

Proof.
By (
29.3
)
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
is an SCT. Here we may infer this also by
[
5
, Theorem 10 (b)]
from the fact that
z\mapsto\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th^{\chi}(z)}
is exponentially bounded
(cf. after Eq. (
13.6
)). The assertion follows from (
21.1
) imitating the proof for (
14
).∎

So causality

W^{\textsc{w}\chi}(g)\,E^{\textsc{w}}(\Gamma)\,W^{\textsc{w}\chi}(g)^{-1}\,\leq\,E^{\textsc{w}}(\Gamma_{g})

(21.3)

(
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
,
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{S}
) holds. See (
7.2
) for equivalent formulations of causality.

(103) Summary.

As already checked in sec.
20
, the results on the propagation of the border of a Dirac wave function in sec.
15.2
hold equally, by the same proofs, for Weyl wave functions. In particular, for a Weyl state
\psi
localized in a bounded region one has (cf. (
15.2
))

e\big(W^{\textsc{w}\chi}(t)\psi\big)=e(\psi)+|t_{e}^{\chi}|-|t-t_{e}^{\chi}|

(21.4)

Furthermore, still examined in sec.
20
, the results in sec.
17
regarding the effect of a boost on the localization stay valid for the Weyl systems. The proofs are the same except for using (
22
), which it is harder to prove than the analog (
16
) for the Dirac system. This is due to a remarkable difference between the long term behavior of the probability of localization of the Dirac system and the Weyl systems, compare sec.
16
with sec.
22
. The key result in this context is (
22
) providing a rather explicit description of the time evolution of the radially symmetric Weyl wave functions.
So an important result is

W(\varsigma\alpha)E(\{xe\leq\alpha\})W(\varsigma\alpha)^{-1}E(\{xe\geq 0\})=\lim_{\varsigma\rho\to\infty}W(A_{\rho e})(\{0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\,\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\})W(A_{\rho e})^{-1}

(21.5)

for
W=W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E=E^{\textsc{w}}
(cf. (
17
)), which relates the effects on localization of boost and time evolution. The left hand side tends to
E(\{xe\geq 0\})
for
\alpha\to\infty
, which is one of the completeness results, see (
22
) and the comments on it.
Hence last not least
the Lorentz contraction holds also for Weyl states
\psi
(cf. (
17.1
)), i.e., for every
\delta>0
one has

\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}\big(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:-\delta\leq xe\leq\delta\}\big)\,W^{\textsc{w}\chi}(A_{\rho e})\psi\!\!\parallel\,\to 1,\;\;|\rho|\to\infty

(21.6)

22.
Long-term behavior of the probability of localization of Weyl states

Let
\psi_{t}:=W^{\textsc{w}\chi}(t)\psi
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
denote the (right- respectively left-handed) Weyl state
\psi
evolved in time.
As for the Dirac system in sec.
16
we will study the behavior of the probability of localization for large
|t|
. The following result (
22
) is stronger than the analogous (
16
) for the Dirac system. (Occasionally we will omit the superscript
pos
.)

(104) Theorem.
Let
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2})
be a Weyl state. Then for every
v\in]0,1[

E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{v|t|})\,\psi_{t}\to 0,\;\;|t|\to\infty

If
\mathcal{F}\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, then for every
N>0
there is a finite constant
C_{N}
such that
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{v|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq C_{N}(1+|t|)^{-N}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.
Suppose first
\varphi:=\mathcal{F}\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{C}^{2})
. For the estimation of the integral

(\psi^{\eta}_{t}(x))_{l}=(2\pi)^{-3/2}\int\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}(px+t\eta\chi\,|p|)}(\varphi^{\eta}(p))_{l}\,\operatorname{d}^{3}p

(22.1)

for
l=1,2
(cf. Eq. (
16.1
)) consider
\phi^{\eta\chi}(p):=(|x|+|t|)^{-1}\big(px-t\eta\chi\,|p|\big)
. Then
\nabla\phi^{\eta\chi}(p)=(|x|+|t|)^{-1}\big(x-\frac{t\eta\chi}{|p|}p\big)
and
|\nabla\phi^{\eta\chi}(p)|\geq(|x|+|t|)^{-1}(|t|-|x|)
for
p\neq 0
. Now suppose
|x|\leq v|t|
. Then
|\nabla\phi^{\eta\chi}(p)|\geq(v|t|+|t|)^{-1}(|t|-v|t|)=\frac{1-v}{1+v}>0
. This implies (cf.
[
28
, (1.209)]
) for
\eta,\chi\in\{+,-\}
,
l=1,2
, and for every
N>0
that there is a finite constant
A_{N}
with

\big|\big(\psi^{\eta}_{t}(x)\big)_{l}\big|\leq A_{N}(1+|x|+|t|)^{-N}\textrm{ \;if\; }|x|\leq v|t|

Hence
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{v|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\sum_{\eta}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{v|t|})\psi^{\eta}_{t}\!\!\parallel
and
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{v|t|})\psi^{\eta}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=\int_{B_{v|t|}}|\psi^{\eta}_{t}(x)|^{2}\operatorname{d}x^{3}=\sum_{l}\int_{B_{v|t|}}\big|\big(\psi^{\eta}_{t}(x)\big)_{l}\big|^{2}\operatorname{d}x^{3}\leq 8\pi A_{N}^{2}\int_{0}^{v|t|}(1+r+|t|)^{-2N}r^{2}\operatorname{d}r\leq 8\pi A_{N}^{2}(1+|t|)^{-2N}\int_{0}^{v|t|}r^{2}\operatorname{d}r\leq\frac{8\pi}{3}v^{3}A_{N}^{2}(1+|t|)^{-2N+3}
if
N>\frac{3}{2}
.
Hence
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{v|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq C_{N}(1+|t|)^{-N}
for
N>0
and

C_{N}:=(16\pi/3)^{\frac{1}{2}}A_{N+\frac{3}{2}}
.

Now consider a general Weyl state
\psi
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Set
\varphi:=\mathcal{F}\psi
and choose
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\},\mathbb{C}^{2})
with
\parallel\!\!\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
. Hence
\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
for
\psi^{\prime}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{\prime}
. Let
P_{t}:=E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{v|t|})
. By the foregoing result there is a constant
C_{1}
such that
\parallel\!\!P_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq C_{1}(1+|t|)^{-1}
for all
t
. Let
\tau:=2C_{1}/\varepsilon
. Then
\parallel\!\!P_{t}\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!P_{t}(\psi_{t}-\psi^{\prime}_{t})\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!P_{t}\psi^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq

\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+C_{1}(1+|t|)^{-1}\leq\varepsilon
for
|t|\geq\tau
.∎

So roughly speaking, in the long term the Weyl fermion tends to infinity with any subluminal velocity. This however does not imply that
E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\,\psi_{t}\to 0
as
|t|\to\infty
. The result (
22
)(b) also shows that in general neither the other extremum
E^{\textsc{w}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\,\psi_{t}\to 0
occurs. This is one of the main differences between Weyl systems and Dirac system (
16
).

A function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{n})
is called radially symmetric if
\psi(x)=g(|x|)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
g:[0,\infty[\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
is measurable and
jg
with the identity map
j(r):=r
is square integrable. Note that
\parallel\!\!\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}=4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{2}|g(r)|^{2}\operatorname{d}r
.

(105) Theorem.
Let
\psi=g(|\cdot|)
be a radially symmetric Weyl state in the position representation. Then for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\varsigma\in\{+,-\}
one has

\psi_{t}=A^{+}_{t}+A^{-}_{t}+R_{t}

with

•

A^{\varsigma}_{t}(x):=\pi^{\chi\varsigma}(\frac{1}{|x|}x)\frac{|x|+\varsigma t}{|x|}g\big(\big||x|+\varsigma t\big|\big)

and the remainder

•

R_{t}(x)=h^{\chi}(\frac{1}{|x|}x)\frac{1}{2|x|^{2}}\big(G\big(\big||x|+t\big|\big)-G\big(\big||x|-t\big|\big)\big)
with
G(r):=-\int_{0}^{r}\rho g(\rho)\operatorname{d}\rho

Furthermore,
A^{\varsigma}_{t},R_{t}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and

(a)

\langle A^{+}_{t}(x),A^{-}_{t}(x)\rangle=0

(b)

2\parallel\!\!A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=1-\varsigma\operatorname{sgn}(t)\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}
, in particular
A^{\varsigma}_{t}\to 0
for
\varsigma t\to\infty

(c)

\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}
if
-\varsigma t\geq 0

(d)

R_{t}\to 0
for
|t|\to\infty

and finally

(e)

\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}\to\frac{1}{2}
for
|t|\to\infty

Proof.
Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the Fourier-sine transformation on
L^{2}([0,\infty[)
given by
(\mathcal{S}h)(s):=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(rs)\,h(r)\operatorname{d}r
if
h
is integrable. Recall that

\mathcal{S}
is unitary with
\mathcal{S}^{-1}=\mathcal{S}
. One verifies that
\varphi:=\mathcal{F}\psi
is radially symmetric
\varphi=f(|p|)
with

jf=\mathcal{S}(jg)

(1)

Indeed, for
R>0
and
B\in SU(2)
, consider
(2\pi)^{3/2}\varphi^{R}(p):=\int_{B_{R}}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}xp}\psi(x)\operatorname{d}^{3}x=\int_{B_{R}}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}B\cdot xB\cdot p}\psi(x)\operatorname{d}^{3}x=\int_{B_{R}}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}xB\cdot p}g(|x|)\operatorname{d}^{3}x
. Choose
B=B(p)^{-1}
(see Eq. (
27.8
)) and use spherical coordinates
r=|x|,x_{1}=r\cos(\phi)\sin(\theta),x_{2}=r\sin(\phi)\sin(\theta),x_{3}=r\cos(\theta)
. Then, substituting
\cos(\theta)
by the variable
\xi
,
\varphi^{R}(p)=(2\pi)^{-3/2}\int_{B_{R}}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}x_{3}|p|}g(|x|)\operatorname{d}^{3}x=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{-1}^{1}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}r|p|\xi}\operatorname{d}\xi\,g(r)r^{2}\operatorname{d}r=\frac{1}{|p|}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{0}^{R}\sin(|p|r)rg(r)\operatorname{d}r
. The limit in the mean for
R\to\infty
yields
\varphi(p)=\frac{1}{|p|}(\mathcal{S}(jg))(|p|)
, whence (1).

\bullet
Similarly compute
\psi_{t}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th^{\chi}(\cdot)}\varphi)
.
Let
(2\pi)^{3/2}\psi_{t}^{R}(x):=\int_{B_{R}}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}px}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th^{\chi}(p)}\varphi(p)\operatorname{d}^{3}p=\int_{B_{R}}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}pB\cdot x}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th^{\chi}(B^{-1\cdot}p)}f(|p|)\operatorname{d}^{3}p=\int_{B_{R}}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}pB\cdot x}B^{-1}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th^{\chi}(p)}Bf(|p|)\operatorname{d}^{3}p
by Eq. (
27.7
).

Note that

h^{\chi}(p)^{2}=|p|^{2}I_{2}
. Hence

\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th^{\chi}(p)}=\cos(t|p|)I_{2}+\operatorname{i}\sin(t|p|)\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{|p|}$}}h^{\chi}(p)

In spherical coordinates
s=|p|,p_{1}=s\cos(\phi)\sin(\theta),p_{2}=s\sin(\phi)\sin(\theta),p_{3}=s\cos(\theta)
one has

h^{\chi}(p)=\chi s\big(\cos(\phi)\sin(\theta)\sigma_{1}+\sin(\phi)\sin(\theta)\sigma_{2}+\cos(\theta)\sigma_{3}\big)

For
B=B(x)^{-1}
the exponent
p\,B\cdot x
becomes
p_{3}|x|=s|x|\cos(\theta)
, which does not depend on the variable
\phi
. So the integrations
\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sin(\theta)\operatorname{d}\theta\operatorname{d}\phi
can be easily performed. Using Eq. (
27.9
),
\psi_{t}^{R}(x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{-1}^{1}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}s|x|\xi}\big(\cos(ts)I_{2}+\operatorname{i}\sin(ts)\xi h^{\chi}(\frac{1}{|x|}x)\big)f(s)\operatorname{d}\xi\,s^{2}\operatorname{d}s=\frac{1}{|x|}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{0}^{R}\sin(s|x|)\cos(ts)\,sf(s)\operatorname{d}s\;\,+\,\;h^{\chi}(\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{|x|}$}}x)\frac{1}{|x|}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{0}^{R}\cos(s|x|)\sin(ts)\,sf(s)\operatorname{d}s\,\;+\,\,R^{\textsc{r}}_{t}(x)
,

R^{\textsc{r}}_{t}(x):=-h^{\chi}(\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{|x|}$}}x)\textnormal{\footnotesize{$\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}$}}\textnormal{\tiny{$\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}$}}\int_{0}^{R}\sin(s|x|)\sin(ts)f(s)\operatorname{d}s

(2)

Next we use
2\sin(s|x|)\cos(ts)=\sin(s(|x|+t))+\sin(s(|x|-t))
,
2\cos(s|x|)\sin(ts)=\sin(s(|x|+t))-\sin(s(|x|-t))
, and
u=\operatorname{sgn}(u)\,|u|
for
u\in\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, the limit in the mean of
\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{0}^{R}\sin(s\,|u|)sf(s)\operatorname{d}s
for
R\to\infty
is
(\mathcal{S}(jf))(|u|)=|u|g(|u|)
by (1). Thus, putting things together for
u=|x|\pm t
, one confirms the formula for
A_{t}^{\varsigma}
.

\bullet
As to the formula for
R_{t}
, according to (2) the claim is

(\mathcal{S}(f_{t}))(r)=-\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{2}$}}\big(G(|r+t|)-G(|r-t|)\big)

(3)

for
f_{t}(s):=\sin(ts)f(s)
. Indeed, as
|f_{t}(s)|\leq s|f(s)|
, one has
f_{t}\in L^{2}
and in (2) the limit in the mean for
R\to\infty
exists. Moreover,
jg
is locally integrable as
jg\in L^{2}
, whence
G
is well-defined.

In (2) use
-2\sin(sr)\sin(ts)=\cos(s(r+t))-\cos(s(r-t))
. In proving (3) first assume
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}
, i.e.,
f
continuous with compact support. Then, for
u\geq 0
,
G(u)=-\int_{0}^{u}\rho g(\rho)\operatorname{d}\rho=-\int_{0}^{u}(\mathcal{S}(jf))(\rho)\operatorname{d}\rho=-\int_{0}^{u}\textnormal{\tiny{$\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}$}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(\rho s)sf(s)\operatorname{d}s\operatorname{d}\rho
, which by Fubini’s theorem equals
\textnormal{\tiny{$\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}$}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos(su)f(s)\operatorname{d}s-\textnormal{\tiny{$\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}$}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(s)\operatorname{d}s
. By this formula for
G(u)
with
u=|r\pm t|
, (3) follows.

Now consider a general
f
. Fix
t\neq 0
. Denote the right hand side of (3) by
G_{t}(r)
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. Choose
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}
with
\parallel\!\!jf-jf^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{1+2|t|}
. Then
\parallel\!\!\mathcal{S}f_{t}-G_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!\mathcal{S}f_{t}-\mathcal{S}f^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel+

\parallel\!\!\mathcal{S}f^{\prime}_{t}-G^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!G^{\prime}_{t}-G_{t}\!\!\parallel
. As just shown,
\parallel\!\!\mathcal{S}f^{\prime}_{t}-G^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel=0
. Next,

\parallel\!\!\mathcal{S}f_{t}-\mathcal{S}f^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel=

\parallel\!\!f_{t}-f^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel=\parallel\!\!h_{t}(jf-jf^{\prime})\!\!\parallel\leq|t|\parallel\!\!jf-jf^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
with
h_{t}(s):=\sin{ts}/s
, as
|h_{t}(s)|\leq|t|
. Finally,
G^{\prime}_{t}-G_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{|r-t|}^{|r+t|}\big(\mathcal{S}(jf)-\mathcal{S}(jf^{\prime})\big)(\rho)\operatorname{d}\rho
, whence
\parallel\!\!G^{\prime}_{t}-G_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{|r-t|}^{|r+t|}

\parallel\!\!\big(\mathcal{S}(jf)-\mathcal{S}(jf^{\prime})\big)(\rho)\!\!\parallel\operatorname{d}\rho\leq\frac{1}{2}\big||r+t|-|r-t|\big|^{1/2}\parallel\!\!\mathcal{S}(jf)-\mathcal{S}(jf^{\prime})\!\!\parallel\leq\frac{1}{2}(2|t|)^{1/2}\parallel\!\!jf-jf^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/2
. Hence
\parallel\!\!\mathcal{S}f_{t}-G_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon
, which proves the claim.

We turn to the proof of (a) – (e).

(a) Recall that
\pi^{\chi\varsigma}(x)
are projections in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
with
\pi^{\chi-}(x)=I_{2}-\pi^{\chi+}(x)
.

(b)
2\int|A_{t}^{\varsigma}(x)|^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}x=\int\big(\frac{|x|+\varsigma t}{|x|}\big)^{2}\big\langle\big(I_{2}+\varsigma h^{\chi}(\frac{1}{|x|}x)\big)g\big(\big||x|+\varsigma t\big|\big),g\big(\big||x|+\varsigma t\big|\big)\big\rangle\operatorname{d}^{3}x=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-1}^{1}(r+\varsigma t)^{2}\langle(I_{2}+\varsigma\chi\xi\sigma_{3})g(|r+\varsigma t|),g(|r+\varsigma t|)\rangle\operatorname{d}\xi\operatorname{d}r=4\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}(r+\varsigma t)^{2}|g(|r+\varsigma t|)|^{2}\operatorname{d}r=4\pi\int_{\varsigma t}^{\infty}r^{\prime 2}|g(|r^{\prime}|^{2}\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}<\infty
, whence
A^{\varsigma}_{t}\in L^{2}
and hence also
R_{t}\in L^{2}
. Moreover, as
