\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}=4\pi\int_{0}^{|t|}r^{2}|g(r)|^{2}\operatorname{d}r
, (b) holds.

(c) As in (b) one computes
2\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=2\int_{B_{|t|}}|A_{t}^{\varsigma}(x)|^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}x=4\pi\int_{0}^{|t|}(r+\varsigma t)^{2}|g(|r+\varsigma t|)|^{2}\operatorname{d}r=4\pi\int_{-|t|}^{0}r^{\prime 2}|g(|r^{\prime}|)|^{2}\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}=\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}
as
|t|+\varsigma t=0
by assumption.

(d) First assume
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}
. Then
R_{t}(x)=\lim_{R\to\infty}R^{\textsc{r}}_{t}(x)
in (2) for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
. Since
h(s):=\frac{\sin(s|x|)}{|x|}f(s)
is integrable,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(ts)h(s)\operatorname{d}s\to 0
as
|t|\to\infty
by the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma. Hence

R_{t}(x)\to 0,\;|t|\to\infty
for every
x\neq 0
.

Next use the obvious estimation
\big|\sin(s|x|)/|x|\big|\leq s
if
|x|\leq 1
and
\leq 1/|x|
if
|x|>1
. Therefore, as
|R_{t}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{|x|}\textnormal{\tiny{$\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}$}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\frac{\sin(s|x|)}{|x|}\big|\parallel\!\!f(s)\!\!\parallel\operatorname{d}s
, one has
|R_{t}(x)|\leq M(x)
with
M(x):=\frac{C}{|x|}
if
|x|\leq 1
and
M(x)\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{2}}
if
|x|>1
for some finite constant
C
. Note
\int M(x)^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}x=8\pi C^{2}<\infty
. So by dominated convergence
\int|R_{t}(x)|^{2}\operatorname{d}x^{3}\to 0
for
|t|\to\infty
, as claimed.

Now consider a general
f
. Let
\varepsilon>0
. There exists a radially symmetric
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}
with

\parallel\!\!\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/6
. Plainly
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}
. Let
\psi^{\prime}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi^{\prime}
. Then
\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/6
, whence
\parallel\!\!\psi_{t}-\psi^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/6
for all
t
. As in (b) one computes

\parallel\!\!A_{t}^{\varsigma}-A_{t}^{\prime\,\varsigma}\!\!\parallel^{2}=2\pi\int_{\varsigma t}^{\infty}r^{\prime 2}|g(|r^{\prime}|)-g^{\prime}(|r^{\prime}|)|^{2}\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}
, whence
\parallel\!\!A_{t}^{\varsigma}-A_{t}^{\prime\,\varsigma}\!\!\parallel\to 0
for
\varsigma t\to\infty
and
\parallel\!\!A_{t}^{\varsigma}-A_{t}^{\prime\,\varsigma}\!\!\parallel\to\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel

for
\varsigma t\to-\infty
.

Hence for all
|t|
large enough one has
\parallel\!\!R^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/6
and
\parallel\!\!A_{t}^{\varsigma}-A_{t}^{\prime\,\varsigma}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/3
. Therefore
\parallel\!\!R_{t}\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!R^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!R_{t}-R^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel=\parallel\!\!R^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!\psi_{t}-A^{+}_{t}-A_{t}^{-}-(\psi^{\prime}_{t}-A_{t}^{\prime+}-A_{t}^{\prime-})\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!R^{\prime}_{t}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+

\parallel\!\!A_{t}^{+}-A_{t}^{\prime-}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!A_{t}^{-}-A_{t}^{\prime-}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon
.

(e) Due to (d) and (a) one has
\lim_{|t|\to\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=\lim_{|t|\to\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})(A_{t}^{+}+A_{t}^{-})\!\!\parallel^{2}=\lim_{|t|\to\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})A_{t}^{+}\!\!\parallel^{2}+\lim_{|t|\to\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})A_{t}^{-}\!\!\parallel^{2}
. Hence the assertion follows by (b) and (c).∎

(106) Corollary.
Let
\psi
be radially symmetric around some point
b\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, i.e.,
\psi=g(|\cdot-b|)
. Then

(a)

\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\pm\,2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{|b|\xi}\xi\,r^{2}\,\langle g(r),h^{\chi}(\frac{1}{|b|}b)g(r)\rangle\operatorname{d}r\operatorname{d}\xi

(b)

\frac{1}{4}\leq\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}\leq\frac{3}{4}

(c)

\lim_{|t|\to\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|xe|\leq|t|\})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel=1

Proof.
(a) In order to make easier the computation we first perform the Euclidean transformation
h:=(\beta e_{3},B(-b)^{-1})
,
\beta:=|b|
getting
W^{\textsc{w}}(h)E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{|t|})\psi_{t}=E^{\textsc{w}}(h\cdot B_{|t|})W^{\textsc{w}}(h)\psi_{t}=E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta,|t|})\tilde{g}_{t}(|\cdot|)

with
B_{\beta,|t|}:=\beta e_{3}+B_{|t|}
,
\tilde{g}:=B(-b)^{-1}g
, and
\langle g(r),h^{\chi}(\frac{1}{|b|}b)g(r)\rangle=-\chi\langle\tilde{g}(r),\sigma_{3}\tilde{g}(r)\rangle
by Eq. (
27.9
). So we have to compute
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta,|t|})\tilde{\psi}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}
for
\tilde{\psi}:=\tilde{g}(|\cdot|)
. Henceforth we may omit the tilde.

According to (
22
),
E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta,|t|})\psi_{t}=\sum_{\varsigma}E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta,|t|})A^{\varsigma}_{t}+E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta,|t|})R_{t}
with
R_{t}\to 0
for
|t|\to\infty
and
\parallel\!\!\sum_{\varsigma}E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta|t|})A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=\sum_{\varsigma}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta,|t|})A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}
. Hence by (
22
)(b) it suffices to compute

\lim_{\varsigma t\to-\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta|t|})A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}
.

Suppose at once
|t|>\beta
. Then a point in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with polar angle
\theta\in[0,\pi]
is on the surface of
B_{\beta,|t|}
if and only if, according to the cosine rule,
|t|^{2}=\beta^{2}+r(\theta)^{2}-2r(\theta)\beta\cos(\theta)
or, equivalently,
r(\theta)=\sqrt{|t|^{2}-\beta^{2}\sin^{2}(\theta)}+\beta\cos(\theta)
. Let
\tilde{r}(\xi):=\sqrt{|t|^{2}-\beta^{2}(1-\xi^{2})}+\beta\xi
. It satisfies
\tilde{r}\big(\cos(\theta)\big)=r(\theta)
.

Now,

\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta,|t|})A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=\int 1_{B_{\beta,|t|}}(x)\big(\frac{|x|+\varsigma t}{|x|}\big)^{2}\big\langle\pi^{\chi\varsigma}(\frac{1}{|x|}x)g\big(\big||x|+\varsigma t\big|\big),g\big(\big||x|+\varsigma t\big|\big)\big\rangle\operatorname{d}x^{3}
. It becomes
2\pi\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h_{t}(r^{\prime},\xi)\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}\operatorname{d}\xi
with
h_{t}(r^{\prime},\xi):=1_{[\varsigma t,\tilde{r}(\xi)+\varsigma t]}(r^{\prime})k(r^{\prime},\xi)
and
k(r^{\prime},\xi):=r^{\prime 2}\big\langle\frac{1}{2}(I_{2}+\varsigma\chi\xi\sigma_{3})g(|r^{\prime}|),g(|r^{\prime}|)\big\rangle
by elementary computation. Obviously

|h_{t}(r^{\prime},\xi)|\leq r^{\prime 2}|g(|r^{\prime}|)|^{2}1_{[-1,1]}(\xi)

which is integrable on
]-\infty.\infty[\times[-1,1]
. We are going to apply dominated convergence for
\varsigma t\to-\infty
.

For
\varsigma t<0
one has
\varsigma t=-|t|
and hence
\tilde{r}(\xi)+\varsigma t=\sqrt{|t|^{2}-\beta^{2}(1-\xi^{2})}+\beta\xi-|t|\to\beta\xi
. So

h_{t}(r^{\prime},\xi)\to 1_{]-\infty,\beta\xi]}(r^{\prime})k(r^{\prime},\xi)
.
Therefore
\lim_{\varsigma t\to-\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(B_{\beta,|t|})A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=2\pi\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{0}k(r^{\prime},\xi)\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}\operatorname{d}\xi+2\pi\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{\beta\xi}k(r^{\prime},\xi)\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}\operatorname{d}\xi
. One easily does the
\xi
-integration for the first summand obtaining
2\pi\int_{-\infty}^{0}r^{\prime 2}\parallel\!\!g(|r^{\prime}|)\!\!\parallel^{2}\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}
. As to the second summand one finds
\int_{-1}^{0}\int_{0}^{\beta\xi}k(r^{\prime},\xi)\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}\operatorname{d}\xi=\int_{-1}^{0}\int_{-\beta\xi}^{0}k(-r^{\prime},\xi)\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}\operatorname{d}\xi=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\beta\xi}-k(-r,-\xi)\operatorname{d}r\operatorname{d}\xi
. Since
k(r,\xi)-k(-r,-\xi)=\varsigma\chi\xi r^{2}\langle\sigma_{3}g(r),g(r)\rangle
, the proof of (a) is accomplished.

(b) Estimate
\big|\,2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{|b|\xi}\xi\,r^{2}\,\langle g(r),h^{\chi}(\frac{1}{|b|}b)g(r)\rangle\operatorname{d}r\operatorname{d}\xi\big|\leq\,2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\xi\,r^{2}\,|g(r)|^{2}\operatorname{d}r\operatorname{d}\xi=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{2}\xi\operatorname{d}\xi=\frac{1}{4}
, whence the assertion.

(c) Arguing as for (a), due to the Euclidean motion
h=(-B(\varsigma e)^{-1}b,B(\varsigma e)^{-1})
with
\varsigma=\pm
such that
\beta:=\varsigma be\geq 0
, it suffices to consider the case
b=0
, to replace
\{|xe|\leq|t|\}
with
S_{\beta,|t|}:=\{|x_{3}-\beta|\leq|t|\}
,
\beta\geq 0
, and to show

\lim_{\varsigma t\to-\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(S_{\beta,t})A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel=1
.

Suppose at once
|t|>\beta
. By elementary trigonometry a point in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with polar angle
\theta\in[0,\pi]
is on the boundary of
\{|x_{3}-\beta|\leq|t|\}
if and only if
r(\theta)=\frac{|t|+\beta}{\cos(\theta)}
for
0\leq\theta<\frac{\pi}{2}
and
r(\theta)=\frac{|t|-\beta}{\cos(\pi-\theta)}
for
\frac{\pi}{2}<\theta\leq\pi
.

Hence, as in (a),

\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(S_{\beta,|t|})A^{\varsigma}_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=2\pi\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}h_{t}(r^{\prime},\xi)\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}\operatorname{d}\xi
with
\tilde{r}(\xi)=\frac{\beta}{\xi}+\frac{|t|}{|\xi|}
,
\xi\neq 0
. If
\xi\not\in\{-1,0,1\}
, then
\tilde{r}(\xi)+\varsigma t\to\infty
and
h_{t}(r^{\prime},\xi)\to k(r^{\prime},\xi)
for
\varsigma t\to-\infty
.
Finally, one has
2\pi\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}k(r^{\prime},\xi)\operatorname{d}r^{\prime}\operatorname{d}\xi=2\pi\big(\int_{-1}^{1}\operatorname{d}\xi\big)\big(\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{2}\parallel\!\!g(r)\!\!\parallel^{2}\operatorname{d}r\big)+\varsigma\chi\,\pi\big(\int_{-1}^{1}\xi\operatorname{d}\xi\big)\big(\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{2}\langle\sigma_{3}g(r),g(r)\rangle\operatorname{d}r\big)=1+0
, thus accomplishing the proof. ∎

For the Weyl states
\psi
in (
22
) one has
\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus B_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel\geq\gamma
for large
|t|
, for every
\gamma<1/2
.
So the proof of (
16
) via (
16
) for the Dirac system cannot be adopted to the Weyl systems since (
16
) for Weyl states is not available at this stage. However, the result for the Weyl systems analogous to (
16
) holds true and is derived in (
22
) by means of (
22
)(c) due to appendix (
30.1
). As (
22
) shows, (
22
)(c) actually is valid for all Weyl states.

(107) Theorem.

I=\lim_{|\alpha|\to\infty}W^{\textsc{W}\chi}(\alpha)E^{\textsc{W}}(\{|xe|\leq|\alpha|\})W^{\textsc{W}\chi}(\alpha)^{-1}
.

Proof.

P(\alpha):=W^{\textsc{W}\chi}(\alpha)E^{\textsc{W}}(\{|xe|\leq|\alpha|\})W^{\textsc{W}\chi}(\alpha)^{-1}
is an orthogonal projection. It suffices to show
P(\alpha)\psi\to\psi
for all elements
\psi
of a total set
L
. Indeed, given
\varepsilon>0
and a state
\psi
there is
\psi^{\prime}
in the linear hull
\langle L\rangle
with
\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon/3
. Then, by assumption,
\parallel\!\!\psi-P(\alpha)\psi\!\!\parallel\leq

\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!\psi^{\prime}-P(\alpha)\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+

\parallel\!\!P(\alpha)\psi^{\prime}-P(\alpha)\psi\!\!\parallel\leq 2\parallel\!\!\psi-\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel+\parallel\!\!\psi^{\prime}-P(\alpha)\psi^{\prime}\!\!\parallel\leq\varepsilon
for all
|\alpha|
large enough. — Furthermore
it suffices to show
\parallel\!\!P(\alpha)\psi\!\!\parallel\to\parallel\!\!\psi\!\!\parallel
for every
\psi\in L
. Indeed,
\langle(I-P(\alpha))\psi,P(\alpha)\psi\rangle=0
, whence
\parallel\!\!\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}=\parallel\!\!(I-P(\alpha))\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}+\parallel\!\!P(\alpha)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}
. By assumption
\parallel\!\!(I-P(\alpha))\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}=\parallel\!\!\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}-\parallel\!\!P(\alpha)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}\to 0
. This means
(I-P(\alpha))\psi\to 0
as claimed. — Finally,
\parallel\!\!P(\alpha)\psi\!\!\parallel=\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{W}}(\{|xe|\leq|\alpha|\})\psi_{-\alpha}\!\!\parallel
. Thus the result holds by appendix (
30.1
) and (
22
)(c).∎

Arguing by means of time reversal it is clear that it suffices to prove (
22
) for
\alpha\to\infty
(or
\alpha\to-\infty
). We like to recall that, due to (
10
)(f), (
22
) is equivalent to
I=P^{>}_{\sigma}+P^{<}_{\sigma}
and the other similar equalities.
Hence, for the Weyl systems, also the version analogous to
(
16
) is equivalent to (
22
). Therefore the density result analogous to (
17
) concerning late-change states is valid, too.

(108) Corollary.
Consider finitely many directions
e^{1},\dots,e^{m}
, i.e.,
e^{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e^{j}|=1
. Then
I=
s-
\lim_{|\alpha|\to\infty}W^{\textsc{W}\chi}(\alpha)E^{\textsc{W}}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|xe^{1}|\leq|\alpha|,\dots,|xe^{m}|\leq|\alpha|\})W^{\textsc{W}\chi}(\alpha)^{-1}
.

Proof.
Let
P(\alpha,m)
denote the orthogonal projection on the right hand side. We proceed by induction on
m
. The base case
m=1
holds by (
22
). It follows the inductive step
m\to m+1
. Let
P(\alpha):=W^{\textsc{W}\chi}(\alpha)E^{\textsc{W}}(\{|xe^{m+1}|\leq|\alpha|\})W^{\textsc{W}\chi}(\alpha)^{-1}
. Check
P(\alpha,m+1)=P(\alpha)P(\alpha,m)=P(\alpha,m)P(\alpha)
. Then for every state
\psi
,
\parallel\!\!P(\alpha,m)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}=

\parallel\!\!P(\alpha)P(\alpha,m)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}+\parallel\!\!\big(I-P(\alpha)\big)P(\alpha,m)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}
, whence
\parallel\!\!P(\alpha,m+1)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}=\parallel\!\!P(\alpha,m)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}-\parallel\!\!P(\alpha,m)\big(I-P(\alpha)\big)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}\to 1+0
by assumption and since
\parallel\!\!P(\alpha,m)\big(I-P(\alpha)\big)\psi\!\!\parallel\leq

\parallel\!\!\big(I-P(\alpha)\big)\psi\!\!\parallel\to 0
by (
22
). Since
\parallel\!\!\big(I-P(\alpha,m+1)\big)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}=1-\parallel\!\!P(\alpha,m+1)\psi\!\!\parallel^{2}
, this implies
\big(I-P(\alpha,m+1)\big)\psi\to 0
, finishing the proof.∎

For
e^{1},e^{2},e^{3}
the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
C_{|t|}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|xe^{1}|\leq|t|,\dots,|xe^{3}|\leq|t|\}
is the cube with sides parallel to the axes
\mathbb{R}^{3}e^{j}
circumscribed about the ball
B_{|t|}
. Choosing more directions
e^{1},\dots,e^{m}
one gets a figure
F_{|t|}
which is even much more close to
B_{|t|}
. Nevertheless, by (
22
),
\lim_{|t|\to\infty}\parallel\!\!E^{\textsc{w}}(F_{|t|})\psi_{t}\!\!\parallel^{2}=1
for every Weyl state
\psi
. This is remarkable in view of the result (
22
)(b).

23.
No Causal Spin Zero Systems

The results in sec.
28.1
,
29.3
imply that there are no (massive or massless) spin–
0
systems with causal time evolution. Actually, this is a rather general fact not restricted to relativistic systems, see
[
5
, Corollary 2]
. One may ask about its relevance for the

Klein-Gordon equation

-\partial^{2}_{t}\Psi(t,x)=-\left(\nabla^{2}-m^{2}\right)\Psi(t,x)

(23.1)

which is a well-established relativistic wave equation for the description of a massive spinless particle. It is manifestly relativistic as for every solution
\Psi
of Eq. (
23.1
) and every
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
the transformed wave function
\Psi_{g}(\mathfrak{x}):=\Psi(g^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x})
with
\mathfrak{x}=(t,x)
is still a solution. (For a detailed discussion of Eq. (
23.1
)
see
[
69
]
). We will show explicitly that, in contrast to what holds for the Dirac and Weyl equations, the multiplication of
\Psi
by the space variables
x_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
and by the indicator functions
1_{\Delta}
of measurable sets
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
does not define observables as these operators turn out to be not self-adjoint.
This failure is expected due to the fact that otherwise the canonical PM
E(\Delta)\Psi:=1_{\Delta}\Psi
would (as in the Dirac and Weyl cases) constitute a causal WL, which as we know does not exist.

The starting point for the discussion of Eq. (
23.1
) are their plane wave solutions
(t,p)\mapsto\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\eta\epsilon(p)t+\operatorname{i}px}
,
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\eta\in\{-1,+1\}
with
\epsilon(p)=\sqrt{|p|^{2}+m^{2}}
. Superposing these waves one gets the linear space
\mathcal{H}^{\textsc{kg}}
of solutions of Eq. (
23.1
)

\Psi(t,x)=\sum_{\eta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,\varphi_{\eta}(p)\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\eta\epsilon(p)t}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}px}\,\frac{\operatorname{d}^{3}p}{\sqrt{2\epsilon(p)}},\quad\varphi_{\eta}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

(23.2)

More precisely,
\Psi(t,\cdot)=\sum_{\eta}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left((2\epsilon)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\eta\epsilon t}\varphi_{\eta}\right)
, which is well-defined as
\frac{|\varphi_{\eta}|}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\in L^{2}
. The factor
(2\epsilon)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
contributes to the Lorentz invariant measure

\frac{\operatorname{d}^{3}p}{2\epsilon(p)}
so that

\Psi_{g}(\mathfrak{x})=\sum_{\eta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(W^{\eta}(g)\varphi_{\eta})(p)\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\eta\epsilon(p)t}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}px}\,\frac{\operatorname{d}^{3}p}{\sqrt{2\epsilon(p)}}
holds. Here

\big(W^{\eta}(\mathfrak{a},A)\varphi\big)(p)=\sqrt{\epsilon(q^{\eta})/\epsilon(p)}\,\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}\cdot\,\mathfrak{a}}\,\varphi(q^{\eta})

(23.3)

is the representation
[m,0,\eta]
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
from Eq. (
3.6
). Recall that
W^{\eta}
is unitary.

Let
\tau\equiv((\tau,0),I_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
denote the time translation by
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
. Then one easily checks that consistently
\Psi_{\tau}(t,x)=\Psi(t-\tau,x)
. In the following
\widehat{\Psi_{t}}:=\mathcal{F}\Psi(t,\cdot)
.

(109) Lemma.
The space
\mathcal{H}^{\textsc{kg}}
of solutions
\Psi
of the Klein-Gordon equation given by Eq.
(
23.2
)
provided with the inner product

\langle\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\epsilon(p)\,\overline{\widehat{\Psi_{t}}(p)}\,\widehat{\Psi^{\prime}_{t}}(p)+\frac{1}{\epsilon(p)}\,\overline{\partial_{t}\widehat{\Psi_{t}}(p)}\,\partial_{t}\widehat{\Psi^{\prime}_{t}}(p)\right)\operatorname{d}^{3}p

which does not depend on time
t
, is a Hilbert space, on which
W^{\textsc{kg}}(g)\Psi:=\Psi_{g}
is a representation
[m,0,+]\oplus[m,0,-]
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
. In particular, the time evolution of the wave functions determined by the Klein-Gordon equation is unitary.

Proof.
Let
\iota:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathcal{H}^{\textsc{kg}}
,
\iota(\varphi_{+},\varphi_{-}):=\Psi
be the linear surjection given by Eq. (
23.2
). Then, as shown,
W^{\textsc{kg}}\,\iota=\iota\,(W^{+}\oplus W^{-})
holds. Since
\widehat{\Psi_{t}}=(2\epsilon)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{\eta}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\eta\epsilon t}\varphi_{\eta}
and
\partial_{t}\widehat{\Psi_{t}}=-\operatorname{i}(\epsilon/2)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{\eta}\eta\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}\eta\epsilon t}\varphi_{\eta}
it follows
\varphi_{\eta}=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\eta\epsilon t}\left((\epsilon/2)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\Psi_{t}}+\operatorname{i}\eta(2\epsilon)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{t}\widehat{\Psi_{t}}\right)
, whence
\iota
is also injective with
\iota^{-1}\Psi=(\varphi_{+},\varphi_{-})
. Then
\langle\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}\rangle:=\langle\iota^{-1}\Psi,\iota^{-1}\Psi^{\prime}\rangle
is a inner product on
\mathcal{H}^{\textsc{kg}}
making
\mathcal{H}^{\textsc{kg}}
a Hilbert space and
W^{\textsc{kg}}
unitary. It is easy to check the above formula for
\langle\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}\rangle
.
\Box

Now, as to the posed question about the self-adjointness of the multiplication operator
(X_{j}\Psi)(t,x)=x_{j}\Psi(t,x)
, one easily verifies that in momentum space, which is the carrier space of
W^{+}\oplus W^{-}
, the latter equals

X^{mom}_{j}=-\frac{\operatorname{i}}{2}\frac{P_{j}}{P^{2}+m^{2}I}+\operatorname{i}\partial_{p_{j}}

(23.4)

revealing a non-vanishing bounded skew-adjoint part.

24.
Lattice Generated by the Non-Timelike Relation

As shown in (
11.5
), (
11.5
)
there are maximal spacelike sets which are not bases, i.e., for which the sets of dependency are not the whole spacetime
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. One may feel this fact to be an unsatisfactory property of the spacelike relation
\perp
. It turns out that the weaker orthogonality
\perp^{\prime}
meaning non-equal and non-timelike, i.e.

\mathfrak{x}\perp^{\prime}\mathfrak{y}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathfrak{x}\neq\mathfrak{y}\textnormal{ and }(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0

generates a lattice
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, which from a mathematical point of view has a richer structure than
\mathcal{M}
. In Cegla, Jadczyk 1977
[
70
, Theorem 1]
it is shown that
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\subset,\perp^{\prime})
is a complete orthomodular lattice and, what is more, that
(\mathbb{R}^{4},\perp^{\prime})
is a complete
D
-space, which means that every
\perp^{\prime}
-complete set
M=M^{\land^{\prime}}
is the
\perp^{\prime}
-completion of any maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set contained in
M
. In

[
70
, Corollary 1]
it is also shown that every timelike line intersects every maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Hence, every maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set is a
\perp^{\prime}
-base. More generally, as a consequence noted in (
24.2
), for every
\perp^{\prime}
-set its set of
\perp^{\prime}
-determinacy equals its
\perp^{\prime}
-completion.

Therefore in addition to the well-known spacelike hyperplanes there are also the non-timelike not spacelike hyperplanes, which are
\perp^{\prime}
-bases as well. An example of such a hyperplane is
\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\}
. The question is about their meaning. Obviously they do not constitute a reference frame. In fact there is no direction
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,

\mathfrak{e}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=1
with
\mathfrak{e}\cdot(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{x}^{\prime})=0

\forall

\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}\in\sigma
, which would determine the time coordinate of the frame. One may attempt to consider such a hyperplane to be some high boost limit of a spacelike hyperplane. This idea is concretized in sec.
26
, when localization operators are attributed to
\perp^{\prime}
-complete regions. Thereby rather interesting properties of causal systems are predicted, which are confirmed for the Dirac and the Weyl systems.

\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonality, in contrast to
\perp
-orthogonality, considers lightlike separated events to be independent. This is reasonable within classical mechanics where all objects are macroscopic and move slower than light. Let us remark that Casini 2002
[
56
]
studies
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonality on general spacetime manifolds with dimension
\geq 2
showing that the respective lattice
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is complete orthomodular and atomic.
[
56
, sec. 3]
proposes an interpretation of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
in terms of propositions for classical particles. In the first instance, to every
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
a proposition is attributed, namely the proposition that the particle passes through
M
. But, as observed by
[
56
, sec. 3]
, in general the logical opposite of the proposition attributed to
M
(i.e., the particle does not meet
M
) is not a proposition represented by some
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. To be clear note first that a classical particle in Minkowski space is described by a timelike line. Let
\mathcal{T}_{M}
denote the set of all timelike lines meeting
M
, which represents the set of all particles which pass through
M
. The equality
\mathcal{T}_{M_{1}}=\mathcal{T}_{M_{2}}
means that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
represent the same proposition. One easily verifies
\mathcal{T}_{M_{1}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{M_{2}}=\emptyset

\Leftrightarrow

M_{1}\perp^{\prime}M_{2}
. This means that the propositions attributed to
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
exclude each other if and only if they are
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonal. Put
\mathcal{T}:=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}
. There is the following result.

(110) Lemma.

\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{M}=\mathcal{T}_{L}
if and only if
\mathcal{T}_{M}=\mathcal{T}_{M^{\land^{\prime}}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{L}=\mathcal{T}_{M^{\perp^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{M}=\mathcal{T}_{L}
implies
\mathcal{T}_{M}\cap\mathcal{T}_{L}=\emptyset
, whence
L\subset M^{\perp^{\prime}}
and hence
\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{M^{\land^{\prime}}}\subset\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{M}=\mathcal{T}_{L}\subset\mathcal{T}_{M^{\perp^{\prime}}}
. — Let
A
be a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set in
M^{\land^{\prime}}
and
B
a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set in
M^{\perp^{\prime}}
. Then
A\cap B=\emptyset
and
A\cup B
is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set. Therefore
u\in\mathcal{T}_{M^{\perp^{\prime}}}

\Leftrightarrow

u\cap M^{\perp^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset\Leftrightarrow u\cap B\neq\emptyset
(by (
24.2
))
\Leftrightarrow u\cap A=\emptyset

\Leftrightarrow

u\cap M^{\land^{\prime}}=\emptyset
(by (
24.2
))
\Leftrightarrow

u\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{M^{\land^{\prime}}}
. This proves
\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{M^{\land^{\prime}}}=\mathcal{T}_{M^{\perp^{\prime}}}
, whence the assertion.∎

Hence (
24
) clarifies that only the set of propositions represented by
\perp^{\prime}
-complete sets is negation-closed. This is one reason why consider
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
for an adequate description of spatial-temporal localization of classical particles. But, obviously, not every reasonable physical proposition concerning the
latter belongs to
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. For instance the proposition that the particle passes through
M

and
passes through
L
for
M,L\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
in general does not correspond to the conjunction
M\land^{\prime}L
representing the proposition that the particle passes through
M\cap L
. (
M\land^{\prime}L
is merely
the maximal proposition in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
implying
M
and
L
.)

The question is about the
relevance of
\perp^{\prime}
for quantum mechanics.
The concept of a causal system does not discern
\perp
- and
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonality as shown in (
26
), despite the fact that motions at the speed of light really occur as the
motion of the border of the Dirac and Weyl wave functions (see (
15.2
), (
21.1
)). Moreover, as already remarked above, the respective lattices
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
show significant differences, and just the latter proves to be particularly relevant for causal systems.

Before tackling the question of a possible rep of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
(
24.3
) and proving the implications for the Dirac and Weyl systems (see sec.
25
,
26
) we like to study
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
in some detail. The examples at the level of exercises mainly serve to become more familiar with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and can be skipped.

24.1.
\perp^{\prime}
-sets and non-timelike hyperplanes

Let
A
be a subset of Minkowski space.
A
is said to be non-timelike or
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonal or a
\perp^{\prime}
-set, if
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
for every
\mathfrak{x},\mathfrak{y}\in A
. Note that every one-point set is a
\perp^{\prime}
-set.

Let
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
and let
A\subset M
be a
\perp^{\prime}
-set. Then there is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set in
M
containing
A
. This holds true by an obvious application of Zorn’s lemma. In particular, every set
M
contains a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set in
M
.

By definition, a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Any maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set
A
is closed. (Indeed, let
\mathfrak{x}_{n}\in A
with
\mathfrak{x}_{n}\to\mathfrak{x}
for some
\mathfrak{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Then, for all
\mathfrak{y}\in A
,
0\geq(\mathfrak{x}_{n}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\to(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}
, whence
\mathfrak{x}\in A
.) Every
\perp^{\prime}
-set is contained in a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set. For the following lemma see
[
72
, (2)]
.

(111) Lemma.

\Sigma
is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set if and only if it is the graph
\{\big(\xi(x),x\big):x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\}
of a map
\xi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
|\xi(x)-\xi(y)|\leq|x-y|
.
\xi
is uniquely determined. Clearly,
\xi
is Lipschitz continuous.

Proof.
Let
\Sigma
be a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. By
[
70
, Corollary 1]
the timelike line
(0,x)+\mathbb{R}(1,0)
intersects
\Sigma
. The intersection consists of one point only as
\Sigma
is
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonal. Hence there is a unique
\xi(x)\in\mathbb{R}
with
(\xi(x),x)\in\Sigma
. Since
\Sigma
is
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonal,
|\xi(x)-\xi(y)|\leq|x-y|
holds. — Conversely, obviously
\Sigma:=\operatorname{graph}(\xi)
is
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonal. Assume
\mathfrak{y}\in\Sigma^{\perp^{\prime}}
. Then
(\xi(y),y)\in\Sigma
and
|y_{0}-\xi(y)|\leq|y-y|=0
, whence the contradiction
\mathfrak{y}=(\xi(y),y)
. Hence
\Sigma
is maximal.
∎

Plainly, every non-timelike hyperplane
\kappa
is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set. Also,
\kappa
is a non-timelike hyperplane if and only if it is the graph of
\xi(x):=\rho+xe
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, for unique
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
and
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|e|\leq 1
or, equivalently, if
\kappa=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\rho\}
for unique
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
and non-spacelike

\mathfrak{e}=(1,e)
, and
\kappa
is non-timelike not spacelike if and only if
|e|=1
.
Every non-timelike not spacelike hyperplane
\kappa
is given by
\kappa=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\}
for the Poincaré transformation

g=\big((\rho,0),B\big)h
with
B\in SU(2)
satisfying
B\cdot(0,0,1)=e
and arbitrary
h

leaving
\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\}
invariant (see (
26
)).

If
\kappa
is a non-timelike not spacelike hyperplane and if
\sigma
is a spacelike hyperplane then
\pi:=\kappa\cap\sigma
is a spacelike plane. Let
M
be a subset of one of the two open half-hyperplanes which constitute
\kappa\setminus\pi
. Then, according to the definition in sec.
10
, one determines the open half-hyperplane
\sigma_{M}
in
\sigma\setminus\pi
such that
M
does not lie at the same side of
\pi
as
\sigma_{M}
.

(112) Example.
Let
\sigma,\tau
be two non-intersecting spacelike hyperplanes and let
\kappa
be a non-timelike not spacelike hyperplane. Then

\sigma_{M}\cup M\cup\tau_{M}

is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set.
Here
M
denotes the closed subset of
\kappa
between
\sigma
and
\tau
.

-9,-1)(2,3)

Proof.
We anticipate the result (
24.2
). So it suffices to show that every timelike line
l=\mathfrak{y}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{z}
,
\mathfrak{z}=(1,z),|z|<1
intersects
\sigma_{M}\cup M\cup\tau_{M}
just once.

Let
\sigma=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\rho_{\sigma}\}
,
\tau=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\rho_{\tau}\}
,
\kappa=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa}=\rho_{\kappa}\}
with
\mathfrak{e}=(1,e)
,
|e|<1
and
\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa}=(1,e_{\kappa})
,
|e_{\kappa}|=1
and assume without restriction
\rho_{\sigma}<\rho_{\tau}
so that
M=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\kappa:\rho_{\sigma}-\rho_{\kappa}\leq\mathfrak{x}\cdot(\mathfrak{e}-\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa})\leq\rho_{\tau}-\rho_{\kappa}\}
,

\sigma_{M}=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:\mathfrak{x}\cdot(\mathfrak{e}-\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa})<\rho_{\sigma}-\rho_{\kappa}\}
, and
\tau_{M}=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\tau:\mathfrak{x}\cdot(\mathfrak{e}-\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa})>\rho_{\tau}-\rho_{\kappa}\}
. Moreover,
\kappa=\kappa_{1}\cup M\cup\kappa_{2}
with
\kappa_{1}:=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\kappa:\mathfrak{x}\cdot(\mathfrak{e}-\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa})<\rho_{\sigma}-\rho_{\kappa}\}
and
\kappa_{2}:=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\kappa:\mathfrak{x}\cdot(\mathfrak{e}-\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa})>\rho_{\tau}-\rho_{\kappa}\}
.

Now one notes
l\cap\kappa=\{\mathfrak{y}+s\mathfrak{z}\}
for
s:=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{z}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa}}(\rho_{\kappa}-\mathfrak{y}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa})
and
l\cap\sigma=\{\mathfrak{y}+t\mathfrak{z}\}
for
t:=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{z}\cdot\mathfrak{e}}(\rho_{\sigma}-\mathfrak{y}\cdot\mathfrak{e})
, due to
\mathfrak{z}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa}>0
,
\mathfrak{z}\cdot\mathfrak{e}>0
. By a short computation one finds

(\mathfrak{y}+s\mathfrak{z})\cdot(\mathfrak{e}-\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa})<\rho_{\sigma}-\rho_{\kappa}\;\Leftrightarrow\;(\mathfrak{y}+t\mathfrak{z})\cdot(\mathfrak{e}-\mathfrak{e}_{\kappa})<\rho_{\sigma}-\rho_{\kappa}

This proves
l\cap\kappa_{1}\neq\emptyset

\Leftrightarrow

l\cap\sigma_{M}\neq\emptyset
.
Analogously
l\cap\kappa_{2}\neq\emptyset

\Leftrightarrow

l\cap\tau_{M}\neq\emptyset
holds. A moment of reflection shows the proof.

Alternatively, because of Poincaré covariance, for the proof of (
24.1
) it suffices to verify that for
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|=1
and
\alpha>0

\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,xe<0\}\cup\{\mathfrak{x}:0\leq x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\}\cup\{x_{0}=\alpha,xe>\alpha\}

(24.1)

is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set.
20
20

20

Let
P^{\prime}
denote the set in Eq. (
24.1
) for
e=(0,0,1)
.

(i) We show
\sigma_{M}\cup M\cup\tau_{M}=g\cdot P^{\prime}
for some
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
. Indeed, let
\sigma,\tau,\kappa
be as in the proof of (
24.1
). Then
\sigma=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
and
\tau=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\alpha\}
with
\alpha:=(1-e^{2}_{\sigma})^{-1/2}(\rho_{\tau}-\rho_{\sigma})
for
g=g_{0}\,(b,B)
with
g_{0}:=\big((\rho_{\sigma},0),Q(\mathfrak{e})\big)
and arbitrary
(b,B)\in ISU(2)
(see the footnote in sec.
10
). Now,
\kappa^{\prime}:=g_{0}^{-1}\kappa
is a non-timelike not spacelike hyperplane and hence
\kappa^{\prime}=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}\}
for some
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}=(1,e^{\prime})
,
|e^{\prime}|=1
and
\rho^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. Specify
(b,B)
such that
e^{\prime}=B\cdot(0,0,1)
and
be^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}
, whence
\kappa^{\prime}=(b,B)\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\}
. Thus
\kappa=g\cdot\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\}
. Finally, following the definition of
M
,
\sigma_{M}
, and
\tau_{M}
, one verifies the assertion.

(ii) For the sake of completeness we show that the only
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
leaving
P^{\prime}
invariant are
g=\big((0,a_{1},a_{2},0),\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\varphi\sigma_{3}/2})
,
a_{1},a_{2},\varphi\in\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, these
g
obviously satisfy
g\cdot P^{\prime}=P^{\prime}
. Hence for a general
g=(\mathfrak{a},A)
assume at once
\mathfrak{a}=(a_{0},0,0,a_{3})
. Observe first
\mathfrak{a}=g\cdot 0\in P^{\prime}
. Next check
\{\mathfrak{x}\in P^{\prime}:\mathfrak{x}^{\cdot 2}=0\}=\{s\mathfrak{e}:s\in[0,\alpha]\}
for
\mathfrak{e}:=(1,0,0,1)
. Since
A
acts orthocronously,
(A\cdot\mathfrak{e})_{0}>0
. Therefore
g\cdot s\mathfrak{e}\in P^{\prime}
for
s\in[0,\alpha]
excludes
a_{0}=0
or
a_{3}=\alpha
. It follows
\mathfrak{a}=a_{0}\mathfrak{e}
and
g\cdot s\mathfrak{e}\in M:=\{\mathfrak{x}:0\leq x_{0}=x_{3}\leq\alpha\}
. Hence
(A\cdot\mathfrak{e})_{0}=(A\cdot\mathfrak{e})_{3}
. Because of
(A\cdot\mathfrak{e})^{\cdot 2}=0
one has
A\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\lambda\mathfrak{e}
with
\lambda>0
.
Then (see  (
26
))

A\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\lambda\mathfrak{e}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad A\in A_{\ln(\lambda)}\,E(2)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad A=\,\operatorname{e}^{\ln(\lambda)\sigma_{3}/2}\,\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\varphi\sigma_{3}/2}\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&w\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

with
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}
,
w=u+\operatorname{i}v\in\mathbb{C}
. So
A
leaves
M
invariant and hence
(A\cdot\mathfrak{x})_{0}=\operatorname{e}^{\rho}(x_{0}+ux_{1}-vx_{2})\in[0,\alpha]

\forall

\mathfrak{x}\in M
, whence
u=v=0
and
\rho=0
, thus accomplishing the proof.
∎

24.2.
\perp^{\prime}
-completion and set of
\perp^{\prime}
-determinacy

Let
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Then
M^{\land^{\prime}}:=M^{\perp^{\prime}\perp^{\prime}}
denotes the
\perp^{\prime}
-completion of
M
. Moreover,
M
is said to be
\perp^{\prime}
-
complete
if
M=M^{\land^{\prime}}
.
The set of
\perp^{\prime}
-determinacy of
M
is defined as

M^{\sim^{\prime}}:=\{\mathfrak{x}:\forall\;\mathfrak{z}\textnormal{ with }\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}>0\;\exists\,s\in\mathbb{R}\textnormal{ with }\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z}\in M\}

It consists of all points
\mathfrak{x}
such that every timelike line through
\mathfrak{x}
meets
M
.

Obviously,
M\subset M^{\sim^{\prime}}
. Further,
M^{\sim^{\prime}\sim^{\prime}}=M^{\sim^{\prime}}
holds. (Indeed, let
\mathfrak{x}\in M^{\sim^{\prime}\sim^{\prime}}
. For
\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}>0
there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z}\in M^{\sim^{\prime}}
. Hence, there is
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
with
(\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z})+s^{\prime}\mathfrak{z}\in M
, whence
\mathfrak{x}\in M^{\sim^{\prime}}
.) Clearly
M^{\sim^{\prime}}_{1}\subset M^{\sim^{\prime}}_{2}
if
M_{1}\subset M_{2}
.

(113) Lemma.

M^{\sim^{\prime}}\subset M^{\land^{\prime}}
.

Proof.
Fix
\mathfrak{x}\in M^{\sim^{\prime}}
. Then there is a timelike
\mathfrak{w}
with
\mathfrak{x}+\mathfrak{w}\in M
. Hence
\big((\mathfrak{x}+\mathfrak{w})-\mathfrak{x}\big)^{\cdot 2}=\mathfrak{w}^{\cdot 2}>0
, whence
\mathfrak{x}\not\in M^{\perp^{\prime}}
. Now let

\mathfrak{y}\in M^{\perp^{\prime}}
. Then
\mathfrak{z}:=\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y}\neq 0
. Assume
\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}>0
. Then there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z}\in M
. Clearly,
s\neq 1
as
\mathfrak{y}\not\in M
. It follows the contradiction
0\geq\big((\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z})-\mathfrak{y}\big)^{\cdot 2}=(\mathfrak{z}-s\mathfrak{z})^{\cdot 2}=(1-s)^{2}\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}>0
. Thus
\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
contrary to the assumption. This implies
\mathfrak{x}\in M^{\perp^{\prime}\perp^{\prime}}=M^{\land^{\prime}}
.∎

Thus, by (
24.2
), one has
M\subset M^{\sim^{\prime}}=M^{\sim^{\prime}\sim^{\prime}}\subset M^{\land^{\prime}}
, whence
M=M^{\sim^{\prime}}
if
M=M^{\land^{\prime}}
. But
M=M^{\sim^{\prime}}
does not imply that
M
is
\perp^{\prime}
-complete. A simple example is
M:=\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
for
\mathfrak{e}=(1,0,0,0)
with
M=M^{\sim^{\prime}}
and
M^{\land^{\prime}}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
. (Indeed,
M=M^{\sim^{\prime}}
is obvious. Next note
M^{\perp^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
, since generally
M^{\perp^{\prime}}\cap M=\emptyset
, and
s\mathfrak{e}\not\perp^{\prime}(2+s,0,0,1)
with
(2+s,0,0,1)\in M
. Hence
M^{\perp^{\prime}}=\emptyset
, whence
M^{\land^{\prime}}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
.)

(114) Lemma.
The one-point sets are
\perp^{\prime}
-complete.

Proof.
This is easily checked by (
24.2
).∎

(115) Example
for the
\perp^{\prime}
-completion of a two-point set. Let
s\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. Then

\{0,(s,0,0,0)\}^{\land^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x|<|\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{s}{2}$}}|,\,|x_{0}-\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{s}{2}$}}|+|x|\leq|\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{s}{2}$}}|\}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{s}{2}$}},|x|<|\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{s}{2}$}}|\}^{\land^{\prime}}

Proof.
We proceed as for (
11.1
) and may be brief.
\{(t,0,0,0)\}^{\perp^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{y}:|y_{0}-t|\leq|y|\}\setminus\{(t,0,0,0)\}
for every
t
. Hence
D:=\{0\}^{\perp^{\prime}}\cap\{(s,0,0,0)\}^{\perp^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{y}:|y_{0}-\frac{s}{2}|+|\frac{s}{2}|\leq|y|\}
and
\{0,(s,0,0,0)\}^{\land^{\prime}}=D^{\perp^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}-y_{0}|\leq|x-y|\;\forall\,\mathfrak{y}\in D\}\setminus D=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}-\frac{s}{2}|+|y_{0}-\frac{s}{2}|\leq\big||x|-|y|\big|\;\forall\;\mathfrak{y}\in D\}\setminus D
. Hence
|x|<|\frac{s}{2}|
for
\mathfrak{x}\in D^{\perp^{\prime}}
, whence one infers the first =. The second = follows as for (
11.1
) applying (
24.2
).∎

(116) Theorem.
Let
A
be a
\perp^{\prime}
-set. Then
A^{\sim^{\prime}}=A^{\land^{\prime}}
.

Proof.
Because of (
24.2
) it remains to show
A^{\land^{\prime}}\subset A^{\sim^{\prime}}
. Let
\Lambda
be a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set with
A\subset\Lambda
, see sec.
24.1
. Put
B:=\Lambda\setminus A
. Then
B\perp^{\prime}A
, whence
B\perp^{\prime}A^{\land}
.

Now fix
\mathfrak{x}\in A^{\land^{\prime}}
and let
\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}>0
. Then, by
[
70
, Corollary 1]
, there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z}\in\Lambda
. Assume
\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z}\in B
. Then
(\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z})\perp^{\prime}\mathfrak{x}
. This implies
s\neq 0
and the contradiction
0\geq\big((\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z})-\mathfrak{x}\big)^{\cdot 2}=s^{2}\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}>0
. Thus
\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z}\in A
. This proves
\mathfrak{x}\in A^{\sim^{\prime}}
.∎

(117) Example.
Let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
r>0
and
\Delta:=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=a_{0},|x-a|<r\}
. Then

\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}-a_{0}|+|x-a|\leq r\}\setminus\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=a_{0},|x-a|=r\}

Indeed, without restriction assume
\mathfrak{a}=0
. Then, by (
24.2
),
\mathfrak{x}\in\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}
if and only if for every
|z|<1
there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{x}+s(1,z)\in\Delta
. This means that
s=-x_{0}
and
|x-x_{0}z|<r
for all
|z|<1
. Then either
x_{0}=0
and
|x|<r
or
x_{0}\neq 0
and
|y|+(1-\varepsilon)|x_{0}|<r
for all
\varepsilon>0
, whence the claim.

Let
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be
\perp^{\prime}
-complete. Then a set
A\subset M
is said to be a
\perp^{\prime}
-base of
M
if
A
is a
\perp^{\prime}
-set with
A^{\sim^{\prime}}=M
. (Recall
A^{\sim^{\prime}}=A^{\land^{\prime}}
by (
24.2
).)
Every
\perp^{\prime}
-base

A
of
M
is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set in
M
, since
M=A^{\sim^{\prime}}\subset A^{\land^{\prime}}=A^{\perp^{\prime}\perp^{\prime}}
, whence
A^{\perp^{\prime}}\cap M\subset M^{\perp^{\prime}}\cap M=\emptyset
. Conversely, recall that by
[
70
, Theorem 1]
every maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set
A
in
M
is a
\perp^{\prime}
-base of
M
. Hence, in particular, every
\perp^{\prime}
-complete
set has a
\perp^{\prime}
-base. —
By definition, a set is a
\perp^{\prime}
-base
if it is a
\perp^{\prime}
-base for
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. So for
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
we point out

A\perp^{\prime}\textnormal{-base }\Leftrightarrow\;A\textnormal{ maximal $\perp^{\prime}$-set}

(24.2)

(118) Lemma.
Let
(M_{\iota})_{\iota}
be a family in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
such that
M_{\iota}\perp^{\prime}M_{\kappa}
if
\iota\neq\kappa
. Then
\big(\cup_{\iota}M_{\iota}\big)^{\sim^{\prime}}=\big(\cup_{\iota}M_{\iota}\big)^{\land^{\prime}}
.

Proof.
Let
A_{\iota}
be a
\perp^{\prime}
-base of
M_{\iota}
. Put
A:=\cup_{\iota}A_{\iota}
. Obviously
A
is a
\perp^{\prime}
-set. Hence, by (
24.2
),
\big(\cup_{\iota}M_{\iota}\big)^{\sim^{\prime}}\supset\big(\cup_{\iota}A_{\iota}\big)^{\sim^{\prime}}=A^{\sim^{\prime}}=A^{\land^{\prime}}=\lor^{\prime}_{\iota}A^{\land^{\prime}}_{\iota}=\lor^{\prime}_{\iota}M_{\iota}=\big(\cup_{\iota}M_{\iota}\big)^{\land^{\prime}}
. The proof is accomplished by (
24.2
).∎

(119) Lemma.
Let
A
be a subset of a
\perp^{\prime}
-base
\Sigma
. Then
(i)

\Sigma\setminus A=A^{\perp^{\prime}}\cap\Sigma
,
(ii)

(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\perp^{\prime}}=A^{\land^{\prime}}
,
(iii)

(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\land^{\prime}}=A^{\perp^{\prime}}
,
(iv)

A=A^{\land^{\prime}}\cap\Sigma
, and finally
(v)

A\supset\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in A^{\land^{\prime}}}\{\mathfrak{x}\in\Sigma:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}>0\}
.

Proof.
Recall first
A\subset\Sigma
,
A\cap A^{\perp^{\prime}}=\emptyset
, and
\Sigma\setminus A\subset A^{\perp^{\prime}}
as
\Sigma
is a
\perp^{\prime}
-set. Hence (i).
— Then obviously
A^{\land^{\prime}}\subset(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\perp^{\prime}}
. We show now
(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\perp^{\prime}}\subset A^{\sim^{\prime}}
. Then (ii) follows by (
24.2
). So let
\mathfrak{x}\in(\Sigma\setminus A)^{\perp^{\prime}}
. This means
(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0
,
\mathfrak{x}\neq\mathfrak{y}
for all
\mathfrak{y}\in\Sigma\setminus A
. Let
\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}>0
. As
\Sigma^{\sim^{\prime}}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
there is
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z}\in\Sigma
. Assume
\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z}\in\Sigma\setminus A
. Then
s\neq 0
and the contradiction
0\geq(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{x}+s\mathfrak{z})^{\cdot 2}=s^{2}\mathfrak{z}^{\cdot 2}>0
follows. Therefore
\mathfrak{x}-s\mathfrak{z}\in A
showing
\mathfrak{x}\in\tilde{A}
. — By orthocomplementation (ii) implies (iii). — Further, (iv) equals (i) for
\Sigma\setminus A
in place of
A
because of (ii).
— Finally, as to (v), consider
\mathfrak{x}\in\Sigma\setminus A
. This implies
\mathfrak{x}\in A^{\perp^{\prime}}
and hence,
\mathfrak{x}\perp^{\prime}A^{\land^{\prime}}
, which implies
\mathfrak{x}\in\bigcap_{\mathfrak{y}\in A^{\land^{\prime}}}\{\mathfrak{x^{\prime}}\in\Sigma:(\mathfrak{x^{\prime}}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0\}
as
\mathfrak{x}\in\Sigma
.
∎

24.3.
\perp^{\prime}
-diamonds

A set
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
is called a
\perp^{\prime}
-diamond if there is a subset
\Delta
of a non-timelike hyperplane
\sigma
with
M=\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}
.
\Delta
is called a
flat
(
\perp^{\prime}
-)
base
of
M
. By (
24.2
)(iii),
M^{\perp^{\prime}}=(\sigma\setminus\Delta)^{\land^{\prime}}
is a
\perp^{\prime}
-diamond, too.

(120) Example
for
\perp^{\prime}
-diamonds.
Clearly,
\emptyset
and
\mathbb{R}^{4}
are
\perp^{\prime}
-diamonds. Let
0<a\leq b
and
\Gamma:=\{\mathfrak{x}:(x_{0},x_{3})=(r\,a,r\,b)\textrm{ for }r\in[0,1]\,\}
. Then
\Gamma^{\perp^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}|\leq-x_{3}\}\cup\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}-a|\leq x_{3}-b\}
,
\Gamma^{\land^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{x}:a-b\leq x_{0}-x_{3}\leq 0,\,0\leq x_{0}+x_{3}\leq a+b\}
, and
(\Gamma\setminus\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}=0\})^{\perp^{\prime}}=\Gamma^{\perp^{\prime}}\setminus\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=a,x_{3}=b\}
,
(\Gamma\setminus\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}=0\})^{\land^{\prime}}=\Gamma^{\land^{\prime}}\setminus\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}=0\}
.

We verify the claim for
(\Gamma\setminus\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}=0\})^{\land^{\prime}}
using (
24.2
). The remaining formulae follow similarly. So,
(\Gamma\setminus\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}=0\})^{\sim^{\prime}}
if and only if for every
\mathfrak{z}=(1,z)
,
|z|<1
there are
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
r\in]0,1]
such that
x_{0}+s=ra
,
x_{3}+sz_{3}=rb
. Eliminating
r
and
s
one finds the equivalent condition
0<x_{3}-x_{0}z_{3}\leq b-az_{3}
for all
|z_{3}|<1
. This means

|x_{0}|\leq x_{3}
,
x_{3}>0
and
x_{3}-b\leq-|x_{0}-a|
, whence the assertion.∎

For
a<b
the bases of the
\perp^{\prime}
-diamonds in (
24.3
) are spacelike. For
a=b
the
\perp^{\prime}
-diamond
\Gamma^{\land^{\prime}}
coincides with is own base
\Gamma
, which is not spacelike. More generally, for
\perp^{\prime}
-diamonds with not spacelike bases see (
24.3
).

(121) Lemma.
Let
\kappa
be a non-timelike not spacelike hyperplane. This means
\kappa=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\rho\}
with some
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
and some
lightlike
\mathfrak{e}=(1,e)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
with
|e|=1
. Let
\Delta\subset\kappa
. Then
\mathfrak{y}\in\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}
if and only if

\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}+\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=0,\;\big(x-(xe)e\big)^{2}<\lambda\,xe\}\subset\Delta

(1)

with
\lambda:=\rho-\mathfrak{y}\cdot\mathfrak{e}
and
\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}:=\mathfrak{y}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda(1,-e)\in\kappa
. In particular one has

\Delta=\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}

(2)

if
\Delta
is bounded or, more generally, if for every
\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}\in\kappa
there is
(\xi_{n})
with
\liminf\xi_{n}=-\infty
,
\limsup\xi_{n}=\infty
such that
\mathfrak{y}^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\mathfrak{e}\not\in\Delta
.

Proof.
By (
24.2
) one has to determine the intersection
C
of
\kappa
with the interior of the light-cone with apex
\mathfrak{y}
. Check
\mathfrak{z}\cdot\mathfrak{e}\neq 0
. So for every timelike
\mathfrak{z}
one finds
(\mathfrak{y}-s\mathfrak{z})\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\rho
with
s=\lambda/(\mathfrak{z}\cdot\mathfrak{e})
. Check also that
\mathfrak{x}=\mathfrak{z}/(\mathfrak{z}\cdot\mathfrak{e})
for some timelike
\mathfrak{z}
if and only if
\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=1
and
|x|<|x_{0}|
. Hence
C=\mathfrak{y}+\lambda\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=1,|x|<|x_{0}|\}
, which equals the left hand side of (1) by the substitution
\mathfrak{x}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathfrak{x}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}(1,-e)
. As to (2),
note that
\lambda=0
if and only if
\mathfrak{y}\in\Delta
. Hence, if

\mathfrak{y}\in\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}\setminus\Delta
, then the projection of
\Delta
onto the space
\mathbb{R}^{3}\equiv\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
contains the interior of a circular paraboloid with axis
e
and minimum point
y-\frac{1}{2}\lambda e
.
∎

With regards to sec.
26
the
\perp^{\prime}
-diamonds in (
24.3
) are particularly interesting, since they have a spacelike flat base and a not spacelike one as well.

Let
\pi
be a spacelike plane, let
\sigma
be any spacelike hyperplane containing
\pi
and let
\sigma^{>}
be one of the two open half-hyperplanes constituting
\sigma\setminus\pi
.
For every non-timelike hyperplane
\tau
containing
\pi
let
\tau^{>}
denote the half-hyperplane constituting
\tau\setminus\pi
, which is at the same side of
\pi
as
\sigma^{>}
(cf. (
10
)).
There are exactly two different non-timelike not spacelike hyperplanes
\tau
, denoted by
\kappa_{\pm}
, which contain
\pi
.
21
21

21

Explicitly, let
\sigma=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma}=\rho_{\sigma}\}
,

\pi=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:xe=\rho\}
with

\mathfrak{e}_{\sigma}=(1,e_{\sigma})
,
|e_{\sigma}|<1
,

e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|=1
,
\rho_{\sigma},\rho\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\tau
is a non-timelike hyperplane containing
\pi
if and only if there is
\lambda\in[\lambda_{-},\lambda_{+}]
with
\lambda_{\pm}:=-e_{\sigma}e\pm\sqrt{1-e_{\sigma}^{2}+(e_{\sigma}e)^{2}}
such that

\tau=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{\tau}=\rho_{\tau}\}\textnormal{ with }\mathfrak{e}_{\tau}=(1,e_{\tau}),\,e_{\tau}:=e_{\sigma}+\lambda e,\;\rho_{\tau}:=\rho_{\sigma}-\lambda\rho

Only for
\lambda\in\{\lambda_{-},\lambda_{+}\}
,
\tau\equiv\chi_{\pm}
is not spacelike. — This is easily checked using
1\geq|e_{\sigma}+\lambda e|^{2}=e_{\sigma}^{2}+2\lambda e_{\sigma}e+\lambda^{2}
.

(122) Lemma.

(\tau^{>})^{\land^{\prime}}=(\sigma^{>})^{\land^{\prime}}
and
(\sigma^{>})^{\land^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{\tau}\tau^{>}
,
(\sigma^{>})^{\perp^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{\tau}\tau^{\leq}
. In particular,
(\kappa_{-}^{>})^{\land^{\prime}}=(\kappa_{+}^{>})^{\land^{\prime}}=(\sigma^{>})^{\land^{\prime}}
.

Proof.
Because of Poincaré symmetry it suffices to check the assertion for

\sigma=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0\}
,
\pi=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,x_{3}=0\}
,
\sigma^{>}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,x_{3}>0\}
. Then, indeed, a short computation shows that the non-timelike hyperplanes containing
\pi
are
\tau=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}-\alpha x_{3}=0\}
for
\alpha\in[-1,1]
. So, the not spacelike hyperplanes are
\kappa_{\pm}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}\mp x_{3}=0\}
, and
\tau^{>}=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}-\alpha x_{3}=0,x_{3}>0\}
.

Now we compute
(\tau^{>})^{\perp^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{y}:(\mathfrak{y}-\mathfrak{x})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0\,\forall\mathfrak{x}\in\tau^{>}\}\setminus\tau^{>}=\{\mathfrak{y}:|y_{0}-\alpha x_{3}|\leq|y_{3}-x_{3}|\,\forall x_{3}>0\}\setminus\tau^{>}
. Therefore
\mathfrak{y}\not\in(\tau^{>})^{\perp^{\prime}}
if
y_{3}>0
and hence
(\tau^{>})^{\perp^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{y}:y_{3}\leq 0,|y_{0}-\alpha x_{3}|\leq x_{3}-y_{3}\,\forall x_{3}>0\}=\{\mathfrak{y}:y_{3}\leq-|y_{0}|\}
independently of
\alpha\in[-1,1]
. Similarly one verifies

\{\mathfrak{y}:y_{3}\leq-|y_{0}|\}^{\perp^{\prime}}=\{\mathfrak{y}:y_{3}\geq|y_{0}|\}\setminus\{0\}
. The assertion follows.∎

24.4.
The lattices
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}

The set
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}}
of all
\perp^{\prime}
-complete subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
is a complete orthomodular lattice (cf.
[
70
, sec. 4]
). It is atomic without the covering property (see (
24.2
), (
24.2
)). Moreover, all its elements are measurable.

As to the proof of the latter,
for
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
consider
the set of
\perp^{\prime}
-influence

M^{\lor^{\prime}}:=M\cup\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in M}\{\mathfrak{x}:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}>0\}

which obviously equals
\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus M^{\perp^{\prime}}
. Following (
11.3
) one has
U(M)\subset M^{\lor^{\prime}}\subset M^{\lor}
, whence
M^{\lor^{\prime}}
is measurable. It follows that the
\perp^{\prime}
-complete sets are measurable.

The above formula for
M^{\lor^{\prime}}
also implies that
M^{\lor^{\prime}}
is Borel if
M
is so. It follows that
M^{\land^{\prime}}
is Borel if
M
is Borel (cf.
[
70
, sec. 4]
). Therefore the set

\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}:=\{M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}:M\;\perp^{\prime}\textrm{-complete Borel set}\}

(24.3)

is a sublattice of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, which is
\sigma
-complete orthomodular atomic without the covering property.

Furthermore, it is worth be noted that for every
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
there is
M_{0}\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
,
M_{0}\subset M
with
\lambda^{4}(M\setminus M_{0})=0
. (Indeed, let
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. Since
M
is measurable there is a Borel
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
B\subset M
with
\lambda^{4}(M\setminus B)=0
. Then
M_{0}:=B^{\land^{\prime}}
satisfies the assertion.)

Finally, by
[
70
, Lemma 4.1]
, if
M
is
\perp^{\prime}
-complete and
A
is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set in
M
, then
M
is Borel if and only if
A
is Borel.

25.
A Rep
of the Lattice of Borel Sets
Generated by the Non-Timelike Relation

The interest in the causal structure of spacetime continues to exist and many articles are dedicated to this subject. However despite the common conviction that causal logics should be relevant for quantum theory it seems that not many attempts have been made to study a possible relationship other than the already mentioned assignment in local quantum theory. We cite Borowiec, Cegla, Jadczyk, Jancewicz 1977-1979
[
22
]
,
[
71
]
,
[
72
]
,
[
73
]
, where reps of the causal logic are related to Poincaré covariant conserved (operator) density currents.

Let us start stating that there is no rep of the lattice
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
generated by the non-timelike relation. This is due to the fact that all
\perp^{\prime}
-complete sets are measurable. It is quite the situation concerning
the lattice
\mathcal{M}
of causally complete sets, and also the proof of (
12
) applies quite literally.

In contrast the lattice
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
has reps. Recall that a rep
(W,F^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
regards a relativistic
system described by the representation
W
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
with
F^{\prime}
a projection valued normalized monotone locally orthogonal
\sigma
-orthoadditive map on
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
being Poincaré covariant with respect to
W
.

The existence of a rep of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
is per se a remarkable fact. However, the reps
(W,F^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
, which we know and are going to present, have a mass-squared operator with spectrum
]-\infty,\mu^{2}]
for every
\mu\geq 0
and infinite spinor dimension so that we do not consider them to represent a physical system (cf. the beginning of sec.
27
).

The following is based on the construction of a particular representation
W^{\prime}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
in Doplicher, Regge, Singer 1968
[
74
]
.

Recall that
\mathcal{T}
denotes the set of all timelike straight lines in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Every
u\in\mathcal{T}
is of the form
u=\mathfrak{x}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{v}
with
\mathfrak{v}^{\cdot 2}>0
. Clearly
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{T}
in the natural way. For
u_{0}:=\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
,
\mathfrak{e}:=(1,0,0,0)
one has the stabilizer subgroup
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{u_{0}}=\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}\times SU(2)
. Their irreducible representations are equivalent to
d^{\mu,j}(t,B):=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mu\,t}D^{j}(B)

for
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}/2
.

Let
d
be a representation of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{u_{0}}
acting on
H_{d}
. We build the induced representation
W^{\prime}:=d^{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
as described in sec.
30.2
. To this identify

\mathcal{T}\,\equiv\,\mathbb{R}^{3}\times O_{1}\;(\subset\mathbb{R}^{6})

where
O_{1}:=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|v|<1\}
by the parametrization
u=(x,v)\equiv(0,x)+\mathbb{R}(1,v)
. Endow
\mathcal{T}
with the Lebesgue measure
m
, the restriction of
\lambda^{6}
to
\mathcal{T}
.

Then
the group action becomes
\mathfrak{a}\cdot(x,v)=(x+a-a_{0}v,v)
,
B\cdot(x,v)=(B\cdot x,B\cdot v)
, and
A_{\rho}\cdot(x,v)=(y,w)
with

y:=\big(x_{1},x_{2},\cosh(\rho)x_{3}\big)-\frac{\sinh(\rho)x_{3}}{\cosh(\rho)+\sinh(\rho)v_{3}}\big(v_{1},v_{2},

\sinh(\rho)+\cosh(\rho)v_{3}\big)

and
w:=\frac{1}{\cosh(\rho)+\sinh(\rho)v_{3}}\big(v_{1},v_{2},\sinh(\rho)+\cosh(\rho)v_{3}\big)
.

For general
A\in SL(2,\mathbb{C})
one has
A=A_{\rho e}B
with
B\in SU(2)
and
A_{\rho e}:=(AA^{*})^{1/2}
representing the pure velocity transformation along
e\in\mathbb{R}
,
|e|=1
determined by
A_{\rho e}=B(p)A_{\rho}B(p)^{-1}
(
27.9
).

So a straight forward computation yields for
g=(\mathfrak{a},A)

\frac{\operatorname{d}m^{g}}{\operatorname{d}m}(x,v)=\big(A^{-1}\cdot(1,v)\big)_{0}^{-5}=\big(\cosh(\rho)-\sinh(\rho)\,ev\big)^{-5}=\big(1-\tanh^{2}(\rho)\big)^{5/2}\big(1-\tanh(\rho)\,ev\big)^{-5}

(1)

Hence
m
is quasi-invariant under
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
.

A Borel section for the surjection
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{T}
,
g\mapsto g\cdot u_{0}
is given by
q(u):=\big(\mathfrak{x}-\frac{x_{0}}{v_{0}}\mathfrak{v},Q(\mathfrak{v})\big)
for
u=\mathfrak{x}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{v}
,
\mathfrak{v}^{\cdot 2}>0
with the canonical cross section
Q
(
3.3
). The Wigner rotation becomes

R(u,g)=\left(\Big(\frac{x_{0}}{|v_{0}|}-\frac{(g^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x})_{0}}{|(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{v})_{0}|}\Big)\sqrt{\mathfrak{v}^{\cdot 2}}\,\mathfrak{e},\,Q(\mathfrak{v})^{-1}AQ(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{v})\right)

(2)

We are going to study
W^{\prime}
given in sec.
30.2
(2)

(W^{\prime}(g)\psi)(u)=\sqrt{(\operatorname{d}m^{g}/\operatorname{d}m)(u)}\,d\big(R(u,g)\big)\psi(g^{-1}u)

(3)

W^{\prime}
acts on
L^{2}(\mathcal{T},H_{d})
.

(W^{\prime},E^{can})
is a canonical system of imprimitivity on
(\tilde{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{T})
, which is irreducible if
d
is irreducible (cf. sec.
30.2
). It is trivially extended to all (Lebesgue) measurable subsets of

\mathcal{T}
.

Next let us determine the spectrum of the mass-squared operator
C
in case that
d
is irreducible.
To this we compute
K(t,b):=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}W^{\prime}(t,b)\mathcal{F}^{{}^{\prime}-1}
, where
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\psi)(p,v):=(2\pi)^{-3/2}\int\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}px}\psi(x,v)\operatorname{d}^{3}x
for
\psi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}
. For
g=(t,b)
the derivative (1) is constant
=1
and
R(u,g)
becomes
(t\sqrt{1-|v|^{2}}\,\mathfrak{e},I_{2})
. Hence
\big(K(t,b)\,\varphi\big)(p,v)=\exp(-\operatorname{i}p\,b)\,\exp\big(\operatorname{i}t(p\,v+\mu\sqrt{1-|v|^{2}})\big)\varphi(p,v)
. So
C
is the multiplication operator by
\gamma(p,v):=\big(p\,v+\mu\sqrt{1-|v|^{2}}\big)^{2}-|p|^{2}
, and its spectrum equals the essential numerical range of
\gamma
. As
\gamma
is continuous the latter is the closure of its codomain. One finds spectrum
(C)=\,]-\infty,\mu^{2}]
.

Now we turn to the skillful twist in
[
74
]
regarding
(W^{\prime},E^{can})
. Recall

\mathcal{T}_{M}=\{u\in\mathcal{T}:u\cap M\neq\emptyset\}

for every
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
The following properties of
\mathcal{T}_{M}
(up to the last) are easy to verify:

•

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}=\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}=\emptyset

•

M_{1}\subset M_{2}

\Rightarrow

\mathcal{T}_{M_{1}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{M_{2}}

•

\cup_{\iota}\mathcal{T}_{M_{\iota}}=\mathcal{T}_{\cup_{\iota}M_{\iota}}

•

\mathcal{T}_{M_{1}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{M_{2}}=\emptyset

\Leftrightarrow

M_{1}\perp^{\prime}M_{2}

•

\mathcal{T}_{M_{1}}=\mathcal{T}_{M_{2}}

\Leftrightarrow

M^{\sim^{\prime}}_{1}=M^{\sim^{\prime}}_{2}

•

\mathcal{T}_{M}=\mathcal{T}_{M^{\land^{\prime}}}
if
M
is
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonal (use (
24.2
))

•

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{M}
if
M
is maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonal (by (
24.2
))

•

g\cdot\mathcal{T}_{M}=\mathcal{T}_{g\cdot M}\;\forall\,g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}

•

\mathcal{T}_{M}\equiv\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in M}\{(y-y_{0}v,v):v\in O_{1}\}

•

\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{M^{\land^{\prime}}}=\mathcal{T}_{M^{\perp^{\prime}}}
(see (
24
))

In general
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is not measurable. This holds true even if
M
is
\perp^{\prime}
-complete and hence measurable.

(123) Example.
In order to provide a
\perp^{\prime}
-complete
M
with non-measurable
\mathcal{T}_{M}
, let
Y\subset[0,1]^{3}
be any non-measurable set with respect to
\lambda^{3}
. Obviously there is a
\sigma
-compact
C\subset Y
satisfying
\lambda^{3}(C)=\sup\{\lambda^{3}(K):K\subset Y\textrm{ compact}\}<\infty
. Then clearly
Y_{0}:=Y\setminus C
is still not measurable. Moreover,
for every Borel set of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
B\subset Y_{0}
one has due to tightness

\lambda^{3}(B)=\sup\{\lambda^{3}(K):K\subset B\textrm{ compact}\}=0
. Now, put
M:=\{0\}\times Y_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

As
\lambda^{4}(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})=0
,
M
is measurable with respect to
\lambda^{4}
.

M
is even
\perp^{\prime}
-complete. Indeed, let
\mathfrak{x}\in M^{\sim^{\prime}}
. Then one easily checks
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|x-y|<|x_{0}|\}\subset Y_{0}
. This implies
x_{0}=0
, whence
\mathfrak{x}\in M
. Hence
M^{\sim^{\prime}}=M
. By (
24.2
)
M^{\sim^{\prime}}=M^{\land^{\prime}}
since
M
is a
\perp^{\prime}
-set. Therefore
M
is
\perp^{\prime}
-complete.

The claim is that
\mathcal{T}_{M}=Y_{0}\times O_{1}
is not measurable with respect to
\lambda^{6}
. — First let us exclude that
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is a null set. Assume the contrary. Then there is a Borel set
B
of
\mathbb{R}^{6}
with
B\supset\mathcal{T}_{M}
and
\lambda^{6}(B)=0
. So, by Tonelli’s theorem,
0=\lambda^{6}(B)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\lambda^{3}(B_{v})\operatorname{d}\lambda^{3}(v)
.
Hence
\lambda^{3}(B_{v})=0
for some
v\in O_{1}
. This is a contradiction, since
B_{v}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(y,v)\in B\}\supset\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(y,v)\in\mathcal{T}_{M}\}=Y_{0}
. — Now assume that
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is measurable. Then there is a Borel set
B
of
\mathbb{R}^{6}
with
B\subset\mathcal{T}_{M}
and
\lambda^{6}(\mathcal{T}_{M}\setminus B)=0
. Clearly
\lambda^{6}(B)>0
since
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is not a null set. So, by Tonelli’s theorem,
0<\lambda^{6}(B)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\lambda^{3}(B_{v})\operatorname{d}\lambda^{3}(v)
. Hence
\lambda^{3}(B_{v})>0
for some
v\in O_{1}
. This is a contradiction, since
B_{v}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(y,v)\in B\}\subset\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(y,v)\in\mathcal{T}_{M}\}=Y_{0}
.∎

Fortunately one has

(124) Lemma.
If
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
is Borel then
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is measurable with respect to
\lambda^{6}
.

Proof.
Consider the map

f:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times O_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{4}\times O_{1},\;f(\xi,x,v):=(\xi,x+\xi v,v)

and the projection
\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times O_{1}}(\xi,x,v):=(x,v)
. One easily verifies

\mathcal{T}_{M}\,=\,\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times O_{1}}\big(f^{-1}(M\times O_{1})\big)
.
Now, if
M
is Borel, then
f^{-1}(M\times O_{1})
is Borel, whence
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is measurable by the Measurable Projection Theorem. (More precisely, as
f^{-1}(M\times O_{1})
is Borel in the product space of the Suslin spaces
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times O_{1}
, the projection
\mathcal{T}_{M}

is Suslin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times O_{1}
, which together with its Lebesgue measurable sets is a complete measurable space. Since
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times O_{1}
is metric, the assertion follows. See e.g.
[
75
]
.)
∎

(125) Theorem.
Recall
W^{\prime}=d^{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}}

(3)
and put
F^{\prime}(M):=E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{M})
for
M\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
. Then

(a)

(W^{\prime},F^{\prime})
is a rep of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
.

(b)

(W^{\prime},E)
is a causal system with
E(\Delta):=E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{\Delta})
,
\Delta\in\mathfrak{S}
. In particular
E(\Delta)=F^{\prime}(\Delta^{\land^{\prime}})
if
\Delta
is Borel, and
\big(E(\Delta)\psi\big)(x)=1_{\Delta}(x)\psi(x)
for
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathcal{S})\equiv L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times O_{1},H_{d})
for
\mathcal{S}:=L^{2}(O_{1},H_{d})
,
if
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is measurable and
\Delta\equiv\{0\}\times\Delta
.

(c)

W^{\prime}|_{ISU(2)}
is induced by the representation
D
of
SU(2)
in
\mathcal{S}
given by
\big(D(B)f\big)(v):=d(0,B)f(B^{-1}\cdot v)
. If
d=d^{\mu,j}
then the spectrum of
C
is
]-\infty,\mu^{2}]
and the spinor space
\mathcal{S}
is
L^{2}(O_{1},\mathbb{C}^{2j+1})
.

Proof.
By (
25
)
F^{\prime}
is well-defined. We use now tacitly the above list of properties of

\mathcal{T}_{M}
. Normalization and monotony are clear. — If
M_{1}\perp^{\prime}M_{2}
then
\mathcal{T}_{M_{1}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{M_{2}}=\emptyset
, whence
F^{\prime}(M_{1})F^{\prime}(M_{2})=E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{M_{1}})E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{M_{2}})=0
. — If
M_{n}\perp^{\prime}M_{m}
for
n\neq m
, then
\mathcal{T}_{M_{n}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{M_{m}}=\emptyset
and hence
\sum_{n}F^{\prime}(M_{n})=\sum_{n}E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{M_{n}})=E^{can}(\cup_{n}\mathcal{T}_{M_{n}})=E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{\cup M_{n}})=E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{(\cup M_{n})^{\sim^{\prime}}})=E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{(\cup M_{n})^{\land^{\prime}}})=F^{\prime}\big((\cup M_{n})^{\land^{\prime}}\big)=F^{\prime}(\lor^{\prime}M_{n})
using (
24.2
). — Since
W^{\prime}(g)E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{M})W^{\prime}(g)^{-1}=E^{can}(g\cdot\mathcal{T}_{M})=E^{can}(\mathcal{T}_{g\cdot M})
, covariance of
F^{\prime}
follows.

(W^{\prime},E)
is a causal system by the following (
25
). The spectrum of
C
is already determined above.
For the remainder see sec.
27.1
.∎

The question remains open whether the causal system
(W^{\prime},E)
in (
25
)
is irreducible if
d
is irreducible.

(126) Proposition.
Let
(W,F^{\prime})
be a rep of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
. Set
E(\Delta):=F^{\prime}(\Delta^{\land^{\prime}})
for Borel
\Delta\in\mathfrak{S}
. Then
E
extends to all
\Delta\in\mathfrak{S}
, and
(W,E)
is a causal system.

Proof.
Let
\sigma
be a spacelike hyperplane. Let
\Delta_{n}\subset\sigma
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
be Borel and disjoint. Then
M_{n}:=\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}_{n}
is Borel (see sec.
24.4
) and
M_{n}^{\perp^{\prime}}=\Delta_{n}^{\perp^{\prime}}=(\sigma\setminus\Delta_{n})^{\land^{\prime}}\supset\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}_{m}=M_{m}
for
n\neq m
by (
24.2
)(iii). Hence
M_{n}\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
are mutually orthogonal, whence
\sum_{n}E(\Delta_{n})=\sum_{n}F^{\prime}(M_{n})=F^{\prime}(M)
for
M:=\lor^{\prime}_{n}M_{n}
by
\sigma
-orthoadditvity of
F^{\prime}
. For
\Delta:=\bigcup_{n}\Delta_{n}
obviously one has
\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}=(\bigcup_{n}M_{n})^{\land^{\prime}}=M
, whence
E(\Delta)=F^{\prime}(\Delta^{\land^{\prime}})=F^{\prime}(M)
. This proves
\sigma
-additivity for
E
. — Moreover,
E(\sigma)=F^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{4})=I
and
E(\emptyset)=F^{\prime}(\emptyset)=0
. —
Poincaré covariance carries over from
F^{\prime}
to
E
since
(g\cdot\Delta)^{\land^{\prime}}=g\cdot\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}
. — Now extend
E
to all of
\mathfrak{S}
, see (
6.2
). —
Spacelike separated
\Delta,\Gamma\in\mathfrak{S}
obviously are
\perp^{\prime}
- orthogonal. Let
\Delta_{0}\subset\Delta
be Borel with
E(\Delta_{0})=E(\Delta)
and similarly be
\Gamma_{0}
.
So the completions
\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}_{0}
and
\Gamma^{\land^{\prime}}_{0}
are
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonal, whence
E(\Delta)\,E(\Gamma)=E(\Delta_{0})\,E(\Gamma_{0})=F^{\prime}(\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}_{0})\,F^{\prime}(\Gamma^{\land^{\prime}}_{0})=0
by local
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonality of
F^{\prime}
. Hence
E
is causal by (
7.2
)(f). — Thus
(W,E)
is a causal system.∎

Coming back to (
25
), there the map
F^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
into the lattice of projections is not a lattice homomorphism.
Indeed by monotony of
F^{\prime}
one has
F^{\prime}(M\land^{\prime}L)\leq F^{\prime}(M)\land F^{\prime}(L)
for all
M,L\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
. But equality does not hold in general. For instance,
M:=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}-1|+|x|\leq 1\}
and
L:=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x_{0}+1|+|x|\leq 1\}
are
\perp^{\prime}
-diamonds with
\mathcal{T}_{M\cap L}=\mathcal{T}_{\{0\}}=\{0\}\times O_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{M}\cap\mathcal{T}_{L}=\{(x,v):|v|\leq 1-|x|,|x|\leq 1\}
. Since
\lambda^{6}(\mathcal{T}_{M\cap L})=0
and
\lambda^{6}(\mathcal{T}_{M}\cap\mathcal{T}_{L})=4\pi^{2}/45>0
, one has
F^{\prime}(M\land^{\prime}L)=0
and
F^{\prime}(M)\land F^{\prime}(L)\neq 0
.

Hence the quantum proposition
F^{\prime}(M)\land F^{\prime}(L)
meaning that the particle passes through
M

and
passes through
L

does not
imply the proposition
F^{\prime}(M\land^{\prime}L)
meaning that the particle passes through
M\cap L
.

Plainly this fact is in full accordance with physical intuition and thus the latter does not require a lattice homomorphism from
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
into the quantum proposition system. Actually mathematics does not even allow a reasonable homomorphism. More precisely one has

(127) Lemma.
Let
h
be a homomorphism from
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
into the lattice of projections of a separable Hilbert space, i.e.,
h
satisfies
h(M\land^{\prime}L)=h(M)\land h(L)
and
h(M\lor^{\prime}L)=h(M)\lor h(L)
for all
M,L
. Then
h
vanishes at all bounded
M\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
.

Proof.
Clearly,
h
is monotone. (Indeed, if
L\subset M
then
h(L)=h(L\land^{\prime}M)=h(L)\land h(M)\leq h(M)
.) Since
h(M)\leq h(\mathbb{R}^{4})
for all
M
it is no restriction to assume
h(\mathbb{R}^{4})=I
. Now
remind of the fact that
h
preserves orthocomplementation. (Indeed,
0=h(\emptyset)=h(M\land^{\prime}M^{\perp^{\prime}})=h(M)\land h(M^{\perp^{\prime}})=h(M)h(M^{\perp^{\prime}})
and
I=h(\mathbb{R}^{4})=h(M\lor^{\prime}M^{\perp^{\prime}})=h(M)\lor h(M^{\perp^{\prime}})
, whence
h(M^{\perp^{\prime}})=I-h(M)=h(M)^{\perp}
.) Next consider the atoms

\{\mathfrak{a}\}
for
\mathfrak{a}=(k,a)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see (
24.2
)). Fix
k
. Then they are mutually orthogonal and so are
h(\{\mathfrak{a}\})
. Since the Hilbert space is separable,
h(\{\mathfrak{a}\})=0
up to countable many. Therefore there are
a_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|a_{k}|<\frac{1}{|k|}
and
h(\{\mathfrak{a}_{k}\})=0
for
\mathfrak{a}_{k}:=(k,a_{k})
.

Now consider the diamond
M_{n}:=\{\mathfrak{a}_{n}\}\lor^{\prime}\{\mathfrak{a}_{-n}\}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
(cf.
[
56
]
at the end of sec. 3). Clearly
h(M_{n})=0
. Note that
\{(n,0)\}\lor^{\prime}\{(-n,0)\}=\{\mathfrak{x}:|x|<0,|x_{0}|+|x|\leq n\}
. This makes clear that every bounded
M
is contained in some
M_{n}
. Hence
h
vanishes at all bounded
M\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
.∎

Clearly
h
in (
25
) is locally orthogonal and orthoadditive. If
h
is
\sigma
-orthoadditive, then by (
26
)(c) it follows
h=0
.

26.
Extension of Causal Localizations to Non-Timelike Hyperplanes

We do not know whether there exists a rep
(W,F^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
with non-negative mass-squared operator and finite spinor dimension. But
in (
26
) we derive some consequences of a rep in general, which refer to more profound properties of causal systems. Then in (
26
) these are shown to be valid for the Dirac and the Weyl systems. Hence, in addition to its mere mathematical convenience, the non-timelike relation
\perp^{\prime}
seems to have physical relevance. It draws attention to the non-timelike not spacelike hyperplanes, of which Poincaré covariant localizations did not take note so long.

Of course, by physical grounds we stay with the concept of causality as described by a causal system
(W,E)
defined in (
9
). So local orthogonality of
E
, which is equivalent to causality (see (
7.2
)(f)), refers to spacelike separation rather than to non-timelike separation. However, this does not discredit
\perp^{\prime}
since by (
26
) local orthogonality and local
\perp^{\prime}
-orthogonality coincide.

(128) Lemma.
Let
\Delta,\Gamma\in\mathfrak{S}
. Then

\Delta\perp\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta\perp^{\prime}\Gamma\Rightarrow(\Delta\setminus N)\perp\Gamma\textnormal{ for some null set }N\subset\Delta\Rightarrow E(\Delta)\,E(\Gamma)=0

Proof.
The first arrow
\Rightarrow
holds obviously, the last one due to (
6.2
)(a). As to the second arrow suppose
\Delta\perp^{\prime}\Gamma
. Let
\sigma
be a spacelike hyperplane with
\Delta\subset\sigma
. Then check
N:=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\Delta:\mathfrak{x}\not\perp\Gamma\}\subset\ \{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma\cap\Gamma^{\perp^{\prime}}:\mathfrak{x}\not\perp\Gamma\}\subset\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\leq 0\textnormal{ for all }\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma,(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}\geq 0\textnormal{ for some }\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma\}\subset\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:\sup_{\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma}(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}=0\}
, which is the null set
N(\Gamma,\sigma)
in (
7.3
).∎

Moreover, the analogue of (
12
) for
\perp^{\prime}
-diamonds is valid. It shows in particular that
E
assigns to all measurable spacelike flat bases of a
\perp^{\prime}
-diamond the same operator.

(129) Lemma.

Let
\sigma,\tau
be spacelike hyperplanes. Consider a
\perp^{\prime}
-diamond
M=\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}
with measurable flat base
\Delta\subset\sigma
. Let
\Gamma\subset M\cap\tau
be a measurable set. Then
E(\Gamma)\leq E(\Delta)
.
If
\Gamma^{\land^{\prime}}=M
then
E(\Delta)=E(\Gamma)
.

-7,-1)(3,2.5)

Proof.
By (
7.3
),
M(\Gamma,\sigma)=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{y}\in\Gamma}\{\mathfrak{x}\in\sigma:(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{y})^{\cdot 2}>0\}\subset\Gamma_{\sigma}
equals
\Gamma_{\sigma}
up to a null set of
\sigma
. Recall
\Gamma\subset\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}
. Then, by (
24.2
)(v),
M(\Gamma,\sigma)\subset\Delta
. So causality (
7.2
)(a) implies
E(\Gamma)\leq E(\Gamma_{\sigma})=E(M(\Gamma,\sigma))\leq E(\Delta)
. ∎

We turn to the announced properties of a rep
F^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
. To this end we need the results in (
26
) and (
26
).

(130) Lemma.
Let
F^{\prime}
be a rep of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
.

(a)

Let
L,M\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
with
L\subset M
. Then
F^{\prime}(M\cap L^{\perp^{\prime}})=F^{\prime}(M)-F^{\prime}(L)
. In particular,
F^{\prime}
is monotone.

(b)

Let
M_{n}\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
M_{1}\supset M_{2}\supset M_{3}\supset\dots
. Then
F^{\prime}(\bigcap_{n}M_{n})=\lim_{n}F^{\prime}(M_{n})
.

(c)

Let
M_{n}\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
M_{1}\subset M_{2}\subset M_{3}\subset\dots
. Then
F^{\prime}(\bigvee_{n}M_{n})=\lim_{n}F^{\prime}(M_{n})
.

Proof.

(a) By orthomodularity on has the orthogonal decomposition
M=L\lor(M\cap L^{\perp^{\prime}})
. Hence orthoadditivity of
F^{\prime}
yields
F^{\prime}(M)=F^{\prime}(L)+F^{\prime}(M\cap L^{\perp^{\prime}})\geq F^{\prime}(L)
. — (b) Put
M:=\bigcap_{n}M_{n}
. Apply (
11.3
) and
\sigma
-orthoadditivity of
F^{\prime}
to
(M_{n})
as well to
(M_{1},M_{2},\dots,M_{m},\emptyset,\emptyset,\dots)
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
F^{\prime}(M_{1}\cap M^{\perp^{\prime}})=\sum_{n}F^{\prime}(M_{n}\cap M_{n+1}^{\perp^{\prime}})=\lim_{m}\sum_{n=1}^{m}F^{\prime}(M_{n}\cap M_{n+1}^{\perp^{\prime}})=\lim_{m}F^{\prime}(M_{1}\cap M_{m}^{\perp^{\prime}})
, whence
F^{\prime}(M_{1})-F^{\prime}(M^{\perp^{\prime}})=F^{\prime}(M_{1})-\lim_{m}F^{\prime}(M_{m})
proving (b). — (c) follows from (b) for
(M_{n}^{\perp^{\prime}})
as generally
F^{\prime}(M^{\perp^{\prime}})=I-F^{\prime}(M)
for
M\in\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
.∎

(131) Lemma.
Let
\chi
be the non-timelike not spacelike hyperplane
\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}:=\{g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}:g\cdot\chi\subset\chi\}
the invariance subgroup. Then

\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}=\Big\{(\mathfrak{a},A)\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}:\mathfrak{a}\in\chi,\;A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}z&w\\
0&1/z\end{array}\right),z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\},w\in\mathbb{C}\Big\}

Hence
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}=IST(2)
, i.e., the inhomogeneous group of upper triangular
2\times 2
matrices with determinant
1
. So
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
if and only if

g=\big(\mathfrak{a},\,\operatorname{e}^{\zeta\sigma_{3}/2}\big)\big(0,\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&w\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\big)

with
\mathfrak{a}\in\chi
and
\zeta=\rho+\operatorname{i}\varphi,w=u+\operatorname{i}v\in\mathbb{C}
. The first factor
\big(\mathfrak{a},\,\operatorname{e}^{\zeta\sigma_{3}/2}\big)
represents the obvious part consisting in a translation
\mathfrak{a}
along
\chi
, a boost
\Lambda(\operatorname{e}^{\rho\,\sigma_{3}/2})
along the third axis, and a rotation
\Lambda(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\varphi\,\sigma_{3}/2})
in the
x_{1}x_{2}
plane. Put
r:=\frac{1}{2}|w|^{2}
. The second factor acts by

\Lambda\big(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&w\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\big)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1+r&u&-v&-r\\
u&1&0&-u\\
-v&0&1&v\\
r&u&-v&1-r\end{array}\right)

Proof.
Write
\chi=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot\mathfrak{e}=0\}
with
\mathfrak{e}:=(1,0,0,1)
. Then

g=(\mathfrak{a},A)\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
if and only if
\chi=g\cdot\chi=\{\mathfrak{x}:(g^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x})\cdot\mathfrak{e}=0\}=\{\mathfrak{x}:\mathfrak{x}\cdot(A\cdot\mathfrak{e})=\mathfrak{a}\cdot(A\cdot\mathfrak{e})\}
, whence equivalently
\mathfrak{a}\cdot(A\cdot\mathfrak{e})=0
and
A\cdot\mathfrak{e}=\lambda\mathfrak{e}
for some
\lambda\neq 0
, see after (
24.1
). Actually
\lambda>0
as
A
acts orthocronously. This means
\mathfrak{a}\in\chi
and
A=A_{\ln(\lambda)}A^{\prime}
with
A^{\prime}\in SL(2,\mathbb{C})_{\mathfrak{e}}=E(2)
. Recall
A\in E(2)\Leftrightarrow A_{22}=\overline{A}_{11}
,
A_{21}=0
. The remainder follows by explicit computation.
∎

Now let
W(\alpha)
denote the time shift by
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and recall
that
A_{\rho e}=\operatorname{exp}(\frac{\rho}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{3}e_{k}\sigma_{k})
acts as the boost in direction
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|=1
with rapidity
\rho
. Let
\varsigma\in\{1,-1\}
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{\infty}
denote the set of all measurable subsets of non-timelike not spacelike hyperplanes.

(132) Theorem.
Let
(W,F^{\prime})
be a rep of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
. Set
E(\Delta):=F^{\prime}(\Delta^{\land^{\prime}})
for Borel
\Delta\in\mathfrak{S}
and
P(\Delta):=F^{\prime}(\Delta^{\land^{\prime}})
for Borel

\Delta\in\mathfrak{S}_{\infty}
. Then
E
and
P
extend to all sets of
\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{\infty}
, respectively, and

(a)

(W,E)
is a causal system

(b)

(W,P)
is a Poincaré covariant localization. More precisely, on every non-timelike not spacelike hyperplane
P
is a PM and
W(g)P(\Delta)W(g)^{-1}=P(g\cdot\Delta)
holds for all
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
and
\Delta\in\mathfrak{S}_{\infty}

There is a close relationship between
E
and
P
. Let
\alpha>0
and
e\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
|e|=1
. One has

(c)

P(\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\})=\lim_{\varsigma\rho\to\infty}E(A_{\rho e}\cdot\{x_{0}=0,0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\,\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\})
, where the limit exists since the projections decrease as
\varsigma\rho
increases

(d)

P(\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\})=E(\{x_{0}=\varsigma\alpha,xe\leq\alpha\})E(\{x_{0}=0,xe\geq 0\})

(e)

P(\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\})=E(\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,xe\geq 0\})

Proof.
(a)
(W,E)
is a causal system by (
25
).

(b) Poincaré covariance of
P
follows immediately from the Poincaré covariance of
F^{\prime}
since
(g\cdot\Delta)^{\land^{\prime}}=g\cdot\Delta^{\land^{\prime}}
. — As noted after (
24.1
) a non-timelike hyperplane is a maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set and hence by Eq. (
24.2
) a
\perp^{\prime}
-base. Now
\sigma
-additivity of
P
on every non-timelike not spacelike hyperplane follows as the
\sigma
-additivity of
E
in (
25
). — Extend
P
to all of
\mathfrak{S}_{\infty}
, see (
6.2
).

(c) Obviously the sets
M_{\rho}:=\{-2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-2\varsigma\rho}\leq\varsigma x_{0}-xe\leq 0,0\leq\varsigma x_{0}+xe\leq 2\alpha\}
,
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
converge to
M:=\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\}
for
\varsigma\rho\to\infty
, as

M_{\rho^{\prime}}\subset M_{\rho}\quad\textnormal{if\, }\varsigma\rho<\varsigma\rho^{\prime}\quad\textnormal{and\; }M=\cap_{\rho}\,M_{\rho}

We check
M_{\rho}=\Gamma_{\rho}^{\land^{\prime}}
for
\Gamma_{\rho}:=A_{\rho e}\cdot\{x_{0}=0,0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\}
(
=\{0\leq-\sinh(\rho)\,x_{0}+\cosh(\rho)\,xe\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\}
).
Indeed,
\Gamma_{\rho}^{\land^{\prime}}=A_{\rho e}\cdot\{x_{0}=0,0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\}^{\land^{\prime}}=A_{\rho e}\cdot\{-2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\leq x_{0}-xe\leq 0,0\leq x_{0}+xe\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho}\}=\{-2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho-\rho}\leq x_{0}-xe\leq 0,0\leq x_{0}+xe\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\varsigma\rho+\rho}\}=M_{\rho}
. —
Hence
M_{\rho}

is a
\perp^{\prime}
-diamond with the spacelike flat base
\Gamma_{\rho}
, and
M=M^{\land^{\prime}}
, cf. also (
24.3
)(2). So using (
26
)(b)
one infers
P(M)=F^{\prime}(M)=\lim_{\varsigma\rho\to\infty}F^{\prime}(M_{\rho})=\lim_{\varsigma\rho\to\infty}E(\Gamma_{\rho})
showing (c).

-6,-2.5)(3,3)

(d) By
Eq. (
10.3
