) the right hand side of (d) equals
I-E(\{x_{0}=\varsigma\alpha,xe>\alpha\})-E(\{x_{0}=0,xe<0\})
and hence by definition
=I-F^{\prime}(\{x_{0}=\varsigma\alpha,xe>\alpha\}^{\land^{\prime}})-F^{\prime}(\{x_{0}=0,xe<0\}^{\land^{\prime}})
, and further
=I-F^{\prime}(\{\varsigma x_{0}=xe>\alpha\}^{\land^{\prime}})-F^{\prime}(\{\varsigma x_{0}=xe<0\}^{\land^{\prime}})
due to (

), and finally
=F^{\prime}(\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\}^{\land^{\prime}})=P(\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\})
by orthoadditivity of

.

-8,-1)(2,3)

(e) By (

) one has

.∎

Let us comment on (
26
). The question is what does it mean that a state
\varphi
is
localized
in
\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\}
, i.e.
P(\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\})\varphi=\varphi
. Clearly it is senseless saying that the spatial probability of the system in
\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\}
in the state
\varphi
is
1
, since
\{\varsigma x_{0}=xe\}
is not a reference frame. In the following one recognizes
\varphi
to be a

late-change state

in case of a Dirac or Weyl state.

According to (c), the answer is that
\varphi
is just a state for which the boosted state
W(A_{\varsigma\rho e})\varphi
is localized in
\{x_{0}=0,0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\}
for every rapidity
\rho
.
(Recall Poincaré covariance (
6.2
)(b).)
This is particularly interesting for
\rho\to\infty
as the region
\{x_{0}=0,0\leq xe\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\}
of localization shrinks to an arbitrarily narrow strip at the origin. This is pure Lorentz contraction shown for Dirac and Weyl wave functions in sec.
17
and (
21.1
), respectively.

Furthermore, according to (d),
\varphi
is just a state which is localized in
\{x_{0}=0,xe\geq 0\}

and which at time
\varsigma\alpha
, i.e.,
W(\varsigma\alpha)\varphi
, is localized in
\{x_{0}=0,xe\leq\alpha\}
. (Note that the right hand side of (d) is the product of two projection which commute due to causality, and recall Poincaré covariance (
6.2
)(b).) The equality of the right hand sides of (c) and (d) is shown to be valid for Dirac and Weyl states in (
17
) and (
21.1
), respectively. Moreover, by Eq. (
10.3
), Eq. (
10.4
), and (
10
),
the right hand side of (d)
is a non-trivial projection with infinite dimensional eigenspaces.

Since
P(\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\})=\cup_{\alpha>0}P(\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\})

by (b), actually (e) is a completeness property. Every state localized in
\{x_{0}=0,x_{3}\geq 0\}
can be approximated by the particular states described in (c), (d). This is shown to be true for Dirac and Weyl states in (
17
) and (
22
), respectively.

Hence to every rep
(W,F^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
there are related a causal system
(W,E)
and a Poincaré covariant localization
(W,P)
on
\mathfrak{S}_{\infty}
satisfying (
26
)(c), (d), (e). These relations, which refer to more profound properties of causal systems, are easily elucidated by its geometrical meaning (cf. above). Now we are going to prove the existence of the localization
(W,P)
for the Dirac and the Weyl systems. It is obvious that much more efforts have to be made towards a possible construction of a rep
(W,F^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}borel}
. But, at least, the Dirac and the Weyl systems allow for a further Poincaré covariant extension of
E
(recall (
6.2
) for the first extension) to PM on all non-timelike hyperplanes. More precisely on has (
26
).

Let
\chi
denote the non-timelike not spacelike hyperplane
\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\}
and recall
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
in (

).

(133) Theorem.
Let
(W,E)
be a causal system, which is the finite orthogonal sum of Dirac and Weyl systems.
Then there is a unique Poincaré covariant localization
(W,P)
on
\mathfrak{S}_{\infty}
such that

P(\{\mathfrak{x}\in\chi:0\leq\varsigma x_{3}\leq\alpha\})=\lim_{\rho\to\infty}E\big(\operatorname{e}^{\,\rho\,\sigma_{3}/2}\cdot\{x_{0}=0,0\leq\varsigma x_{3}\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\}\big)

(1)

for all
\alpha>0
,
\varsigma\in\{-1,1\}
. The limit in
(1)
exists since the projections decrease as
\rho
increases.

Proof.
Obviously it is no restriction assuming that
(W,E)
is the Dirac system or a Weyl system. First, in (a) and (b), we prove the existence of
P
. Put
\Delta_{\rho}:=\{x_{0}=0,0\leq\varsigma x_{3}\leq 2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\}
.

(a) Let
(W,E)
be the Dirac system. Then
E^{\textsc{d}\,mom}=\mathcal{F}E^{can}\mathcal{F}^{-1}
,
\big(E^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(\Delta_{\rho})\varphi\big)(p)=\int k_{\rho}(s-p_{3})\varphi(p_{1},p_{2},s)\operatorname{d}s
for integrable
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
with
k_{\rho}(u):=\frac{\exp(\,\operatorname{i}2\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}\varsigma\,u\,)-1}{2\pi\operatorname{i}\varsigma\,u}
. Hence, using Poincaré covariance one finds according to Eq. (
13.1
)

\big(E^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(A_{\rho}\cdot\Delta_{\rho})\varphi\big)(p)=\sum_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\int k_{\rho}(s-q^{\eta}_{3})\pi^{\eta}(p)s(A_{\rho})^{-1}\pi^{\eta^{\prime}}(p_{1},p_{2},s)s(A_{\rho})\varphi(p_{1},p_{2},s^{\prime})\operatorname{d}s

with
q_{3}^{\eta}=\cosh(\rho)p_{3}-\eta\sinh(\rho)\epsilon(p)
and
s^{\prime}:=\eta^{\prime}\sinh(\rho)\epsilon(p_{1},p_{2},s)+\cosh(\rho)s
. Note that
s=\cosh(\rho)s^{\prime}-\eta^{\prime}\sinh(\rho)\epsilon(p_{1},p_{2},s^{\prime})
. We apply Eq. (
13.3
) in order to move
s(A_{\rho})
to the left and substitute the variable of integration
s
by
s^{\prime}
. One finds

\big(E^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(A_{\rho}\cdot\Delta_{\rho})\varphi\big)(p)=\sum_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\int k_{\rho}(s-q^{\eta}_{3})\pi^{\eta}(p)s(A_{\rho})^{-2}\pi^{\eta^{\prime}}(p_{1},p_{2},s^{\prime})\varphi(p_{1},p_{2},s^{\prime})\operatorname{d}s^{\prime}

(2)

Now it is easy to perform in (1) the limit
\rho\to\infty
. Check
|k_{\rho}(u)|\leq\frac{1}{\pi}\alpha\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}
and
s(A_{\rho})^{-2}=\operatorname{diag}(\operatorname{e}^{-\rho},\operatorname{e}^{\rho},\operatorname{e}^{\rho},\operatorname{e}^{-\rho})
, whence
\parallel\!\!k_{\rho}(s-q^{\eta}_{3})s(A_{\rho})^{-2}\!\!\parallel\leq\frac{\alpha}{\pi}
for
\rho\geq 0
. Due to dominated convergence one gets for every
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\big(E^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(A_{\rho}\cdot\Delta_{\rho})\varphi\big)(p)=\sum_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\int k(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\pi^{\eta}(p)S\pi^{\eta^{\prime}}(p^{\prime})\varphi(p^{\prime})\operatorname{d}p_{3}^{\prime}

(3)

where
\mathfrak{p}:=(\eta\epsilon(p),p)
and
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}=(p_{0}^{\prime},p^{\prime})
with
p^{\prime}_{1}:=p_{1}
,
p^{\prime}_{2}:=p_{2}
,
p^{\prime}_{0}:=\eta^{\prime}\epsilon(p^{\prime})
, and
k(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}):=\frac{\exp(\operatorname{i}\alpha\varsigma(p_{0}-p_{3}-p^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\prime}_{3}))-1}{\operatorname{i}\pi\varsigma(p_{0}-p_{3}-p^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\prime}_{3})}
,
S:=\operatorname{diag}(0,1,1,0)
.

The right hand side of (1) is a special case of the right hand side of (
26
)(c). Hence, by (
26
), the limit
\lim_{\rho\to\infty}E^{\textsc{d}\,mom}(A_{\rho}\cdot\Delta_{\rho})\varphi
exists, whence for integrable
\varphi
its value at every

One notes that

is just the kernel for

in (

)(b). Actually the next step is to show that the shell representation of

Straightforward computations yield the unwieldy

\displaystyle S\pi^{\eta}(p)V(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon(p)}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\

\displaystyle U_{\eta}(p)\pi^{\eta^{\prime}}(p)S

\displaystyle\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\sqrt{\epsilon(p)-\eta p_{3}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\

Now it is easy to verify that in the shell representation the right hand side of (3) coincides for every

The considerations so far show by (

. We turn to its uniqueness. Let

(c) Put

. For

,

\langle a,b[_{\chi}:=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\chi:a_{1}\leq x_{3}-x_{1}<b_{1},a_{2}\leq x_{3}+x_{2}<b_{2},a_{3}\leq x_{3}<b_{3}\}

in

The claim is that every figure is the union of finitely many disjoint boxes and that

\mathcal{Q}_{\chi}
is an algebra.

Indeed, it is well-known that the unions of finitely many disjoint boxes
\langle a,b[:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:a_{k}\leq x_{k}<b_{k},k=1,2,3\}
,
a,b\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
form an algebra
\mathcal{Q}^{3}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
22
22

22

\mathcal{Q}^{3}
is
\cap
-stable since the intersection of two boxes is a box. Check that the complement of a box

is the union of disjoint boxes. Then also the complement of a figure is the union of finitely many disjoint boxes. Therefore
\mathcal{Q}^{3}
is an algebra. Finally, let

be a figure with boxes

. Then

is the union of disjoint boxes.
The bijective linear map

(with

. This confirms the claim.

(d) The claim now is
P|_{\mathcal{Q}_{\chi}}=P^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{Q}_{\chi}}
. Indeed, due to (1),

and

coincide at the boxes
\{0\leq x_{3}<\alpha\}
and
\{-\alpha\leq x_{3}<0\}
,
\alpha>0
. (Note that

and

,

.) By continuity

,

are equal also at the boxes
\{0\leq x_{3}<\infty\}
,
\{-\infty<x_{3}<0\}
. Then one easily infers from additivity that

,

coincide at all boxes

,
\alpha,\beta\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}
,
\alpha\leq\beta
.

Next check
\{\mathfrak{x}\in\chi:a_{1}\leq x_{3}-x_{1}<b_{1}\}=\kappa\cdot B(a_{1},b_{1})
and
\{\mathfrak{x}\in\chi:a_{2}\leq x_{3}+x_{2}<b_{2}\}=\kappa^{\prime}\cdot B(a_{2},b_{2})
for

,
\kappa^{\prime}:=I_{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{i}\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})
in

, see (
26
). Hence

\langle a,b[_{\chi}=\big(\kappa\cdot B(a_{1},b_{1})\big)\cap\big(\kappa^{\prime}\cdot B(a_{2},b_{2})\big)\cap B(a_{3},b_{3})
.
Therefore
P(\langle a,b[_{\chi})=P\big(\kappa\cdot B(a_{1},b_{1})\big)P\big(\kappa^{\prime}\cdot B(a_{2},b_{2})\big)P\big(B(a_{3},b_{3})\big)
and hence
P(\langle a,b[_{\chi})=W(\kappa)P\big(B(a_{1},b_{1})\big)W(\kappa)^{-1}\,W(\kappa^{\prime})P\big(B(a_{2},b_{2})\big)W(\kappa^{\prime})^{-1}\,P\big(B(a_{3},b_{3})\big)
by

-covariance. This equation holds equally for
P^{\prime}
in place of

. Since generally
P\big(B(\alpha,\beta)\big)=P^{\prime}\big(B(\alpha,\beta)\big)
, it follows
P(\langle a,b[_{\chi})=P^{\prime}(\langle a,b[_{\chi})
for all boxes, whence the claim.

(e) Now we are ready for the proof of
P=P^{\prime}
. For every vector
\varphi

defines a finite

\mathcal{Q}_{\chi}
. It allows a unique extension to a measure

(see e.g.

\langle\varphi,P(\cdot)\varphi\rangle
and

at Borel sets.
One infers that

and

coincide at Borel sets, whence

arguing as in (
6.2
).∎

(134) Corollary.

P
from
(
26
)
satisfies
(c), (d), (e)
in
(
26
)
. One obtains
an alternative proof of
(

)
and
(

)
.

Proof.
Note
B(\varsigma e)\cdot\{x_{0}=x_{3},0\leq\varsigma x_{3}<\alpha\}=\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe<\alpha\}
. Hence by covariance one infers that (c) and (1) are equivalent. So (c) holds.
Therefore, due to (
17
) and Eq. (
21.5
)
also (d) holds. Hence, by
(
10
)(b),
P(\{0\leq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\})\leq E(\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,xe\geq 0\})
. Replacing
(e,\varsigma)
by
(-e,-\varsigma)
one gets

P(\{0\geq\varsigma x_{0}=xe\})\leq E(\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,xe\leq 0\})
. The sum of the left hand sides is
I
. Also the sum of the right hand sides equals
I
. This proves that the inequalities actually are equalities. Hence (e) holds and
I=\lim_{|\alpha|\to\infty}E(\{x_{0}=\alpha,|xe|\leq|\alpha|\})
follows.∎

As an example consider the maximal
\perp^{\prime}
-set
\sigma_{M}\cup M\cup\tau_{M}
from (

). One has

Indeed, it suffices to verify this equation in the special case Eq. (

). This follows easily from

P(\{\mathfrak{x}:0\leq x_{0}=xe\leq\alpha\})=E(\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=\alpha,xe\leq\alpha\})-E(\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=0,xe\leq 0\})
, which holds by

), Eq. (

). Furthermore, because of (

-9,-1)(2,3)

27.
Systems with Causal Time Evolution

After having studied in detail the massive and massless causal systems of fundamental importance, i.e., in sec.
13

up to sec.
19
the Dirac system

(W^{\textsc{d}},E^{\textsc{d}})
for every positive mass and in sec.
21
, sec.
22
the two Weyl systems
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
, now the goal is to determine all other causal systems.
In so doing we will consider only
physical systems
, i.e., systems with non-negative mass-squared operator

C=H^{2}-P_{1}^{2}-P_{2}^{2}-P_{3}^{2}\,\,\geq\,0

and finite spinor dimension (sec.
27.2
). Then it will turn out that the Dirac and the Weyl systems are the only irreducible causal systems. Actually we will not succeed as yet to rule out certain obviously unlikely irreducible massive and massless SCT, see the conclusions at the end of sec.
28
and sec.
29
. Recall that there exist causal systems (
25
), which do not satisfy the properties
C\geq 0
and finite spinor dimension.
We start studying a preliminary object, the SCT. For this recall the definitions in sec.
6.2
and
7.1
, in particular (
7.1
).

(135) Definition.
A
system with causal time evolution
(SCT) consists of a representation
W
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
and, with respect to
W
, a Poincaré covariant WL
E
such that the time evolution is causal, i.e.,

W(t)E(\Delta)W(t)^{-1}\leq E(\Delta_{t})

for all measurable
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
and all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. An SCT is called
massive
if
C>0
, and
massless
if
C=0
. If
(W,E)
is an SCT then any SCT, which is unitarily equivalent to
(SWS^{-1},E)
, where
S
is some unitary operator commuting with the representation
W|_{\mathbb{R}\otimes ISU(2)}
of the little kinematical group, is said to be
unitarily related
to
(W,E)
.

Clearly, causal systems are SCT. A complete explicit description of the SCT is provided in the following sections. The massive ones are treated in
[
5
]
and here we summarize the relevant results in (
28.1
). The massless SCT are derived in sec.
29
. The main result on massless SCT is (
29.3
).

27.1.
Position representation

In analyzing the SCT
(W,E)

the imprimitivity theorem by Mackey 1949, 1955
[
76
]
,
[
77
]
, reported in (
30.2
), is essential. By this theorem, up to unitary equivalence,
W|_{ISU(2)}
is a representation induced from a representation
D
of
SU(2)
and
E
is the related system of imprimitivity. Let
D
act on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{S}
. Then
(W,E)
is unitarily equivalent to the position representation
(W^{pos},E^{can})
(cf.
[
1
]
,
[
78
]
), which is characterized by

(W^{pos}(b,B)\psi)(x):=D(B)\,\psi(B^{-1}\cdot(x-b)),\quad E^{can}(\Delta)\psi:=1_{\Delta}\psi

(27.1)

on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathcal{S})
with
(b,B)\in ISU(2)
and
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
a measurable set.
E^{can}
is called the
canonical
PM. Note that
W
is not determined by
W|_{ISU(2)}
.

The spin
-j
components for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}/2
of
W|_{ISU(2)}
are the eigenspaces to the eigenvalues
j(j+1)
of the square of the spin vector operator
S:=J-X\times P
. The orbital angular momentum
X\times P
refers to the
causal
position operator
X
, which in position representation is given by
\big(X_{k}^{pos}\psi\big)(x)=x_{k}\psi(x)
.

27.2.
Spinor basis

By Eq. (
27.1
), for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
the vector
\psi(x)
is a spinor, whence
\mathcal{S}
is called the spinor space. Its dimension is called the
spinor dimension
of
W
. Clearly it is an invariant and characteristic for the SCT.

We shall write
D
as the orthogonal sum
\oplus_{j}\nu_{j}D^{(j)}
of the spin components, where

\nu_{j}\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\infty\}

(27.2)

is the
multiplicity
of the irreducible representation
D^{(j)}
for every spin
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}/2
. Then the spinor dimension is
\sum_{j}(2j+1)\nu_{j}
.
The standard matrix form of
D^{(j)}
satisfies

D^{(j)}(\operatorname{diag}(c,\overline{c}))=\operatorname{diag}(c^{2j},c^{2j-2},\dots,c^{-2j+2},c^{-2j})

(27.3)

for
c\in\mathbb{C}
,
|c|=1
. Accordingly the standard basis for
\mathbb{C}^{2j+1}
is denoted by
(|j,s\rangle)
, where the label
s
runs through the helicity values
2j,2j-2,\dots,-2j+2,-2j
. In order to obtain a basis of the spinor space
\mathcal{S}
one has to take account of the multiplicities
\nu_{j}
by an additional index
\iota
indicating the
\iota
-th copy of
D^{(j)}
. One gets the orthogonal spinor basis

(|j,\iota,s\rangle)\text{ with }j\in|s|+\mathbb{N}_{0},\;\iota=1,2,\dots,\nu_{j},\,s=2j,2j-2,\dots,-2j+2,-2j

(27.4)

From now on we will consider only SCT with
finite spinor dimension
. So all
\nu_{j}
are finite and only finitely many of them are non-zero. From a physical point of view this is a reasonable restriction as infinite dimensional spinor theories have not found application so far.

27.3.
Momentum representation of time evolution

The Fourier transformation
\mathcal{F}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathcal{S})\simeq\mathcal{S}\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
acts as
I_{\mathcal{S}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Then the momentum representation
W^{mom}:=\mathcal{F}\,W^{pos}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}
satisfies

\big(W^{mom}(b,B)\varphi\big)(p)=\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}D(B)\,\varphi(B^{-1}\cdot p)

(27.5)

Let
H
be the generator of the time evolution
W(t)=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}tH}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Since
H
is translational invariant
there are self-adjoint matrices
h(p)
acting on
\mathcal{S}
such that

(H^{mom}\varphi)(p)=h(p)\varphi(p)

(27.6)

defined on its natural domain.
H
is rotational invariant, too. So
h
is rotational covariant and
h
can be chosen such that
h(\rho\,e_{3})
,
\rho\geq 0
, commutes
with
D(B)
if
B
is diagonal, and
h(p)=D(B(p))h(|p|e_{3})D(B(p))^{-1}
, whence

h(B\cdot p)=D(B)\,h(p)\,D(B)^{-1}

(27.7)

for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
without exception (see
[
5
, Eq. (14)]
).

Here
B(p)
denotes the
helicity cross section
satisfying
B(p)\in SU(2)
and
|p|B(p)\cdot e_{3}=p
for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Explicitly

B(p)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{+}&-\overline{b}\,a_{-}\\
b\,a_{-}&a_{+}\end{array}\right),\quad a_{\pm}:=\sqrt{\frac{|p|\pm p_{3}}{2|p|}},\quad b:=\frac{p_{1}+ip_{2}}{|p_{1}+ip_{2}|}\textrm{ for }(p_{1},p_{2})\neq 0

(27.8)

and
B(\alpha e_{3})
equals
I_{2}
if
\alpha\geq 0
and
-i\sigma_{2}
if
\alpha<0
. Note
B(\lambda p)=B(p)
for all
\lambda>0
and for
|p|=1
the important relation

B(p)\,\sigma_{3}\,B(p)^{-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}p_{k}\sigma_{k}

(27.9)

Analogous formulae hold for
W(t)
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
in place of
H
such that

\big(W^{mom}(t)\varphi\big)(p)=v_{t}(p)\varphi(p),\quad\,v_{t}(p)=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th(p)}

(27.10)

27.4.
Causal time evolution

As proven in
[
5
, Theorem 1]
the causal time evolution of the SCT
(W,E)

implies that
h
is linear in momentum, i.e., for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

h(p)=A_{1}p_{1}+A_{2}p_{2}+A_{3}p_{3}+M

(27.11)

where
A_{k}
,
M
are constant self-adjoint matrices in spinor space with
||\sum_{k}p_{k}A_{k}||=\gamma|p|
for some
\gamma\in[0,1]
.
By rotational covariance Eq. (
27.7
) of
h
one has

D(B)\big(\sum_{k=1}^{3}p_{k}A_{k}\big)\,D(B)^{-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}(B\cdot p)_{k}A_{k},\quad D(B)MD(B)^{-1}=M

(27.12)

Further, since the mass-squared operator
C
is supposed to be non-negative, by
[
5
, Lemma 16, Lemma 1]
one has
\big(C^{mom}\varphi\big)(p)=M^{2}\varphi(p)
. Hence
C
commutes with
(W,E)
and the spectrum of
C
is finite non-negative. This implies

(136) Lemma.
Every SCT is the finite orthogonal sum of SCT with
C=m^{2}I
for pairwise different scalars
m\geq 0
. The matrices
A_{k}
,
M
satisfy
\{A_{k},A_{j}\}=2\delta_{kj}I
,
\{A_{k},M\}=0
.

In particular
\gamma=1
follows. Further analysis will reveal the structure of the matrices
A_{k}
,
M
.

28.
Massive Causal Systems

In
[
5
]
massive SCT are treated in detail.
23
23

23

In
[
5
]
a massive SCT is called a massive causal system.
We summarize the main results from
[
5
]
providing a complete description of the massive SCT. Then the aim is to single out the massive causal systems. To this, in view of (
7.2
), we study the dependence of their localizations on the frame of reference.

28.1.
Construction of the massive SCT

One starts from the fundamental Dirac localization
(V^{\textsc{d}},U^{\textsc{d}},E^{\textsc{d}}):=(W^{\textsc{d}}|_{\mathbb{R}\otimes ISU(2)},E^{\textsc{d}})
(see sec.
13
). By a simple construction, which enlarges the spinor space, one gets for every positive mass
m
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}
the
n
-th
Dirac tensor-localization

(V_{n},U_{n},E_{n}):=(V^{\textsc{d}},U^{\textsc{d}},E^{\textsc{d}})\otimes(I_{n},U^{(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2})},I_{n})

(28.1)

with
I_{n}
the identity on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
U^{(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}(b,B):=D^{(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}(B)
. In particular,
h_{n}(p)=h^{\textsc{d}}(p)\otimes I_{n}
with
h^{\textsc{d}}(p)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\alpha_{k}p_{k}+\beta m
, whence the Dirac tensor-matrices are
\alpha_{k}\otimes I_{n}
,
\beta\otimes I_{n}
. In the position representation
E_{n}
is the canonical PM. The time evolutions
V_{n}

are causal. The Dirac tensor-localizations are irreducible and mutually unitarily inequivalent.

Every representation
(V_{n},U_{n})
for mass
m>0
of the little kinematical group can be extended to a representation
W_{n}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{n})
. For
(t,b,A)\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
the latter is given by

\big(W^{mom}_{n}(t,b,A)\varphi\big)(p):=\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\left(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\eta\epsilon(p)}\pi^{\eta}(p)s(A)^{*-1}\otimes D^{(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\big(R(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},A)\big)\right)\varphi(q^{\eta})

(28.2)

As to the notations recall
Eq. (
13.1
) and following. Furthermore recall the Wigner rotation

R(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},A)

with respect to the canonical cross section
Q
with
the important property
R(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},B)=B
for
B\in SU(2)
(see (
3.3
)). Note that
W_{1}
is just the Dirac representation
W^{\textsc{d}}
.
Hence for every mass
m>0
a sequence
(W_{n},E_{n})
of irreducible and mutually unitarily inequivalent massive SCT is constructed.

(W_{1},E_{1})
is the Dirac system
(W^{\textsc{d}},E^{\textsc{d}})
studied in sec.
13
.

From
[
5
, sec. B,D,E]
we extract

(137) Theorem.

(i)
Every massive SCT
(W,E)
is a unique orthogonal sum of finitely many massive SCT for different masses
m>0
.

(ii)
If
(W,E)
is a massive SCT for mass
m>0
, then
it is unitarily related to a finite orthogonal sum of
(W_{n},E_{n})
for mass
m
with uniquely determined multiplicities.

(iii)
Finally, a massive SCT
(W,E)
is unitarily equivalent to an orthogonal sum of finitely many
(W_{n},E_{n})
with possibly various masses if and only if the booster of
W
satisfies

N=\frac{1}{2}\{X,H\}+\frac{\operatorname{sgn}(H)}{C^{1/2}+|H|}P\times\left(J-X\times P-\frac{\operatorname{i}}{4}[X,H]\times[X,H]\right)

(28.3)

where
X=(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})
denotes the causal position operator associated with
E
.

28.2.
Determination of the massive causal systems

By (
14
) the Dirac localization is causal and hence, by (
7.2
), frame-independent. In contrast, we recall, there is no Dirac state localized by NWL in a bounded region with respect to two frames moving relative to each other (see sec.
3.3
). This may be not surprising as, with respect to NWL, the Dirac time evolution is not causal. But, surprisingly, as shown in (
28.2
), an SCT need not localize frame-independently.

Let
(W,E)
be an irreducible massive SCT. By (
28.1
)(i),(ii) it is unitarily related to a finite orthogonal sum of
(W_{n},E_{n})
for some mass
m>0
. So, in the position representation,
E
is the canonical PM and, in the momentum representation,

\big(W^{mom}(t,b,A)\varphi\big)(p)=\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}s(p)^{-1}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\eta\epsilon(p)}w^{\eta}(A,p)s(q^{\eta})\varphi(q^{\eta})

(28.4)

for almost all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Here,
w^{\eta}(A,p):=\pi^{\eta}(p)s(A)^{*-1}\otimes D^{\prime}\big(R(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},A)\big)
with some finite dimensional representation
D^{\prime}
of
ISU(2)
. Further, according to (
27
),
S
is some unitary operator commuting with
W|_{\mathbb{R}\times ISU(2)}
so that
(S^{mom}\varphi)(p)=s(p)\varphi(p)
with unitary matrices
s(p)
acting on spinor space, satisfying rotational covariance
s(B\cdot p)=D(B)s(p)D(B)^{-1}
for
D:=(D^{\frac{1}{2}}\oplus D^{\frac{1}{2}})\otimes D^{\prime}
, and commuting with the projections on the energy eigenspaces
s(p)(\pi^{\eta}(p)\otimes I^{\prime})=(\pi^{\eta}(p)\otimes I^{\prime})s(p)
with
I^{\prime}:=D^{\prime}(I_{2})
. Therefore
s(p)=D(B(p))s(|p|e_{3})D(B(p))^{-1}
, and
s(re_{3})
,
r\geq 0
commutes with
D(B)
for
B
diagonal and with
(r\alpha_{3}+m\beta)\otimes I^{\prime}
(see Eq. (
27.12
) with
h(p)=h^{\textsc{d}}(p)\otimes I^{\prime}
in Eq. (
27.11
)).

(138) Theorem.
Let
(W,E)
be an irreducible massive SCT, which localizes frame-independently. Suppose that
p\mapsto s(p)
(see
Eq. (
28.4
)
) has an entire extension. Then
(W,E)
is unitarily related to a finite multiple of
the Dirac system
(W^{\textsc{d}},E^{\textsc{d}})
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi
be localized in a bounded region. Then, by assumption, so is
\varphi_{\rho}:=W^{mom}(A_{\rho})\varphi
, where
A_{\rho}:=\operatorname{e}^{\frac{\rho}{2}\sigma_{3}}
,
\rho\neq 0
. Hence, by the Paley-Wiener Theorem, these functions are the restriction of entire functions on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, still denoted by
\varphi
and
\varphi_{\rho}
.
As
q^{\eta}=(p_{1},p_{2},-\eta\beta\epsilon+\alpha p_{3})
with
\alpha:=\cosh\rho
,
\beta:=\sinh\rho
, and
\epsilon=\sqrt{p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}+p_{3}^{2}+m^{2}}
, there are entire functions
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
such that
(s(A_{\rho})^{*-1}\otimes I_{n})s(q^{\eta})\varphi(q^{\eta})=\phi_{1}(p)-\eta\epsilon\phi_{2}(p)
. Furthermore, explicit computation yields

R(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},A_{\rho})=d(\eta p)^{-1/2}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\gamma(m+\epsilon)-\delta\eta p_{3}&\delta\eta(p_{1}-\operatorname{i}p_{2})\\
-\delta\eta(p_{1}+\operatorname{i}p_{2})&\gamma(m+\epsilon)-\delta\eta p_{3}\end{array}\right)

(28.5)

with
d(p):=(m+\epsilon)(m+\alpha\,\epsilon-\beta p_{3})
and constants
\gamma:=\cosh\frac{\rho}{2}
,
\delta:=\sinh\frac{\rho}{2}
.

Note that each component
D^{(j)}_{kl}(M)
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
2j
of the entries of
M
. Hence there are matrix-valued functions
A^{(j)}
,
B^{(j)}
with polynomial entries such that

D^{(j)}\big(R(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},A_{\rho})\big)=\varDelta^{(j)}(\eta p)\big(A^{(j)}(\eta p)+\epsilon B^{(j)}(\eta p)\big)
with
\varDelta^{(j)}(\eta p):=d(\eta p)^{-j}I_{2j+1}
. Recall that
D^{\prime}
is the orthogonal sum of some
D^{(j)}
. Accordingly we write
D^{\prime}\big(R(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},A_{\rho})\big)=\varDelta^{\prime}(\eta p)\big(A^{\prime}(\eta p)+\epsilon B^{\prime}(\eta p)\big)
.
Hence, by Eq. (
28.4
), one has

2s(p)\varphi_{\rho}(p)=\sum_{\eta}\left(\big(\text{{\it\small{I}}}_{4}+\frac{\eta}{\epsilon}h^{\textsc{d}}(p)\big)\otimes\varDelta^{\prime}(\eta p)\big(A^{\prime}(\eta p)+\epsilon B^{\prime}(\eta p)\big)\right)\big(\phi_{1}(p)-\eta\epsilon\phi_{2}(p)\big)

Now we examine the point
p_{*}:=(\operatorname{i}m,0,0)
. There
\epsilon
vanishes. Hence
one infers
\sum_{\eta}\big(\eta I_{4}\otimes\varDelta^{\prime}(\eta p_{*})A^{\prime}(\eta p_{*})\big)\,\xi_{*}=0
for
\xi_{*}:=(h^{\textsc{d}}(p_{*})\otimes I^{\prime})\phi_{1}(p_{*})=\big(h^{\textsc{d}}(p_{*})s(A_{\rho}^{*-1})\otimes I^{\prime}\big)s(p_{*})\varphi(p_{*})
. Here, plainly,
s(p_{*})
denotes the analytic continuation of
s
at
p_{*}
. As
\varDelta^{\prime}(\eta p_{*})A^{\prime}(\eta p_{*})=D^{\prime}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\gamma&\operatorname{i}\eta\delta\\
-\operatorname{i}\eta\delta&\gamma\end{array}\right)
, one gets equivalently

\xi_{*}=\left(I_{4}\otimes D^{\prime}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha&\operatorname{i}\beta\\
-\operatorname{i}\beta&\alpha\end{array}\right)\right)\,\xi_{*}

(
\star
)

This condition implies that
D^{\prime}
is a multiple of the unit representation. Indeed, assume the contrary. Recall that
\varphi(p_{*})=\int\operatorname{e}^{mx_{1}}\psi(x)\operatorname{d}^{3}x

for some
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4}\times\mathbb{C}^{n})
with bounded support. So one may alter
\psi
a bit within its support to guarantee
\varphi(p_{*})\neq 0
. Further, note that
s(p_{*})
is invertible, as the equation
I=s(z)s(\overline{z})^{*}
holds true by analytic continuation for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Hence one may multiply
\psi
by a suitable constant spinor matrix in order that
\xi_{*}
does not satisfy Eq.
(\star)
. This accomplishes the proof. ∎

(139) Corollary.

The SCT
(W_{n},E_{n})
for
n>1
do not localize frame-independently and hence are not causal.

Proof.
The result in (
28.2
) applies with
s(p)=I_{4n}
for all
p
.∎

In conclusion we are convinced that the assumption of analyticity of s in (
28.2
) can be dropped.
Compare also the analogous result (
29.4
) for massless SCT going without such an assumption.
Anyway, due to (
7.2
), the higher Dirac tensor-systems
(W_{n},E_{n})
,
n>1
are revealed to be mathematical artefacts, interim results in determining the massive causal systems.
It is quite sure that the only irreducible massive SCT, which are causal, are unitarily equivalent to the Dirac systems
(W^{\textsc{d}},E^{\textsc{d}})
for masses
m>0
studied in sec.
13
.

29.
Massless Causal Systems

The analysis of the massless causal systems proceeds analogously to that of the massive ones. However there is an important difference arising from the matrices
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},M
(
27.4
). In the massive case one is dealing with the Clifford algebra with four basis elements, which has only one irreducible representation, the
4
-dimensional Dirac representation (see
[
5
, Lemma 2]
). The massless case
M=0
, however, is determined by the Clifford algebra generated by three basis elements, which has just two inequivalent irreducible representations, the
2
-dimensional Weyl representations (see (
29
)).

(140) Lemma.
Let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
be any (respectively, self-adjoint) square matrices. Then, they satisfy
\{A_{k},A_{j}\}=2\delta_{kj}I
if and only if, up to (respectively, unitary) equivalence,

A_{k}=(\sigma_{k}\otimes I^{\prime})\oplus(-\sigma_{k}\otimes I^{\prime\prime})

with
\sigma_{k}
,
k=1,2,3
the Pauli matrices and
I^{\prime},I^{\prime\prime}
unit matrices, and
\oplus
the direct (respectively, orthogonal) sum.

Proof.
The group
G
generated by the Clifford algebra
\{\alpha_{k},\alpha_{j}\}=2\delta_{kj}1
,
k,j=1,2,3
has order
16
. Its elements are
1,-1
and all words in
\alpha_{k}
which result from
\pm\,\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}
by canceling at most
2
of them. There are
10
conjugacy classes, namely
\{1\}
,
\{-1\}
,
\{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\},

\{-\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\}
, and
\{g,-g\}
for all other group elements
g
. Every representation of
G
, which maps
-1
to
I
, actually is a representation of the abelian group
G/\{1,-1\}
. Hence there are
8
inequivalent one-dimensional representations of
G
.
So, up to equivalence, there are just two inequivalent irreducible representation
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
left. Let
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
be their dimensions. They satisfy
16=8\cdot 1^{2}+d_{1}^{2}+d_{2}^{2}
, whence
d_{1}=d_{2}=2
. One easily checks that
\rho_{1}(\pm 1):=\pm I_{2}
and
\rho_{1}(\alpha_{k}):=\sigma_{k}
,
k=1,2,3
defines an irreducible representation of
G
. Similarly does
\rho_{2}(\pm 1):=\pm I_{2}
and
\rho_{2}(\alpha_{k}):=-\sigma_{k}
,
k=1,2,3
. Since
\operatorname{tr}(\rho_{1}(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}))=2\operatorname{i}\neq\operatorname{tr}(\rho_{2}(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}))=-2\operatorname{i}
,
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are inequivalent. By the same reason,
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
are inequivalent even as representations of the Clifford algebra, known as Weyl representations. Finally, if one limits oneself to self-adjoint representations of the algebra, which means to unitary representations of the group, equivalence becomes unitary equivalence. Moreover, the direct sum must be replaced by an orthogonal sum. ∎

(141) Lemma.

Let
D
be a finite dimensional representation of
SU(2)
and let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
be self-adjoint matrices. They satisfy rotational covariance
D(B)\big(\sum_{k=1}^{3}p_{k}A_{k}\big)\,D(B)^{-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}(B\cdot p)_{k}A_{k}
(cf.
Eq. (
27.12
)
) and
\{A_{k},A_{j}\}=2\delta_{kj}I
(cf.
(
27.4
)
) if and only if, up to unitary equivalence,
A_{k}=(\sigma_{k}\otimes I^{\prime})\oplus(-\sigma_{k}\otimes I^{\prime\prime})
and

D=(D^{(\frac{1}{2})}\otimes D^{\prime})\oplus(D^{(\frac{1}{2})}\otimes D^{\prime\prime})

for some representations
D^{\prime}
,
D^{\prime\prime}
of
SU(2)
.

Proof.
By Lemma (
29
) we may assume at once
A_{k}=(\sigma_{k}\otimes I^{\prime})\oplus(-\sigma_{k}\otimes I^{\prime\prime})
. Recall the relation
B(\sum_{k}p_{k}\sigma_{k})B^{*}=\sum_{k}(B\cdot p)_{k}\sigma_{k}
.
So the representation
L:=(D^{(\frac{1}{2})}\otimes I^{\prime})\oplus(D^{(\frac{1}{2})}\otimes I^{\prime\prime})
of
SU(2)
satisfies
L(B)\big(\sum_{k=1}^{3}p_{k}A_{k}\big)\,L(B)^{-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}(B\cdot p)_{k}A_{k}
. This implies the “if”part of the assertion. As to the “only if”part, note that
L(B)^{-1}D(B)
commutes with
A_{k}
,
k=1,2,3
.
Note that
A_{k}=A^{\prime}_{k}\oplus A^{\prime\prime}_{k}
with
A^{\prime}_{k}:=\sigma_{k}\otimes I^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime\prime}_{k}:=-\sigma_{k}\otimes I^{\prime\prime}
determines the primary decomposition of the representation of
G
(see the proof of (
29
)) defined by
A_{k}
,
k=1,2,3
. Therefore
L(B)^{-1}D(B)=R^{\prime}(B)\oplus R^{\prime\prime}(B)
for some
R^{\prime}(B)
and
R^{\prime\prime}(B)
, which commute with
A^{\prime}_{k}
and
A^{\prime\prime}_{k}
, respectively.
Hence
R^{\prime}(B)=I_{2}\otimes D^{\prime}(B)
and
R^{\prime\prime}(B)=I_{2}\otimes D^{\prime\prime}(B)
with uniquely determined matrices
D^{\prime}(B)
,
D^{\prime\prime}(B)
for every
B\in SU(2)
. Then
D(B)=L(B)\big(R^{\prime}(B)\oplus R^{\prime\prime}(B)\big)
yields the formula on
D
. The latter implies that
D^{\prime}
,
D^{\prime\prime}
are representations of
SU(2)
.∎

29.1.
The massless WL with causal time evolution

The following results (
29.1
), (
29.1
), and (
29.1
) are an immediate consequence of the foregoing considerations (
27.4
), (
29
), (
29
). Recall that
\big(U^{pos}(b,B)\psi\big)(x)=D(B)\,\psi(B^{-1}\cdot(x-b))
and
E^{pos}=E^{can}
, i.e.,
E^{pos}(\Delta)\psi=1_{\Delta}\psi
. A WL with causal time evolution (
7.1
) is called massless if
C=0
.

(142) Theorem.
Let
(V,U,E)
be a massless WL with causal time evolution of smallest spinor dimension. Then the spinor dimension is
2
and
D=D^{(\frac{1}{2})}
. Moreover, there is
\chi\in\{+,-\}
such that, up to unitary equivalence,

H^{\chi pos}=\chi\left(\sigma_{1}\frac{1}{\operatorname{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\sigma_{2}\frac{1}{\operatorname{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}+\sigma_{3}\frac{1}{\operatorname{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{3}}\right)

(29.1)

The result in (
29.1
) is a derivation of the Weyl equations as a mere consequence of the principle of causality in relativistic quantum mechanics.

(143) Definition.
The massless WL with causal time evolution, for which in position representation
H
is given by
Eq. (
29.1
) and
D=D^{(\frac{1}{2})}
,
is called the
right-handed
, respectively
left-handed

Weyl localization
. It is denoted by
(V^{\textsc{w}\chi},U^{\textsc{w}},E^{\textsc{w}})
with
\chi=+
and
\chi=-
, respectively.

(144) Theorem.
Let
(V,U,E)
be an irreducible massless WL with causal time evolution. Then there exists and
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\chi\in\{+,-\}
such that
(V,U,E)
is unitarily equivalent to the massless WL with causal time evolution
(V_{n}^{\chi},U_{n},E_{n})
given by

\displaystyle V_{n}^{\chi}(t)

\displaystyle=V^{\textsc{w}\chi}(t)\otimes I_{n}

time translations

\displaystyle U_{n}(b)

\displaystyle=U^{\textsc{w}}(b)\otimes I_{n}

space translations

\displaystyle U_{n}(B)

\displaystyle=U^{\textsc{w}}(B)\otimes D^{(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}(B)

rotations

\displaystyle E_{n}(\Delta)

\displaystyle=E^{\textsc{w}}(\Delta)\otimes I_{n}

localization operators

where
I_{n}
is the identity on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

(145) Definition.

Let the massless WL with causal time evolution
(V_{n}^{\chi},U_{n},E_{n})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
be called
Weyl tensor-localizations
.

Note that the Weyl tensor-localizations for
n=1
are just the Weyl localizations. The spinor space of the Weyl tensor-localizations for
n
is
\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{n}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{2n}
. Hence the spinor dimension is
2n
. As
D_{n}\simeq D^{(\frac{1}{2})}\otimes D^{(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\simeq D^{(\frac{n}{2}-1)}\oplus D^{(\frac{n}{2})}
, the helicity spectrum is
\{-\frac{n}{2},-\frac{n}{2}+1,\dots,\frac{n}{2}\}
with multiplicity
1
for helicity
\frac{n}{2},-\frac{n}{2}
and
2
else.

(146) Theorem.
Every massless WL with causal time evolution is unitarily equivalent to a finite orthogonal sum of Weyl tensor-localizations with uniquely determined multiplicities.

We will show that each representation
(V_{n}^{\chi},U_{n})
of the little kinematical group can be extended to an, up to unitary equivalence, unique representation
W_{n}^{\chi}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
. The following insertion sec.
29.2
provides some facts used for this objective.

29.2.
On irreducible massless representations of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}

Let
\mathcal{O}^{0,\eta}
for
\eta=\pm
denote the light half-cone
\{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\{0\}:p_{0}=\eta\,|p|\}
endowed with the Lorentz invariant measure and let
\mathcal{O}^{0}:=\mathcal{O}^{0,+}\cup\mathcal{O}^{0,-}=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\{0\}:|p_{0}|=|p|\}
denote the light cone.

The
1
-dimensional representations
(\mathfrak{a},A)\mapsto\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}(\eta a_{0}-a_{3})}(A_{11})^{2s}
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}/2
of the inhomogeneous little group
IE(2)\sqsubset\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
(recall
A\in E(2)
if and only if
A\in SL(2,\mathbb{C})
with
A_{21}=0
,
A_{22}=\overline{A}_{11}
) induce the irreducible finite spinor dimensional massless representations of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
. They act on
L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{0,\eta})
by

\big(U^{0,s,\eta}(\mathfrak{a},A)\phi\big)(\mathfrak{p}):=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}k(\mathfrak{p},A)^{2s}\,\phi(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})

(29.2)

see e.g.
[
79
, Eq. (1.1.15)]
. Here

k(\mathfrak{p},A):=H(\mathfrak{p},A)_{11}=\frac{(|p|+\eta p_{3})\overline{A_{22}}-\eta(p_{1}-\operatorname{i}p_{2})\overline{A_{12}}}{|(|p|+\eta p_{3})\overline{A_{22}}-\eta(p_{1}-\operatorname{i}p_{2})\overline{A_{12}}|}

(29.3)

(if the denominator
\neq 0
) with the Wigner rotation
H(\mathfrak{p},A):=H(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}A\,H(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})
with respect to the
helicity cross section

H(\mathfrak{p}):=B\big(\eta\,p\big)A_{\ln|p|}
, cf. Eq. (
27.8
) and recall
A_{\rho}=\operatorname{e}^{\frac{\rho}{2}\sigma_{3}}
. (The canonical cross section is not defined for
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{0,\eta}
).
Since
H(\mathfrak{p})\cdot\big(\eta(e_{0}+e_{3})\big)=\mathfrak{p}
, one has
H(\mathfrak{p},A)\in E(2)
.

As expected,
\eta
denotes the sign of the energy. Indeed, the representation of
|H|^{-1}H
by
U^{0,s,\eta}
equals
\eta I
. We are going to clarify the significance of
s
. By explicit computation one finds that for the representation
U^{0,s,\eta}
the helicity operator
|P|^{-1}JP
equals
\eta sI
.

(147) Lemma.

U^{0,s,-}
is antiunitarily equivalent to
U^{0,-s,+}
. More precisely,
U^{0,-s,-}=\mathcal{C}U^{0,s,+}\mathcal{C}^{-1}
for the antiunitary transformation
\mathcal{C}:L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{0,+})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{0,-})
,
(\mathcal{C}\phi)(\mathfrak{p}):=\overline{\phi(-\mathfrak{p})}
.
\mathcal{C}
is uniquely determined up to a constant phase.

Proof.
According to Eq. (
29.3
),
k(-\mathfrak{p},A)=k(\mathfrak{p},A)
holds for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{0}
. Further recall that
U^{0,s,\eta}
is irreducible. Then the assertion is easily verified.∎

We will denote by
[0,s,\eta]
any representation of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
, which is unitarily equivalent to
U^{0,s,\eta}
. Hence in view of (
29.2
) one has the following consistent

(148) Interpretation.

[0,s,+]
represents a massless particle with
positive energy
and helicity
s\in\mathbb{Z}/2
, and

[0,-s,-]
represents its antiparticle, which has
positive energy
, zero mass, and helicity
s
, too.

For later use we cast
U^{0,s,\eta}
into a more convenient form. By the isomorphism
X_{\eta}:L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{0,\eta})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
(X_{\eta}\phi)(p):=(2|p|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\phi(\eta|p|,p)
one passes to the representation
X_{\eta}U^{0,s,\eta}X_{\eta}^{-1}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In the case of positive energy
\eta=+
it has already the desired form

\big(W^{0,s,+}(t,b,A)\varphi\big)(p):=(|q^{+}|/|p|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t|p|}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}k(\mathfrak{p}^{+},A)^{2s}\,\varphi(q^{+})

(29.4)

In the case of negative energy
\eta=-
one carries out a further unitary transformation by
X_{s}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
(X_{s}\varphi)(p):=b(p)^{2s}\varphi(p)
. Then
X_{s}X_{-}U^{0,s,-}X_{-}^{-1}X_{s}^{-1}
equals

\big(W^{0,s,-}(t,b,A)\varphi\big)(p):=(|q^{-}|/|p|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}t|p|}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}\big(b(p)k(\mathfrak{p}^{-},A)\overline{b(q^{-})}\,\big)^{2s}\,\varphi(q^{-})

(29.5)

Recall
b(p)=\frac{p_{1}+\operatorname{i}p_{2}}{|p_{1}+\operatorname{i}p_{2}|}
for
(p_{1},p_{2})\neq 0
, and

\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}=(\eta|p|,p)
and
\mathfrak{q}^{\eta}=(\eta|q^{\eta}|,q^{\eta})=A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{\eta}
.

(149) Lemma.
Let
s\in\mathbb{Z}/2
. The representations

\big(K^{s,+}(t,b,B)\varphi\big)(p):=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t|p|}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}\kappa(p,B)^{2s}\,\varphi(B^{-1}\cdot p)

(29.6)

and

\big(K^{s,-}(t,b,B)\varphi\big)(p):=\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}t|p|}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}\kappa(p,B)^{-2s}\,\varphi(B^{-1}\cdot p)

(29.7)

on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
of the little kinematical group are extended by
Eq. (
29.4
)
and
Eq. (
29.5
)
, respectively,
to representations of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
. Here
\kappa(p,B):=\big(B(p)^{-1}B\,B(B^{-1}\cdot p)\big)_{11}
for
p\neq 0
.

Proof.
Note that for
p\neq 0
the Wigner rotation
B(p)^{-1}B\,B(B^{-1}\cdot p)\in SU(2)
is diagonal as it leaves
(0,0,1)
fixed. Since
A_{\ln|p|}
is diagonal, too,
H(\mathfrak{p}^{+},B)=B(p)^{-1}B\,B(B^{-1}\cdot p)=\operatorname{diag}\big(\kappa(p,B),\overline{\kappa(p,B)}\,\big)
with
\kappa(p,B)=k(\mathfrak{p}^{+},B)
. This already shows
W^{0,s,+}(t,b,B)=K^{s,+}(t,b,B)
.

For the negative energy case note first that
H(-\mathfrak{p})=H(\mathfrak{p})
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{0}
. Hence
k(\mathfrak{p}^{-},B)=\kappa(-p,B)
. It remains to check

\kappa(-p,B)=\overline{b(p)}\,\overline{\kappa(p,B)}\,b(q),\;q:=B^{-1}\cdot p

(1)

Indeed,
\operatorname{diag}\big(\kappa(-p,B),\overline{\kappa(-p,B)}\,\big)=B(-p)^{-1}B\,B(-q)=C(p)B(p)^{-1}B\,B(q)C(q)^{-1}=C(p)\operatorname{diag}\big(\kappa(p,B),\overline{\kappa(p,B)}\,\big)C(q)^{-1}=\operatorname{diag}\big(\overline{b(p)}\,\overline{\kappa(p,B)}\,b(q),b(p)\kappa(p,B)\,\overline{b(q)}\big)
where

C(p):=B(-p)^{-1}B(p)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-\overline{b(p)}\\
b(p)&0\end{array}\right),\;b(p)=\frac{p_{1}+ip_{2}}{|p_{1}+ip_{2}|}

(2)

∎

We will need the integral decomposition of the representations
K^{s,\eta}
of the little kinematical group.

(150) Lemma.
There is the following set of mutually inequivalent irreducible representations of the little kinematical group:
U^{r,s,\eta}_{\mathbb{R}\otimes ISU(2)}
on
L^{2}(S_{r}^{2})
with
S_{r}^{2}:=\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|p|=r\}
for
r>0
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}/2
, and
\eta=\pm
, given by

\big(U^{r,s,\eta}_{\mathbb{R}\otimes ISU(2)}(t,b,B)\varphi\big)(p)=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\eta tr}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}\kappa(p,B)^{2\eta s}\varphi(B^{-1}\cdot p)

Then the integral decomposition
K^{s,\eta}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}U^{r,s,\eta}_{\mathbb{R}\otimes ISU(2)}4\pi r^{2}\operatorname{d}r
holds.

Proof.
The first part of the assertion follows from general representation theory for semi-direct products of locally compact groups. The second part is easily verified.∎

Let us add a result on covariance of the helicity cross section which we need in (
29.3
).

(151) Lemma.
For all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
and
C=\operatorname{diag}(\gamma,\overline{\gamma})
with
\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
,
|\gamma|=1
one has

B(C\cdot p)=CB(p)\,C^{-1}
. Also,
H(\mathfrak{p},C)=B(p)^{-1}CB(C^{-1}\cdot p)=C
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{0}
, implying
k(\mathfrak{p},C)=\kappa(p,C)=\gamma
.

Proof.
Explicit calculation yields

(C\cdot p)_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(\gamma^{2}+\overline{\gamma}^{2})p_{1}+\frac{1}{2\operatorname{i}}(\gamma^{2}-\overline{\gamma}^{2})p_{2},\quad(C\cdot p)_{2}=-\frac{1}{2\operatorname{i}}(\gamma^{2}-\overline{\gamma}^{2})p_{1}+\frac{1}{2}(\gamma^{2}+\overline{\gamma}^{2})p_{2},\quad(C\cdot p)_{3}=p_{3}
. Recall Eq. (
27.8
). One finds
a_{\pm}(C\cdot p)=a_{\pm}(p)
and
b(C\cdot p)=\overline{\gamma}^{2}b(p)
, whence the first part of assertion. — Therefore
B(p)^{-1}CB(C^{-1}\cdot p)=C
for all
p\neq 0
. Finally recall that
A_{\ln|p|}
is diagonal and hence commutes with
C
. ∎

29.3.
Derivation of the massless SCT

Regarding the notations see sec.
29.2
. Moreover let

h^{\chi}(p):=\chi(\sigma_{1}p_{1}+\sigma_{2}p_{2}+\sigma_{3}p_{3}),\quad\pi^{\chi\eta}(p):=\frac{1}{2}\big(I_{2}+\frac{\eta}{|p|}h^{\chi}(p)\big)

for
\chi\in\{+,-\}
. Particular attention requires the rotation
R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A)
in
Eq. (
29.8
), which replaces the Wigner rotation

R(\mathfrak{p},A)
in Eq. (
28.2
) in the massive case.

(152) Lemma.

Let
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{0}
and
A\in SL(2,\mathbb{C})
. Set
\mathfrak{q}:=A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}
. Choose
B,B^{\prime}\in SU(2)
and
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
such that
A=B^{\prime}A_{\rho}B
. Set

R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A):=B^{\prime}B(B^{\prime-1}\cdot p)B(B\cdot q)^{-1}B

(29.8)

Then
R_{0}
is well-defined, i.e. the right hand side does not depend on the particular choice of
B^{\prime},B,\rho
. Furthermore,

(\alpha)

R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A)\in SU(2)

(\beta)

R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},B)=B

(\gamma)

R_{0}(\lambda\mathfrak{p},A)=R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A)
for all
\lambda>0

(\delta)

R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A)=\lim_{m\to 0}R(\mathfrak{p}^{m},A)
with
\mathfrak{p}^{m}:=(\operatorname{sgn}(p_{0})\sqrt{|p|^{2}+m^{2}},p)
,
m>0

(\epsilon)

R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A)R_{0}(\mathfrak{q},A^{\prime})=R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},AA^{\prime})

for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{0}
,
A,A^{\prime}\in SL(2,\mathbb{C})
, and
B\in SU(2)
.

Proof.

(\alpha)
is obvious. — If
\rho=0
, i.e.,
A=B^{\prime}B\in SU(2)
, then
B(B\cdot q)^{-1}=B(B^{\prime-1}\cdot p)^{-1}
, whence
R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A)=B^{\prime}B=A
. So
(\beta)
holds.

We examine the ambiguity of the representation
A=B^{\prime}A_{\rho}B
for
A\in SL(2,\mathbb{C})\setminus SU(2)
. Note that
A=(B^{\prime}B)(B^{-1}A_{\rho}B)
is the polar decomposition of
A
. This is unique. Therefore, the eigenvalues
\operatorname{e}^{\rho/2}
and
\operatorname{e}^{-\rho/2}
of
B^{-1}A_{\rho}B
determine
\rho\neq 0
. Moreover, as the eigenvalues are not equal,
B
is unique up to a diagonal matrix
C\in SU(2)
multiplied from the left. But when
B
is replaced with
CB
then
B^{\prime}
has to be replaced by
B^{\prime}C^{-1}
in order to leave unchanged
B^{\prime}B
. Conversely,
B^{\prime}C^{-1}A_{\rho}CB
still equals
A
as
A_{\rho}
and
C
commute.

Now we show that
R_{0}
is well-defined. In view of
(\beta)
assume at once
A\not\in SU(2)
. Consider the representation
A=B^{\prime}C^{-1}A_{\rho}CB
. Then the right hand side of
Eq. (
29.8
) reads
B^{\prime}C^{-1}B(C\cdot B^{\prime-1}\cdot p)B(C\cdot B\cdot q)^{-1}CB
, which actually does not depend on
C
by (
29.2
).

(\gamma)
holds as
B(\lambda p)=B(p)
for
\lambda>0
,
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
.

(\delta)
By definition
R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A_{\rho})=B(p)B(q)^{-1}
for
A=A_{\rho}
. Recall
\alpha=\cosh\rho
,
\beta=\sinh\rho
and
\gamma=\cosh\frac{\rho}{2}
,
\delta=\sinh\frac{\rho}{2}
.
Explicit computation using Eq. (
27.8
) yields

R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A_{\rho})=\big(|p|(\alpha|p|-\beta\eta p_{3})\big)^{-1/2}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\gamma|p|-\delta\eta p_{3}&\delta\eta(p_{1}-\operatorname{i}p_{2})\\
-\delta\eta(p_{1}+\operatorname{i}p_{2})&\gamma|p|-\delta\eta p_{3}\end{array}\right)

(29.9)

which shows
(\delta)
for
A=A_{\rho}
by Eq. (
28.5
). As to the general case
A=B^{\prime}A_{\rho}B
note that
R(\mathfrak{p}^{m},A)=B^{\prime}R(B^{\prime-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{m},A_{\rho})B
by the analogous properties
(\beta)
and
(\epsilon)
for
R
, which are easy to verify for
R
(in place of
R_{0}
).
Hence
R(\mathfrak{p}^{m},A)\to B^{\prime}R_{0}(B^{\prime-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}^{m},A_{\rho})B=B^{\prime}B(B^{\prime-1}\cdot p)B(B\cdot q)B=R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A)
by definition.

(\epsilon)
follows immediately from
(\delta)
, as
R
satisfies the analogous property. ∎

It is interesting that the limit in (
29.3
)
(\delta)
exists whereas the limit of
Q(\mathfrak{p}^{m})
for
m\to 0
does not if
p\neq 0
. Moreover one notes
R_{0}(\mathfrak{p},A)\cdot q=\frac{|q|}{|p|}p
as an easy consequence of
|p|B(p)\cdot e_{3}=p
for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
.

(153) Theorem.

For every massless WL with causal time evolution there exists a representation of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
extending the representation of the little kinematical group.

Let
\chi\in\{+,-\}
. For
(t,b,A)\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
and
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2n})
put

\big(W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(t,b,A)\varphi\big)(p):=\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}\left(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\eta|p|}\pi^{\chi\eta}(p)s^{\chi}(A)^{*-1}\otimes D^{(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\big(R_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},A)\big)\right)\varphi(q^{\eta})

with
s^{+}(A):=A
,
s^{-}(A):=A^{*-1}
. Then
W_{n}^{\chi}
is a representation of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
, which extends
(V_{n}^{\chi},U_{n})
.

Proof.
Due to (
29.1
) we may turn at once to the Weyl tensor-localization
(V_{n}^{\chi},U_{n},E_{n})
. Obviously
W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(B)=U_{n}^{mom}(B)
for
B\in SU(2)
, and
\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}th^{\chi}(p)}\pi^{\chi\eta}(p)=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\eta|p|}\pi^{\chi\eta}(p)
holds. Hence
W_{n}^{\chi}
is an extension of
(V_{n}^{\chi},U_{n})
. Then, using the relation

\pi^{\chi\eta}(p)s^{\chi}(A)^{*-1}=(|q^{\eta}|/|p|)\,s^{\chi}(A)\pi^{\chi\eta}(q^{\eta})

(29.10)

which is mainly a consequence of the fundamental relation
A(\sum_{\mu=0}^{3}\mathfrak{p}_{\mu}\sigma_{\mu})A^{*}=\sum_{\mu=0}^{3}(A\cdot\mathfrak{p})_{\mu}\sigma_{\mu}
with
\sigma_{0}:=I_{2}
for
A\in SL(2,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
and by means of the property (
29.3
)
(\epsilon)
, one verifies the group multiplication law
W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(\mathfrak{a}+A\cdot\mathfrak{a}^{\prime},AA^{\prime})=W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(\mathfrak{a},A)W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime},A^{\prime})
. Finally one checks unitarity of
W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(A)
.∎

(154) Definition.

(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}}):=(W_{1}^{\chi},E_{1})
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
, is called the
\chi
-handed
Weyl system
.

The representation
W_{n}^{\chi}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
in (
29.3
) can be constructed as follows giving an insight into its composition. By the unitary transformation
(Y_{0}\varphi)(p):=D(B(p)^{-1})\,\varphi(p)
with
D:=D^{(\frac{1}{2})}\otimes D^{(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}
we go to the energy representation
Y_{0}(V_{n}^{\chi mom},U_{n}^{mom})Y_{0}^{-1}
of
(V_{n}^{\chi},U_{n})
. It reads

\big(V_{n}^{\chi erg}(t)U_{n}^{erg}(b,B)\varphi\big)(p)=\big(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t\chi|p|\sigma_{3}}\otimes I_{n}\big)\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}D\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\kappa(p,B)&0\\
0&\overline{\kappa(p,B)}\end{array}\right)\varphi(B^{-1}\cdot p)

(29.11)

Hence
(V_{n}^{\chi erg},U_{n}^{erg})\simeq\big(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}K^{\chi(\frac{n}{2}-i),\chi}\big)\oplus\big(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}K^{-\chi(\frac{n}{2}-i),-\chi}\big)
. Each
K^{s,\eta}
is extended to
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
by
W^{0,s,\eta}
according to (
29.2
). Thus one gets an extension to
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
of
(V_{n}^{\chi erg},U_{n}^{erg})
by

W_{n}^{\chi erg}\simeq\big(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}W^{0,\chi(\frac{n}{2}-i),\chi}\big)\oplus\big(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}W^{0,-\chi(\frac{n}{2}-i),-\chi}\big)

(29.12)

It satisfies

\big(W_{n}^{\chi erg}(A_{\rho})\varphi\big)(p)=\big(\sqrt{|q^{\chi}|/|p|}\,\varphi_{1}(q^{\chi}),\sqrt{|q^{-\chi}|/|p|}\,\varphi_{2}(q^{-\chi})\big)

as
k(\mathfrak{p},A_{\rho})=1
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{0}
by Eq. (
29.3
) and
b(p)=b(q^{-})
for
A=A_{\rho}
. By some computations one goes back to the momentum representation obtaining
W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(A_{\rho})
.
For general
A=B^{\prime}A_{\rho}B
with
B^{\prime},B\in SU(2)
one obtains
W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(A)
in (
29.3
) from
W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(A)=U_{n}^{mom}(B^{\prime})W_{n}^{\chi\,mom}(A_{\rho})U_{n}^{mom}(B)
.

(155) Lemma.
Let
(V,U)
be a representation of the little kinematical group with
C=0
and finite helicity spectrum. Then all representations
W
of

\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
extending
(V,U)
are unitarily equivalent.

Proof.
This is an immediate consequence of the representation theory of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
because the mass-squared operator
C
, the sign of the energy operator
\operatorname{sgn}(H)
, and the helicity operator
|P|^{-1}JP
are already determined by
(V,U)
.∎

(156) Theorem.
Let
(V,U,E)
be a massless WL with causal time evolution. Let
X
be the associated causal position operator (recall
Eq. (
2.2
)
). Then there is a unique representation
W_{E}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
which extends
(V,U)
such that its booster is given by

N=\frac{1}{2}\{X,H\}+\frac{\operatorname{sgn}(H)}{|H|}P\times\left(J-X\times P-\frac{\operatorname{i}}{4}[X,H]\times[X,H]\right)

(29.13)

The representation
W_{n}^{\chi}
from
(
29.3
)
satisfies this relation. Any extension
W
of
(V,U)
to
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
is unitarily equivalent to
W_{E}
.

Proof.
Uniqueness of
W_{E}
is obvious as the representation of all generators of the Poincaré group is set. Due to (
29.1
) it suffices to prove the existence of
W_{E}
for
(V_{n}^{\chi},U_{n})
of the Weyl tensor-localization. It is not hard to show that the booster of
W_{n}^{\chi}
from (
29.3
) satisfies the formula for
N
. In particular check
\frac{d}{dt}R_{0}(p^{\eta},\operatorname{e}^{\frac{t}{2}\sigma_{k}})|_{t=0}=\frac{\operatorname{i}\eta}{|p|}\frac{1}{2}(p_{l}\sigma_{m}-p_{m}\sigma_{l})
with
(k,l,m)
a cyclic permutation of
(1,2,3)
and verify that

I_{2}\otimes\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{d}^{(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{1}{2})}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})

is given by
J-X\times P-\frac{\operatorname{i}}{4}[X,H]\times[X,H]
.
The last part of the assertion holds true by (
29.3
). ∎

The next result, which follows from (
29.3
), shows how a general massless SCT is built of the systems
(W_{n}^{\chi},E_{n})
. Recall (
27
).

(157) Theorem.
Every massless SCT
(W,E)
is unitarily related to
(W_{E},E)
. The latter is unitarily equivalent to a finite orthogonal sum of the systems
(W_{n}^{\chi},E_{n})
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with uniquely determined multiplicities.

As to (
29.3
) note that a massless SCT
(W,E)
in general is not unitarily equivalent to
(W_{E},E)
. The example (
29.3
) shows that there are massless SCT
(W,E)
which are irreducible although
(W_{E},E)
is not. In view of the result (
29.4
) we study the following special case.

(158) Lemma.
Let

\chi\in\{+,-\}
and let
(W,E)
be a massless SCT unitarily related to a finite multiple of
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
.
Let
Q
be an orthogonal projection commuting with
(W,E)
. Then there is a unitary transformation
S
such that
W=SW_{E}S^{-1}
and which commutes with
Q
.

Proof.
By assumption there are unitary
S
and
U
such that
W=SW_{E}S^{-1}
and
(W_{E},E)=U\,\big(I_{\nu}\otimes(W^{\chi}_{1},E_{1})\big)\,U^{-1}
with multiplicity
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
and
I_{\nu}
the identity on
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
. Therefore it is no restriction assuming
W=S\,(I_{\nu}\otimes W^{\chi}_{1})\,S^{-1}
and
E=I_{\nu}\otimes E_{1}
. Recall that
S
commutes with
W|_{\mathcal{K}}=(I_{\nu}\otimes W^{\chi}_{1})|_{\mathcal{K}}
, where
\mathcal{K}
denotes the little kinematical group
\mathbb{R}\otimes ISU(2)
.

As
Q
commutes with
(W,E)
, it commutes with its restriction to the Euclidean group
ISU(2)
, which is
I_{\nu}\otimes(U_{1},E_{1})
. Now,
(U_{1},E_{1})
is the system of imprimitivities induced by
D=D^{\frac{1}{2}}
on
SU(2)
and therefore irreducible. Hence
Q=Q_{\nu}\otimes I
with
Q_{\nu}
an orthogonal projection matrix on
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
I
the identity on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Recall that
Q
commutes with
W
. Hence
S^{-1}QS
commutes with
I_{\nu}\otimes W^{\chi}_{1}
. By Eq. (
29.12
),
I_{\nu}\otimes W_{1}^{\chi erg}=(I_{\nu}\otimes W^{0,\chi/2,\chi})\oplus(I_{\nu}\otimes W^{0,\chi/2,-\chi})
. Recall that
W^{0,s,\eta}
is
[0,s,\eta]
and, hence, irreducible and mutually inequivalent. Consequently one has
S^{erg-1}QS^{erg}=(Q_{\nu}^{\chi}\otimes I^{\prime})\oplus(Q_{\nu}^{-\chi}\otimes I^{\prime})=(Q_{\nu}^{\chi}\oplus Q_{\nu}^{-\chi})\otimes I^{\prime}
with orthogonal projection matrices
Q_{\nu}^{\chi}
,
Q_{\nu}^{-\chi}
on
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
and
I^{\prime}
the identity on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C})
.

Similarly, as
S^{erg}
commutes with
I_{\nu}\otimes W_{1}^{\chi erg}|_{\mathcal{K}}=(I_{\nu}\otimes K^{\chi/2,\chi})\oplus(I_{\nu}\otimes K^{\chi/2,-\chi})
, one infers from (
29.2
) that
(S^{erg}\varphi)(p)=s(|p|)\varphi(p)

with unitary matrices
s(r)=s^{\chi}(r)\oplus s^{-\chi}(r)
on
\mathbb{C}^{\nu}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\nu}
for
r\geq 0
.

Therefore
s^{\chi}(r)^{-1}Q_{\nu}\,s^{\chi}(r)=Q^{\chi}_{\nu}
for almost all
r\geq 0
, and also for
-\chi
in place of
\chi
. Hence there is
r_{0}\geq 0
such that
s(r)s(r_{0})^{-1}
commutes with
Q_{\nu}^{\chi}\oplus Q_{\nu}^{-\chi}
for almost all
r\geq 0
.
Then
(S^{erg}_{Q}\varphi)(p):=s(|p|)s(r_{0})^{-1}\varphi(p)
,
(S^{erg}_{0}\varphi)(p):=s(r_{0})\varphi(p)
define unitary transformations
S_{Q}
,
S_{0}
such that
S=S_{Q}S_{0}
. Moreover,
S_{0}
commutes with
I_{\nu}\otimes W^{\chi}_{1}
, whence
W=S(I_{\nu}\otimes W^{\chi}_{1})S^{-1}=S_{Q}(I_{\nu}\otimes W^{\chi}_{1})S_{Q}^{-1}
. Finally,
S_{Q}
commutes with
Q
, thus concluding the proof.∎

(159) Example
for an
irreducible
massless SCT
(W,E)
unitarily related to
2\,(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
.
For its construction we refer to the proof of (
29.3
).
Let
s^{\pm}(r):=I_{2}\cos r\pm\operatorname{i}\sigma_{2}\sin r
for
r\geq 0
, which depends continuously on
r
. Choose
r_{0}=0
.
Then
0,I_{2}
are the only projections invariant under
\{s^{\pm}(r)s^{\pm}(0)^{-1}:r\geq 0\}
. Hence
(W,E)
is irreducible by (
29.3
).

For the momentum representation
S^{mom}=Y_{h}^{-1}S^{erg}Y_{h}
of
S
one gets
(S^{mom}\varphi)(p)=s(p)\varphi(p)
with
s(p):=\exp(\operatorname{i}\sigma_{2}\otimes\sum_{k=1}^{3}\sigma_{k}p_{k})
.

29.4.
Determination of the massless causal systems

It does not suffice to examine the
(W^{\chi}_{n},E_{n})
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
, but all irreducible massless SCT have to be considered which as in the above example are unitarily related to finite orthogonal sums of the former.

(160) Theorem.
Let
(W,E)
be an irreducible massless SCT, which localizes frame-independently. Then
(W,E)
is unitarily related to a finite multiple of
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
for some
\chi\in\{+,-\}
. In particular,
(W_{n}^{\chi},E_{n})
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
,
n>1
do not localize frame-independently.

Proof.
Recall
A_{\rho}=\operatorname{e}^{\frac{\rho}{2}\sigma_{3}}
.
By (
29.3
) and (
29.3
), up to unitary equivalence, the momentum representation of

W(A_{\rho})
reads

\big(W^{mom}(A_{\rho})\varphi\big)(p)=s(p)^{-1}\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}w^{\eta}(p)s(q^{\eta})\varphi(q^{\eta})

(
1
)

for almost all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Here,
w^{\eta}(p):=\bigoplus_{\chi}\pi^{\chi\eta}(p)s^{\chi}(A_{\rho})^{*-1}\otimes D^{\chi}\big(R_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},A_{\rho})\big)
with some finite dimensional representation
D^{\chi}
of
ISU(2)
, and, according to (
27
), there is some unitary operator
S
commuting with
W|_{\mathbb{R}\times ISU(2)}
so that
(S^{mom}\varphi)(p)=s(p)\varphi(p)
with unitary matrices
s(p)
acting on spinor space.

Now let
\varphi
be localized in a bounded region. By assumption so is
\varphi_{\rho}:=W^{mom}(A_{\rho})\varphi
. Hence, by the Paley-Wiener Theorem, these functions are the restriction of entire functions on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, still denoted by
\varphi
and
\varphi_{\rho}
. We need to exploit only their continuity, in particular at
p=0
.

Since
S
is rotation-invariant, one has
s(p)=D(B(p))s(|p|e_{3})D(B(p))^{-1}
, where
D:=(D^{(\frac{1}{2})}\otimes D^{+})\oplus(D^{(\frac{1}{2})}\otimes D^{-})
. Let
I^{\chi}:=D^{\chi}(I_{2})
. As
S
is also time translation-invariant,
s(|p|e_{3})
commutes with
(\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t|p|\sigma_{3}}\otimes I^{+})\oplus(\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}t|p|\sigma_{3}}\otimes I^{-})
for all
t
(see the energy representation Eq. (
29.11
)). Hence, equivalently,
s(re_{3})
,
r>0
commutes with
P^{\eta}
, where
P^{+}:=(\big(\tiny{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&0\end{array}}\big)\otimes I^{+})\oplus(\big(\tiny{\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&1\end{array}}\big)\otimes I^{-})
and
P^{-}:=I-P^{+}
.

One has
D(B(p))^{-1}w^{\eta}(p)D(B(q^{\eta}))=\sqrt{|q^{\eta}|/|p|}\,P^{\eta}
(see the energy representation of
W^{\chi}_{n}(A_{\rho})
after Eq. (
29.12
)). Let
t^{\eta}(p):=s(|p|e_{3})^{-1}s(|q^{\eta}|e_{3})\sqrt{|q^{\eta}|/|p|}\,P^{\eta}
for
p\neq 0
. Clearly
t^{\eta}
commutes with
P^{\pm}
and
t^{\eta}(p)=t^{\eta}(p^{\prime})
if
|p|=|p^{\prime}|
and
|q^{\eta}|=|q^{\prime\eta}|
. By
(1)
,

\varphi_{\rho}(p)=\sum_{\eta=\pm 1}D(B(p))t^{\eta}(p)D(B(q^{\eta}))^{-1}\varphi(q^{\eta})

(
2
)

holds for almost all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

(a) First we will redefine
t^{\eta}
on some null set such that it becomes continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus Z
with
Z:=\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Fix
p_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. One has
\varphi(p)=(2\pi)^{-3/2}\int\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}px}\psi(x)\operatorname{d}^{3}x
for
\psi:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\varphi
with compact support. Hence, if necessary
it suffices to alter
\psi
a bit on its support in order to achieve
\varphi(q^{\eta}_{*})\neq 0
for
\eta=\pm
. Choose matrices
u_{i}
acting on the spinor space such that the squared matrix

\phi(p):=(u_{1}\varphi(p),u_{2}\varphi(p),\dots,u_{n}\varphi(p))
, where
n
is the dimension of the spinor space, is invertible at
p=q_{*}^{+}
and
p=q_{*}^{-}
. By continuity there is a neighborhood
N(p_{*})
of
p_{*}
such that
\phi(q^{\eta})
is still invertible for all
p\in N(p_{*})
,
\eta=\pm
.

Clearly, the functions
u_{i}\psi(x)
have compact support with
u_{i}\varphi(p)
their Fourier transforms. Define the matrix
\phi_{\rho}(p)
replacing
\varphi
with
\varphi_{\rho}
in
\phi(p)
. Then
(2)
holds for
\phi_{\rho}(p)
and
\phi(q^{\eta})
in place of
\varphi_{\rho}(p)
and
\varphi(q^{\eta})
, respectively. Hence, as
t^{\eta}(p)
commutes with
P^{+}
and
P^{-}
, one finds

t^{\eta}(p)=P^{\eta}D(B(p))^{-1}\,\phi_{\rho}(p)\phi(q^{\eta})^{-1}D(B(q^{\eta}))

(
3
)

for almost all
p\in N(p_{*})
.

Assume
p_{*}\not\in Z
. Then the right hand side of
(3)
is continuous on
N(p_{*})\setminus Z
because of Eq. (
27.8
) and since
q^{\eta}_{1}=p_{1}
,
q^{\eta}_{2}=p_{2}
. Hence, redefining
t^{\eta}
by
(3)
, one gets
t^{\eta}
continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus Z
and
t^{\eta}(p)=s(|p|e_{3})^{-1}s(|q^{\eta}|e_{3})\sqrt{|q^{\eta}|/|p|}\,P^{\eta}
for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus L
for some Lebesgue null set
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

(b) Let
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus Z
and
\eta=\pm
. Clearly,
t^{\eta}(p)
still commutes with
P^{+}
and
P^{-}
. We show that
t^{\eta}(p)=t^{\eta}(p^{\prime})
still holds if
|p|=|p^{\prime}|
and
|q^{\eta}|=|q^{\prime\eta}|
or, equivalently, if
|p|=|p^{\prime}|
and
p_{3}=p^{\prime}_{3}
, since
q^{\eta}=(p_{1},p_{2},\alpha p_{3}-\beta\eta|p|)
and hence
|q^{\eta}|=\alpha|p|-\beta\eta p_{3}
.

Indeed, assume the contrary. Then there are
p_{*},p_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus Z
with
|p_{*}|=|p_{*}^{\prime}|
,
p_{*3}=p^{\prime}_{*3}
, and
t^{\eta}(p_{*})\neq t^{\eta}(p_{*}^{\prime})
. By continuity
t^{\eta}(p)\neq t^{\eta}(p^{\prime})
holds true for all
p\in N
and
p^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}
, where
N
and
N^{\prime}
are some neighborhoods in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus Z
of
p_{*}
and
p^{\prime}_{*}
, respectively. Using cylindrical coordinates
z,r,\varphi
for
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, there is an open disc
Q\subset\mathbb{R}\times]0,\infty[
around
(z_{*},r_{*})
and open intervals
I
and
I^{\prime}
around
\varphi_{*}
and
\varphi^{\prime}_{*}
, respectively, such that
Q\times I\subset N
and
Q\times I^{\prime}\subset N^{\prime}
. By Tonelli’s theorem there is
\varphi\in I
and a Lebesgue null set
M\subset Q
with
(Q\setminus M)\times\{\varphi\}\subset N\setminus L
.
\varphi^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\subset Q
are determined analogously. Hence there is
q\in Q\setminus(M\cup M^{\prime})
.
Then
p
and
p^{\prime}
given by
(q,\varphi)
and
(q,\varphi^{\prime})
, respectively, yield the contradiction
t^{\eta}(p)=t^{\eta}(p^{\prime})
.

(c) Consider
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus Z
. Then
\lambda p\in N(0)\setminus Z
for all small
\lambda>0
. So, by
(3)
,
t^{\eta}(\lambda p)=P^{\eta}D(B(\lambda p))^{-1}\phi_{\rho}(\lambda p)\phi(\lambda q^{\eta})\,D(B(\lambda q^{\eta}))\to T^{\eta}(p)
for
\lambda\to 0
with

T^{\eta}(p):=P^{\eta}D(B(p))^{-1}X\,D(B(q^{\eta}))

(
4
)

and
X:=\phi_{\rho}(0)\phi(0)^{-1}
independent of
p
. It follows from (b) that
T^{\eta}(p)
commutes with
P^{+}
and
P^{-}
, and that
T^{\eta}(p^{\prime})=T^{\eta}(p)
if
|p^{\prime}|=|p|
,
p^{\prime}_{3}=p_{3}
. We will infer from (4) and these properties that
D^{+}
and
D^{-}
are multiples of
D^{(0)}
.

(d) Write
X
as a
2\times 2
-block matrix according to the orthogonal sum of
D
with entries
X_{\chi\chi^{\prime}}
,
\chi,\chi^{\prime}=\pm
. Then

T^{+}(p)_{++}=\left(\big(\tiny{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&0\end{array}}\big)B(p)^{-1}\otimes D^{+}(B(p))^{-1}\right)\,X_{++}\,\big(B(q^{+})\otimes D^{+}(B(q^{+}))\big)
. According to the tensor structure of the factors write
X_{++}
as a
2\times 2
-block matrix with entries
U_{\varsigma\varsigma^{\prime}}
,
\varsigma,\varsigma^{\prime}=\pm
. (The indices refer to the helicity values
s=\varsigma\frac{1}{2}
.) So,
T^{+}(p)=T^{+}(p)P^{+}
implies
T^{+}(p)_{++,+-}=D^{+}(B(p))^{-1}\big((a_{+}(p)U_{++}+\overline{b}a_{-}(p)U_{-+})(-\overline{b})a_{-}(q^{+})+(a_{+}(p)U_{+-}+\overline{b}a_{-}(p)U_{--})a_{+}(q^{+})\big)D(B(q^{+}))=0
using Eq. (
27.8
). Equivalently,
(|p|^{2}-p^{2}_{3})C=(-p_{1}+\operatorname{i}p_{2})(|p|-p_{3})A+(p_{1}+\operatorname{i}p_{2})(|p|+p_{3})B

with
A:=\operatorname{e}^{\rho/2}U_{-+}
,
B:=\operatorname{e}^{-\rho/2}U_{+-}
, and
C:=\operatorname{e}^{\rho/2}U_{++}-\operatorname{e}^{-\rho/2}U_{--}
. As
A,B,C
are independent of
p
, and since the equation holds for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus Z
, A=B=C=0 follows. Hence
U_{+-}=U_{-+}=0
and
U_{--}=\operatorname{e}^{\rho}U_{++}
. Let
U
denote the constant matrix
\operatorname{e}^{\rho/2}U_{++}
. Then a short computation yields

T^{+}(p)_{++}=\sqrt{|q^{+}|/|p|}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}D^{+}(B(p))^{-1}UD^{+}(B(q^{+}))&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right)

(
5
)

Similarly one computes
T^{+}(p)_{+-}=0
,
T^{+}(p)_{-+}=0
and

T^{+}(p)_{--}=\sqrt{|q^{+}|/|p|}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&D^{-}(B(p))^{-1}VD^{-}(B(q^{+}))\end{array}\right)

(
6
)

for some constant matrix
V
. From the definition of
t^{+}(p)
it follows that
U
and
V
are unitary. Analogous formulae hold for
T^{-}(p)
.

(e) Next we exploit that
T^{\eta}(p)=T^{\eta}(p^{\prime})
if
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus Z
with
|p|=|p^{\prime}|
,
p_{3}=p^{\prime}_{3}
. It implies by
(5)
that the matrices
D^{+}\big(B(q^{\prime+})B(q^{+})^{-1}\big)
and
D^{+}\big(B(p^{\prime})B(p)^{-1}\big)
are unitarily equivalent by means of
U
. Hence they have the same spectrum.

Choose
p,p^{\prime}
such that
b(p)=b(q^{+})=1
(i.e.,
p_{2}=0
) and
b(p^{\prime})=b(q^{\prime+})=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\varphi}
for
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. Then the trace of
B(p^{\prime})B(p)^{-1}
is
2-2a_{-}(p)^{2}(1-\cos\varphi)
. Hence its eigenvalues are
\lambda(p),\overline{\lambda(p)}
with
|\lambda(p)|=1
and
\Re(\lambda(p))=1-a_{-}(p)^{2}(1-\cos\varphi)
. Therefore
B(p^{\prime})B(p)^{-1}
is unitarily equivalent to
\operatorname{diag}(\lambda(p),\overline{\lambda(p)})
by some
B\in SU(2)
. Consequently,
D^{+}\big(B(p^{\prime})B(p)^{-1}\big)

is unitarily equivalent to
D^{+}\big(\operatorname{diag}(\lambda(p),\overline{\lambda(p)})\big)
by
D^{+}(B)
. As
D^{(j)}\big(\operatorname{diag}(\lambda(p),\overline{\lambda(p)})\big)=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda(p)^{2j},\lambda(p)^{2j-2},\dots,\lambda(p)^{-2j})
for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}/2
, the spectrum
\Sigma(p)
of
D^{+}\big(B(p^{\prime})B(p)^{-1}\big)
equals

\{\lambda(p)^{2m}:m=-j_{f},-j_{f}+1,\dots,j_{f}\}
for some half-integer
j_{f}
or equals
\{\lambda(p)^{2m}:m=-j_{b},-j_{b}+1,\dots,j_{b}\}
for some integer
j_{b}
or equals the union of these sets. Replacing
p
by
q^{+}
one obtains the spectrum
\Sigma(q^{+})
of
D^{+}\big(B(q^{\prime+})B(q^{+})^{-1}\big)
.

(f) Now we claim that
D^{+}
is a multiple of
D^{(0)}
. Check that
\lambda(p)\neq 1
,
\lambda(q^{+})\neq 1
and
\Re(\lambda(p))\neq\Re(\lambda(q^{+}))
as
\varphi\neq 0
. Hence
\lambda(p)\neq\lambda(q^{+})
and
\lambda(p)\neq\overline{\lambda(q^{+})}
. Choose
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\lambda(p)
is not a root of unity. This is possible as
a_{-}(p)\neq 0
.

For the proof assume the contrary. As shown above,
\Sigma(p)
and
\Sigma(q^{+})
coincide. Hence, if
\lambda(p)\in\Sigma(p)
, then
there are
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0,-1\}
such that
\lambda(p)^{2a+1}=\lambda(q^{+})
and
\lambda(q^{+})^{2b+1}=\lambda(p)
, and the contradiction
\lambda(p)^{(2a+1)(2b+1)-1}=1
follows. If

\lambda(p)^{2}\in\Sigma(p)
, then there are
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{-1,1\}
such that
\lambda(p)^{2a}=\lambda(q^{+})^{2}
and
\lambda(q^{+})^{2b}=\lambda(p)^{2}
, and the contradiction
\lambda(p)^{2ab-2}=1
follows. This proves the claim.

Analogously one shows that
D^{-}
is a multiple of
D^{(0)}
.

(g) By the result achieved so far,
W=\nu^{+}W^{+}_{1}\oplus\nu^{-}W^{-}_{1}
with multiplicities
\nu^{\chi}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\chi=\pm
. It remains to show that
S
decomposes accordingly. Let
\mathcal{K}
denote the little kinematical group
\mathbb{R}\otimes ISU(2)
. As
S
commutes with
W|_{\mathcal{K}}
, it suffices to show that
W^{+}_{1}|_{\mathcal{K}}
does not contain an irreducible subrepresentation which is equivalent to a subrepresentation of
W^{-}_{1}|_{\mathcal{K}}
. Hence we need the decomposition of
W^{\chi}_{1}|_{\mathcal{K}}
. By Eqs. (
29.12
), (
29.11
),
W^{\chi}_{1}|_{\mathcal{K}}\simeq K^{\chi/2,\chi}\oplus K^{\chi/2,-\chi}
holds, whence the assertion by (
29.2
).∎

This result (
29.4
), due to (
7.2
), definitely proves that the higher Weyl tensor-systems
(W^{\chi}_{n},E_{n})
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
,
n>1
are mathematical artefacts, interim results in determining the massless causal systems.

In sec.
21.1
it is shown that the Weyl systems
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
are causal. The following example concerns an irreducible massless SCT unitarily related to
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
, which localizes frame-independently without being causal.

(161) Example.
Let
W:=SW^{\textsc{w}\chi}S^{-1}
with
S:=W^{\textsc{w}\chi}(r)
for
r\equiv(r,0,I_{2})
with
r\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, and
E:=E^{\textsc{w}}
. Then obviously
(W,E)
is an irreducible massless SCT unitarily related to

(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
. One has
W(h)E(\Gamma)W(h)^{-1}=W^{\textsc{w}\chi}(rhr^{-1})E^{\textsc{w}}(\Gamma)W^{\textsc{w}\chi}(rhr^{-1})^{-1}
for all
h\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
and
\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}
, whence

W(h)E(\Gamma)W(h)^{-1}\leq E(\Gamma_{rhr^{-1}})

(1)

by causality of
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
. Then

(a)
(W,E)
localizes frame-independently.

(b)
(W,E)
is not causal.

Proof.
(a) is obvious by (1). — (b) In (1) specify
h=A_{\rho}
for
\rho\neq 0
and, for the moment,
\Gamma=\{0\}
as subset of the spacelike hyperplane
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then
A_{\rho}\cdot 0=0
, whence
\Gamma_{h}=\{0\}
. Furthermore,
rhr^{-1}=(r(1-\cosh\rho),0,0,-r\sinh\rho,A_{\rho})
and
rhr^{-1}\cdot\Gamma=\{(r(1-\cosh\rho),0,0,-r\sinh\rho)\}
, whence
x\in\Gamma_{rhr^{-1}}

\Leftrightarrow

r^{2}(\cosh\rho-1)^{2}-x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}-(x_{3}+r\sinh\rho)^{2}\geq 0
.
For
x\in\Gamma_{rhr^{-1}}
, therefore,
x_{3}
lies in the closed interval with endpoints
r(\operatorname{e}^{-\rho}-1)
and
r(1-\operatorname{e}^{\rho})
, which are either both positive or both negative, thus excluding
x_{3}=0
. Hence
\Gamma_{h}\cap\Gamma_{rhr^{-1}}=\emptyset
. By continuity this still holds true if
\Gamma=\{0\}
is replaced by
\Gamma=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|y|<\epsilon\}
for
\epsilon>0
small enough. This implies
W(h)E(\Gamma)W(h)^{-1}\not\leq E(\Gamma_{h})
confirming the claim.∎

(162) Lemma.
Let the massless causal system
(W,E)
be unitarily related to
\nu\,(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
for some
\chi\in\{+,-\}
with multiplicity
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
by the unitary transformation
S
. Suppose
SE(B)S^{-1}\leq E(B^{\prime})
, where
B
,
B^{\prime}
are two balls with equal radius. Then
(W,E)
is unitarily equivalent to
\nu\,(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
.

Proof.
Without restriction
W=S\,\nu W_{1}^{\chi mom}\,S^{-1}
and
E=\nu\,E_{1}^{mom}=\mathcal{F}E^{can}\mathcal{F}^{-1}
. Recall that then
(S\varphi)(p)=s(p)\varphi(p)
, where
s:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\nu\times 2\nu}
is measurable and
s(p)
unitary.

(a) First we show that
s(p)=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}(a-a^{\prime})p}s_{0}
, where
a
and
a^{\prime}
are the midpoints of
B
and
B^{\prime}
, respectively, and
s_{0}
is some constant.
Indeed, let
R
denote the radius of
B
and
B^{\prime}
, let
B_{R}
be the open ball around the origin with radius
R
and set
S^{\prime}:=W(a^{\prime})^{-1}SW(a)
. Then
a+B_{R}=B
,
B_{R}=B^{\prime}-a^{\prime}
, whence
S^{\prime}E(B_{R})S^{\prime-1}=W(a^{\prime})^{-1}SW(a)E(B_{R})W(a)^{-1}S^{-1}W(a^{\prime})=W(a^{\prime})^{-1}SE(B)S^{-1}W(a^{\prime})\leq W(a^{\prime})^{-1}E(B^{\prime})W(a^{\prime})=E(B_{R})
. Moreover check by (
29.3
) for
n=1
that
(S^{\prime}\varphi)(p)=\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}(a-a^{\prime})p}s(p)\varphi(p)
.
Now, following the proof of
[
5
, Theorem 10]
for
\delta=0
one infers that
(S^{\prime}\varphi)(p)=s_{0}\varphi(p)
for some constant matrix
s_{0}
. This shows the assertion.

(b) Now one shows that
S
commutes with
\nu W_{1}^{\chi mom}\equiv W_{1}^{\chi mom}\otimes I_{\nu}
thus concluding the proof.
Indeed, as
S
commutes with
\nu W_{1}^{\chi mom}|_{SU(2)}
one has
s(B\cdot p)=(B\otimes I_{\nu})s(p)(B^{-1}\otimes I_{\nu})
, whence
\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}bp}s_{0}=(B\otimes I_{\nu})s_{0}(B^{-1}\otimes I_{\nu})
with
b:=\big((B^{-1}-I_{2})\cdot(a-a^{\prime})\big)p
, for almost all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and all
B\in SU(2)
. This implies

(B^{-1}-I_{2})\cdot(a-a^{\prime})=0
and hence
a=a^{\prime}
. So
s_{0}
commutes with
B\otimes I_{\nu}
for all
B\in SU(2)
. Therefore there is
s_{\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu\times\nu}
such that
s_{0}=I_{2}\otimes s_{\nu}
, whence the assertion.
∎

It remains to find out those irreducible SCT unitarily related to multiples of
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
for some
\chi\in\{+,-\}
that are causal.
The foregoing considerations clearly suggest that up to unitary equivalence the only ones
are the two Weyl systems

(W^{\textsc{w}\chi},E^{\textsc{w}})
,
\chi\in\{+,-\}
studied in sec.
21
.

30.
Appendix

30.1.
A total set in
L^{p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{m})
of radially symmetric functions

The Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a Radon measure. The following result is valid for any Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. As to the definition of a Radon measure on a topological Hausdorff space see e.g.
[
45
, 2.1.1 (9)]
, cf. also
[
80
, 1.5 Definition]
. A subset
L
of a topological vector space
X
is said to be
total
if the linear hull
\langle L\rangle
of
L
is dense in
X
.

(163) Theorem.
Let
\mu
be a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
m,d\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq p<\infty
. Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}
denote the elements of the standard basis of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
. Then
\{1_{B}e_{i}:B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\textnormal{ closed ball},\;i=1,\dots,m\}
is total in
L^{p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

Proof.
(a)
Let
L
be total in the topological vector space
X
. Let
M\subset X
with
L\subset\overline{\langle M\rangle}
. Then
M
is total in
X
.
This is obvious as
X=\overline{\langle L\rangle}\subset\overline{\langle M\rangle}
.

(b)
Let also
L^{\prime}
be total in the topological vector space
X^{\prime}
. Then
M:=(L\times\{0\})\cup(\{0\}\times L^{\prime})
is total in
X\times X^{\prime}
.
This is obvious as
\langle M\rangle=\langle L\times L^{\prime}\rangle=\langle L\rangle\times\langle L^{\prime}\rangle
, which is dense in
X\times X^{\prime}
.

Due to (b) the assertion is readily reduced to the claim that
M:=\{1_{B}:B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\textnormal{ closed ball}\}
is total in
L^{p}:=L^{p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})
.

It follows from Lebesgue’s ladder that
\{1_{A}:A\textnormal{ measurable},\;\mu(A)<\infty\}
is total in
L^{p}
. As
\mu
is finite at compact sets, for
\varepsilon>0
and
A
measurable with
\mu(A)<\infty
there is
R>0
great enough such
\mu(A\setminus B_{R})<\varepsilon
. Therefore even
\{1_{A}:A\textnormal{ measurable bounded}\}
is total in
L^{p}
. Furthermore,
due to the outer regularity of
\mu
, for
\varepsilon>0
and
A
measurable bounded there is an open bounded
U\supset A
with
\mu(U\setminus A)<\varepsilon
. Consequently,
L:=\{1_{U}:U\textnormal{ open bounded}\}
is total in
L^{p}
.

Thus due to (a) it remains to show that
L\subset\overline{\langle M\rangle}
. So for
U
open bounded consider the set
\mathcal{B}:=\{B:B\subset U,B\textnormal{ closed ball}\}
. Then according to Vitali’s covering theorem
[
80
, 2.8 Theorem]
there are countably many mutually disjoint
B_{i}\in\mathcal{B}
with
\mu(U\setminus\bigcup_{i}B_{i})=0
. Hence, for
\varepsilon>0
there is
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
\mu(U\setminus V)<\varepsilon^{p}
for
V:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}
. Consequently,
\parallel\!\!1_{U}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}1_{B_{i}}\!\!\parallel_{p}=\parallel\!\!1_{U}-1_{V}\!\!\parallel_{p}=\parallel\!\!1_{U\setminus V}\!\!\parallel_{p}=\mu(U\setminus V)^{1/p}<\varepsilon
, accomplishing the proof.∎

30.2.
Imprimitivity Theorem

Let
G
be a locally compact group with countable base acting
on a locally compact space
X
. Let
H
be a separable Hilbert space. Then a system of imprimitivity
(V,E)
for
(G,X)
on
H

is a representation
V
of
G
on
H
, an action of
G
on
X
, and a PM
E
on
X
in
H
such that
V(g)E(M)V(g)^{-1}=E(gM)
for all
g\in G
and Borel sets
M\subset X
.

Let
G
act continuously and transitively on
X
and let
x_{0}\in X
be any point.
Then every system of imprimitivity
(V,E)
for
(G,X)
on
H
is unitarily equivalent to a canonical system
(U^{\textsc{g}},E^{can})
on
L^{2}_{m}(X,H_{\textsc{u}})
. Here

E^{can}(M)\psi:=1_{M}\psi

(1)

for Borel set
M\subset X
is the canonical PM on
X
, and
U
is a representation of the stabilizer subgroup
G_{x_{0}}
in
x_{0}
on the Hilbert space
H_{\textsc{u}}
and
U^{\textsc{g}}
the representation of
G
induced by
U
, i.e.,

(U^{\textsc{g}}(g)\psi)(x):=\sqrt{(\operatorname{d}m^{g}/\operatorname{d}m)(x)}\,U\big(R(x,g)\big)\psi(g^{-1}x)

(2)

where
m
is the up equivalence unique non trivial regular Borel measure on
X
, which is quasi-invariant under
G
,
m^{g}(M):=m(g^{-1}M)
for Borel set
M\subset X
,
\operatorname{d}m^{g}/\operatorname{d}m
the Radon-Nikodym derivative,
q:X\to G
a Borel section with respect to the surjection
G\to X
,
g\mapsto gx_{0}
, and the Wigner rotation
R(x,g):=q(x)^{-1}g\,q(g^{-1}x)
.

Two canonical systems of imprimitivity for
(G,X)
are equivalent, respectively irreducible, if and only if the corresponding representations of the stabilizer subgroup are equivalent, respectively irreducible.

30.3.
Systems of imprimitivity for
(\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi},\chi)

The aim is to extend a causal WL to non-timelike not spacelike hyperplanes. As studied in sec.
26
, a rep of the lattice of the
\perp^{\prime}
-complete regions (i.e., of the causal logic) would require such an extension.

By Poincaré covariance it suffices to consider the hyperplane
\chi=\{\mathfrak{x}:x_{0}=x_{3}\}
. Then by (
26
)
the invariance subgroup
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\subset\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
equals

IST(2)=\{(\mathfrak{a},A):\mathfrak{a}\in\chi,A\in SL(2,\mathbb{C}),A_{21}=0\}

One gets the canonical systems of imprimitivity for
(\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi},\chi)
by (
30.2
