). First note that the Lebesgue measure
\lambda_{\chi}
on
\chi
is quasi-invariant under
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
. Indeed, let
j:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\chi
,
j(x):=(x_{3},x)
. Then
\lambda_{\chi}=j(\lambda)
with
\lambda
the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Hence
\lambda_{\chi}^{(\mathfrak{a},A)}(M)=\lambda\big(-j^{-1}(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{a})+j^{-1}(A^{-1}\cdot M)\big)=\det\big(j^{-1}\Lambda(A^{-1})j\big)\,\lambda_{\chi}(M)=|A_{11}|^{-2}\lambda_{\chi}(M)
, where by (
26
) the last equation holds since
j^{-1}\Lambda(\operatorname{e}^{-\rho\sigma_{3}/2})j=\operatorname{diag}(1,1,\operatorname{e}^{-\rho})
and

j^{-1}\Lambda\big(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&w\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\big)j=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
u&-v&1\end{array}\right)

In particular,
\operatorname{d}\lambda_{\chi}^{(\mathfrak{a},A)}/\operatorname{d}\lambda_{\chi}=|A_{11}|^{-2}
is constant. — Next we choose
0\in\chi
to be the fixed point. Then
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\to\chi
,
(\mathfrak{a},A)\mapsto(\mathfrak{a},A)\cdot 0=\mathfrak{a}
is the natural surjection and obviously
q:\chi\to\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
,
q(\mathfrak{a}):=(\mathfrak{a},I_{2})
is a Borel section of it. Moreover,
ST(2)

is the stabilizer subgroup in
0
, and the Wigner rotation is
R\big(\mathfrak{x},(\mathfrak{a},A)\big)=A
. Indeed,
q(\mathfrak{x})^{-1}(\mathfrak{a},A)\,q\big((\mathfrak{a},A)^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x}\big)=(-\mathfrak{x},I_{2})(\mathfrak{a},A)\,q\big(-A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{a},A^{-1})\cdot\mathfrak{x}\big)=(-\mathfrak{x}+\mathfrak{a},A)\,q(-A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{a}+A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x})=(-\mathfrak{x}+\mathfrak{a},A)(-A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{a}+A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x},I_{2})=\big(-\mathfrak{x}+\mathfrak{a}+A\cdot(-A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{a}+A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{x}),A\big)=(0,A)\equiv A
.

Now
let
U
be any representation of
ST(2)
on the Hilbert space
H_{\textsc{u}}
. Then the representation of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
induced by
U
acting on
L^{2}(\chi,H_{\textsc{u}})
, here called
V^{can}
, reads

\big(V^{can}(\mathfrak{a},A)\psi\big)(\mathfrak{x})=|A_{11}|^{-1}U(A)\psi\big(A^{-1}\cdot(\mathfrak{x}-\mathfrak{a})\big)

and
(V^{can},P^{can})
, where
P^{can}
denotes the canonical PM, is a system of imprimitivity.

Shell representation.
Recall that
\mathcal{O}^{m,\eta}
for
m>0
,
\eta=\pm
denotes the mass shell
\{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:p_{0}=\eta\epsilon(p)\}
and let
\mathcal{O}^{m}:=\mathcal{O}^{m,+}\cup\mathcal{O}^{m,-}
be the double shell
\{\mathfrak{p}\cdot\mathfrak{p}=m^{2}\}
endowed with the Lorentz invariant measure. We shall use
the shell representation
(V^{shell},P^{shell})
, which is equivalent to
(V^{can},P^{can})
. The former acts on
L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{m},H_{\textsc{u}})
by

(a)

\big(V^{shell}(\mathfrak{a},A)\phi\big)(\mathfrak{p})=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}U(A)\phi(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})

The form of
P^{shell}(M)
,
M\subset\chi
is complicated. For
M:=\{\mathfrak{x}\in\chi:0\leq\varsigma x_{3}\leq\alpha\}
,
\alpha>0
,
\varsigma=\pm 1
and
\phi\in L^{1}\cap L^{2}
we get explicitly

(b)

\big(P^{shell}(M)\phi\big)(\mathfrak{p})=\textrm{\footnotesize{$\sqrt{|p_{0}-p_{3}|}$}}\sum_{\eta^{\prime}=+,-}\int k(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\frac{\sqrt{|p^{\prime}_{0}-p^{\prime}_{3}|}}{2|p^{\prime}_{0}|}\phi(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\operatorname{d}p^{\prime}_{3}

where
p^{\prime}_{1}:=p_{1}
,
p^{\prime}_{2}:=p_{2}
,
p^{\prime}_{0}:=\eta^{\prime}\epsilon(p^{\prime})
, and
k(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}):=\frac{\exp(\operatorname{i}\alpha\varsigma(p_{0}-p_{3}-p^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\prime}_{3}))-1}{\operatorname{i}\pi\varsigma(p_{0}-p_{3}-p^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\prime}_{3})}
. A similar formula regarding
P^{shell}(M)
is available if
M
is a Borel set with finite Lebesgue measure.

Let us sketch how to get the shell representation. Let
\mathfrak{e}:=(1,0,0,1)
, consider the bijection
l:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{4}/\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
,
l(p):=(0,p)+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
and endow
\mathbb{R}^{4}/\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
with the measure
l(\lambda)
, where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then
\psi\mapsto\varphi:=\mathcal{F}(\psi\circ j)\circ l^{-1}
defines an isomorphism
\iota:L^{2}(\chi,H_{\textsc{u}})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4}/\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e},H_{\textsc{u}})
. For integrable
\psi
one has
\varphi(\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})=\big(\frac{1}{2\pi}\big)^{3/2}\int\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{y}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}\psi(\mathfrak{y})\operatorname{d}^{3}y
with
\mathfrak{y}:=j(y)
. Let
\hat{V}:=\iota V^{can}\iota^{-1}
. It is easy to verify
\big(\hat{V}(\mathfrak{a},A)\varphi\big)(\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})=|A_{11}|\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}U(A)\varphi(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})
.

Put
\mathcal{K}^{\eta}:=\{\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}:\operatorname{sgn}(p_{0}-p_{3})=\eta\}
and
\mathcal{K}:=\mathcal{K}^{+}\cup\mathcal{K}^{-}
. Then
\mathcal{K}=(\mathbb{R}^{4}/\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})\setminus(\chi/\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})
, where
\chi/\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
is a
l(\lambda)
-null set.

Now we pass to the double shell by the bijection
k:\mathcal{O}^{m}\to\mathcal{K}
,
k(\mathfrak{p}):=\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
. Indeed,
k|_{\mathcal{O}^{m,\eta}}\to\mathcal{K}^{\eta}
is a bijection since
\operatorname{sgn}(p_{0})=\operatorname{sgn}(p_{0}-p_{3})
for every
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{m}
and since
\mathcal{O}^{m,\eta}\cap(\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})=\{\mathfrak{p}+\frac{m^{2}-\,\mathfrak{p}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}{2(p_{0}-p_{3})}\mathfrak{e}\}
is a one-point set (and not empty) just for every
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
with
\operatorname{sgn}(p_{0}-p_{3})=\eta
. Moreover,
k^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})=\mathfrak{p}+\frac{m^{2}-\,\mathfrak{p}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}{2(p_{0}-p_{3})}\mathfrak{e}
for every
\mathfrak{p}\not\in\chi
.

One claims that
\kappa:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4}/\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e},H_{\textsc{u}})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{m},H_{\textsc{u}})
,
\kappa(\varphi):=\sqrt{2|p_{0}-p_{3}|}\varphi\circ k
is an isomorphism. Indeed, note first
\kappa^{-1}(\phi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2|p_{0}-p_{3}|}}\phi\circ k^{-1}
. Then, on the one hand
\parallel\!\!\varphi\!\!\parallel^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{K}}\parallel\!\!\varphi(\cdot)\!\!\parallel^{2}\operatorname{d}l(\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{p_{3}=0\}}\parallel\!\!\varphi\circ l(\cdot)\!\!\parallel^{2}\operatorname{d}\lambda
. On the other hand
\parallel\!\!\kappa(\varphi)\!\!\parallel^{2}=\sum_{\eta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}2(\epsilon(p)-\eta p_{3})

\parallel\!\!\varphi\big((\eta\epsilon(p),p)+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}\big)\!\!\parallel^{2}\frac{\operatorname{d}^{3}p}{2\epsilon(p)}=\sum_{\eta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\epsilon(p)-\eta p_{3}}{\epsilon(p)}\parallel\!\!\varphi\circ l(T^{\eta}(p)\!\!\parallel^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}p
with the coordinate transformation
T^{\eta}(p):=p-\eta\epsilon(p)e_{3}
. Note
T^{+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times]-\infty,0[
,

T^{-}(\mathbb{R}^{3})=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times]0,\infty[
, and the Jacobian determinant equals
1-\eta\frac{p_{3}}{\epsilon(p)}
. Hence by the substitution rule one has
\parallel\!\!\kappa(\varphi)\!\!\parallel^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times]-\infty,0[}\parallel\!\!\varphi\circ l(p^{\prime})\!\!\parallel^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}p^{\prime}+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times]0,\infty[}\parallel\!\!\varphi\circ l(p^{\prime})\!\!\parallel^{2}\operatorname{d}^{3}p^{\prime}
, whence the claim.

Finally, one easily verifies
V^{shell}=\kappa\hat{V}\kappa^{-1}
as indicated in (a). Also the formula regarding
P^{shell}(M)=\kappa\iota\,P^{can}(M)(\kappa\iota)^{-1}
in (b) follows by a similar computation.

Cone representation.
We will need also the cone representation
(V^{cone},P^{cone})

acting on
L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{0},H_{\textsc{u}})
equivalent to
(V^{can},P^{can})
. Recall the light half-cone
\mathcal{O}^{0,\eta}
for
\eta=\pm
and the light cone
\mathcal{O}^{0}
endowed with the Lorentz invariant measure. The above formulae (a) and (b) for the shell representation hold literally for the equivalent cone representation simply putting
m=0
. In particular,
\epsilon(p)
becomes
|p|
.

One passes to the cone replacing
k
by
k_{0}:\mathcal{O}^{0}\setminus\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}\to\mathcal{K}
,
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}):=\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
. Note that
k_{0}|_{\mathcal{O}^{0,\eta}}\to\mathcal{K}^{\eta}
is a bijection since
\operatorname{sgn}(p_{0})=\operatorname{sgn}(p_{0}-p_{3})
for every
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{0}\setminus\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
and since
(\mathcal{O}^{0,\eta}\setminus\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})\cap(\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})=\{\mathfrak{p}-\frac{\mathfrak{p}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}{2(p_{0}-p_{3})}\mathfrak{e}\}
is a one-point set (and not empty) just for every
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
with
\operatorname{sgn}(p_{0}-p_{3})=\eta
. Moreover,
k_{0}^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}+\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e})=\mathfrak{p}-\frac{\mathfrak{p}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}{2(p_{0}-p_{3})}\mathfrak{e}
for every
\mathfrak{p}\not\in\chi
, and note that
\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e}
in a null set of
\mathcal{O}^{0}
. Then like in the shell case one shows that
\kappa_{0}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4}/\mathbb{R}\mathfrak{e},H_{\textsc{u}})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{m},H_{\textsc{u}})
,
\kappa_{0}(\varphi):=\sqrt{2|p_{0}-p_{3}|}\varphi\circ k_{0}
is an isomorphism with
\kappa_{0}^{-1}(\phi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2|p_{0}-p_{3}|}}\phi\circ k_{0}^{-1}
.

Finally, one verifies
V^{cone}=\kappa_{0}\hat{V}\kappa_{0}^{-1}
and
P^{shell}(M)=\kappa_{0}\iota\,P^{can}(M)(\kappa_{0}\iota)^{-1}
as claimed.

30.4.
Energy and shell representation of the Dirac system and of
W^{D}|_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}}

The energy representation
(W^{\textsc{d}\,erg},E^{\textsc{d}\,erg})
of the Dirac system is related to the momentum representation Eq. (
13.1
) by the unitary self-adjoint transformation
Y(=Y^{-1})
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})

given by

(Y\varphi)(p)=Y(p)\varphi(p),\;Y(p):=\left(\frac{m}{2\epsilon(p)}\right)^{1/2}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}Q(\mathfrak{p}^{+})&Q(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{-1}\\
Q(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{-1}&-Q(\mathfrak{p}^{+})\end{array}\right)

where
\mathfrak{p}^{+}=(\epsilon(p),p)
and
Q(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
denotes the canonical cross section given in (
3.3
). The transformation
Y
commutes with the representation of the Euclidean group due to the covariance
\operatorname{diag}(B,B)Y(p)\operatorname{diag}(B^{*},B^{*})=Y(B\cdot p)
, and diagonalizes the time translations since
h(p)=Y(p)\operatorname{diag}\big(\epsilon(p),-\epsilon(p)\big)Y(p)^{-1}
. For
(W^{\textsc{d}\,erg},E^{\textsc{d}\,erg}):=Y^{-1}(W^{\textsc{d}\,mom},E^{\textsc{d}\,mom})Y
one has

W^{\textsc{d}\,erg}=\oplus_{\eta}W^{\textsc{d}\,\eta},\;\big(W^{\textsc{d}\,\eta}(t,b,A)\varphi_{\eta}\big)(p)=\sqrt{\epsilon(q^{\eta})/\epsilon(p)}\,\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}(\eta t\epsilon(p)-bp)}R(\mathfrak{p}^{\eta},A)\varphi_{\eta}(q^{\eta})

where
W^{\textsc{d}\,\eta}
acts on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, cf. Eq. (
3.6
) for
j=1/2
. The computations use the formulae regarding the canonical cross section in (
3.3
) and the identity

\pi^{\eta}(p)=\frac{1}{2}\big(I_{2}+\frac{\eta}{\epsilon(p)}h^{\textsc{d}}(p)\big)=\frac{m}{2\epsilon(p)}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}Q(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{2\eta}&\eta I_{2}\\
\eta I_{2}&Q(\mathfrak{p}^{+})^{-2\eta}\end{array}\right)

As to the shell representation consider the isomorphism
\xi:L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{m},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{4})
with
(\xi\phi)(p):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\epsilon(p)}}\big(\phi(\mathfrak{p}^{+}),\phi(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\big)
and
(\xi^{-1}\varphi)(\mathfrak{p})=\sqrt{2\epsilon(p)}\varphi_{\eta}(p)
with
\varphi=(\varphi_{+},\varphi_{-})
and
\varphi_{\eta}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Then
(W^{\textsc{d}\,shell},E^{\textsc{d}\,shell}):=\xi^{-1}(W^{\textsc{d}\,erg},E^{\textsc{d}\,erg})\xi
acts on
L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{m},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and

\big(W^{\textsc{d}\,shell}(\mathfrak{a},A)\phi\big)(\mathfrak{p})=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}R(\mathfrak{p},A)\phi(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})

A last unitary transformation is performed in order to cast
W^{\textsc{d}}|_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}}
into the form
V^{shell}
in sec.
30.3
, namely
(W^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{d}\,shell},E^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{d}\,shell}):=Z^{-1}(W^{\textsc{d}\,shell},E^{\textsc{d}\,shell})Z
with
Z:L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{m},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{m},\mathbb{C}^{2})

(Z\phi)(\mathfrak{p})=Z(\mathfrak{p})\phi(\mathfrak{p}),\;Z(\mathfrak{p}):=(m/|p_{0}-p_{3}|)^{1/2}\left(Q(\mathfrak{p})\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&1\end{array}\big)}+\eta\,Q(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&0\end{array}\big)}\right)

Check
I_{2}=Z(\mathfrak{p})Z(\mathfrak{p})^{*}
using
(|p_{0}-p_{3}|/m)\,I_{2}=Q(\mathfrak{p})^{2}\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&1\end{array}\big)}+\,\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&0\end{array}\big)}Q(\mathfrak{p})^{-2}
. Finally one finds

(W^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{d}\,shell}(\mathfrak{a},A)\phi)(\mathfrak{p})=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{a}\cdot\mathfrak{p}}U(A)\phi(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})

for
(\mathfrak{a},A)\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
and
U(A):=\operatorname{diag}\big(\frac{A_{11}}{|A_{11}|},\frac{\overline{A}_{11}}{|A_{11}|}\big)
, hence
W^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{d}\,shell}|_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}}=V^{shell}
, see sec.
30.3
(a).

30.5.
Energy and cone representation of the Weyl systems and of
W^{\textsc{w}\chi}|_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}}

The energy representation
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,erg},E^{\textsc{d}\,erg})
of the Weyl system is
defined as

(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,erg},E^{\textsc{w}\,erg}):=(Y^{\chi})^{-1}(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,mom},E^{\textsc{w}\,mom})Y^{\chi}

where the momentum representation is given in Eq. (
21.1
) and the unitary transformation
Y^{\chi}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{C}^{2})
reads

(Y^{+}\varphi)(p)=B(p)\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&b(p)\end{array}\big)}\varphi(p),\,(Y^{-}\varphi)(p)=B(p)\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\big)}\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&\overline{b(p)}\end{array}\big)}\varphi(p)

with
B(p)
from Eq. (
27.8
). Cf. (
29.3
) and (
29.12
). Note that
Y^{\chi}
relatively to
Y_{0}^{-1}
has an additional unitary transformation yielding
W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,erg}
particularly clear.
Explicitly one finds for
\varphi=(\varphi_{+},\varphi_{-})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2})

\displaystyle\big(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,erg}(t,b,A)\Tiny{\big(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi_{+}\\
\varphi_{-}\end{array}\big)}\big)(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}bp}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(|q^{+}|/|p|)^{1/2}\;\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}t|p|}\;k(\mathfrak{p}^{+},A)^{\chi}\;\varphi_{+}(q^{+})\\
(|q^{-}|/|p|)^{1/2}\operatorname{e}^{-\operatorname{i}t|p|}k(\mathfrak{p}^{-},A)^{\chi}\;\varphi_{-}(q^{-})\end{array}\right)

For the computations use Eq. (
27.9
), the fact that
B(\eta p)^{-1}AB(\eta q)=A_{\ln|p|}H(\mathfrak{p},A)A_{\ln|q|}^{-1}\in E(2)
by definition of
H(\mathfrak{p},A)
, and
B(-p)=C(p)^{-1}B(p)
,
C(p)\operatorname{diag}(\alpha,\beta)=\operatorname{diag}(\beta,\alpha)C(p)
for
C(p)=-C(p)^{-1}
in
Eq. (
29.2
)(2).

One passes to the cone representation by the isomorphism
\xi_{0}:L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{0})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{C}^{2})
,
(\xi_{0}\phi)(p):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2|p|}}\big(\phi(\mathfrak{p^{+}}),\phi(\mathfrak{p^{-}})\big)
,
(\xi_{0}^{-1}\varphi)(\mathfrak{p})=\sqrt{2|p|}\,\varphi_{\eta}(p)
. For
(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,cone},E^{\textsc{w}\,cone}):=\xi^{-1}_{0}(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,erg},E^{\textsc{w}\,erg})\xi_{0}
one finds

\big(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,cone}(\mathfrak{a},A)\phi\big)(\mathfrak{p})=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{p}\cdot\mathfrak{a}}k(\mathfrak{p},A)^{\chi}\phi(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})

(cf. Eq. (
29.2
)).

A last unitary transformation is performed in order to cast
W^{\textsc{w}\chi}|_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}}
into the form
V^{cone}
in sec.
30.3
, namely
(W^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{w}\chi\,cone},E^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{w}\,cone}):=(Z^{\chi})^{-1}(W^{\textsc{w}\chi\,cone},E^{\textsc{w}\,cone})Z^{\chi}
with
Z^{\chi}:L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{0})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{0})
,
(Z^{\chi}\phi)(\mathfrak{p}):=\overline{b(\mathfrak{p})}^{\chi}\phi(\mathfrak{p})
with
b(\mathfrak{p}):=b(p)
. Then

\big(W^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{w}\chi\,cone}(\mathfrak{a},A)\phi\big)(\mathfrak{p})=\operatorname{e}^{\operatorname{i}\mathfrak{p}\cdot\mathfrak{a}}k^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p},A)^{\chi}\phi(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})

with
k^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p},A):=b(\mathfrak{p})k(\mathfrak{p},A)\overline{b(A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p})}
. Now, by the remarkable property
k^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p},A)=\frac{\overline{A}_{11}}{A_{11}}
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{O}^{0}
,
A\in ST(2)
shown below one has

W^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{w}\chi\,cone}|_{\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}}=V^{cone}

for
U(A):=\operatorname{diag}\big(\frac{\overline{A}_{11}}{|A_{11}|},\frac{A_{11}}{|A_{11}|}\big)^{\chi}
.

It remains to show
k^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p},A)=\overline{A}_{11}/A_{11}
. Let
A=CA_{\rho}A^{\prime}
with
C=\operatorname{diag}(\gamma,\overline{\gamma})
,
|\gamma|=1
and
A^{\prime}_{11}=1
according to
(
26
). Then
k(\mathfrak{p},A)=k(\mathfrak{p},CA_{\rho})k(\mathfrak{q},A^{\prime})
for
\mathfrak{q}=(CA_{\rho})^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}
. By Eq. (
29.3
),
k(\mathfrak{p},CA_{\rho})=\gamma
. Explicit computation using the formula for
\Lambda(A^{\prime})
in (
26
) shows
k(\mathfrak{q},A^{\prime})=b(q^{\prime})\overline{b(q)}
for
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}:=A^{{}^{\prime}-1}\cdot\mathfrak{q}
.
Now the assertion follows, since
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}:=A^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}
and since
q=C^{-1}\cdot p
, whence
b(q)=\gamma^{2}b(p)
by a short computation.

30.6.
Extension of a positive-operator-valued content to a POM

The main result is (
30.6
). By physical reasons we are interested in the corollary (
30.6
).

(164) Theorem.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an algebra of subsets of a set
X
,
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{Q}
, and
G
a positive-operator-valued content on
\mathcal{Q}
. Let
m
be a normalized content on
\mathcal{Q}
controlling
G
, i.e.,

m(M_{i})\to 0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad G(M_{i})\to 0

as strong limit for every sequence
(M_{i})_{i}
in
\mathcal{Q}
. Then there is a POM
\hat{G}
on
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
extending
G
if and only if
m
is
\sigma
-additive. The extension
\hat{G}
is unique. Finally, if
G
is projection-valued then
\hat{G}
is a PM.

Proof.
Let
\hat{G}
be a POM extending
G
. By
\sigma
-additivity of
\hat{G}
one has
\lim_{i}G(M_{i})=\lim_{i}\hat{G}(M_{i})=0

for
(M_{i})
in
\mathcal{Q}
with
M_{i}\downarrow\emptyset
. Hence
\lim_{i}m(M_{i})=0
. Therefore
m
is
\sigma
-additive (see e.g.
[
81
, 3.2]
).

Conversely, let
m
be
\sigma
-additive. There exists a unique extension
\hat{m}
of
m
on
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfying

\hat{m}(\Delta)=\inf\big\{\sum_{j}m(K_{j}):K_{j}\in\mathcal{Q},\Delta\subset\bigcup_{j}K_{j}\big\}

\forall\;\Delta\in\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
(see e.g.
[
81
, 5.2, 5.7]
).
Put

\mathcal{C}(\Delta):=\big\{(M_{ij}):M_{ij}\in\mathcal{Q},\;M_{ij}\downarrow_{i},\;M_{ij}\uparrow_{j}\cup_{j}M_{ij}\supset\Delta,\;\hat{m}(\Delta)=\hat{m}(\cap_{i}\cup_{j}M_{ij})\big\}

(1)

It follows
\mathcal{C}(\Delta)\neq\emptyset
. Indeed, note first that there are
K_{ij}\in\mathcal{Q}
with
\Delta\subset\cup_{j}K_{ij}
and
\hat{m}(\Delta)=\inf_{i}\big(\sum_{j}m(K_{ij})\big)
. Then
\hat{m}(\Delta)\leq\hat{m}(\cup_{j}K_{ij})\leq\sum_{j}m(K_{ij})
, whence
\hat{m}(\Delta)=\inf_{i}\hat{m}(\cup_{j}K_{ij})
. Put
L_{ij}:=\cup_{l=1}^{j}K_{il}
and finally let
M_{ij}:=\cap_{l=1}^{i}L_{lj}
. Now verify
\cup_{j}M_{ij}=\cap_{l=1}^{i}\cup_{j}L_{lj}=\cap_{l=1}^{i}\cup_{j}K_{lj}
. Then it is easy to show
(M_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta)
. —
From (1) it follows immediately

\forall\,(M_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta),\;i,n\in\mathbb{N}\;\;\exists\;i_{n},\,j_{n,i}\in\mathbb{N}:\;\;\;\hat{m}(\Delta\vartriangle M_{ij})\leq\textnormal{\tiny{$\frac{1}{n}$}}\;\;\;\forall\;i\geq i_{n},\,j\geq j_{n,i}

(2)

Put

\hat{G}(\Delta):=\lim_{i}\lim_{j}G(M_{ij})\quad\forall\Delta\in\hat{\mathcal{Q}},\;(M_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta)

(3)

According to
[
82
, 4.28 (b)]
the strong limits exist and yield bounded operators. Since
0\leq G(M_{ij})\leq I
also
0\leq\hat{G}(\Delta)\leq I
holds. If
G(M_{ij})
are projections then
\hat{G}(\Delta)
is a projection by
[
82
, 4.32]
.

(a) First one shows that
\hat{G}(\Delta)
in (3) does not depend on the choice of
(M_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta)
.
Let
(M^{l}_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta)
,
l=1,2
. Then
(M^{0}_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta)
for
M^{0}_{ij}:=M^{1}_{ij}\cap M^{2}_{ij}
. Let
\hat{G}^{l}(\Delta):=\lim_{i}\lim_{j}G(M^{l}_{ij})
,
l=0,1,2
. Obviously
\hat{G}^{0}(\Delta)\leq\hat{G}^{1}(\Delta)
. Assume
\hat{G}^{0}(\Delta)<\hat{G}^{1}(\Delta)
. Then there exists a vector
\varphi
with

\langle\varphi,\hat{G}^{1}(\Delta)\varphi\rangle-\langle\varphi,\hat{G}^{0}(\Delta)\varphi\rangle=:2c>0
. From (3) for
\hat{G}^{l}(\Delta)
,
l=0,1
one infers that there exists
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
a
j_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\langle\varphi,G(M^{1}_{ij}\setminus M^{0}_{ij})\varphi\rangle\geq c
for all
i\geq i_{0}
and
j\geq j_{i}
. This and (2) applied to
(M^{l}_{ij})
,
l=0,1
imply that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there are
i_{n},j_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
L^{l}_{n}:=M^{l}_{i_{n}j_{n}}
satisfies

\langle\varphi,G(L^{1}_{n}\setminus L^{0}_{n})\varphi\rangle\geq c
and

m(L^{1}_{n}\setminus L^{0}_{n})=\hat{m}(\Delta\vartriangle L^{1}_{n}\vartriangle\Delta\vartriangle L^{0}_{n})\leq\hat{m}(\Delta\vartriangle L^{1}_{n})+\hat{m}(\Delta\vartriangle L^{0}_{n})\leq\frac{2}{n}
24
24

24

The symmetric difference
\vartriangle
is a commutative and associative
set-theoretic operation.
contradicting the assumption that
m
controls
G
. Similarly one shows
\hat{G}^{0}(\Delta)=\hat{G}^{2}(\Delta)
.

(b) Obviously (a) implies
\hat{G}|_{\mathcal{Q}}=G
. Hence, in particular,
\hat{G}(\emptyset)=0
,
\hat{G}(X)=I
.

(c) Now additivity of
\hat{G}
is shown. Let
\Delta^{l}\in\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
and
(M^{l}_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta^{l})
for
l=1,2
.
Put
M^{l}:=\cap_{i}\cup_{j}M^{l}_{ij}
.
Further put
L_{ij}:=M^{1}_{ij}\cup M^{2}_{ij}
and
K_{ij}:=M^{1}_{ij}\cap M^{2}_{ij}
. Keep in mind
M_{ij}\downarrow_{i}
,
M_{ij}\uparrow_{j}
and equally for
(L_{ij})
and
(K_{ij})
.

Note
\Delta^{1}\cup\Delta^{2}\subset\cap_{i}\cup_{j}L_{ij}=M^{1}\cup M^{2}
. As to the less trivial inclusion
\subset
of the equality let
x\not\in M^{1}\cup M^{2}
.
Then for
l=1,2
there exists
i_{l}
with
x\not\in\cup_{j}M^{l}_{i_{l}j}
. Hence
x\not\in(\cup_{j}M^{l}_{ij})\cup(\cup_{j}M^{l}_{ij})=\cup_{j}L_{ij}
for
i\geq i_{1},i_{2}
, whence
x\not\in\cap_{i}\cup_{j}L_{ij}
.

Note
\Delta^{1}\cap\Delta^{2}\subset\cap_{i}\cup_{j}K_{ij}=M^{1}\cap M^{2}
. Indeed, clearly

\Delta^{1}\cap\Delta^{2}\subset M^{1}\cap M^{2}
. If
x\in M^{1}\cap M^{2}
, then, for every
i
,
x\in(\cup_{j}M^{1}_{ij})\cap(\cup_{j}M^{2}_{ij})
, whence
x\in M^{1}_{ij_{1}}\cap M^{2}_{ij_{2}}
for some
j_{1},j_{2}
and hence
x\in K_{ij}
for
j\geq j_{1},j_{2}
. Therefore
x\in\cap_{i}\cup_{j}K_{ij}
. — Conversely, if

x\not\in M^{1}\cap M^{2}
, then
x\not\in M^{l}
for some
l
. Hence
x\not\in\cap_{i}\cup_{j}K_{ij}
.

Now, by these inclusions,
\hat{m}(\Delta^{1})+\hat{m}(\Delta^{2})=\hat{m}(\Delta^{1}\cup\Delta^{2})+\hat{m}(\Delta^{1}\cap\Delta^{2})\leq\hat{m}(M^{1}\cup M^{2})+\hat{m}(M^{1}\cap M^{2})=\hat{m}(M^{1})+\hat{m}(M^{2})=\hat{m}(\Delta^{1})+\hat{m}(\Delta^{2})
. It follows
\hat{m}(\Delta^{1}\cup\Delta^{2})=\hat{m}(M^{1}\cup M^{2})
and
\hat{m}(\Delta^{1}\cap\Delta^{2})=\hat{m}(M^{1}\cap M^{2})
. This shows that
(L_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta^{1}\cup\Delta^{2})
and
(K_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta^{1}\cap\Delta^{2})
.

By the last result one may perform the limits in (3) for each summand of
G(L_{ij})+G(K_{ij})=G(M^{1}_{ij})+G(M^{2}_{ij})
thus obtaining the additivity of
\hat{G}
.

(d) The claim is
\lim_{l\to\infty}\hat{G}(\Delta^{l})=0
for
(\Delta^{l})
in
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
with
\Delta^{l}\downarrow_{l}\emptyset
, which together with additivity is equivalent to
\sigma
-additivity of
\hat{G}
. Assume
\lim_{l\to\infty}\hat{G}(\Delta^{l})>0
. Then there exists a vector
\varphi
with

\langle\varphi,\hat{G}(\Delta^{l})\varphi\rangle=:2c>0
for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(M^{l}_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta^{l})
. Then, according to (3), for
l
there exists
i_{l}
and for every
i
a
j_{l,i}
such that
\langle\varphi,G(M^{l}_{ij})\varphi\rangle\geq c
for all
i\geq i_{l}
and
j\geq j_{l,i}
.
This and (2) applied to
(M^{l}_{ij})
imply that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there are
l_{n},i_{n},j_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\hat{m}(\Delta^{l_{n}})\leq\frac{1}{n}
and such that

L_{n}:=M^{l_{n}}_{i_{n}j_{n}}
satisfies

\langle\varphi,G(L_{n})\varphi\rangle\geq c
and

m(L_{n})=\hat{m}(\Delta^{l_{n}}\vartriangle L_{n}\vartriangle\Delta^{l_{n}})\leq\hat{m}(\Delta^{l_{n}}\vartriangle L_{n})+\hat{m}(\Delta^{l_{n}})\leq\frac{2}{n}
. This contradicts the assumption that
m
controls
G
.

(e) It remains to show uniqueness of the extension
\hat{G}
. For every vector
\varphi
,

M\mapsto\langle\varphi,G(M)\varphi\rangle
is a finite
\sigma
-additive content on
\mathcal{Q}
. It allows a unique extension to a measure on
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}

(see e.g.
[
81
, 5.7]
). Therefore this extension coincides with
\Delta\mapsto\langle\varphi,\hat{G}(\Delta)\varphi\rangle
. This ends the proof.∎

A normalized content controlling
G
is easily available, namely

(165) Lemma.
Let the Hilbert space be separable and
\{\varphi_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
a total set of unit vectors.
Then
m(M):=\sum_{n}2^{-n}\langle\varphi_{n},G(M)\varphi_{n}\rangle
defines a normalized content
m
controlling
G
in
(
30.6
)
.

Proof.

m(M_{i})\to 0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad G(M_{i})\varphi_{n}\to 0
for every
n
by dominated convergence, since generally
\langle\varphi,G(M)\varphi\rangle=\parallel\!\!\sqrt{G(M)}\varphi\!\!\parallel^{2}
and
\parallel\!\!G(M)\varphi\!\!\parallel\leq\parallel\!\!\sqrt{G(M)}\varphi\!\!\parallel
using
\parallel\!\!G(M)\!\!\parallel\leq 1
. Hence

G(M_{i})\varphi\to 0
for a dense set of vectors
\varphi
, whence
G(M_{i})\to 0
.∎

Recall that the unions of finitely many disjoint boxes
\langle a,b[:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:a_{k}\leq x_{k}<b_{k},k=1,\dots,d\}
,
a,b\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
form an algebra
\mathcal{Q}^{d}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which generates the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(cf. the proof of (
26
)).

(166) Lemma.
Let
m
be a finite content on
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{Q}^{d})
, which is translation quasi-invariant, i.e., for every
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and every sequence
(M_{i})_{i}
in
\mathcal{Q}^{d}

m(M_{i})\to 0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad m(b+M_{i})\to 0

Moreover, for every box
B
and every
\epsilon>0
there is a bounded box
B^{\prime}\subset B
such that
m(B\setminus B^{\prime})<\epsilon
. Then
m
is
\sigma
-additive.

Proof.

(a) Let
B:=\langle a,b[\,\in\mathcal{Q}^{d}
be a non-empty box with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\epsilon>0
. The claim is that there is
b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
a_{k}<b^{\prime}_{k}<b_{k}
,
k=1,\dots,d
such that for
B^{\prime}:=\langle a,b^{\prime}[

m(B)\leq m(B^{\prime})+\epsilon

Indeed, let
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
c_{k}<b_{k}
,
k=1,\dots,d
and put
\Delta(c,b):=\langle a,b[\,\setminus\langle a,c[
. Obviously, the result follows if

\inf_{c}m(\Delta(c,b))=0
. Assume the contrary and a fortiori
a=(-\infty,\dots,-\infty)
. Then by translation quasi-invariance
\inf_{c}m(d+\Delta(c,b))>0
for every
d\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Choose
d:=tf
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
f:=(1,1,\dots,1)
. Then
d+\Delta(c,b)=\Delta(c+tf,b+tf)
. Since
]0,1[
is uncountable, there are
\delta>0
and a strictly monotonic sequence
(t_{n})
in
]0,1[
such that
\inf_{c}m(\Delta(c+t_{n}f,b+t_{n}f))\geq\delta
for all
n
. If
(t_{n})
is increasing then
\Delta\big(b+\frac{1}{2}(t_{n+1}+t_{n})f,b+t_{n+1}f\big)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
are mutually disjoint and contained in
\langle a,b+f[
. Each set has content
\geq\delta
. Hence additivity of
m
implies the contradiction
m(\langle a,b+f[\,)=\infty
. If
(t_{n})
is decreasing then consider
\big(\Delta(b+\frac{1}{2}(t_{n+1}+t_{n})f,b+t_{n}f)\big)_{n}
.

(b) Due to (a) and by the assumption on
m
it follows that generally for every non-empty box
B=\langle a,b\,[
and every
\epsilon>0
there are
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
a_{k}\leq a^{\prime}_{k}<b^{\prime}_{k}<b_{k}

\forall

k
and
m(B)\leq m(B^{\prime})+\epsilon
. Hence the closure of
B^{\prime}
is compact and contained in
B
. Now, by a well-known compactness argument we show for
m
the continuity from above which is equivalent to
\sigma
-additivity.

(c) Let
(M_{n})_{n}
be a decreasing sequence in
\mathcal{Q}^{d}
such that
\delta:=\lim_{n}m(M_{n})>0
. One has to show
\bigcap_{n}M_{n}\neq\emptyset
.
Recall that the figure
M_{n}
is the union of finitely many disjoint boxes
B
. Replace every
B
by some
B^{\prime}
as above forming a new figure
M^{\prime}_{n}\subset M_{n}
such that

m(M_{n})-m(M_{n}^{\prime})\leq 2^{-n}\delta

Let
L_{n}:=M_{1}^{\prime}\cap\dots\cap M_{n}^{\prime}
. Then
(L_{n})_{n}
is a decreasing sequence in
\mathcal{Q}^{d}
with
\overline{L}_{n}\subset\overline{M}_{n}^{\prime}\subset M_{n}
. In particular,
\overline{L}_{n}
is compact. It suffices to show

\overline{L}_{n}\neq\emptyset
for every
n
, since then by the finite intersection property
\emptyset\neq\bigcap_{n}\overline{L}_{n}\subset\bigcap_{n}M_{n}
.

Assume
\overline{L}_{n}=\emptyset
and hence
L_{n}=\emptyset
for some
n
. Then
\delta\leq m(M_{n})=m(M_{n}\setminus L_{n})=m\big(\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}(M_{n}\setminus M_{k}^{\prime})\big)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}m(M_{n}\setminus M_{k}^{\prime})\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}m(M_{k}\setminus M_{k}^{\prime})\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}2^{-k}\delta
, which is a contradiction. Hence the result follows.∎

(167) Corollary.
Let the Hilbert space be separable. Let
G
be a positive-operator-valued content on
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{Q}^{d})
. Let
U(b)
be unitary with
U(b)G(M)U(b)^{-1}=G(b+M)
for all
M\in\mathcal{Q}^{d}
,
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Moreover, let for every box
B
, every vector
\varphi
, and every
\epsilon>0
exist a bounded box
B^{\prime}\subset B
with
\langle\varphi,G(B\setminus B^{\prime})\varphi\rangle\leq\epsilon
.

Then there is a unique POM
T
on the Borel sets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
G=T|_{\mathcal{Q}^{d}}
. If
G
is projection-valued, then
T
is a PM. If
S
is a Borel-algebra homomorphism with
S(\mathcal{Q}^{d})\subset\mathcal{Q}^{d}
and
U(S)
unitary with
U(S)G(M)U(S)^{-1}=G\big(S(M)\big)
for all
M\in\mathcal{Q}^{d}
, then one has
U(S)T(\Delta)U(S)^{-1}=T\big(S(\Delta)\big)
for all Borel sets
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.
Let
m
be the normalized content on
\mathcal{Q}^{d}
controlling
G
from (
30.6
). Then
m
is translation quasi-invariant. Indeed, let
(M_{i})_{i}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{Q}^{d}
,
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\lim_{i}m(M_{i})=0\Rightarrow\lim_{i}G(M_{i})=0\Rightarrow\lim_{i}G(b+M_{i})=\lim_{i}U(b)G(M_{i})U(b)^{-1}=0\Rightarrow\lim_{i}m(b+M_{i})=0
.

We like to apply (
30.6
). Let
\epsilon>0
. Let
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
with
\sum_{n>n^{\prime}}2^{-n}<\frac{\epsilon}{2}
. Let
B
be a box. By assumption there is a bounded box
B_{1}\subset B
with

\langle\varphi_{1},G(B\setminus B_{1})\varphi_{1}\rangle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
.Then, by monotony of
G
, there is a bounded box
B_{1}\subset B_{2}\subset B
with
\langle\varphi_{2},G(B\setminus B_{2})\varphi_{2}\rangle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
, and so on. Hence there is a bounded box
B^{\prime}\subset B
such that
\langle\varphi_{n},G(B\setminus B^{\prime})\varphi_{n}\rangle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
for all
n=1,\dots,n^{\prime}
. It follows
m(B\setminus B^{\prime})<\epsilon
.
Hence
m
is
\sigma
-additive by (
30.6
).

Thus, by (
30.6
), there is the unique POM
T
as asserted, which is an PM if
G
is projection-valued.

As to the last part of the assertion one has
m(M_{i})\to 0\;\Leftrightarrow\;m(S(M_{i}))\to 0
for all
(M_{i})
in
{\mathcal{Q}^{d}}
. Let
\Delta\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be Borel,
(M_{ij})\in\mathcal{C}(\Delta)
, and
(L_{n}):=(M_{i_{n}j_{n}})
such that
\hat{m}(\Delta\vartriangle L_{n})\to 0
(cf. the proof of (
30.6
), in particular (1) and (2)). Therefore
\hat{m}\big(S(\Delta)\vartriangle S(L_{n})\big)\to 0
, whence easily
\big(S(M_{ij})\big)\in\mathcal{C}\big(S(\Delta)\big)
. Therefore, by (3) of the proof of (
30.6
),
T\big(S(\Delta)\big)=\lim_{i}\lim_{j}T\big(S(M_{ij})\big)=\lim_{i}\lim_{j}U(S)T(M_{ij})U(S)^{-1}=U(S)T(\Delta)U(S)^{-1}
.∎

The following example shows that
G
in (
30.6
) need not be
\sigma
-additive without the assumption that
G
is determinated by its values at bounded boxes. More precisely, there is a translation-covariant non-
\sigma
-additive projection-valued content
G
on
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{Q}^{d})
in any Hilbert space
\neq\{0\}
. Hence, in particular,
[
1
, Theorem A5]
is not generally valid.

Let
R_{n}:=\{x:x_{i}\geq n,i=1,\dots,d\},n\in\mathbb{N}
. For
M\in\mathcal{Q}^{d}
set
G(M):=I
if
R_{n}\subset M
for some
n
and
G(M):=0
otherwise. Now let
M=B_{1}\cup\dots\cup B_{k}
with disjoint boxes
B_{l}
. The claim is that
R_{n_{0}}\subset M
for some
n_{0}
if and only if there is just one box
B_{l}
containing some
R_{m}
. A moment of reflection shows that this implies the additivity of
G
. — As to the claim note first that at most one box may contain some
R_{m}
since the boxes are disjoint. Now let
S:=\{(m,\dots,m):m\in\mathbb{N},m\geq n_{0}\}
. As
S\subset M
there is some
B_{l}=\langle a,b[
such that
B_{l}\cap S
is infinite. This implies for every
i
that
m<b_{i}
for infinitely many
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence
b_{i}=\infty
for
i=1,\dots,d
. Thus
R_{m}\subset B_{l}
for
m\geq\max_{i}a_{i}
proving the claim. — Now let
U(b)
for
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any unitary operator. Since obviously
G(b+M)=0\Leftrightarrow G(M)=0
, translational covariance holds. — Finally,
G
is not
\sigma
-additive, as e.g. the boxes
B_{n}:=\langle(n-1,\dots,n-1),(n,\dots,n)[
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
are disjoint, their union is
B:=\langle(0,\dots,0),(\infty,\dots,\infty)[
, but
G(B_{n})=0
,
G(B)=I
.
